Part IAncient India

14 units29 verified PYQs119 self-test questions

Part I · Unit I01Sources and How Historians WorkAll periods (focus: ancient India)

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Sources of ancient Indian history: literary, archaeological, epigraphic, numismatic, foreign accounts

Mains: GS-I: Indian history — the basis for every ancient and medieval topic

Rarely a full Mains question by itself, but source facts (decipherment, first inscriptions, foreign travellers) are steady Prelims material and strengthen every answer.

The big picture

History is built from evidence. For ancient India, written texts are few, late or religious, so historians combine four kinds of sources: literary (religious and secular texts), epigraphic (inscriptions), numismatic (coins) and archaeological (sites and objects), plus accounts of foreign visitors.

Each source has a bias: court poets praise their patrons, religious texts present ideals, foreign visitors misunderstand. The historian's craft is to compare sources, date them and ask who wrote them and why.

Exam focus

A good Mains answer cites evidence: "The Allahabad pillar inscription shows…" is stronger than a bare claim. This unit gives you the vocabulary of evidence for the whole notebook.

WhenWhat happened
1784Asiatic Society of Bengal founded by William Jones
1837James Prinsep deciphers Brahmi (Ashokan edicts)
1861Archaeological Survey of India; Alexander Cunningham first Director-General
1902John Marshall becomes Director-General of the ASI
1915Ashoka named "Devanampiya Ashoka" in the Maski edict
1921–22Harappa (Daya Ram Sahni) and Mohenjodaro (R.D. Banerji) excavated

Core notes

I01.1 Literary sources

TypeExamplesValue and limits
Vedic literatureFour Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas, UpanishadsMain source for c. 1500–600 BCE; religious, little chronology
Epics and PuranasRamayana, Mahabharata; 18 PuranasDynastic lists (Puranas) useful but composed and revised over centuries
Buddhist textsTripitaka, Jatakas, Dipavamsa, Mahavamsa (Sri Lanka)Social and economic life of the 6th c. BCE onwards
Jain textsAgamas, Kalpasutra, ParishishtaparvanMahavira, Magadha, Chandragupta Maurya traditions
DharmashastrasManusmriti, Yajnavalkya SmritiNorms of society and law — ideals, not always practice
Secular worksArthashastra, Mudrarakshasa, Harshacharita (Banabhatta), Rajatarangini (Kalhana, 1148)Kalhana's work is the closest to a critical history in ancient India
Sangam literatureTamil poems (c. 3rd c. BCE – 3rd c. CE)Society of the far south (I09)

I01.2 Inscriptions and coins

Epigraphy (the study of inscriptions) gives dated, contemporary, official evidence.

  • Scripts: Brahmi (written left to right; ancestor of most Indian scripts) and Kharosthi (right to left, north-west). James Prinsep deciphered Brahmi in 1837.
  • Texts as sources: the Critical Edition of the Mahabharata, led by V.S. Sukthankar at the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute, Pune (1919–1966), compared manuscripts from all over India and showed both a common core and wide regional variation. B.B. Lal's excavation at Hastinapura (1951–52) tried to match the Mahabharata with archaeology.
  • Earliest deciphered inscriptions: Ashoka's edicts (3rd c. BCE), mostly in Prakrit; also Greek and Aramaic in the north-west.
  • Key inscriptions: Hathigumpha (Kharavela), Junagadh (Rudradaman, 150 CE — first long Sanskrit inscription), Allahabad pillar (Samudragupta, by Harishena), Aihole (Pulakeshin II, by Ravikirti), Uttaramerur (Chola village assemblies).
  • Land grants on copper plates (from the Gupta period) show agrarian change and the rise of landed intermediaries.

Numismatics (the study of coins): punch-marked coins (silver, from c. 6th–5th c. BCE); Indo-Greek coins with portraits and names; Kushan gold coins; Gupta gold coins (the dinara); the decline in coin finds after the Guptas is used in the debate on early medieval "feudalism".

I01.3 Archaeology

  • The Archaeological Survey of India was founded in 1861 under Alexander Cunningham, who identified many Buddhist sites using Xuanzang's account.
  • John Marshall (DG from 1902) directed the Harappan excavations; Mortimer Wheeler introduced stratigraphic digging (1940s).
  • Dating methods: stratigraphy (layers), radiocarbon (C-14), dendrochronology, thermoluminescence.
  • Archaeology is the only source for prehistory and the Harappan civilisation, whose script is undeciphered.

I01.4 Foreign accounts

VisitorPeriodWork and value
Herodotus5th c. BCEHistories: north-west India under the Persians
MegasthenesChandragupta Maurya's courtIndica (survives in quotations): Pataliputra, "seven castes"
Author of the Periplus of the Erythraean Sea1st c. CEPorts and Indo-Roman trade
Ptolemy, Pliny1st–2nd c. CEGeography; Pliny complained of Roman gold drain to India
Faxian (Fa-hien)Chandragupta II (c. 399–414)Buddhism, peaceful Gupta society; did not name the king
Xuanzang (Hiuen Tsang)Harsha (c. 630–645)Si-yu-ki; studied at Nalanda
Yijing (I-tsing)Late 7th c.Nalanda; Buddhist practice
Al-BiruniWith Mahmud of GhazniKitab-ul-Hind (c. 1030): Indian science, religion, society. Born in Khwarizm (973), taken to Ghazni as a hostage (1017), learnt Sanskrit; wrote in Arabic, 80 chapters. Named three barriers to understanding India: language, religious difference and local insularity. Relied on Brahmanical texts and accepted their account of caste, but rejected the idea of pollution as against nature
Debate

Foreign accounts are valuable outsiders' views but often misread Indian society: Megasthenes' "seven castes" mixes occupation with varna, and his claim that India had no slavery contradicts Indian texts.

What historians say

Colonial scholars (James Mill, V.A. Smith) saw ancient India as static and despotic; nationalist historians (R.C. Majumdar, K.P. Jayaswal) stressed its glories; Marxist historians (D.D. Kosambi, R.S. Sharma, Romila Thapar) used sources to study economy and society. Kosambi combined texts, archaeology and fieldwork. Today historians such as Upinder Singh stress weighing all source types together.

Prelims fact bank

  • James Prinsep deciphered Brahmi in 1837.
  • Kharosthi was written right to left; Brahmi left to right.
  • The Asiatic Society of Bengal was founded by William Jones in 1784.
  • The ASI was founded in 1861; Alexander Cunningham was its first Director-General.
  • The Maski edict (found 1915) names Ashoka directly.
  • Rudradaman's Junagadh inscription (150 CE) is the first long Sanskrit inscription.
  • Harishena composed the Allahabad pillar inscription of Samudragupta.
  • Ravikirti composed the Aihole inscription of Pulakeshin II.
  • Kalhana wrote the Rajatarangini (1148) on Kashmir.
  • Megasthenes wrote Indica at Chandragupta Maurya's court.
  • Faxian visited during the reign of Chandragupta II.
  • Xuanzang visited during Harsha's reign and studied at Nalanda.
  • Al-Biruni wrote Kitab-ul-Hind.
  • Al-Biruni's Kitab-ul-Hind, in Arabic, has 80 chapters.
  • The Periplus of the Erythraean Sea describes 1st-century Indo-Roman trade.
Exam traps
  • Prinsep deciphered Brahmi; the Harappan script is still undeciphered.
  • NCERT Class 12 gives 1838 for Prinsep's decipherment of Brahmi; most references give 1837. If both appear in options, prefer 1837.
  • Faxian (Gupta) ≠ Xuanzang (Harsha) ≠ Yijing (late 7th c.).
  • Junagadh: Rudradaman (Sanskrit, 150 CE); also Skandagupta's inscription on the same rock.
  • Megasthenes' Indica survives only in quotations by later Greek writers.

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

Discuss the importance of inscriptions as a source of ancient Indian history.

How to frame it: Nature → Examples → Limits

Model answer (43 words)

Inscriptions are contemporary, often dated and official. Ashoka's edicts reveal his dhamma; the Allahabad pillar records Samudragupta's conquests; the Uttaramerur inscription details Chola village assemblies; land grants show agrarian change. Yet prashastis exaggerate, so they must be checked against coins, texts and archaeology.

2 marks · 15 words

Who was James Prinsep?

How to frame it: Identity

Model answer (13 words)

Official of the Asiatic Society who deciphered Brahmi in 1837, unlocking Ashoka's edicts.

Revision card

Remember
  • Four source types + foreign accounts.
  • Prinsep 1837 (Brahmi); Jones 1784 (Asiatic Society); ASI 1861 (Cunningham); Marshall 1902.
  • Inscriptions: Ashoka, Hathigumpha, Junagadh 150 CE, Allahabad, Aihole, Uttaramerur.
  • Coins: punch-marked → Indo-Greek → Kushan gold → Gupta dinara.
  • Visitors: Megasthenes, Periplus, Faxian, Xuanzang, Yijing, Al-Biruni.

Part I · Unit I02Prehistoryc. 2 million years ago – c. 1000 BCE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Prehistoric cultures of India: Palaeolithic, Mesolithic, Neolithic, Chalcolithic

Mains: GS-I: Ancient Indian history — prehistoric cultures

Mostly Prelims: site–phase–region matching (Bhimbetka, Mehrgarh, Burzahom, Chirand, Bagor, Adamgarh). For Rajasthan sites see R02 and R03.

The big picture

Prehistory is the long period before writing. In India it is divided by tool technology and way of life:

  1. Palaeolithic (Old Stone Age): hunter-gatherers using hand-axes, then flakes, then blades.
  2. Mesolithic (Middle Stone Age, after c. 10,000 BCE): microliths, early herding, rock art.
  3. Neolithic (New Stone Age, from c. 7000 BCE at Mehrgarh): farming, polished tools, villages.
  4. Chalcolithic (copper–stone, c. 3000–1000 BCE): village farming cultures using copper, outside and after the Harappan zone.

The key change is the shift from food gathering to food production, which made settled villages and eventually cities possible.

Exam focus

Mehrgarh pushed back the start of farming in South Asia to c. 7000 BCE and showed that the Harappan civilisation grew from local roots.

WhenWhat happened
c. 2 million–1.5 lakh years agoLower Palaeolithic (Acheulian hand-axes; Attirampakkam, Isampur)
c. 1.5 lakh–40,000 years agoMiddle Palaeolithic (flake tools; Nevasa)
c. 40,000–10,000 years agoUpper Palaeolithic (blades; Belan valley)
c. 10,000 BCEMesolithic begins; microliths
c. 7000 BCEMehrgarh: early farming and herding
c. 2700 BCEBurzahom (Kashmir) Neolithic pit dwellings
c. 3000–1000 BCEChalcolithic cultures: Ahar, Kayatha, Malwa, Jorwe
1863Robert Bruce Foote finds the first Palaeolithic tool at Pallavaram (Chennai)

Core notes

I02.1 The Palaeolithic

Robert Bruce Foote found the first Palaeolithic tool in India at Pallavaram near Chennai (1863).

PhaseToolsKey sites
LowerHand-axes, cleavers (Acheulian); choppers (Sohanian, north-west)Attirampakkam (Tamil Nadu), Isampur (Karnataka), Hunsgi, Sohan valley (Punjab, Pakistan), Didwana (Rajasthan)
MiddleFlake tools: scrapers, pointsNevasa (Maharashtra, on the Pravara), Narmada valley
UpperBlades, burins; bone toolsBelan valley (UP), Bhimbetka, Kurnool caves

Hathnora (Narmada valley, Madhya Pradesh) has yielded a hominin skull fossil ("Narmada Man").

I02.2 The Mesolithic

  • Microliths — small geometric blades used in composite tools (arrows, sickles).
  • Sites: Bagor (Rajasthan), Adamgarh (MP) — both with early domesticated animals; Sarai Nahar Rai, Mahadaha and Damdama (UP) with burials; Langhnaj (Gujarat).
  • Bhimbetka (Raisen, MP): rock shelters with paintings from the Upper Palaeolithic onward — animals, hunting, dancing; a UNESCO World Heritage Site, discovered by V.S. Wakankar (1957).

I02.3 The Neolithic

RegionSiteFeatures
North-westMehrgarh (Baluchistan)From c. 7000 BCE; wheat, barley, cattle, sheep; mud-brick houses; earliest farming in South Asia
KashmirBurzahom, GufkralPit dwellings; dogs buried with masters; bone tools
Ganga plainKoldihwa, Mahagara (Belan valley); Chirand (Bihar)Early rice (claims for Koldihwa); bone tools at Chirand
SouthBrahmagiri, Maski, Utnur, Piklihal, SanganakalluAsh mounds from cattle pens
North-eastDaojali Hading (Assam)Shouldered celts; links with South-East Asia

Lahuradewa (UP) has early evidence of rice cultivation (claims go back to c. 6000 BCE or earlier).

I02.4 Chalcolithic cultures

Farming villages using copper and stone, mostly after or outside the Harappan zone:

  • Ahar–Banas (Rajasthan: Ahar, Gilund, Balathal) — R03.
  • Kayatha (MP), Malwa culture (Navdatoli, Eran).
  • Jorwe (Maharashtra: Inamgaon, Daimabad, Nevasa) — Inamgaon had a large settlement with a chief's house; Daimabad yielded bronze sculptures (chariot, rhinoceros).
  • Ochre Coloured Pottery and Copper Hoard culture in the Ganga–Yamuna doab.

Inamgaon burials (on the Ghod, a tributary of the Bhima; occupied c. 1600–700 BCE): adults were buried lying straight with the head to the north, often inside houses, with pots of food and water; one man was buried seated in a large four-legged clay jar in the courtyard of a five-roomed house with a granary, probably a chief.

Megaliths ("big stones", from c. 1000 BCE) marked burials across the Deccan, south India, the north-east and Kashmir. Graves contain Black and Red Ware, iron tools and weapons, sometimes horse bones and gold ornaments. Types include cists (stone boxes, some with port-holes for later burials of the same family), dolmens, menhirs and urn burials. At Brahmagiri (Karnataka, excavated by Wheeler), one skeleton had 33 gold beads while others had only a few pots, showing status differences. Other sites: Hallur, Maski (Karnataka), Adichanallur (urn burials, Tamil Nadu), Junapani (Vidarbha).

Common features: black-and-red painted pottery, mud houses, cattle, cereals, child burials under floors (Maharashtra), no writing.

What historians say

H.D. Sankalia (Deccan College) and V.N. Misra built Indian prehistoric archaeology; G.R. Sharma excavated the Belan valley sites. The French–Pakistani excavations at Mehrgarh under Jean-François Jarrige (from 1974) changed the understanding of farming origins, showing a local route to food production rather than diffusion from West Asia alone.

Prelims fact bank

  • Robert Bruce Foote found the first Palaeolithic tool at Pallavaram (1863).
  • Attirampakkam (Tamil Nadu) is a major Acheulian site.
  • Sohanian chopper tools come from the Sohan valley (north-west).
  • Nevasa (Pravara river) is a type site of the Middle Palaeolithic.
  • Hathnora (Narmada valley) yielded the "Narmada Man" fossil.
  • Bhimbetka rock shelters were discovered by V.S. Wakankar.
  • Bagor and Adamgarh give early evidence of animal domestication.
  • Mehrgarh has the earliest farming in South Asia (c. 7000 BCE).
  • Burzahom (Kashmir) had pit dwellings and dog burials.
  • Chirand (Bihar) is known for Neolithic bone tools.
  • Southern Neolithic sites have ash mounds (e.g. Utnur).
  • Daimabad (Maharashtra) yielded Chalcolithic bronze sculptures.
  • Inamgaon is a major Jorwe culture site.
Exam traps
  • Mehrgarh is Neolithic (pre-Harappan), in Baluchistan — not a Harappan city.
  • Burzahom: pit dwellings (Kashmir); Chirand: bone tools (Bihar).
  • Bhimbetka is in Madhya Pradesh (Raisen district).
  • Chalcolithic = copper + stone; Bronze Age Harappan cities are separate.

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

Why is Mehrgarh important in Indian prehistory?

How to frame it: Location, date → Evidence → Significance

Model answer (42 words)

Mehrgarh (Baluchistan), from c. 7000 BCE, shows early cultivation of wheat and barley, domestication of cattle, sheep and goats, and mud-brick houses. It is the earliest farming site in South Asia and shows a continuous local development leading towards the Harappan civilisation.

2 marks · 15 words

What are ash mounds?

How to frame it: Definition

Model answer (14 words)

Mounds of burnt cattle dung from Neolithic cattle pens in south India (e.g. Utnur).

Revision card

Remember
  • Palaeolithic: Foote 1863 Pallavaram; Lower (Attirampakkam, Sohan), Middle (Nevasa), Upper (Belan); Hathnora fossil.
  • Mesolithic: microliths; Bagor, Adamgarh, Sarai Nahar Rai; Bhimbetka (Wakankar).
  • Neolithic: Mehrgarh 7000 BCE; Burzahom pits; Chirand bone tools; Koldihwa rice; south ash mounds.
  • Chalcolithic: Ahar, Kayatha, Malwa, Jorwe (Inamgaon, Daimabad).

Part I · Unit I03The Harappan Civilisationc. 2600 – 1900 BCE (mature phase)

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Harappan civilisation: sites, town planning, economy, religion, decline

Mains: GS-I: Indus–Saraswati civilisation — features and decline

A favourite at every level. Mains asks features of town planning, economy or reasons for decline; Prelims asks site–river–discoverer–find matching.

The big picture

The Harappan (Indus, or Indus–Saraswati) civilisation was South Asia's first urban culture, contemporary with Mesopotamia and Egypt. At its height it covered about 1.3 million sq km — larger than either — from Baluchistan to western UP and from Jammu to Gujarat.

It had planned cities with drains, standardised bricks and weights, a script, long-distance trade and craft specialisation — but no clear temples, palaces or royal tombs, which makes its political system a puzzle. After about 1900 BCE the cities declined and life continued in smaller villages.

Exam focus

Its uniformity across a vast area is the key analytical point: something — a state, merchants, or shared ideology — coordinated bricks, weights and town plans.

WhenWhat happened
c. 7000–3300 BCEEarly food-producing phase (Mehrgarh)
c. 3300–2600 BCEEarly Harappan regional cultures (Kot Diji, Kalibangan I)
c. 2600–1900 BCEMature Harappan
c. 1900–1300 BCELate Harappan; de-urbanisation
1921Daya Ram Sahni excavates Harappa
1922R.D. Banerji excavates Mohenjodaro
1924John Marshall announces the civilisation

Core notes

I03.1 Major sites

SiteRiver / regionDiscovererNotable finds
HarappaRavi (Punjab, Pakistan)Daya Ram Sahni, 1921Granaries, working floors, cemetery R-37
MohenjodaroIndus (Sindh)R.D. Banerji, 1922Great Bath, "granary", bronze dancing girl, "priest-king", Pashupati seal
ChanhudaroIndusN.G. Majumdar, 1931Bead-making; no citadel
KalibanganGhaggar (Rajasthan)A. Ghosh, 1952Ploughed field (pre-Harappan), fire altars
LothalBhogava (Gujarat)S.R. Rao, 1954Dockyard, bead factory, Persian Gulf seal
DholaviraKutch (Gujarat)J.P. Joshi; R.S. Bisht excavatedThree-part city, water reservoirs, signboard; UNESCO 2021
BanawaliHaryanaR.S. Bisht, 1974Toy plough
RakhigarhiHaryanaExcavations from 1960s; large-scale from 1997One of the largest Harappan sites
RoparSutlej (Punjab)Y.D. SharmaDog buried with man
SurkotadaKutchJ.P. JoshiHorse remains (disputed)
SutkagendorMakran coastAurel SteinWesternmost site
AlamgirpurHindon (UP)Y.D. SharmaEasternmost site
MandaChenab (J&K)—Northernmost
DaimabadPravara (Maharashtra)—Southernmost (with late Harappan phase)

I03.2 Town planning

  1. Grid layout: streets at right angles; main roads north–south and east–west.
  2. Two parts: a raised citadel (west) with public buildings and a lower town (east) for houses. Dholavira had three parts.
  3. Standardised baked bricks in the ratio 1:2:4.
  4. Drainage: covered drains along streets, connected to house drains — unmatched in the ancient world.
  5. Houses around courtyards, with wells, bathrooms, doors opening on side lanes.
  6. Public buildings: the Great Bath (Mohenjodaro), granaries (Harappa), dockyard (Lothal), reservoirs (Dholavira).

I03.3 Economy and society

Agriculture: wheat, barley, peas, sesame, mustard, cotton (earliest in the world; Greeks later called it sindon), rice at Lothal and Rangpur. Ploughed field at Kalibangan. Domesticated cattle, sheep, goats; the humped bull is common on seals.

Crafts: bead-making (Chanhudaro, Lothal), copper and bronze work, shell, seals of steatite, pottery (red with black paint). Chanhudaro was given over almost entirely to crafts (beads, shell, metal, seals, weights); Nageshwar and Balakot, near the coast, specialised in shell bangles, ladles and inlay. Beads used carnelian (its red colour came from firing), jasper, steatite, gold and faience, an artificial glazed material of powdered quartz or sand bound with gum, made into beads, bangles and tiny pots.

Raw materials: Lothal lay near carnelian (Bharuch), steatite (south Rajasthan, north Gujarat) and metal sources; expeditions went to the Khetri copper belt of Rajasthan (home of the Ganeshwar–Jodhpura culture) and to south India for gold. Copper also came from Oman: both Omani ore and Harappan artefacts carry traces of nickel, and a Harappan jar has been found at Omani sites.

Trade: standard weights (binary then decimal, often cubical chert); internal exchange of raw materials; external trade with Mesopotamia, which called the region Meluhha; intermediate ports Dilmun (Bahrain) and Magan (Oman). Shortughai (Afghanistan) was a trading outpost for lapis lazuli.

Society and polity: craft specialisation and differences in house size show social differences. No palaces or royal burials; scholars debate whether rule was by priests, merchants, a city-state elite or a single state.

I03.4 Religion, script and art

  • Religion: terracotta female figurines (mother goddess); the "Pashupati" seal (a horned figure surrounded by animals, identified by Marshall as proto-Shiva); worship of trees (pipal), animals (humped bull), linga-like stones; fire altars at Kalibangan and Lothal.
  • Script: about 375–400 signs, written mostly right to left (boustrophedon on some), on seals; inscriptions are short (the longest has about 26 signs) and the script is not alphabetic; still undeciphered. Dholavira has a unique large signboard of inlaid white stone signs.
  • Discovery: railway builders in the 1850s carried away Harappa's bricks for track ballast; Alexander Cunningham published a Harappan seal in 1875 but did not recognise its age; Daya Ram Sahni (Harappa, 1921) and R.D. Banerji (Mohenjodaro, 1922) found seals in pre-historic levels, and John Marshall announced the new civilisation in 1924.
  • Art: bronze "dancing girl" (lost-wax method), stone "priest-king", seals with the "unicorn", terracotta toys.
  • Burials: extended burials with pots; joint burial at Lothal.

I03.5 Decline

TheoryProponentAssessment
Aryan invasionMortimer WheelerRejected: no evidence of massacre; dates do not fit
FloodsMarshall, Mackay, S.R. RaoLocal, not general
Tectonic changes, river shiftsRaikes; othersDrying of the Ghaggar–Hakra (Saraswati) affected many sites
Climate change (aridity)Gurdip Singh; recent studiesWeakening monsoon c. 2200–1900 BCE is now widely accepted as a major factor
Ecological exhaustionFairservisOver-use of land and forests
Decline of trade—Mesopotamian trade fell off c. 1900 BCE

By about 1800 BCE most Mature Harappan sites in Cholistan (Bahawalpur) were abandoned, while new, smaller settlements grew in Gujarat, Haryana and western Uttar Pradesh. Where occupation continued after 1900 BCE, seals, weights, special beads, writing and long-distance trade disappeared: the "Late Harappan" cultures were rural.

Debate

No single cause explains decline. Historians now speak of de-urbanisation, not collapse: people moved east and south into smaller settlements, and many Harappan traits continued.

What historians say

Marshall first described the civilisation (1924); Wheeler proposed the invasion theory, later rejected. Gregory Possehl, Jonathan Mark Kenoyer and Shereen Ratnagar have rebuilt its economy and society. Ratnagar stresses trade; Kenoyer stresses crafts and ideology. The name "Indus–Saraswati" reflects the large number of sites on the dry Ghaggar–Hakra channel; its identification with the Vedic Saraswati is debated.

Prelims fact bank

  • Daya Ram Sahni excavated Harappa (1921); R.D. Banerji excavated Mohenjodaro (1922).
  • John Marshall announced the civilisation in 1924.
  • Harappa is on the Ravi; Mohenjodaro on the Indus.
  • Kalibangan was identified by A. Ghosh (1952); it has a ploughed field.
  • Lothal (Bhogava river) has a dockyard; excavated by S.R. Rao.
  • Dholavira had a three-part plan and water reservoirs; UNESCO site (2021).
  • Harappan bricks were in the ratio 1:2:4.
  • The Great Bath is at Mohenjodaro.
  • Mesopotamian texts call the Harappan region Meluhha.
  • Shortughai (Afghanistan) was a Harappan trading post.
  • The bronze dancing girl was made by the lost-wax method.
  • The Harappan script is undeciphered and mostly written right to left.
  • Mortimer Wheeler proposed the Aryan invasion theory of decline.
  • Chanhudaro had no citadel.
  • Nageshwar and Balakot were Harappan centres of shell working.
  • Alexander Cunningham reported a Harappan seal in 1875 without realising its age.
  • Faience is an artificial glazed material made from powdered quartz or sand and gum.
Exam traps
  • Kalibangan: identified by A. Ghosh (1952); Tessitori only noticed the mounds earlier.
  • Sohgaura (Gorakhpur) is known for a Mauryan-period copper plate, not a Harappan site (UPSC 2019).
  • Lothal is on the Bhogava, Harappa on the Ravi, Mohenjodaro on the Indus, Ropar on the Sutlej.
  • Chanhudaro is the only major city without a citadel.
  • The "Pashupati" identification is Marshall's interpretation, not certain.
  • Wheeler's invasion theory is rejected — do not present it as fact.

RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Describe the main features of Harappan town planning.

How to frame it: Grid → Citadel and lower town → Bricks → Drainage → Houses and public buildings → Significance

Model answer (81 words)

Harappan cities followed a grid, with streets at right angles. Most had a raised western citadel for public buildings and an eastern lower town; Dholavira had three parts. Baked bricks of standard 1:2:4 proportions were used everywhere. Covered street drains connected to house drains — an achievement unmatched in its time. Houses had courtyards, wells and bathrooms. Public structures included Mohenjodaro's Great Bath, Harappa's granaries, Lothal's dockyard and Dholavira's reservoirs. Such uniformity suggests strong civic authority and a concern for hygiene.

5 marks · 50 words

Discuss the causes of the decline of the Harappan civilisation.

How to frame it: Theories → Current view

Model answer (40 words)

Wheeler's Aryan invasion theory is rejected. Floods, river shifts — especially the drying of the Ghaggar–Hakra — a weakening monsoon, ecological exhaustion and the decline of Mesopotamian trade together caused de-urbanisation after c. 1900 BCE. Life continued in smaller settlements.

2 marks · 15 words

What was Meluhha?

How to frame it: Meaning

Model answer (12 words)

Mesopotamian name for the Harappan region, a trading partner, in cuneiform texts.

Revision card

Remember
  • Harappa 1921 (Sahni, Ravi); Mohenjodaro 1922 (Banerji, Indus); Marshall 1924.
  • Kalibangan (Ghosh, ploughed field, fire altars); Lothal (Rao, dockyard); Dholavira (3 parts, reservoirs).
  • Extremes: Sutkagendor W, Alamgirpur E, Manda N, Daimabad S.
  • Town planning: grid, citadel + lower town, 1:2:4 bricks, drains.
  • Economy: cotton, Meluhha trade, weights, beads. Religion: figurines, Pashupati seal.
  • Decline: de-urbanisation; climate, rivers, trade; invasion theory rejected.

Part I · Unit I04The Vedic Agec. 1500 – 600 BCE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Vedic literature; early and later Vedic polity, society, economy and religion

Mains: GS-I: Vedic age — society, polity, religion; Indian knowledge tradition

Comparison of early and later Vedic periods is a classic Mains question; Prelims asks Vedic literature (which Veda, which Upanishad), rivers and assemblies.

The big picture

The Vedic age is named after the Vedas, the oldest texts of India, composed in Sanskrit by people who called themselves Aryas. It has two phases:

  • Early (Rig Vedic), c. 1500–1000 BCE: pastoral, tribal society in the land of seven rivers (Sapta Sindhu, Punjab); cattle as wealth; tribal chiefs checked by assemblies; nature gods (Indra, Agni).
  • Later Vedic, c. 1000–600 BCE: movement east into the Ganga–Yamuna doab; iron and agriculture; territorial kingdoms; the varna system hardens; elaborate sacrifices; the Upanishads question ritual.
Exam focus

The shift from tribe (jana) to territory (janapada) and from cattle to land prepared the way for the Mahajanapadas (I05). Learn the comparison as a table.

WhenWhat happened
c. 1500–1000 BCERig Veda composed; Sapta Sindhu region
c. 1400 BCEBoghazkoi inscription (Anatolia) names Indra, Mitra, Varuna, Nasatyas
c. 1000 BCEPainted Grey Ware and iron in the Ganga–Yamuna doab
c. 1000–600 BCELater Vedic texts: Sama, Yajur, Atharva, Brahmanas, Upanishads

Core notes

I04.1 Vedic literature

TextContents
Rig Veda1,028 hymns in 10 mandalas; mandalas 2–7 oldest ("family books"); 10th has the Purusha Sukta (first mention of four varnas) and Nasadiya Sukta (creation). Gayatri Mantra (mandala 3, to Savitr)
Sama VedaRig Vedic hymns set to melody — root of Indian music
Yajur VedaSacrificial formulas; Shukla (white) and Krishna (black)
Atharva VedaCharms, spells, medicine, popular beliefs
BrahmanasExplanations of ritual (Shatapatha Brahmana)
Aranyakas"Forest texts": meditation on ritual
UpanishadsAbout 108; philosophy of Brahman and Atman; "Satyameva Jayate" from the Mundaka Upanishad
VedangasSix aids: shiksha (phonetics), kalpa (ritual), vyakarana (grammar), nirukta (etymology), chhanda (metre), jyotisha (astronomy)

The Vedas are called shruti ("heard"); later texts (smritis, epics, Puranas) are smriti ("remembered").

UpanishadVedaRemember it for
BrihadaranyakaShukla YajurOldest and longest; Yajnavalkya's teaching on the self
ChandogyaSama"Tat tvam asi" (you are that); importance of Om
IshaShukla YajurVery short; renunciation with action
KathaKrishna YajurDialogue of Nachiketa and Yama on death
TaittiriyaKrishna YajurFive sheaths (koshas) of the self; Brahman as bliss
KenaSamaBrahman as the power behind mind and senses
MundakaAtharvaHigher and lower knowledge; "Satyameva Jayate"
MandukyaAtharvaShortest; Om and the four states of consciousness

A Rig Vedic dialogue: one hymn is a conversation between the sage Vishvamitra and the rivers Beas and Sutlej, worshipped as goddesses; it places the composers in Punjab. The Ganga and Yamuna are named only once in the Rig Veda. Most Rig Vedic hymns pray for cattle, horses and sons; wars were fought for cattle, land and water.

I04.2 Early and later Vedic periods compared

Early Vedic (Rig Vedic)Later Vedic
RegionSapta Sindhu (Punjab, Afghanistan edges); Saraswati most praised riverGanga–Yamuna doab; Kuru–Panchala the centre; later Kosala, Videha
EconomyPastoral; cattle as wealth (gavishti = search for cows = war); some barley farmingSettled agriculture; iron (shyama ayas) for clearing forests; rice, wheat; crafts
PolityTribal chief (rajan) of a jana; assemblies sabha, samiti, vidatha, gana; women attended sabha and vidathaTerritorial kingdoms (rashtra); king grows powerful; rituals Rajasuya, Ashvamedha, Vajapeya; ratnins (officials, e.g. bhagadugha — tax collector, sangrahitri — treasurer); assemblies decline; women excluded
SocietyVarna fluid; division mainly Arya–Dasa; women relatively free (Apala, Ghosha, Lopamudra composed hymns)Four varnas rigid; Shudras subordinate; gotra; three ashramas (fourth later); women's status declines
ReligionNature gods: Indra (most hymns, 250), Agni (200), Varuna (rita, cosmic order), Soma; simple sacrifices for material gainPrajapati, Vishnu, Rudra rise; elaborate sacrifices dominated by priests; Upanishads reject ritual for knowledge

The Battle of Ten Kings (Dasharajna), on the Parushni (Ravi), in which Sudas of the Bharata tribe defeated a confederacy, is described in the Rig Veda (mandala 7).

I04.3 Rivers and geography

Vedic nameModern name
SindhuIndus
VitastaJhelum
AsikniChenab
ParushniRavi
VipashaBeas
ShutudriSutlej
Gomati / Kubha / KrumuGomal / Kabul / Kurram
DrishadvatiGhaggar–Chautang (identification debated)

I04.4 The Aryan question

Debate

Where did the Vedic people come from? The older "invasion" theory is rejected. Most historians and linguists accept migrations of Indo-European-speaking groups into the subcontinent in the second millennium BCE, based on language families (Sanskrit's links with Avestan, Greek, Latin), the Boghazkoi inscription and recent genetic studies. A minority argues for an indigenous origin, identifying the Vedic and Harappan cultures. "Aryan" is a linguistic and cultural term in the Vedas, not a racial one.

What historians say

Max Müller dated the Rig Veda to c. 1200 BCE; B.G. Tilak argued for an Arctic home and much earlier dates. R.S. Sharma (Looking for the Aryans; Material Culture and Social Formations) used the Vedas to trace the change from pastoral tribes to agrarian states. Romila Thapar (From Lineage to State) analysed later Vedic society as a "lineage society" moving towards kingship.

Prelims fact bank

  • Early iron (c. 1000 BCE) has been found at Atranjikhera (Etah, UP) with Painted Grey Ware.
  • Among the Later Vedic ratnins, the bhagadugha collected taxes and the sangrahitri was the treasurer.
  • The Rig Veda has 1,028 hymns in 10 mandalas.
  • The Purusha Sukta (10th mandala) first mentions the four varnas.
  • The Gayatri Mantra is in the 3rd mandala, addressed to Savitr.
  • "Satyameva Jayate" comes from the Mundaka Upanishad.
  • The Battle of Ten Kings was won by Sudas on the Parushni (Ravi).
  • Indra has the most hymns in the Rig Veda; Agni the second most.
  • Varuna upheld rita (cosmic order).
  • Sabha and samiti were Rig Vedic assemblies.
  • Iron is called shyama ayas (black metal) in later Vedic texts.
  • Painted Grey Ware is associated with the later Vedic period.
  • The Boghazkoi inscription (c. 1400 BCE) mentions Vedic gods.
  • Vedas are shruti; epics, Puranas and smritis are smriti.
  • There are six Vedangas.
Exam traps
  • Purusha Sukta (varnas) is in the 10th mandala; Gayatri in the 3rd.
  • Parushni = Ravi; Asikni = Chenab; Vitasta = Jhelum; Shutudri = Sutlej; Vipasha = Beas.
  • Satyameva Jayate — Mundaka Upanishad, not the Rig Veda.
  • Women attended sabha and vidatha in the early Vedic period, not the later.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Compare the polity and society of the early and later Vedic periods.

How to frame it: Region and economy → Polity → Society → Religion → Conclusion

Model answer (90 words)

The early Vedic people lived in Sapta Sindhu as pastoral tribes; later Vedic people moved into the Ganga–Yamuna doab and, with iron, took to settled agriculture. Early rajans were tribal chiefs checked by sabha and samiti; later kings ruled territories, performed Rajasuya and Ashvamedha, and assemblies declined. Early varna was fluid and women joined assemblies and composed hymns; later the four varnas hardened, Shudras were subordinated and women's status fell. Simple nature worship gave way to priest-dominated sacrifice, challenged by the Upanishads. The shift prepared the ground for the Mahajanapadas.

5 marks · 50 words

What is the significance of the Upanishads?

How to frame it: What they are → Ideas → Influence

Model answer (41 words)

The Upanishads, the last part of the Vedas, turned from ritual to knowledge. They teach that Atman (self) and Brahman (ultimate reality) are one, and discuss karma, rebirth and moksha. They influenced Buddhism, Jainism and Vedanta, and gave India "Satyameva Jayate".

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Dasharajna?

How to frame it: Meaning

Model answer (16 words)

Rig Vedic Battle of Ten Kings on the Parushni (Ravi), won by Sudas of the Bharatas.

Revision card

Remember
  • Texts: 4 Vedas → Brahmanas → Aranyakas → Upanishads; 6 Vedangas; shruti vs smriti.
  • Rig Veda: 1,028 hymns, 10 mandalas; Purusha Sukta (10th), Gayatri (3rd).
  • Early: Sapta Sindhu, cattle, rajan + sabha/samiti, fluid varna, Indra/Agni/Varuna.
  • Later: doab, iron, agriculture, kingdoms, rituals, rigid varna, Upanishads.
  • Dasharajna: Sudas, Parushni.

    Show answer

    Ravi.

    Show answer

    Sudas of the Bharata tribe.

  6. Which god has the most hymns in the Rig Veda?
    Show answer

Sources: R.S. Sharma, India's Ancient Past, chs. 10–12.; Upinder Singh, A History of Ancient and Early Medieval India, ch. 5.; Romila Thapar, From Lineage to State.; NCERT (old), R.S. Sharma, Ancient India.

Part I · Unit I05Mahajanapadas and the Rise of Magadhac. 600 – 321 BCE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Sixteen Mahajanapadas; republics; rise of Magadha; Persian and Macedonian invasions

Mains: GS-I: Ancient India — second urbanisation and state formation

Reasons for the rise of Magadha is a standard Mains question; Prelims asks Mahajanapada–capital matching and dynasty sequence.

The big picture

Around the 6th century BCE the Ganga valley saw a second urbanisation (after the Harappan): towns, coins, trade and large states. Buddhist and Jain texts list sixteen Mahajanapadas — some monarchies, some republics (gana-sanghas).

Over two centuries Magadha (south Bihar) absorbed its rivals under the Haryankas, Shishunagas and Nandas, creating the first large empire in the Ganga plain. In the north-west, Persians and then Alexander invaded. The stage was set for the Mauryas.

Exam focus

Magadha's rise is a case study of how geography, resources and policy create power — use the five-factor frame in section 3.

WhenWhat happened
c. 600 BCESixteen Mahajanapadas; NBPW pottery; punch-marked coins
c. 544–492 BCEBimbisara (Haryanka), traditional dates
c. 492–460 BCEAjatashatru; conquest of Vajji and Kosala
c. 518 BCEDarius I of Persia annexes the north-west
c. 460–444 BCEUdayin founds Pataliputra
c. 413–345 BCEShishunagas; Vaishali second Buddhist council (c. 383 BCE)
c. 345–321 BCENandas
326 BCEAlexander defeats Porus at the Hydaspes (Jhelum)

Core notes

I05.1 The sixteen Mahajanapadas

Listed in the Buddhist Anguttara Nikaya (and, with variations, the Jain Bhagavati Sutra):

MahajanapadaCapitalMahajanapadaCapital
AngaChampaMagadhaRajagriha (Girivraja), later Pataliputra
KashiVaranasiKosalaShravasti (also Ayodhya, Saketa)
Vajji (republic)VaishaliMalla (republic)Kushinagar, Pava
ChediShuktimatiVatsaKaushambi
KuruIndraprasthaPanchalaAhichhatra, Kampilya
MatsyaViratnagar (Bairat)ShurasenaMathura
AshmakaPotana (only one south of the Vindhyas, on the Godavari)AvantiUjjayini, Mahishmati
GandharaTaxilaKambojaRajapura

Republics (gana/sangha): Vajji confederacy (with the Lichchhavis), Mallas, Shakyas (Kapilavastu — the Buddha's clan), Koliyas. Decisions were taken in assemblies of clan heads (in the santhagara); the Buddha modelled the Sangha on them.

I05.2 Magadha: dynasties

DynastyRulers and events
HaryankaBimbisara: contemporary of the Buddha; marriage alliances (Kosala, Lichchhavi, Madra); annexed Anga; physician Jivaka. Ajatashatru: killed his father; defeated Kosala and the Vajjis using the mahashilakantaka (catapult) and rathamusala (chariot with mace); built a fort at Pataligrama; first Buddhist council (Rajagriha). Udayin: founded Pataliputra.
ShishunagaShishunaga destroyed Avanti; Kalashoka (Kakavarna) held the second Buddhist council at Vaishali
NandaMahapadma Nanda: called ekarat and "destroyer of all Kshatriyas"; first non-Kshatriya empire; Kalinga conquered (Hathigumpha inscription). Dhana Nanda: huge army and wealth (Greek "Agrammes"); unpopular; overthrown by Chandragupta Maurya (c. 321 BCE)

I05.3 Why Magadha rose

  1. Geography: Rajagriha ringed by hills; Pataliputra a water fort at the confluence of the Ganga, Son and Gandak — control of river trade.
  2. Resources: iron ore from the Chotanagpur region (nearby) for weapons and tools; fertile alluvial soil and rice; forests supplying elephants for war.
  3. Economy: agricultural surplus, trade, punch-marked coins — revenue for a standing army.
  4. Ambitious rulers and ministers (Bimbisara, Ajatashatru, Mahapadma Nanda); new war machines.
  5. Social openness: less bound by Vedic orthodoxy; home of Buddhism and Jainism; mixed population.

I05.4 Villages and towns of the janapada age

  • Village society (north): the grama bhojaka was the hereditary headman and often the largest landowner; he got land cultivated by slaves and hired workers, collected taxes for the king and acted as judge and policeman. Smaller independent landowners were grihapatis; the landless who worked others' fields were dasa karmakaras.
  • Farming: iron ploughshares and transplantation of paddy raised output; kings and villagers built canals, wells, tanks and artificial lakes.
  • Towns: fortified cities with ring wells (stacked pottery rings used as toilets, drains and refuse pits), NBPW pottery, punch-marked coins (silver or copper pieces stamped with symbols, no legend) and craft guilds (shrenis) that also acted as banks.

I05.5 Persian and Macedonian invasions

Persians: Cyrus and Darius I (c. 518 BCE) annexed Gandhara and the Indus region — the 20th and richest satrapy (Herodotus). Effects: Kharosthi script (from Aramaic), Persian influence on Mauryan edicts and pillars, trade.

Alexander (327–325 BCE):

  • Ambhi of Taxila submitted.
  • Battle of the Hydaspes (Jhelum, 326 BCE): Porus defeated but reinstated.
  • His army refused to go beyond the Beas (Hyphasis), fearing the Nanda army.
  • He returned via Sindh and died at Babylon (323 BCE).

Effects: opened land and sea routes to the West; Greek settlements; political vacuum in the north-west that helped Chandragupta Maurya; Greek accounts give the first firm dates in Indian history (326 BCE).

What historians say

R.S. Sharma linked the rise of states to iron, agricultural surplus and the "second urbanisation". D.K. Chakrabarti and others argue that iron's role is overstated and stress agrarian expansion and trade. Romila Thapar contrasts the gana-sanghas with monarchies, noting that the republics were oligarchies of Kshatriya clans, not democracies.

Prelims fact bank

  • The sixteen Mahajanapadas are listed in the Anguttara Nikaya.
  • Ashmaka (capital Potana, on the Godavari) was the only Mahajanapada south of the Vindhyas.
  • In the Vajji sangha many rajas ruled together through assemblies; women, dasas and kammakaras could not take part.
  • Vajji (capital Vaishali) was a republican confederacy including the Lichchhavis.
  • Kosala's capital was Shravasti; Avanti's was Ujjayini; Vatsa's was Kaushambi.
  • Bimbisara annexed Anga; his physician was Jivaka.
  • Ajatashatru used the mahashilakantaka and rathamusala against the Vajjis.
  • Udayin founded Pataliputra.
  • Kalashoka held the second Buddhist council at Vaishali.
  • Mahapadma Nanda was called ekarat.
  • Darius I annexed the Indus region c. 518 BCE.
  • Alexander defeated Porus at the Hydaspes (Jhelum) in 326 BCE.
  • Alexander's army refused to cross the Beas.
Exam traps
  • Ashmaka's capital Potana (Potali) is usually identified with Bodhan (Telangana), not Paithan; some guidebooks wrongly write "Potana (Paithan)".
  • Ajatashatru built the fort at Pataligrama; Udayin founded Pataliputra city.
  • First Buddhist council (Rajagriha) — Ajatashatru; second (Vaishali) — Kalashoka.
  • Hydaspes = Jhelum; Hyphasis = Beas.
  • Gana-sanghas were clan oligarchies, not modern democracies.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

What factors led to the rise of Magadha?

How to frame it: Geography → Resources → Economy → Rulers → Society

Model answer (83 words)

Magadha's capitals were naturally protected: Rajagriha by hills, Pataliputra by the Ganga, Son and Gandak, which also controlled river trade. Nearby iron ore supplied weapons and tools; alluvial soils produced rice surpluses; forests supplied war elephants. Revenue from agriculture and trade, aided by coinage, supported large armies. Able, ruthless rulers — Bimbisara, Ajatashatru, Mahapadma Nanda — used alliances, new war machines and conquest. Magadha's mixed, less orthodox society also adapted readily. Together these made it the first imperial power of the Ganga plain.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the effects of Alexander's invasion?

How to frame it: Routes → Political → Chronology

Model answer (42 words)

Alexander's invasion opened land and sea routes to the West and created Greek settlements. By weakening north-western states, it helped Chandragupta Maurya unify the region. Greek records gave Indian history its first fixed date (326 BCE). Its direct cultural effect was limited.

2 marks · 15 words

Which was the only Mahajanapada south of the Vindhyas?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (8 words)

Ashmaka, with its capital Potana on the Godavari.

Revision card

Remember
  • 16 Mahajanapadas (Anguttara Nikaya); Ashmaka only southern; republics Vajji, Malla, Shakya.
  • Haryanka: Bimbisara (Anga, alliances), Ajatashatru (Vajji, war machines, 1st council), Udayin (Pataliputra).
  • Shishunaga: Avanti; Kalashoka 2nd council. Nanda: Mahapadma (ekarat), Dhana Nanda.
  • Rise of Magadha: geography, iron, soil, elephants, rulers, society.
  • Darius c. 518 BCE; Alexander: Hydaspes 326 BCE, Beas halt.

Self-test

  1. Which text lists the sixteen Mahajanapadas?
    Show answer

    The Anguttara Nikaya.

  2. What was the capital of Kosala?
    Show answer

    Shravasti.

  3. Who founded Pataliputra?
    Show answer

    Udayin.

  4. Where was the second Buddhist council held, and under whom?
    Show answer

    Vaishali, under Kalashoka.

  5. Who was called ekarat?
    Show answer

    Mahapadma Nanda.

  6. Where did Alexander defeat Porus?
    Show answer

    On the Hydaspes (Jhelum), 326 BCE.

  7. Give two geographical advantages of Magadha.
    Show answer

    Protected capitals; river control at Pataliputra; nearby iron ore; fertile soil.

  8. What were the gana-sanghas?
    Show answer

    Republics governed by assemblies of clan heads.

Part I · Unit I06Buddhism, Jainism, Ajivikas and 6th-century BCE Thoughtc. 600 – 300 BCE (with later councils and sects)

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Buddhism and Jainism: founders, teachings, councils, sects, texts; Heterodox schools

Mains: GS-I: Religious movements of the 6th century BCE; Indian knowledge and value system

Causes of the rise of new religions, comparison of Buddhism and Jainism, and Buddhist councils are standard questions.

The big picture

The 6th century BCE was an age of intellectual ferment across the Ganga valley. Wandering teachers (shramanas) challenged Vedic sacrifice and Brahmin dominance. Two of their movements — Buddhism and Jainism — became lasting world religions; others, such as the Ajivikas (fatalists) and the Charvakas/Lokayata (materialists), were influential in their time.

The new religions spoke in the people's languages (Pali, Prakrit), rejected costly animal sacrifice, and appealed to the new urban classes — merchants (setthis) and Kshatriya rulers.

Exam focus

Explain the rise of these movements through society and economy, not only doctrine — the second urbanisation created new classes and new questions.

WhenWhat happened
c. 540–468 BCEMahavira (traditional dates; 599–527 in Jain tradition)
c. 563–483 BCEThe Buddha (traditional; many scholars prefer c. 480–400 BCE)
c. 483 BCEFirst Buddhist council, Rajagriha
c. 383 BCESecond Buddhist council, Vaishali
c. 300 BCEJain council at Pataliputra; Bhadrabahu goes south
c. 250 BCEThird Buddhist council, Pataliputra (Ashoka)
c. 1st–2nd c. CEFourth Buddhist council, Kashmir (Kanishka); Mahayana
5th c. CEJain council at Vallabhi; canon written down

Core notes

I06.1 Why new religions arose

  1. Reaction against Vedic ritualism: costly sacrifices, Brahmin monopoly, animal slaughter.
  2. Varna tensions: Kshatriyas resented Brahmin claims; the Buddha and Mahavira were Kshatriyas.
  3. New economy: iron-age agriculture needed cattle — sacrificial slaughter harmed it; non-violence suited farmers.
  4. Urban classes: merchants and moneylenders, ranked low in the Dharmashastras, welcomed teachings that valued ethics over birth; usury and sea travel were condemned in Brahmanical texts.
  5. Simple teaching in popular languages; the Sangha offered organisation.

I06.2 Buddhism

Life: Siddhartha Gautama, Shakya clan, born at Lumbini (Nepal); left home at 29 (mahabhinishkramana); enlightenment at Bodh Gaya under a pipal tree at 35; first sermon at Sarnath (dharmachakra-pravartana); died at Kushinagar at 80 (mahaparinirvana).

Teachings:

  • Four Noble Truths: life is suffering (dukkha); suffering has a cause (desire, tanha); suffering can end (nirvana); the way is the Eightfold Path.
  • Eightfold Path: right view, intention, speech, action, livelihood, effort, mindfulness, concentration.
  • Middle Path between indulgence and extreme asceticism; no permanent soul (anatta); impermanence (anicca); karma and rebirth; silence on God.
  • Three Jewels: Buddha, Dhamma, Sangha.

Texts: Tripitaka in Pali — Vinaya (monastic rules), Sutta (discourses), Abhidhamma (philosophy); Jatakas (birth stories). The Therigatha (in the Sutta Pitaka) collects verses by nuns. Sri Lankan chronicles, the Dipavamsa and Mahavamsa, record the history of Buddhism on the island.

The Sangha: monks (bhikkhus) and nuns (bhikkhunis) lived on alms. At Ananda's request the Buddha admitted women; his foster mother Mahapajapati Gotami was the first nun. Children needed their parents' consent to join, slaves their masters', royal servants the king's, debtors their creditors' and wives their husbands'. Decisions were by consensus, or else by vote. Monks travelled except in the rainy season; permanent monasteries (viharas), first of wood and brick and later cut in rock in western India, grew from donated land.

CouncilPlace, patronChairOutcome
First (c. 483 BCE)Rajagriha (Saptaparni cave), AjatashatruMahakassapaSutta (Ananda) and Vinaya (Upali) compiled
Second (c. 383 BCE)Vaishali, KalashokaSabakamiSplit: Sthaviravadins and Mahasanghikas
Third (c. 250 BCE)Pataliputra, AshokaMoggaliputta TissaAbhidhamma added; missions abroad
Fourth (1st–2nd c. CE)Kashmir (Kundalvana), KanishkaVasumitra; Ashvaghosha deputySplit into Hinayana and Mahayana; Sanskrit texts

Sects: Hinayana/Theravada (Buddha as teacher; individual salvation; Sri Lanka, Myanmar); Mahayana (Buddha as divine; image worship; bodhisattvas; China, Japan); Vajrayana (tantric; Tibet, Bengal, 7th c. onward). Thinkers: Nagarjuna (Madhyamika), Asanga and Vasubandhu (Yogachara).

Decline in India: revival of Brahmanism and Bhakti; Buddhism's absorption into Hinduism (Buddha as an avatar of Vishnu); corruption and wealth of monasteries; loss of royal patronage; Turkish destruction of Nalanda and Vikramashila (c. 1200).

I06.3 Jainism

Tirthankaras: 24, from Rishabhadeva (Adinath) to Mahavira; the 23rd, Parshvanath, taught four vows.

Mahavira: Vardhamana, born at Kundagrama near Vaishali (Jnatrika clan); left home at 30; attained kaivalya (supreme knowledge) at 42 under a sal tree at Jrimbhikagrama; became Jina (conqueror); died at Pavapuri (near Rajagriha) at 72.

Teachings:

  • Five vows (mahavratas): ahimsa, satya, asteya (non-stealing), aparigraha (non-possession) — Parshvanath's four — plus brahmacharya added by Mahavira.
  • Three Jewels (triratna): right faith, right knowledge, right conduct.
  • Everything (even stones and water) has a soul (jiva); extreme non-violence; strict asceticism, even fasting to death (sallekhana).
  • Anekantavada (many-sidedness of reality) and syadvada (truth is conditional).
  • No creator god; karma binds the soul.

Split: during a famine in Magadha (c. 300 BCE), Bhadrabahu led monks south (with Chandragupta Maurya, per tradition); those who stayed under Sthulabhadra adopted white robes — Shvetambaras; those who returned insisted on nudity — Digambaras.

Councils: Pataliputra (c. 300 BCE, Sthulabhadra) — 12 Angas compiled; Vallabhi (5th c. CE, Devarddhi Kshamashramana) — canon written down. Texts in Ardhamagadhi Prakrit.

Spread: merchant communities; Karnataka (Shravanabelagola), Gujarat and Rajasthan (Dilwara, Ranakpur); patrons Kharavela, Chalukyas, Rashtrakutas (Amoghavarsha).

I06.4 Buddhism and Jainism compared; other schools

BuddhismJainism
SoulNo permanent soul (anatta)Souls in all things (jiva)
PathMiddle PathSevere asceticism
Non-violenceModerateExtreme
SpreadSpread across Asia; declined in IndiaStayed in India; survived to today
LanguagePaliArdhamagadhi Prakrit
CommonRejected Vedic authority and sacrifice; karma and rebirth; Kshatriya founders; monastic orders; lay followers among merchants

Ajivikas: founded by Makkhali Gosala; strict fatalism (niyati) — human effort cannot change destiny; Ashoka and Dasharatha gave them the Barabar caves.

Charvaka / Lokayata: materialism — only perception is valid; no soul, no afterlife (Ajita Keshakambalin, Brihaspati). Purana Kassapa denied moral consequence of actions.

What historians say

Max Weber linked Buddhism to urban and merchant classes; Uma Chakravarti (The Social Dimensions of Early Buddhism) showed how early Buddhism addressed the new social world of gahapatis and setthis. Romila Thapar sees the shramana movements as part of a broad debate rather than a simple "revolt" against Brahmanism.

Prelims fact bank

  • The first Buddhist council met in the Saptaparni cave at Rajagriha.
  • The Buddha was born at Lumbini, enlightened at Bodh Gaya, preached first at Sarnath, died at Kushinagar.
  • The first sermon is called dharmachakra-pravartana.
  • Tripitaka: Vinaya, Sutta, Abhidhamma pitakas.
  • First council: Rajagriha, Ajatashatru, chaired by Mahakassapa.
  • Third council: Pataliputra, Ashoka, chaired by Moggaliputta Tissa.
  • Fourth council: Kashmir, Kanishka, chaired by Vasumitra; Mahayana emerged.
  • Nagarjuna founded the Madhyamika school.
  • Rishabhadeva was the first Tirthankara; Parshvanath the 23rd; Mahavira the 24th.
  • Mahavira was born at Kundagrama and died at Pavapuri.
  • Mahavira added brahmacharya to Parshvanath's four vows.
  • Jain Triratna: right faith, right knowledge, right conduct.
  • Bhadrabahu led Jain monks south; Sthulabhadra stayed — origin of Digambara–Shvetambara split.
  • The Jain canon was written down at the Vallabhi council.
  • Makkhali Gosala founded the Ajivika sect.
  • Ashoka gave the Barabar caves to the Ajivikas.
Exam traps
  • Second Buddhist council split the Sangha into Sthaviravadins and Mahasanghikas; the fourth produced Mahayana.
  • Mahavira added brahmacharya, not ahimsa (ahimsa was already among Parshvanath's four).
  • Digambaras ("sky-clad") followed Bhadrabahu; Shvetambaras followed Sthulabhadra.
  • Ajivikas were fatalists; Charvakas were materialists.
  • Buddhist texts are in Pali; Jain canonical texts in Ardhamagadhi.

RAS Mains questions are quoted as printed. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Explain the causes of the rise of Buddhism and Jainism in the 6th century BCE.

How to frame it: Ritualism → Varna tensions → Agrarian economy → Urban classes → Appeal

Model answer (85 words)

Later Vedic religion had become costly ritual controlled by Brahmins, alienating many. Kshatriyas resented Brahmin claims to superiority; both founders were Kshatriyas. Iron-age agriculture depended on cattle, so non-violence opposed wasteful sacrificial slaughter. New urban merchants and moneylenders, given low status by Brahmanical law, found dignity in ethical religions. Teaching in Pali and Prakrit, rejecting caste barriers and organising the Sangha made the new faiths accessible. Royal patrons such as Bimbisara and Ajatashatru helped. The second urbanisation thus produced both new questions and new followers.

5 marks · 50 words

Compare the teachings of Buddhism and Jainism.

How to frame it: Common → Differences

Model answer (44 words)

Both rejected Vedic authority and sacrifice, accepted karma and rebirth and stressed ethics. Buddhism denied a permanent soul and taught the Middle Path; Jainism saw souls in all things and demanded strict asceticism and extreme non-violence. Buddhism spread across Asia; Jainism remained in India.

2 marks · 15 words

How were Buddhism and Jainism different from the religion of the Samhitas? Give two differences.

How to frame it: Two differences

Model answer (17 words)

They rejected Vedic authority and sacrifice for ethical conduct, and opened salvation to all, regardless of varna.

2 marks · 15 words

Who were the Ajivikas?

How to frame it: Founder, belief

Model answer (14 words)

Fatalist sect of Makkhali Gosala, believing destiny (niyati) governs all; Ashoka gave them caves.

Revision card

Remember
  • Causes: ritualism, varna tensions, cattle economy, urban classes, simple teaching.
  • Buddha: Lumbini, Bodh Gaya, Sarnath, Kushinagar; 4 Truths, 8-fold Path, Middle Path.
  • Councils: Rajagriha (Ajatashatru) → Vaishali (Kalashoka) → Pataliputra (Ashoka) → Kashmir (Kanishka).
  • Sects: Hinayana, Mahayana, Vajrayana. Decline: Bhakti, absorption, patronage, Turks.
  • Mahavira: Kundagrama, kaivalya at 42, Pavapuri; 5 vows; triratna; anekantavada.
  • Split: Bhadrabahu (Digambara), Sthulabhadra (Shvetambara). Vallabhi council.
  • Ajivikas — Makkhali Gosala, fatalism, Barabar caves. Charvaka — materialism.

  5. Which vow did Mahavira add?

  6. Where did Mahavira die?
    Show answer

  7. Who led the Jain monks south during the famine?
    Show answer

  8. Where was the Jain canon written down?
    Show answer

  9. What is anekantavada?

Sources: Upinder Singh, A History of Ancient and Early Medieval India, ch. 6.; Uma Chakravarti, The Social Dimensions of Early Buddhism.; R.S. Sharma, India's Ancient Past, ch. 13.; NCERT, Themes in Indian History Part I, ch. 4.

Part I · Unit I07The Mauryasc. 321 – 185 BCE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Mauryan empire: Chandragupta, Bindusara, Ashoka; administration; dhamma; edicts; decline

Mains: GS-I: Mauryan polity, administration and Ashoka's dhamma

Ashoka's dhamma, Mauryan administration and the causes of decline are classic Mains questions; edict content and locations are Prelims favourites.

The big picture

The Mauryas built India's first empire covering nearly the whole subcontinent. Chandragupta, guided by Kautilya, overthrew the Nandas and expelled the Greek governors; Bindusara extended rule south; and Ashoka, after the bloody Kalinga war, turned to a policy of dhamma — moral rule — and spread it through inscriptions across his empire.

The empire had a large bureaucracy, a standing army and control of the economy, as described in the Arthashastra and by Megasthenes. It declined within fifty years of Ashoka's death.

Exam focus

Ashoka's edicts are the first deciphered royal voice in Indian history — quote their content (tolerance, welfare, non-violence) in answers.

WhenWhat happened
c. 321 BCEChandragupta Maurya overthrows Dhana Nanda
305–303 BCEChandragupta defeats Seleucus; treaty; Megasthenes sent to Pataliputra
c. 297 BCEChandragupta abdicates; goes to Shravanabelagola (Jain tradition)
c. 297–273 BCEBindusara ("Amitraghata")
c. 268–232 BCEAshoka
c. 261 BCEKalinga war (8th year of Ashoka's reign)
c. 250 BCEThird Buddhist council; missions abroad
c. 185 BCEPushyamitra Shunga kills Brihadratha

Core notes

I07.1 Chandragupta and Bindusara

  • Chandragupta (Greek "Sandrocottus", identified by William Jones), with Kautilya (Chanakya, Vishnugupta), took the Punjab after Alexander's death and then Magadha (c. 321 BCE).
  • Defeated Seleucus Nicator (305–303 BCE), gaining Arachosia, Gedrosia, Paropamisadae (Afghanistan, Baluchistan) in return for 500 elephants; a marriage alliance followed; Megasthenes came as ambassador.
  • His governor Pushyagupta built the Sudarshana lake in Saurashtra (Junagadh inscription of Rudradaman).
  • Jain tradition: became a Jain, went south with Bhadrabahu and died at Shravanabelagola by sallekhana.
  • Bindusara: called Amitraghata ("slayer of foes"); extended rule into the Deccan; contacts with Antiochus I (asked for sweet wine, figs and a sophist); patron of Ajivikas.

I07.2 Ashoka and the dhamma

Kalinga war (8th regnal year): Rock Edict XIII records 1,50,000 deported, 1,00,000 killed; Ashoka's remorse led to dhamma-vijaya (conquest by righteousness) in place of war.

Dhamma — a moral code, not a sect:

  • Respect for parents, elders, teachers; kindness to slaves and servants; generosity to Brahmins and shramanas.
  • Tolerance of all sects (Rock Edict XII: restraint in speech, honouring others' sects).
  • Non-violence: restrictions on animal slaughter; royal hunts replaced by dhamma-yatras.
  • Welfare: hospitals for men and animals, wells, shade trees, rest houses (Rock Edict II).
  • Officials: dhamma-mahamattas (appointed in the 13th regnal year) to spread dhamma; rajukas and pradeshikas toured.
  • Missions to Sri Lanka (Mahendra and Sanghamitra), Suvarnabhumi and the Hellenistic kings (Rock Edict XIII names Antiochus II, Ptolemy II and others).
Debate

Was dhamma Buddhist propaganda or statecraft? Ashoka was a Buddhist (Bhabru edict), but dhamma avoided Buddhist doctrine and stressed common ethics. Romila Thapar sees it as a policy to unify a vast, diverse empire; others stress personal conviction. Most historians combine both.

I07.3 The edicts

TypeExamplesContent
14 Major Rock EdictsGirnar, Kalsi, Shahbazgarhi and Mansehra (Kharosthi), Dhauli and Jaugada (Kalinga), Sopara, ErragudiDhamma, welfare, Kalinga (XIII); Dhauli and Jaugada omit XIII and add Separate Kalinga Edicts
Minor Rock EdictsMaski, Gujarra, Bairat, Brahmagiri, RupnathAshoka's Buddhism; Maski names "Ashoka"
7 Pillar EdictsDelhi–Topra (all seven), Delhi–Meerut, Allahabad, Lauriya-NandangarhDhamma summarised
Minor pillar inscriptionsRummindei (Lumbini: tax reduced), Sanchi, Sarnath (schism warning), Allahabad "Queen's edict" (Karuvaki)Buddhist sites and family
Bilingual / foreign scriptKandahar (Greek and Aramaic)Dhamma for the north-west

Language: Prakrit; scripts Brahmi, Kharosthi (north-west), Greek and Aramaic (Afghanistan). Ashoka calls himself Devanampiya Piyadasi. The Sarnath lion capital is India's national emblem.

I07.4 Administration and economy

LevelDetails
CentreKing with council (mantriparishad); 18 tirthas (high officials) in the Arthashastra; samaharta (revenue collector-general), sannidhata (treasurer); adhyakshas (superintendents) of trade, mines, forests, weights
ProvincesCapitals at Taxila (north-west), Ujjain (west), Tosali (Kalinga), Suvarnagiri (south), Pataliputra (centre) — often under princes (kumaras)
Districts / villagesPradeshika, rajuka (land and justice), yukta; gramika at village level
CityMegasthenes: Pataliputra run by 30 officials in 6 boards of 5 (industry, foreigners, births and deaths, trade, manufactures, tax on sales)
ArmySix boards (infantry, cavalry, elephants, chariots, navy, transport); huge standing army
EspionageExtensive spy network (gudhapurushas)

Economy: land revenue (bhaga, usually 1/6, higher in some cases); sita (crown land) under the sitadhyaksha; state control of mines, forests, salt and weapons; punch-marked silver coins (pana); trade routes (Uttarapatha). Megasthenes described seven classes (philosophers, farmers, herdsmen, artisans, soldiers, overseers, councillors).

Tax and tribute: the core around Pataliputra paid regular taxes collected by salaried officials; distant areas paid tribute when it could be taken. The Arthashastra notes blankets from the north-west and gold and precious stones from the south; Suvarnagiri probably tapped the Karnataka gold fields.

Art: polished sandstone pillars (Chunar), Sarnath capital, Rampurva bull (now at Rashtrapati Bhavan), Barabar caves, Didarganj Yakshi (debated date), Dhauli elephant. The Sarnath capital has four lions back to back on a round abacus carved with an elephant, a bull, a horse and a lion separated by wheels, over an inverted lotus. The national emblem (adopted 26 January 1950) shows three of the lions, the wheel, a bull and a horse, with Satyameva Jayate below.

Later Mauryas: Dasharatha, Ashoka's grandson, gave the Nagarjuni hill caves (near Barabar) to the Ajivikas; Jain tradition makes Samprati a great patron of Jainism; Brihadratha, the last ruler, was killed by his general Pushyamitra Shunga (c. 185 BCE).

I07.5 Decline

ExplanationProponent
Brahmanical reaction to Ashoka's pro-Buddhist, anti-sacrifice policies (Pushyamitra a Brahmin)H.P. Shastri
Ashoka's pacifism weakened the armyH.C. Raychaudhuri
Over-centralised administration dependent on the king; weak successors; division of the empireRomila Thapar
Financial crisis: huge army and bureaucracy (debasement of coins)D.D. Kosambi
Provincial revolts, Greek invasions in the north-westGeneral

The last ruler, Brihadratha, was killed by his general Pushyamitra Shunga (c. 185 BCE).

What historians say

The Arthashastra's date is debated: Thomas Trautmann's statistical study suggests it was compiled over centuries, so it may describe an ideal more than Mauryan practice. Romila Thapar (Ashoka and the Decline of the Mauryas) is the standard work on dhamma; Gerard Fussman questioned how centralised the empire really was, arguing for a core with looser control over peripheries.

Prelims fact bank

  • William Jones identified Sandrocottus with Chandragupta Maurya.
  • Chandragupta defeated Seleucus Nicator (305–303 BCE) and gave him 500 elephants.
  • Megasthenes wrote Indica as ambassador to Pataliputra.
  • Pushyagupta built the Sudarshana lake under Chandragupta.
  • Chandragupta died at Shravanabelagola (Jain tradition).
  • Bindusara was called Amitraghata.
  • The Kalinga war took place in Ashoka's 8th regnal year (Rock Edict XIII).
  • Rock Edict XII stresses tolerance among sects.
  • Dhamma-mahamattas were appointed in Ashoka's 13th regnal year.
  • Mahendra and Sanghamitra took Buddhism to Sri Lanka.
  • The Maski edict names "Ashoka".
  • The Kandahar edict is in Greek and Aramaic.
  • Shahbazgarhi and Mansehra edicts are in Kharosthi.
  • The Rummindei pillar records a tax reduction at Lumbini.
  • Mauryan provincial capitals: Taxila, Ujjain, Tosali, Suvarnagiri.
  • The samaharta was the chief revenue collector; the sannidhata the treasurer.
  • Pushyamitra Shunga killed Brihadratha c. 185 BCE.
Exam traps
  • Dhauli and Jaugada lack Rock Edict XIII (Kalinga war) — they have Separate Kalinga Edicts instead.
  • Delhi–Topra has all seven pillar edicts; it was moved to Delhi by Firoz Shah Tughlaq.
  • Only Sarnath has the four-lion capital; Lauriya-Nandangarh and Vaishali (Kolhua) have single-lion capitals, and the Vaishali pillar carries no edict.
  • Sudarshana lake: built under Chandragupta, repaired under Ashoka (Tushaspha), Rudradaman and Skandagupta.
  • Megasthenes' "seven classes" are occupational groups, not varnas.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

ExamQuestionMarks
RAS Pre 2021Among Mauryan-period idols, the one inscribed "Manibhadra" (yaksha) was found at: — Answer (RPSC final key): Parkham—

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Discuss the nature and significance of Ashoka's dhamma.

How to frame it: Context → Content → Means → Nature → Significance

Model answer (87 words)

After the Kalinga war, Ashoka replaced conquest by war with dhamma-vijaya. Dhamma enjoined respect for elders, kindness to servants, generosity, restraint in speech, tolerance of all sects and non-violence to animals. He spread it through edicts in local scripts, dhamma-mahamattas, dhamma-yatras and missions abroad, and through welfare — hospitals, wells, trees. Though Ashoka was a Buddhist, dhamma avoided doctrine and stressed shared ethics, serving to unite a diverse empire. It is an early statement of a welfare state and religious tolerance, still symbolised by India's national emblem.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the causes of the decline of the Mauryan empire?

How to frame it: Theories → Assessment

Model answer (48 words)

Weak successors and the division of the empire, an over-centralised administration dependent on the king, financial strain from a huge army and bureaucracy, provincial revolts and Greek invasions caused decline. Claims of Brahmanical reaction or Ashokan pacifism are less convincing. Pushyamitra Shunga ended the dynasty c. 185 BCE.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Sudarshana lake?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (14 words)

Reservoir in Saurashtra built under Chandragupta by Pushyagupta; later repaired by Rudradaman and Skandagupta.

Revision card

Remember
  • Chandragupta: Kautilya; Seleucus (500 elephants); Megasthenes; Sudarshana lake; Shravanabelagola.
  • Bindusara: Amitraghata; Ajivikas.
  • Ashoka: Kalinga (8th yr, RE XIII); dhamma (RE XII tolerance); dhamma-mahamattas; missions.
  • Edicts: 14 RE, MRE (Maski names Ashoka), 7 PE (Delhi–Topra), Kandahar Greek–Aramaic.
  • Administration: mantriparishad, 18 tirthas, samaharta, sannidhata; provinces Taxila, Ujjain, Tosali, Suvarnagiri; city 6 boards × 5.
  • Decline: weak successors, centralisation, finance, revolts; Pushyamitra c. 185 BCE.

Part I · Unit I08Post-Mauryan India: Shungas, Satavahanas, Indo-Greeks, Shakas, Kushansc. 185 BCE – 300 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Shungas, Kanvas, Kharavela, Satavahanas; Indo-Greeks, Shakas, Parthians, Kushans; trade and art

Mains: GS-I: Post-Mauryan polity, economy, trade with Rome; Gandhara and Mathura art

Kanishka, Gandhara vs Mathura art, Satavahana society and Indo-Roman trade are the usual questions.

The big picture

After the Mauryas, no single empire ruled India for five centuries. In the Ganga valley the Shungas and Kanvas ruled a shrinking Magadha; in Kalinga Kharavela rose; in the Deccan the Satavahanas built a lasting kingdom. The north-west saw a series of Central Asian rulers: Indo-Greeks, Shakas, Parthians and the Kushans, whose empire under Kanishka stretched from Central Asia to the Ganga.

Politically fragmented, this was economically a boom: crafts, guilds, coins, and trade with Rome and China along the Silk Route. It produced Mahayana Buddhism, Gandhara and Mathura art, and classics such as Patanjali and Ashvaghosha.

Exam focus

Don't treat this as a "dark age" — trade, urbanisation and art peaked. That is the analytical point examiners reward.

WhenWhat happened
c. 185–73 BCEShungas (Pushyamitra, Agnimitra)
c. 180 BCE onwardIndo-Greeks; Menander (c. 165–130 BCE)
c. 1st c. BCEKharavela of Kalinga (Hathigumpha inscription)
c. 73–28 BCEKanvas
c. 1st c. BCE – 3rd c. CESatavahanas
58 BCEVikrama Samvat (traditional)
c. 78 CEKanishka's accession; Shaka Samvat
c. 106–130 CEGautamiputra Satakarni
150 CEJunagadh inscription of Rudradaman

Core notes

I08.1 Shungas, Kanvas and Kharavela

  • Pushyamitra Shunga: performed two Ashvamedhas; fought the Indo-Greeks; later Buddhist texts call him a persecutor (debated). Capital Pataliputra, later Vidisha.
  • Agnimitra: hero of Kalidasa's Malavikagnimitram. Heliodorus, a Greek envoy of Antialcidas, set up the Garuda pillar at Besnagar (Vidisha) as a devotee of Vasudeva — early evidence of Bhagavatism.
  • Patanjali's Mahabhashya (on Panini) belongs to this period; Bharhut stupa railings.
  • Kanvas (Vasudeva Kanva) replaced the Shungas.
  • Kharavela of Kalinga (Mahameghavahana, Chedi dynasty): a Jain; Hathigumpha inscription (Udayagiri, Bhubaneswar) records his campaigns, public works and patronage — it mentions a canal dug by a Nanda king.

I08.2 The Satavahanas

  • Called Andhras in the Puranas; capital Pratishthana (Paithan) and later Amaravati/Dhanyakataka.
  • Founder Simuka; Satakarni I performed Ashvamedha (Naneghat inscription of queen Naganika).
  • Gautamiputra Satakarni: defeated the Shaka Nahapana (restruck his coins); Nasik inscription of his mother Gautami Balashri calls him destroyer of Shakas, Yavanas and Pahlavas and protector of varna.
  • Vasishthiputra Pulumavi, Yajnashri Satakarni (ship-motif coins, shows sea trade).
  • Features: rulers named after mothers (metronymics) but succession patrilineal; first to give land grants to Brahmins and Buddhists tax-free; Prakrit inscriptions; lead, potin and copper coins; Buddhist caves (Karle, Nasik, Bhaja) and Amaravati stupa.
  • Hala's Gathasaptashati (Prakrit poetry).

I08.3 Indo-Greeks, Shakas and Parthians

Indo-Greeks (Bactrian Greeks): Demetrius invaded c. 180 BCE. Menander (Milinda), capital Sakala (Sialkot), discussed Buddhism with the monk Nagasena in the Milindapanho. Contributions: the first Indian coins with portraits and legends, gold coins, Greek astronomy terms, the start of Gandhara art.

Shakas (Scythians), in five branches — the most important were the Western Kshatrapas of Ujjain–Saurashtra. Rudradaman I (c. 130–150 CE): defeated the Satavahanas; repaired the Sudarshana lake; Junagadh inscription (150 CE) — first long Sanskrit inscription. Tradition says Vikramaditya of Ujjain defeated the Shakas in 58 BCE (Vikrama Samvat).

Parthians (Pahlavas): Gondophernes; tradition says St Thomas came to his court.

I08.4 The Kushans and Kanishka

  • A branch of the Yuezhi from Central Asia. Kujula Kadphises founded the dynasty; Vima Kadphises issued extensive gold coins with Shiva.
  • Kanishka (accession c. 78 CE — the Shaka era, used in India's national calendar; date debated, some put it c. 127 CE): capitals Purushapura (Peshawar) and Mathura; empire from Central Asia (Kashgar, Khotan) to Varanasi.
  • Patron of Buddhism: fourth Buddhist council (Kashmir); Mahayana. Court: Ashvaghosha (Buddhacharita), Nagarjuna, Vasumitra, the physician Charaka.
  • Coins show Greek, Iranian, Indian and Buddhist deities — evidence of tolerance and diverse subjects.
  • Title Devaputra ("son of god"); the Rabatak inscription gives the genealogy.
  • Kushans controlled the Silk Route between China, Central Asia and the Roman world.

I08.5 Economy, trade and art

Trade with Rome: exports of pepper, spices, textiles, precious stones, ivory; imports of gold and silver coins, wine, amphorae, glass. Ports: Barygaza (Bharuch), Sopara, Muziris, Arikamedu (Roman trading station, Puducherry; finds include amphorae, Roman lamps and glass, and Arretine ware, a stamped red-glazed pottery named after Arezzo in Italy). Pliny complained that India drained Rome of 50 million sesterces a year. The monsoon route (Hippalus) made direct sailing possible.

Guilds (shrenis) organised crafts, acted as banks and donated to Buddhist caves.

Gandhara schoolMathura schoolAmaravati school
RegionNorth-west (Peshawar, Taxila)MathuraAndhra (Krishna valley)
MaterialGrey-blue schist, stuccoRed sandstone (spotted)White marble
StyleGreco-Roman: wavy hair, realistic drapery, muscularIndigenous: smiling face, shaven head, transparent draperyNarrative panels, dynamic figures
ThemesMainly BuddhistBuddhist, Jain and BrahmanicalBuddhist
PatronsKushansKushansSatavahanas, Ikshvakus

What historians say

R.S. Sharma and B.N. Mukherjee stress the commercial boom and Kushan state; Himanshu Prabha Ray shows the role of Buddhist monasteries in Deccan trade networks. The first anthropomorphic Buddha image — Gandhara or Mathura — is debated: A. Foucher argued Gandhara; Ananda Coomaraswamy argued Mathura's independent origin.

Prelims fact bank

  • Pushyamitra Shunga performed two Ashvamedhas.
  • Heliodorus set up the Garuda pillar at Besnagar as a devotee of Vasudeva.
  • Kharavela's Hathigumpha inscription is at Udayagiri, near Bhubaneswar.
  • The Naneghat inscription is of queen Naganika (Satavahana).
  • The Nasik inscription of Gautami Balashri praises Gautamiputra Satakarni.
  • Satavahanas first gave tax-free land grants to Brahmins and Buddhists.
  • Hala wrote the Gathasaptashati.
  • Menander's dialogue with Nagasena is the Milindapanho.
  • Indo-Greeks first issued Indian coins with portraits and gold coins.
  • Rudradaman's Junagadh inscription (150 CE) is the first long Sanskrit inscription.
  • Vima Kadphises issued gold coins on a large scale.
  • Kanishka's accession (c. 78 CE) marks the Shaka era.
  • Kanishka's capitals were Purushapura and Mathura.
  • Ashvaghosha wrote Buddhacharita; Charaka was a physician at Kanishka's court.
  • Arikamedu (Puducherry) was a Roman trading station.
  • Gandhara art used grey schist; Mathura art used red sandstone.
Exam traps
  • Shaka era (78 CE) is linked to Kanishka's accession, though Kanishka was a Kushan.
  • Vikrama Samvat begins 58 BCE; Shaka Samvat 78 CE.
  • Chandragupta II (c. 375–415 CE) took the title Vikramaditya, but could not have founded the Vikrama Samvat of 58 BCE; some guidebooks wrongly say he did.
  • Gandhara = Greek-influenced, schist; Mathura = indigenous, red sandstone; Amaravati = white marble.
  • Nahapana was defeated by Gautamiputra Satakarni; Rudradaman later defeated the Satavahanas.

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

Compare the Gandhara and Mathura schools of art.

How to frame it: Region, material → Style → Themes

Model answer (47 words)

Gandhara art (north-west) used grey schist and showed Greco-Roman features — wavy hair, heavy drapery, realism — mainly for Buddhist themes. Mathura art used red sandstone and an indigenous style — smiling faces, light drapery — for Buddhist, Jain and Brahmanical images. Both flourished under Kushan patronage.

5 marks · 50 words

Assess the achievements of Kanishka.

How to frame it: Empire → Buddhism → Culture → Trade

Model answer (49 words)

Kanishka ruled from Central Asia to the Ganga, with capitals at Purushapura and Mathura. He convened the fourth Buddhist council, which saw the rise of Mahayana, and patronised Ashvaghosha, Nagarjuna and Charaka. His coins show many deities. Control of the Silk Route brought wealth; Gandhara and Mathura art flourished.

5 marks · 50 words

Why is the post-Mauryan period important for trade?

How to frame it: Rome → Silk Route → Guilds, coins

Model answer (43 words)

Monsoon sailing linked Indian ports — Barygaza, Muziris, Arikamedu — to Rome, which paid gold for spices and textiles. The Kushans controlled the Silk Route to China and Central Asia. Guilds organised crafts and banking, and abundant coinage reflected a monetised, urban economy.

Revision card

Remember
  • Shungas: Pushyamitra (Ashvamedha), Agnimitra; Heliodorus pillar (Besnagar). Kanvas. Kharavela — Hathigumpha.
  • Satavahanas: Simuka; Satakarni I (Naneghat); Gautamiputra (Nasik, defeated Nahapana); land grants; Hala.
  • Indo-Greeks: Menander (Milindapanho, Sakala); portrait and gold coins.
  • Shakas: Rudradaman (Junagadh 150 CE). Parthians: Gondophernes.
  • Kushans: Kujula → Vima (gold) → Kanishka (78 CE, 4th council, Ashvaghosha, Charaka).
  • Trade: Rome (Arikamedu, Pliny), Silk Route; guilds. Art: Gandhara, Mathura, Amaravati.
