  3. Whom did Gautamiputra Satakarni defeat?
    Show answer

    The Shaka ruler Nahapana.

  4. What is the Milindapanho?
    Show answer

    Dialogue between Menander and the monk Nagasena.

  5. What is special about the Junagadh inscription?
    Show answer

    First long Sanskrit inscription (Rudradaman, 150 CE).

  6. Which Kushan ruler first issued gold coins widely?
    Show answer

    Vima Kadphises.

  7. Which era begins with Kanishka's accession?
    Show answer

    The Shaka era (78 CE).

  8. Name a Roman trading station in south India.
    Show answer

  9. What material did Mathura sculptors use?

Part I · Unit I09The Sangam Agec. 3rd century BCE – 3rd century CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Sangam literature; Cheras, Cholas, Pandyas; society and economy of the far south

Mains: GS-I: Ancient south India — Sangam polity, society, economy

Mostly Prelims: Sangam texts, kingdoms and capitals, tinai landscapes.

The big picture

"Sangam" refers to assemblies of Tamil poets said to have met at Madurai under Pandya patronage. The poems that survive — about 2,400 — describe the far south (Tamilakam) between roughly the 3rd century BCE and the 3rd century CE, ruled by three crowned dynasties, the Cheras, Cholas and Pandyas, and many chiefs.

It was a society of warrior chiefs, bards, farmers and traders, linked by sea to Rome and South-East Asia. The literature is secular and vivid, describing love (akam) and war and public life (puram).

Exam focus

Sangam texts are among the few early Indian sources independent of Sanskrit traditions — they show regional diversity in ancient India.

WhenWhat happened
3rd c. BCEAshoka's edicts name Cholas, Pandyas, Keralaputras, Satiyaputras
c. 1st c. BCEHathigumpha inscription mentions a confederacy of Tamil states
c. 1st–2nd c. CEPeak of Indo-Roman trade; Muziris, Arikamedu, Kaveripattinam
c. 3rd–6th c. CEKalabhra interregnum

Core notes

I09.1 Sangam literature

  • Tradition speaks of three Sangams at Madurai; only works of the third survive.
  • Tolkappiyam (Tolkappiyar): Tamil grammar and poetics.
  • Ettuttogai (Eight Anthologies) and Pattuppattu (Ten Idylls) — the core Sangam corpus.
  • Patinenkilkanakku (18 minor works), including the Tirukkural of Tiruvalluvar — ethics, wealth and love.
  • Epics (slightly later): Silappadikaram (Ilango Adigal; Kannagi and Kovalan) and Manimekalai (Sittalai Sattanar; Buddhist) — the "twin epics".
  • Themes: akam (inner — love) and puram (outer — war, kings, generosity).

I09.2 The three kingdoms

DynastyRegion and capitalEmblemPortFamous ruler
CheraKerala; Vanji (Karur)BowMuziris, TondiSenguttuvan ("Red Chera"); started the Pattini (Kannagi) cult
CholaKaveri delta; Uraiyur, later PuharTigerKaveripattinam (Puhar)Karikala: battle of Venni; built the Kallanai dam on the Kaveri (tradition)
PandyaMaduraiFishKorkai (pearls)Nedunjeliyan (battle of Talaiyalanganam); patrons of the Sangam

Smaller chiefs (velir) such as Pari and Ay were celebrated for generosity.

I09.3 Society, economy and polity

  • Tinai — five landscapes, each with its occupation and theme: kurinji (hills; hunting), mullai (forest/pasture; herding), marutam (riverine plains; farming), neytal (coast; fishing, salt), palai (dry land; raiding).
  • Kingship hereditary; king advised by the sabha/manram; revenue from land (karai), tribute and plunder.
  • Hero stones (nadukal) for warriors who died in battle.
  • Society: varna was weak; groups such as the panar and viraliyar (bards), vellalar (large landowners), uzhavar (ploughmen), kadaisiyar and adimai (landless labourers, including slaves). Women poets: Avvaiyar, Nachchellaiyar.
  • Religion: Murugan (chief deity, with Kurinji), Mayon (Vishnu), Kotravai (war goddess); Vedic sacrifices and Buddhism and Jainism spread later.
  • Trade: pepper, pearls, muslin, ivory to Rome; Roman coins found widely; Yavanas resident at ports.

What historians say

K.A. Nilakanta Sastri (A History of South India) set the standard chronology. R. Champakalakshmi and Rajan Gurukkal argue that Sangam society was a chiefdom-level, pre-state society organised by kinship and redistribution of plunder, not a full state. Dating the poems is difficult; the 3rd c. BCE–3rd c. CE range is broad.

Prelims fact bank

  • Sangam assemblies were held at Madurai under Pandya patronage (tradition).
  • Tolkappiyam is a work of Tamil grammar by Tolkappiyar.
  • The Sangam corpus: Ettuttogai and Pattuppattu.
  • The Tirukkural was written by Tiruvalluvar.
  • Silappadikaram was written by Ilango Adigal; Manimekalai by Sittalai Sattanar.
  • Chera emblem: bow; Chola: tiger; Pandya: fish.
  • Karikala Chola is credited with the Kallanai dam.
  • Senguttuvan Chera started the Pattini cult of Kannagi.
  • Korkai was the Pandya pearl port; Muziris the Chera port.
  • The five tinai: kurinji, mullai, marutam, neytal, palai.
  • Murugan was the chief deity of the Sangam Tamils.
  • Hero stones are called nadukal.
Exam traps
  • Silappadikaram and Manimekalai are post-Sangam epics, not part of the eight anthologies.
  • Puhar = Kaveripattinam (Chola port); Korkai (Pandya); Muziris (Chera).
  • Akam = love (inner); puram = war and public life (outer).

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

Describe the society and economy of the Sangam age.

How to frame it: Tinai → Social groups → Trade

Model answer (49 words)

Sangam society was organised by five landscapes (tinai) — hills, pasture, plains, coast and dry land — each with its occupation. Bards, landowners, labourers and warrior chiefs formed society; varna was weak. Farming in the river plains and sea trade with Rome in pepper, pearls and textiles brought wealth.

2 marks · 15 words

What are akam and puram?

How to frame it: Meanings

Model answer (14 words)

Two Sangam poetic themes: akam (love, inner life) and puram (war, kings, public life).

Revision card

  • Texts: Tolkappiyam; Ettuttogai, Pattuppattu; Tirukkural; twin epics Silappadikaram, Manimekalai.
  • Chera (bow, Vanji, Muziris, Senguttuvan); Chola (tiger, Uraiyur/Puhar, Karikala); Pandya (fish, Madurai, Korkai).
  • Tinai: kurinji, mullai, marutam, neytal, palai. Murugan. Nadukal. Roman trade.

Part I · Unit I10The Guptasc. 319 – 550 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Gupta rulers, administration, society, economy, culture; the "golden age" debate

Mains: GS-I: Gupta age — polity, culture, science; was it a golden age?

"Was the Gupta age a golden age?" is a classic 10-mark question; Samudragupta's Allahabad inscription and Gupta science and literature are Prelims favourites.

The big picture

The Guptas rebuilt a large empire in north India from Magadha. Samudragupta conquered widely; Chandragupta II took western India from the Shakas. The period saw a flowering of Sanskrit literature (Kalidasa), science (Aryabhata, Varahamihira), temple architecture and art (Sarnath Buddha, Ajanta paintings).

Nationalist historians called it a golden age. Others point to growing caste rigidity, the decline of towns and trade in later centuries, the rise of feudal land grants and worsening position of women and untouchables.

Exam focus

The golden-age debate is the analytical core: prepare both sides with evidence.

WhenWhat happened
c. 319–335Chandragupta I; Gupta era begins (319–20); marries Lichchhavi princess Kumaradevi
c. 335–375Samudragupta; Allahabad pillar inscription
c. 375–415Chandragupta II (Vikramaditya); conquest of the Shakas; Faxian visits
c. 415–455Kumaragupta I; Nalanda founded (tradition)
c. 455–467Skandagupta repels the Hunas; Sudarshana lake repaired
c. 499Aryabhata writes the Aryabhatiya
c. 500–530Huna invasions (Toramana, Mihirakula)
c. 550Gupta rule ends

Core notes

I10.1 Rulers

RulerAchievements
Chandragupta ITitle Maharajadhiraja; marriage to Lichchhavi princess Kumaradevi (king-queen coins); started the Gupta era (319–20 CE)
SamudraguptaAllahabad pillar inscription (prashasti by Harishena, on Ashoka's pillar): uprooted rulers of Aryavarta (north); captured and released 12 kings of Dakshinapatha (south) — grahana-moksha-anugraha; frontier kings and republics (Malavas, Yaudheyas) paid tribute; Shakas, Kushans and Sri Lanka sent homage. Performed Ashvamedha; veena-playing coins — a poet (Kaviraja). V.A. Smith called him the "Napoleon of India".
Chandragupta II (Vikramaditya)Defeated the Western Kshatrapas (Rudrasimha III), gaining Gujarat and its ports — silver coins; Ujjain a second capital; daughter Prabhavatigupta married the Vakataka king; Navaratnas (Kalidasa) traditionally at his court; Mehrauli iron pillar (king "Chandra"); Faxian visited
Kumaragupta IFounded Nalanda (tradition); Pushyamitras attacked
SkandaguptaRepelled the Hunas; repaired the Sudarshana lake (Junagadh inscription, governor Parnadatta); debased gold coins

I10.2 Administration

  • Grand titles (Paramabhattaraka, Maharajadhiraja); divine claims.
  • Less centralised than the Mauryas: feudatories (samantas) ruled many areas.
  • Provinces bhukti (under uparika), districts vishaya (under vishayapati); towns had councils with guild heads (nagarashreshthi, sarthavaha, prathama-kulika).
  • Officials: kumaramatya (high officers), sandhivigrahika (peace and war), mahadandanayaka (justice/army).
  • Officials paid by land grants rather than cash — a step towards feudalism.
  • Land revenue (1/4 to 1/6); taxes bhaga, bhoga, udranga, uparikara; forced labour (vishti).

I10.3 Economy and society

Economy: agrarian expansion; many land grants (agrahara) to Brahmins with fiscal and administrative rights; gold coins (dinara) in abundance — the most gold coins of any ancient Indian dynasty; trade with the Byzantine empire and South-East Asia, but decline in Roman trade after the 3rd century and fewer urban sites in later centuries.

Society:

  • Varna rules stricter; proliferation of jatis; untouchability increased — Faxian notes that chandalas lived outside towns and struck wood to announce their approach.
  • Women: early marriage; first epigraphic sati at Eran (510 CE, Bhanugupta's general Goparaja); women had property rights only in stridhana; some royal women (Prabhavatigupta as regent) were powerful.
  • Guilds: the Mandasor (Dashapura) inscription of a silk weavers' guild (5th century) records how the guild migrated from Lata (south Gujarat) to Dashapura, prospered, changed occupations and built a Sun temple.
  • Religion: Vaishnavism (Bhagavata) favoured — Guptas called themselves Paramabhagavata; Shaivism; Buddhist Nalanda flourished; Puranas compiled.

I10.4 Culture and science

FieldAchievements
LiteratureKalidasa: Abhijnanashakuntalam, Malavikagnimitram, Vikramorvashiyam (plays); Raghuvamsha, Kumarasambhava (epics); Meghaduta, Ritusamhara. Vishakhadatta (Mudrarakshasa, Devichandraguptam); Shudraka (Mrichchhakatika); Vishnu Sharma (Panchatantra); Amarasimha (Amarakosha); Puranas and the epics took final shape
ScienceAryabhata (Aryabhatiya, 499): earth rotates on its axis; causes of eclipses; value of π (3.1416); place value. Varahamihira (Panchasiddhantika, Brihatsamhita). Brahmagupta (628) slightly later. Dhanvantari, Vagbhata (medicine)
MetallurgyMehrauli (Delhi) iron pillar — rust-free for over 1,500 years
ArchitectureBeginning of structural temples: Dashavatara temple, Deogarh (shikhara), Bhitargaon (brick), Tigawa, Sanchi temple 17; Udayagiri caves (Varaha panel)
Painting, sculptureAjanta caves (many under the Vakatakas, contemporaries); Sarnath seated Buddha; Bagh caves
EducationNalanda; Taxila declined

I10.5 The golden-age debate; decline

Debate

For: political unity, prosperity (gold coins), classical Sanskrit literature, science, art and temples, Faxian's picture of a peaceful, mild government.
Against: benefits limited to elites; caste rigidity and untouchability; decline of women's status (sati at Eran); feudal land grants and forced labour; urban decline after the 4th–5th centuries; R.S. Sharma and D.N. Jha reject the "golden age" label as a nationalist construction.
Balanced view: a high point of classical culture for the elite, alongside growing social inequality.

Decline: Huna invasions (Toramana, Mihirakula), weak successors, rise of feudatories (Maukharis, Maitrakas, Later Guptas, Yashodharman of Mandsaur), loss of western trade, financial strain (debased coins).

What historians say

V.A. Smith and nationalist historians (R.C. Majumdar, R.K. Mookerji) celebrated the Guptas. R.S. Sharma (Indian Feudalism) and D.N. Jha stressed land grants, decline of trade and social inequality. Upinder Singh suggests calling it a "classical" age rather than golden.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Gupta era began in 319–20 CE under Chandragupta I.
  • Chandragupta I married the Lichchhavi princess Kumaradevi.
  • Harishena composed the Allahabad pillar inscription of Samudragupta.
  • Samudragupta captured and released 12 southern kings (grahana-moksha-anugraha).
  • V.A. Smith called Samudragupta the "Napoleon of India".
  • Samudragupta's veena-playing coins show him as a musician.
  • Chandragupta II defeated the Shakas and issued silver coins.
  • Faxian visited during the reign of Chandragupta II.
  • Prabhavatigupta married into the Vakataka dynasty.
  • Skandagupta repelled the Hunas.
  • The first epigraphic evidence of sati is at Eran (510 CE).
  • Provinces were bhuktis; districts vishayas.
  • Aryabhata wrote the Aryabhatiya in 499 CE.
  • Varahamihira wrote the Brihatsamhita and Panchasiddhantika.
  • The Dashavatara temple at Deogarh is an early temple with a shikhara.
  • The Mehrauli iron pillar belongs to the Gupta period.
  • Amarasimha wrote the Amarakosha.
Exam traps
  • The Allahabad pillar is an Ashokan pillar re-used by Samudragupta.
  • Napoleon of India = Samudragupta (V.A. Smith), not Chandragupta II.
  • Faxian did not name the Gupta king.
  • Ajanta paintings of this period are largely Vakataka-patronised.
  • Eran sati (510 CE) belongs to Bhanugupta's time.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Was the Gupta period a "golden age"? Examine critically.

How to frame it: Case for → Case against → Conclusion

Model answer (89 words)

The Guptas unified north India; abundant gold coins reflect prosperity. Kalidasa, Vishakhadatta and Amarasimha enriched Sanskrit; Aryabhata and Varahamihira advanced astronomy and mathematics; the Deogarh temple, Sarnath Buddha and the Mehrauli pillar show artistic and technical skill. Faxian describes mild government. Yet these benefits were confined to elites. Caste became rigid and untouchability spread; sati appears at Eran; land grants created feudal intermediaries and forced labour; towns and trade declined later. It was a classical age of high culture for the few rather than a golden age for all.

5 marks · 50 words

What does the Allahabad pillar inscription tell us about Samudragupta?

How to frame it: Author → Conquests → Personal qualities

Model answer (48 words)

Composed by Harishena on an Ashokan pillar, it records Samudragupta's uprooting of northern kings, the capture and release of twelve southern rulers, tribute from frontier kings and republics, and homage from foreign powers. It also praises him as a poet and musician. It is a eulogy, so exaggerated.

2 marks · 15 words

Who was Aryabhata?

How to frame it: Identity

Model answer (11 words)

Gupta-era astronomer (Aryabhatiya, 499 CE) who explained earth's rotation and eclipses.

Revision card

Remember
  • Chandragupta I (319–20 era, Kumaradevi) → Samudragupta (Allahabad, Harishena, Napoleon) → Chandragupta II (Shakas, Faxian, Kalidasa) → Kumaragupta (Nalanda) → Skandagupta (Hunas).
  • Admin: samantas; bhukti, vishaya; land grants; vishti.
  • Society: caste rigidity, chandalas, Eran sati 510.
  • Culture: Kalidasa; Aryabhata 499; Varahamihira; Deogarh; Mehrauli pillar; Ajanta.
  • Golden age: for vs against; classical age.

  2. Who composed the Allahabad pillar inscription?
    Show answer

  3. What was Samudragupta's policy towards southern kings?
    Show answer

    Capture, release and reinstatement (grahana-moksha-anugraha).

  4. Which Gupta ruler defeated the Shakas?
    Show answer

    Chandragupta II.

  5. Who repelled the Hunas?
    Show answer

    Skandagupta.

  6. Where is the first inscriptional evidence of sati?
    Show answer

    Eran (510 CE).

  7. What were bhuktis?
    Show answer

    Provinces of the Gupta empire.

  8. What did Aryabhata explain?
    Show answer

    Earth's rotation, causes of eclipses, value of π.

  9. Name an early Gupta temple with a shikhara.
    Show answer

Part I · Unit I11Harsha, Chalukyas, Pallavas and Rashtrakutasc. 550 – 973 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Harshavardhana; Chalukyas of Badami; Pallavas of Kanchi; Rashtrakutas; art and architecture

Mains: GS-I: Early medieval polity; regional kingdoms; temple architecture

Harsha (Xuanzang, Banabhatta), Pulakeshin II (Aihole), Pallava architecture (Mahabalipuram) and the Rashtrakuta Kailasa temple are standard questions.

The big picture

After the Guptas, power became regional. In the north, Harshavardhana of Thanesar and Kannauj built the last large north Indian kingdom before the Turks. In the Deccan and south, three dynasties dominated and competed: the Chalukyas of Badami, the Pallavas of Kanchi, and later the Rashtrakutas, who replaced the Chalukyas and joined the struggle for Kannauj.

These kingdoms patronised temple building on a grand scale — the roots of the Nagara, Dravida and Vesara styles — and Bhakti saints spread devotional religion in the Tamil country.

Exam focus

The Chalukya–Pallava rivalry and the tripartite struggle (Pratihara–Pala–Rashtrakuta, see I15) are the frameworks for this period.

WhenWhat happened
606–647Harshavardhana
c. 618–619Pulakeshin II defeats Harsha on the Narmada
634Aihole inscription (Ravikirti)
c. 630–645Xuanzang in India
643Kannauj assembly and Prayag assembly
642Narasimhavarman I takes Vatapi; Pulakeshin II killed
c. 700Shore temple, Mahabalipuram (Narasimhavarman II)
c. 753Dantidurga founds Rashtrakuta power
c. 757–773Krishna I; Kailasa temple at Ellora
814–878Amoghavarsha I
949Krishna III defeats the Cholas at Takkolam

Core notes

I11.1 Harshavardhana (606–647)

  • Pushyabhuti dynasty of Thanesar; after his brother Rajyavardhana's murder by Shashanka of Gauda, Harsha took the throne and moved his capital to Kannauj.
  • Defeated by Pulakeshin II on the Narmada (c. 618–619) — the limit of his power southward (Aihole inscription).
  • Sources: Banabhatta's Harshacharita and Kadambari; Xuanzang's Si-yu-ki; Banskhera and Madhuban copper plates.
  • Held the Kannauj assembly (in Xuanzang's honour, 643) and a quinquennial Prayag assembly (Mahamoksha Parishad) where he gave away his wealth.
  • Wrote three Sanskrit plays: Nagananda, Ratnavali, Priyadarshika.
  • Patron of Nalanda; leaned to Mahayana Buddhism but honoured Shiva and Surya.
  • Administration more feudal than the Guptas; officials paid in land; Xuanzang notes revenue was 1/6 and that travel was unsafe (he was robbed).

I11.2 Chalukyas of Badami (Vatapi)

  • Founder Pulakeshin I (fortified Vatapi, c. 543).
  • Pulakeshin II (c. 610–642): defeated Harsha; took the title Dakshinapatheshvara; sent an embassy to the Persian king Khusrau II; his achievements are recorded in the Aihole inscription (634) by the Jain poet Ravikirti; killed when the Pallava Narasimhavarman I took Vatapi (642).
  • Vikramaditya II took Kanchi in revenge (but spared it); queen Lokamahadevi built the Virupaksha temple, Pattadakal.
  • Overthrown by the Rashtrakuta Dantidurga (c. 753). (Later Chalukyas of Kalyani, 973–1190, are a separate line.)
  • Architecture: Aihole ("cradle of Indian temple architecture" — Ladh Khan, Durga temples), Badami caves, Pattadakal (UNESCO; temples in both Nagara and Dravida styles — Papanatha, Virupaksha).

I11.3 Pallavas of Kanchi

RulerAchievements
SimhavishnuFounded the imperial line (late 6th c.)
Mahendravarman I (c. 600–630)Rock-cut cave temples (Mandagapattu); wrote the farce Mattavilasa Prahasana; converted from Jainism to Shaivism under Appar; defeated by Pulakeshin II at Pullalur
Narasimhavarman I (630–668)Took Vatapi (642) — title Vatapikonda; called Mamalla; Mahabalipuram (Mamallapuram) monolithic rathas; naval expedition to Sri Lanka; Xuanzang visited Kanchi
Narasimhavarman II (Rajasimha)Shore temple (Mahabalipuram) and Kailasanatha temple (Kanchi) — structural temples; embassy to China
Nandivarman IIVaikuntha Perumal temple, Kanchi
AparajitaDefeated by Aditya I Chola (late 9th c.) — end of Pallava power

Pallava inscriptions already mention the local assemblies that the Cholas later developed (I12): the sabha of Brahmin landowners, working through sub-committees for irrigation, roads and temples; the ur of non-Brahmin villages; and the nagaram of merchants.

Pallava architecture moved from rock-cut caves (Mahendra style) to monolithic rathas (Mamalla style) to structural temples (Rajasimha style). Kanchi was a great centre of learning (the ghatika); Dandin (Kavyadarsha) and Bharavi (Kiratarjuniya) wrote under Pallava patronage. Nayanars and Alvars spread Bhakti.

I11.4 The Rashtrakutas

  • Founded by Dantidurga (c. 753), feudatory of the Chalukyas, who overthrew Kirtivarman II. Capital later at Manyakheta (Malkhed).
  • Krishna I: built the monolithic Kailasa (Kailashnath) temple at Ellora (cave 16), cut from a single rock.
  • Dhruva and Govinda III: raided north India in the tripartite struggle, defeating the Pratiharas and Palas.
  • Amoghavarsha I (814–878): reigned 64 years; Jain sympathies (Jinasena); wrote Kavirajamarga, an early Kannada work on poetics, and Prashnottara Ratnamalika; founded Manyakheta. Arab merchant Sulaiman ranked him among the four great kings of the world.
  • Indra III sacked Kannauj (916). Krishna III defeated the Cholas at Takkolam (949).
  • Ended by the Chalukya Tailapa II (973).
  • Culture: Ellora (Hindu, Buddhist and Jain caves), Elephanta caves (date debated, often linked with this period); Kannada literature (Pampa, Ponna) began; Arab traders welcomed (the Balhara of Arab writers).

What historians say

Older historians called Harsha the "last great Hindu emperor"; R.S. Sharma treats his kingdom as feudal and loosely held. K.A. Nilakanta Sastri remains the standard for southern dynasties; Burton Stein proposed the "segmentary state" model for south Indian kingdoms (applied mainly to the Cholas, I12). B.D. Chattopadhyaya sees the period as one of regional state formation, not decline.

Prelims fact bank

  • Harsha moved his capital from Thanesar to Kannauj.
  • Pulakeshin II defeated Harsha on the Narmada.
  • Banabhatta wrote Harshacharita and Kadambari.
  • Harsha wrote Nagananda, Ratnavali and Priyadarshika.
  • Harsha held assemblies at Kannauj and Prayag.
  • The Aihole inscription (634) was composed by Ravikirti.
  • Pulakeshin II took the title Dakshinapatheshvara.
  • Narasimhavarman I took Vatapi in 642 and was called Vatapikonda and Mamalla.
  • Mahendravarman I wrote Mattavilasa Prahasana.
  • The Shore temple and Kailasanatha temple (Kanchi) were built by Narasimhavarman II (Rajasimha).
  • Aihole is called the cradle of Indian temple architecture.
  • Pattadakal has temples in both Nagara and Dravida styles.
  • Dantidurga founded the Rashtrakuta dynasty.
  • Krishna I built the Kailasa temple at Ellora.
  • Amoghavarsha I wrote Kavirajamarga.
  • Krishna III defeated the Cholas at Takkolam (949).
Exam traps
  • The Chalukya capital was Badami (Vatapi); Aihole was an early temple and trading centre. NCERT Class 6 calls Aihole the capital.
  • Mahabalipuram rathas: Narasimhavarman I; Shore temple: Narasimhavarman II.
  • Kailasa temple (Ellora) = Rashtrakuta Krishna I; Kailasanatha temple (Kanchi) = Pallava Rajasimha.
  • Chalukyas of Badami (6th–8th c.) ≠ Chalukyas of Kalyani (10th–12th c.) ≠ Chaulukyas (Solankis) of Gujarat.
  • Ravikirti (Aihole, Pulakeshin II) ≠ Harishena (Allahabad, Samudragupta).

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

Describe the contribution of the Pallavas to temple architecture.

How to frame it: Phases → Examples

Model answer (39 words)

Pallava architecture evolved from Mahendravarman I's rock-cut caves, through Narasimhavarman I's monolithic rathas at Mahabalipuram, to Rajasimha's structural Shore temple and Kailasanatha temple at Kanchi. This sequence laid the foundation of the Dravida style later perfected by the Cholas.

5 marks · 50 words

Assess Harshavardhana as a ruler.

How to frame it: Conquests → Administration → Culture

Model answer (49 words)

Harsha unified much of north India from Kannauj but was checked by Pulakeshin II on the Narmada. His administration relied on feudatories and land grants. A playwright and patron of Nalanda and Banabhatta, he held the Kannauj and Prayag assemblies. Xuanzang's account shows a cultured but loosely held kingdom.

2 marks · 15 words

What is the Aihole inscription?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Ravikirti's 634 CE prashasti of Pulakeshin II, recording his victory over Harsha.

Revision card

Remember
  • Harsha: Thanesar → Kannauj; Narmada defeat; Banabhatta, Xuanzang; Kannauj and Prayag assemblies; 3 plays.
  • Chalukyas (Badami): Pulakeshin II (Aihole, Ravikirti, Dakshinapatheshvara; killed 642); Aihole, Badami, Pattadakal.
  • Pallavas: Mahendravarman I (caves, Mattavilasa); Narasimhavarman I (Vatapikonda, rathas); Rajasimha (Shore, Kailasanatha).
  • Rashtrakutas: Dantidurga; Krishna I (Kailasa, Ellora); Govinda III; Amoghavarsha (Kavirajamarga); Krishna III (Takkolam 949).

Part I · Unit I12The Cholasc. 850 – 1279 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Imperial Cholas: rulers, administration, local self-government, naval expeditions, art

Mains: GS-I: Chola administration and village self-government; temple architecture; bronzes

Chola local self-government (Uttaramerur), naval expeditions and the Brihadeshvara temple are perennial questions.

The big picture

The imperial Cholas of Thanjavur were the greatest power in south India from the 10th to the 12th century. Rajaraja I and Rajendra I conquered the Tamil country, Sri Lanka and the Maldives, led an army to the Ganga, and sent a naval expedition to Srivijaya in South-East Asia.

The Cholas are best remembered for village self-government (recorded in the Uttaramerur inscriptions), for the Dravida temple at its peak (Brihadeshvara, Thanjavur) and for bronze sculpture (Nataraja).

Exam focus

The Uttaramerur inscriptions are the best evidence anywhere in early India of elected local bodies — use them in any answer on Indian democratic traditions.

WhenWhat happened
c. 850Vijayalaya takes Thanjavur
c. 871–907Aditya I defeats the Pallavas
907–955Parantaka I; Uttaramerur inscriptions (919, 921)
949Takkolam: Cholas defeated by the Rashtrakutas
985–1014Rajaraja I; Brihadeshvara temple completed (1010)
1014–1044Rajendra I; Ganga expedition; Gangaikondacholapuram
1025Naval expedition against Srivijaya
1070–1122Kulottunga I (Chalukya–Chola union); embassy to China (1077)
1279End of Chola rule (Pandyas)

Core notes

I12.1 Rulers and conquests

RulerAchievements
VijayalayaTook Thanjavur from the Muttaraiyars (c. 850); founded the imperial line
Parantaka ITook Madurai (title Maduraikonda); Uttaramerur inscriptions; defeated at Takkolam (949) by Krishna III
Rajaraja I (985–1014)Destroyed the Chera navy at Kandalur Salai; conquered northern Sri Lanka (Anuradhapura destroyed; Polonnaruva capital); took the Maldives; fought the Western Chalukyas; land survey (1001); built Brihadeshvara (Rajarajeshvara) temple, Thanjavur (1010); titles Mummadi Chola, Shivapadashekhara; endowed the Chudamani Vihara at Nagapattinam for the Srivijaya king
Rajendra I (1014–1044)Completed the conquest of Sri Lanka; Ganga expedition (defeated Mahipala of Bengal) — title Gangaikonda; new capital Gangaikondacholapuram with the Cholagangam tank; naval expedition to Srivijaya (1025, Kadaram) — title Kadaramkonda
Rajadhiraja, Rajendra II, VirarajendraWars with the Western Chalukyas (Koppam, 1054)
Kulottunga IUnion of Eastern Chalukya and Chola houses; abolished some tolls (Sungam tavirtta); embassy of 72 merchants to China (1077)

I12.2 Administration and local self-government

  • King with officials (perundaram, sirudaram); a large army including elephants and a navy.
  • Divisions: empire → mandalam (province) → valanadu → nadu → villages.
  • Local assemblies:
    • Ur — assembly of ordinary villages (landholders).
    • Sabha (mahasabha) — assembly of brahmadeya (Brahmin) villages.
    • Nagaram — assembly of merchants in trading towns.
    • Nadu — assembly of a group of villages.
  • Uttaramerur inscriptions (919 and 921, Parantaka I): describe election of the sabha's committees (variyams) — garden, tank, justice, annual committees — by lot (kudavolai: names on palm leaves drawn from a pot). Qualifications: land ownership, a house, age 35–70, Vedic knowledge; disqualifications: not having submitted accounts, crimes, recent service on a committee.
  • Land revenue: about 1/3 of produce; land survey under Rajaraja I and Kulottunga I.
  • Kinds of land: vellanvagai (non-Brahmin peasant proprietors), brahmadeya (Brahmins), shalabhoga (upkeep of a school), devadana or tirunamattukkani (temples), pallichchhandam (Jain institutions).
  • Taxes: inscriptions use over 400 tax terms; the most frequent are vetti (forced labour) and kadamai (land revenue); others fell on thatching a house, climbing palm trees with a ladder and inheriting property.
  • Rural elites: rich Vellala peasants dominated the nadu; kings gave them titles such as muvendavelan and araiyar and posts at the centre.
Debate

How centralised was the Chola state? Nilakanta Sastri saw a strong centralised monarchy. Burton Stein proposed a "segmentary state": real power lay with peasant (nadu) assemblies, the king having ritual supremacy over a loose structure. Most historians now see a middle position — growing central control in the core over time.

I12.3 Economy, religion and art

Economy: irrigation tanks; temples as landowners, banks and employers; merchant guilds (Manigramam, Ayyavole/Ainnurruvar, also known as the Nanadesis) traded across the Indian Ocean; trade with China and Srivijaya.

Crafts: sculptors (sthapatis) of Svamimalai near Thanjavur cast bronze images and bell-metal lamps; the Saliya weavers of Thanjavur and Uraiyur made temple flags and fine and coarse cotton; the Kaikkolar weavers grew rich enough to endow temples; the Panchalas (Vishwakarma) — smiths, masons and carpenters — were essential to temple building.

Religion: Shaivism dominant; the Nayanar hymns were compiled into the Tirumurai (Nambi Andar Nambi); Vaishnava Alvar hymns into the Nalayira Divya Prabandham (Nathamuni); Ramanuja (11th–12th c.); Sekkilar's Periyapuranam under Kulottunga II; Kamban's Tamil Ramayana.

Art:

  • Brihadeshvara temple, Thanjavur (Rajaraja I, 1010; architect Kunjara Mallan Raja Raja Perunthachan): 66-m vimana, the tallest of its time; the Great Living Chola Temples (UNESCO) include Gangaikondacholapuram and Airavateshvara (Darasuram).
  • Dravida features: tall vimana over the sanctum, later large gopurams (gateways), mandapas, enclosure walls.
  • Bronzes by the lost-wax method; the Nataraja (Shiva as cosmic dancer) is the best-known.

What historians say

K.A. Nilakanta Sastri's The Colas is the classic account. Burton Stein (Peasant State and Society in Medieval South India) introduced the segmentary-state model; Noboru Karashima and James Heitzman used inscriptions to show increasing bureaucratic control and the role of temples in the economy.

Prelims fact bank

  • Vijayalaya founded the imperial Chola line by taking Thanjavur (c. 850).
  • The Uttaramerur inscriptions (919, 921) belong to Parantaka I's reign.
  • Kudavolai was the system of selection by lot from palm leaves in a pot.
  • Ur = assembly of ordinary villages; sabha = brahmadeya villages; nagaram = merchants.
  • Rajaraja I built the Brihadeshvara temple at Thanjavur (1010).
  • Rajaraja I conducted a land survey (1001).
  • Rajendra I took the title Gangaikonda and founded Gangaikondacholapuram.
  • Rajendra I's navy attacked Srivijaya (1025).
  • Kulottunga I sent an embassy of 72 merchants to China (1077).
  • Chola provinces were mandalams; subdivisions valanadu and nadu.
  • The Nataraja bronze is a Chola masterpiece made by the lost-wax method.
  • Svamimalai near Thanjavur was a centre of bronze-casting sthapatis.
  • Sekkilar wrote Periyapuranam; Kamban wrote the Tamil Ramayana.
Exam traps
  • Uttaramerur = Parantaka I (919, 921), not Rajaraja I.
  • Airavateshvara temple, Darasuram, was built by Rajaraja II (12th century), not Rajadhiraja; some guidebooks get this wrong.
  • Gangaikonda = Rajendra I; Mummadi Chola = Rajaraja I.
  • Takkolam (949): the Cholas lost to Rashtrakuta Krishna III.
  • Chola temples: tall vimana dominates; tall gopurams are a later (Pandya–Vijayanagara) emphasis.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Describe the system of local self-government under the Cholas.

How to frame it: Units → Assemblies → Uttaramerur → Functions → Assessment

Model answer (89 words)

The Chola empire was divided into mandalams, valanadus, nadus and villages. Villages had assemblies: the ur in ordinary villages, the sabha in Brahmin brahmadeya villages, and the nagaram in merchant towns; nadu assemblies covered groups of villages. The Uttaramerur inscriptions of Parantaka I (919, 921) describe how the sabha chose committees — for tanks, gardens, justice — by lot (kudavolai), with qualifications of property, age and learning and bars on defaulters. Assemblies managed land, irrigation, temples, taxes and disputes. It was a remarkable, if elite-based, system of local self-rule.

5 marks · 50 words

Describe the naval achievements of the Cholas.

How to frame it: Rajaraja I → Rajendra I → Significance

Model answer (48 words)

Rajaraja I destroyed the Chera fleet at Kandalur, conquered northern Sri Lanka and the Maldives. Rajendra I completed Sri Lanka's conquest and in 1025 sent a fleet against Srivijaya, securing trade routes to China. Chola sea power protected merchant guilds and Tamil commerce across the Bay of Bengal.

2 marks · 15 words

What was kudavolai?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (14 words)

Chola selection by lot: candidates' names on palm leaves drawn from a pot (Uttaramerur).

Revision card

Remember
  • Vijayalaya → Parantaka I (Uttaramerur; Takkolam loss) → Rajaraja I (Sri Lanka, Maldives, survey, Brihadeshvara) → Rajendra I (Ganga, Gangaikondacholapuram, Srivijaya 1025) → Kulottunga I (China embassy).
  • Units: mandalam, valanadu, nadu. Assemblies: ur, sabha, nagaram, nadu. Kudavolai.
  • Segmentary state (Stein) vs centralised (Sastri).
  • Art: Brihadeshvara, Gangaikondacholapuram, Darasuram; Nataraja bronze.

Self-test

    Show answer

  2. Which inscriptions describe Chola village elections?
    Show answer

    Uttaramerur (919, 921).

  3. What was the sabha?
    Show answer

    Assembly of a brahmadeya (Brahmin) village.

  4. Who built the Brihadeshvara temple?
    Show answer

    Rajaraja I (1010).

  5. Which Chola sent a navy to Srivijaya?
    Show answer

    Rajendra I (1025).

  6. What new capital did Rajendra I build?
    Show answer

    Gangaikondacholapuram.

  7. Who proposed the segmentary-state model?
    Show answer

    Burton Stein.

  8. Name two Chola merchant guilds.
    Show answer

    Manigramam and Ayyavole (Ainnurruvar).

Sources: K.A. Nilakanta Sastri, The Colas.; Burton Stein, Peasant State and Society in Medieval South India.; Satish Chandra, History of Medieval India, ch. 3.; NCERT, Our Pasts II (Class VII), ch. 2.

Part I · Unit I13Indian Knowledge and Value SystemAncient and early medieval India

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Six schools of Indian philosophy; purusharthas, ashramas, sanskaras; ancient education

Mains: GS-I: Indian knowledge tradition, value system and philosophy; ancient education

Short notes on darshanas, purusharthas, Nalanda/Taxila and concepts such as rita, dharma and rina are common in both stages.

The big picture

Ancient India developed systematic thought on how to live (values), what is real (philosophy) and how to know (epistemology). The core ideas — dharma, karma, rebirth, moksha — were debated by six "orthodox" schools that accepted the Vedas (astika) and by "heterodox" schools that did not (nastika: Buddhist, Jain, Charvaka, Ajivika).

Knowledge was passed on through the guru–shishya tradition, gurukulas and later great monastic universities such as Taxila, Nalanda and Vikramashila.

Exam focus

In answers, show diversity: Indian thought included ritualists, mystics, logicians, atomists and materialists — not a single "Hindu view".

WhenWhat happened
c. 1500–600 BCEVedas and Upanishads: rita, Brahman, Atman
c. 600–300 BCEShramana schools; early Samkhya
c. 200 BCE – 400 CESutras of the darshanas (Yoga Sutra, Nyaya Sutra, Brahma Sutra)
5th c. CENalanda founded
c. 8th c. CEShankaracharya: Advaita Vedanta; Vikramashila founded by Dharmapala
11th–12th c.Ramanuja: Vishishtadvaita

Core notes

I13.1 Core values and concepts

  • Rita (Vedic cosmic and moral order) → dharma (duty, righteousness, law).
  • Purusharthas — four aims of life: dharma (righteousness), artha (wealth, power), kama (pleasure), moksha (liberation).
  • Ashramas — four stages: brahmacharya (student), grihastha (householder), vanaprastha (retirement), sannyasa (renunciation). Grihastha was praised as supporting all the others.
  • Rinas — three debts: to the gods (deva-rina), sages (rishi-rina) and ancestors (pitri-rina).
  • Sanskaras — rites of passage, traditionally 16 (garbhadhana to antyeshti), including upanayana and vivaha.
  • Marriage and family in the Dharmashastras: the Manusmriti (compiled c. 200 BCE – 200 CE) lists eight forms of marriage; the first four (Brahma, Daiva, Arsha, Prajapatya) were approved and the rest (Asura, Gandharva, Rakshasa, Paishacha) condemned. Brahmanical rules favoured exogamy and gotra rules (a wife took her husband's gotra; no marriage within the gotra), but Satavahana queens kept their fathers' gotra names, and kin marriage (endogamy) was common in the south. Women could keep stridhana (marriage gifts) but had no share in the paternal estate.
  • A different view of kingship: a Buddhist myth in the Sutta Pitaka says people chose a ruler, Mahasammata ("the great elect"), to keep order, so kingship rested on human choice and taxes were payment for his service.
  • Pancha mahayajnas — five daily duties of the householder (to Brahman, gods, ancestors, beings, guests).
  • Karma and rebirth (samsara); liberation (moksha / nirvana / kaivalya).
  • Ethical ideals: ahimsa, satya, daana (charity), seva; tolerance (Ashoka's RE XII; "ekam sat viprah bahudha vadanti" — Rig Veda).

I13.2 The six orthodox darshanas

SchoolFounder / textMain idea
SamkhyaKapilaDualism of purusha (consciousness) and prakriti (matter, with three gunas); originally without God
YogaPatanjali, Yoga SutraEight limbs (ashtanga): yama, niyama, asana, pranayama, pratyahara, dharana, dhyana, samadhi
NyayaGautama (Akshapada), Nyaya SutraLogic and valid means of knowledge (perception, inference, comparison, testimony)
VaisheshikaKanadaAtomism: the world made of atoms (anu, paramanu); categories (padartha)
Mimamsa (Purva)JaiminiAuthority of the Vedas and correct ritual action (karma-kanda)
Vedanta (Uttara Mimamsa)Badarayana, Brahma SutraKnowledge of Brahman from the Upanishads; schools: Advaita (Shankara — non-dualism), Vishishtadvaita (Ramanuja — qualified non-dualism), Dvaita (Madhva — dualism)

They form three pairs: Samkhya–Yoga, Nyaya–Vaisheshika, Mimamsa–Vedanta. Heterodox (nastika): Buddhism, Jainism, Charvaka (materialism), Ajivika (fatalism) — see I06.

I13.3 Education and learning

  • Gurukula: students lived with the teacher; oral learning; Vedas, Vedangas, grammar, logic.
  • Taxila (Takshashila, Gandhara): famous from c. 6th c. BCE; medicine (Jivaka), politics (Kautilya traditionally), grammar (Panini). Not a university with a campus but a cluster of teachers.
  • Nalanda (Bihar): founded in the 5th c. (Kumaragupta I, tradition); patronised by Harsha and the Palas; Xuanzang and Yijing studied there; teachers Shilabhadra, Dharmapala; huge library (Dharmaganja); destroyed by Bakhtiyar Khalji (c. 1200); UNESCO site.
  • Vikramashila (Bihar): founded by the Pala king Dharmapala (c. 800); tantric Buddhism; Atisha Dipankara went to Tibet from here.
  • Others: Vallabhi (Gujarat), Odantapuri, Somapura (Bangladesh), Kanchi ghatika, Jagaddala.
  • Learning in the "64 kalas" (arts) and the 14/18 vidyas; debates (shastrartha).

I13.4 Political and ethical thought

  • Saptanga theory (Arthashastra): the state has seven limbs — swami (king), amatya (ministers), janapada (territory and people), durga (fort), kosha (treasury), danda (army), mitra (ally).
  • Mandala theory: circle of states — neighbour as enemy, neighbour's neighbour as friend.
  • Rajadharma: the king's duty to protect subjects and uphold dharma (Mahabharata Shanti Parva).
  • Dharmashastras (Manu, Yajnavalkya, Narada) set social and legal norms; they were prescriptive and contested.
  • Bhagavad Gita: nishkama karma (action without attachment to results); paths of knowledge, action and devotion.

What historians say

S. Radhakrishnan (Indian Philosophy) and S.N. Dasgupta wrote standard histories of Indian thought. Debiprasad Chattopadhyaya (Lokayata) highlighted materialist traditions; Amartya Sen (The Argumentative Indian) stresses the long tradition of debate and heterodoxy. Historians caution that Dharmashastra ideals (varna, ashrama) describe norms, not how everyone lived.

Prelims fact bank

  • The four purusharthas are dharma, artha, kama and moksha.
  • The four ashramas are brahmacharya, grihastha, vanaprastha and sannyasa.
  • The three rinas are owed to gods, sages and ancestors.
  • Kapila founded Samkhya; Patanjali wrote the Yoga Sutra.
  • Gautama (Akshapada) wrote the Nyaya Sutra; Kanada founded Vaisheshika atomism.
  • Jaimini founded Purva Mimamsa; Badarayana wrote the Brahma Sutra.
  • Shankara taught Advaita; Ramanuja Vishishtadvaita; Madhva Dvaita.
  • Yoga has eight limbs (ashtanga).
  • Vikramashila was founded by the Pala king Dharmapala.
  • Nalanda was destroyed by Bakhtiyar Khalji c. 1200.
  • Atisha Dipankara went to Tibet from Vikramashila.
  • The Arthashastra's saptanga theory lists seven limbs of the state.
Exam traps
  • Vaisheshika (Kanada) is the atomist school; Nyaya (Gautama) is logic.
  • Gautama of Nyaya is not Gautama Buddha.
  • Vikramashila was founded by Dharmapala; Nalanda predates the Palas.
  • Charvaka, Buddhism and Jainism are nastika (reject Vedic authority), not "atheist" in every case.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Write a note on the six schools of Indian philosophy.

How to frame it: Astika vs nastika → Three pairs → Main ideas → Significance

Model answer (78 words)

The six astika schools accepted Vedic authority. Samkhya (Kapila) taught dualism of purusha and prakriti; Yoga (Patanjali) offered the eight-limbed path to liberation. Nyaya (Gautama) developed logic and valid knowledge; Vaisheshika (Kanada) taught atomism. Purva Mimamsa (Jaimini) upheld Vedic ritual; Vedanta (Badarayana) sought Brahman, interpreted by Shankara as non-dual, by Ramanuja as qualified non-dual and by Madhva as dual. Alongside them, Buddhist, Jain and Charvaka schools rejected the Vedas. Together they reflect a rich tradition of rational debate.

2 marks · 15 words

What are the purusharthas?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Four aims of life: dharma (righteousness), artha (wealth), kama (pleasure), moksha (liberation).

5 marks · 50 words

Describe the importance of Nalanda as a centre of learning.

How to frame it: Founding → Patrons → Scholars → End

Model answer (44 words)

Founded in the 5th century under the Guptas, Nalanda was patronised by Harsha and the Palas. It taught Buddhism, logic, grammar and medicine to students from Asia, including Xuanzang and Yijing, and had a vast library. It was destroyed by Bakhtiyar Khalji around 1200.

Revision card

Remember
  • Values: rita → dharma; purusharthas; ashramas; rinas; sanskaras; mahayajnas; karma–moksha.
  • Darshanas: Samkhya (Kapila), Yoga (Patanjali), Nyaya (Gautama), Vaisheshika (Kanada), Mimamsa (Jaimini), Vedanta (Badarayana; Shankara, Ramanuja, Madhva).
  • Education: gurukula; Taxila; Nalanda (5th c.; Bakhtiyar Khalji); Vikramashila (Dharmapala; Atisha).
  • Political thought: saptanga, mandala, rajadharma; Gita nishkama karma.

  2. Who founded the Samkhya school?
    Show answer

    Kapila.

  3. Which school is known for atomism?
    Show answer

    Vaisheshika (Kanada).

  4. Who taught Advaita Vedanta?
    Show answer

    Shankaracharya.

  5. What are the three rinas?
    Show answer

    Debts to the gods, sages and ancestors.

  6. Who founded Vikramashila?
    Show answer

    The Pala king Dharmapala.

  7. Name the seven limbs of the state in the Arthashastra.
    Show answer

    Swami, amatya, janapada, durga, kosha, danda, mitra.

  8. What is nishkama karma?
    Show answer

    Action without attachment to its results (Bhagavad Gita).

Sources: S. Radhakrishnan, Indian Philosophy.; Upinder Singh, A History of Ancient and Early Medieval India.; Amartya Sen, The Argumentative Indian.; NCERT, Themes in Indian History Part I.

Part I · Unit I14Science and Technology in Ancient Indiac. 2600 BCE – 1200 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Mathematics, astronomy, medicine, metallurgy and technology in ancient India; scientists and their works

Mains: GS-I: Contribution of ancient India to science and technology

Scientist–work matching (Aryabhata, Brahmagupta, Bhaskara, Charaka, Sushruta, Nagarjuna) is a Prelims staple; a Mains question on ancient Indian science is common.

The big picture

Ancient India made lasting contributions to science: the decimal place-value system with zero, which spread through the Arab world to Europe; astronomy that calculated eclipses and the earth's rotation; Ayurveda with systematic medicine and surgery; and metallurgy that produced rust-free iron and high-carbon steel.

Much of this knowledge was practical — tied to ritual (geometry of altars), the calendar, agriculture, crafts and health — and was written in Sanskrit treatises (shastras) and passed through teacher lineages.

Exam focus

Use concrete evidence (Bakhshali manuscript, Gwalior zero, iron pillar) rather than general claims — examiners value precision over pride.

WhenWhat happened
c. 2600–1900 BCEHarappan standard weights, measures, town planning
c. 800–500 BCESulbasutras: geometry for altars
c. 4th c. BCEPanini's Ashtadhyayi
c. 1st–3rd c. CECharaka Samhita and Sushruta Samhita take shape
499 CEAryabhatiya
c. 505 CEVarahamihira's Panchasiddhantika
628 CEBrahmagupta's Brahmasphutasiddhanta
876 CEGwalior inscription: early dated zero in India
1150 CEBhaskara II's Siddhanta Shiromani (Lilavati)

Core notes

I14.1 Mathematics

  • Sulbasutras (Baudhayana, Apastamba, Katyayana): geometry for building fire altars; Baudhayana states the relation known as Pythagoras' theorem; approximations of √2.
  • Pingala (Chhandasutra): binary-like patterns in metres.
  • Decimal place-value system and zero: the Bakhshali manuscript (dated variously, 3rd–7th c.) uses a dot for zero; the Gwalior inscription (876 CE) has a written zero; Brahmagupta gave rules for zero and negative numbers.
  • Aryabhata (Aryabhatiya, 499): value of π ≈ 3.1416; sine tables; algebra.
  • Brahmagupta (Brahmasphutasiddhanta, 628, at Bhinmal): rules for zero and negatives; quadratic equations; its Arabic translation spread Indian numerals.
  • Bhaskara II (Siddhanta Shiromani, 1150; parts Lilavati and Bijaganita): arithmetic, algebra, early ideas of calculus.
  • Arab scholars called the numerals "Hindu numerals" (Al-Khwarizmi); Europe called them "Arabic numerals".

I14.2 Astronomy

  • Vedanga Jyotisha (Lagadha): earliest astronomical text, for the ritual calendar.
  • Aryabhata: earth rotates on its axis; eclipses caused by shadows of earth and moon (not Rahu); length of the year. India's first satellite (1975) was named after him.
  • Varahamihira: Panchasiddhantika (summary of five astronomical systems including Greek-influenced Romaka and Paulisha); Brihatsamhita (encyclopaedia: astronomy, weather, architecture, gems).
  • Brahmagupta and Bhaskara I refined calculations; Bhaskara II discussed gravitation-like attraction.

I14.3 Medicine (Ayurveda)

ScholarWorkContribution
CharakaCharaka SamhitaInternal medicine (kayachikitsa); diagnosis, diet, three doshas (vata, pitta, kapha); physician's oath; court of Kanishka (tradition)
SushrutaSushruta SamhitaSurgery (shalya): over 100 instruments, plastic surgery (rhinoplasty), cataract surgery; dissection for anatomy — "father of surgery"
VagbhataAshtanga HridayaSynthesis of Charaka and Sushruta
Jivaka—Physician of Bimbisara (trained at Taxila)
MadhavaMadhava NidanaDiagnosis (pathology), c. 7th–8th c.
PalakapyaHastyayurvedaVeterinary science (elephants)

Ayurveda's eight branches (ashtanga) included medicine, surgery, paediatrics, toxicology and rejuvenation.

I14.4 Metallurgy, chemistry and technology

  • Harappan: bronze casting by the lost-wax method; standard weights; Lothal dockyard.
  • Iron: from c. 1000 BCE; the Mehrauli iron pillar (Gupta, about 7 m) shows rust resistance due to high phosphorus and a protective film; iron beams at Konark and Puri (13th c.).
  • Wootz steel (south India): high-carbon crucible steel exported to make "Damascus" swords.
  • Zinc distillation at Zawar (Rajasthan) — among the earliest in the world (c. 9th c. CE onward for large-scale production; mining much earlier).
  • Copper: Sultanganj Buddha (Gupta, cast copper).
  • Chemistry: Rasashastra (mercury, metals for medicine) associated with a later Nagarjuna (Rasaratnakara); dyes, glass, cosmetics.
  • Linguistics: Panini's Ashtadhyayi — a formal grammar of Sanskrit with about 4,000 rules, admired by modern linguists; Patanjali's Mahabhashya.
  • Architecture: Vastu and Shilpa shastras; Mayamata, Manasara.

What historians say

Debiprasad Chattopadhyaya (Science and Society in Ancient India) argued that empirical science (Ayurveda) flourished but was later held back by caste prejudice against manual work and by religious orthodoxy. Kim Plofker (Mathematics in India) gives a careful account of mathematical texts and their dating; historians warn against inflated claims not supported by texts.

Prelims fact bank

  • Baudhayana's Sulbasutra states the Pythagorean relation.
  • The Bakhshali manuscript uses a dot for zero.
  • The Gwalior inscription (876 CE) has an early written zero.
  • Aryabhata wrote the Aryabhatiya (499) and gave π ≈ 3.1416.
  • Brahmagupta wrote the Brahmasphutasiddhanta (628) at Bhinmal.
  • Bhaskara II wrote Siddhanta Shiromani; Lilavati is its arithmetic part.
  • Varahamihira wrote Panchasiddhantika and Brihatsamhita.
  • Lagadha wrote the Vedanga Jyotisha.
  • Charaka Samhita deals with internal medicine; Sushruta Samhita with surgery.
  • Sushruta described rhinoplasty and cataract surgery.
  • Vagbhata wrote Ashtanga Hridaya.
  • Palakapya wrote Hastyayurveda.
  • Wootz steel was made in south India.
  • Zinc was distilled at Zawar (Rajasthan).
  • Panini wrote the Ashtadhyayi.
Exam traps
  • Brahmagupta (628, Bhinmal) gave rules for zero; Aryabhata (499) used place value but not a zero symbol.
  • Bhaskara I (7th c.) ≠ Bhaskara II (12th c., Lilavati).
  • Charaka = medicine; Sushruta = surgery.
  • Nagarjuna the Buddhist philosopher (Madhyamika) is often distinguished from the later alchemist Nagarjuna.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Discuss the contribution of ancient India to mathematics and astronomy.

How to frame it: Sulbasutras → Zero and place value → Aryabhata → Brahmagupta, Bhaskara → Spread

Model answer (74 words)

The Sulbasutras used advanced geometry, including the Pythagorean relation, for altars. The decimal place-value system with zero — seen in the Bakhshali manuscript and the Gwalior inscription — was India's greatest gift. Aryabhata (499) calculated π, explained eclipses and earth's rotation. Brahmagupta (628) gave rules for zero and negative numbers; Varahamihira compiled astronomical systems; Bhaskara II's Lilavati advanced arithmetic and algebra. Translated into Arabic, these ideas reached Europe as "Arabic numerals", shaping world science.

5 marks · 50 words

Describe the contribution of Charaka and Sushruta to medicine.

How to frame it: Charaka → Sushruta

Model answer (40 words)

Charaka's Samhita systematised internal medicine — diagnosis, diet, the three doshas — and set ethical standards for physicians. Sushruta's Samhita founded surgery, describing over 100 instruments, rhinoplasty, cataract operations and dissection for anatomy. Together they laid the foundations of Ayurveda.

2 marks · 15 words

What is wootz steel?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (11 words)

High-carbon crucible steel from south India, exported to make Damascus swords.

Revision card

Remember
  • Maths: Sulbasutras (Baudhayana); zero — Bakhshali, Gwalior 876; Aryabhata 499 (π); Brahmagupta 628; Bhaskara II 1150 (Lilavati).
  • Astronomy: Vedanga Jyotisha (Lagadha); Aryabhata (rotation, eclipses); Varahamihira.
  • Medicine: Charaka (medicine), Sushruta (surgery), Vagbhata, Madhava, Palakapya.
  • Technology: lost-wax bronze, Mehrauli pillar, wootz, Zawar zinc; Panini.

    Show answer

  5. Who is called the father of surgery?
    Show answer

    Show answer

    Show answer

Part IIMedieval India

11 units21 verified PYQs92 self-test questions

Part II · Unit I15The Rajput Age and the Struggle for Kannaujc. 750 – 1200 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Tripartite struggle; Palas, Pratiharas, Rashtrakutas; Rajput kingdoms; early medieval society

Mains: GS-I: Early medieval India — polity, the feudalism debate

The tripartite struggle and the "Indian feudalism" debate are the analytical questions; Palas and Rajput dynasties are Prelims material. Rajasthan dynasties are covered in detail in R05–R07.

The big picture

Between the 8th and 12th centuries north India was divided among regional kingdoms. Three great powers — the Palas of Bengal, the Gurjara-Pratiharas of western India and the Rashtrakutas of the Deccan — fought for about 150 years over Kannauj, the symbolic capital of north India (the tripartite struggle). After their decline, Rajput dynasties — Chauhans, Paramaras, Chandelas, Gahadavalas, Chaulukyas and others — dominated north India until the Turkish conquest.

Historians debate whether this was an age of feudal fragmentation and decline or of vigorous regional state formation, agrarian expansion and temple culture.

Exam focus

The "feudalism debate" (R.S. Sharma vs his critics) is the theme examiners use to test analytical depth.

WhenWhat happened
c. 750Gopala elected king in Bengal (Pala dynasty)
c. 750–900Tripartite struggle for Kannauj
c. 816Nagabhata II takes Kannauj
c. 810–850Devapala (Pala) at his peak
916Rashtrakuta Indra III sacks Kannauj
c. 950–1050Chandelas build Khajuraho temples
1010–1055Bhoja Paramara of Malwa
1018Mahmud of Ghazni sacks Kannauj
1090–1194Gahadavalas of Kannauj (Govindachandra, Jaichand)

Core notes

I15.1 The tripartite struggle

PowerKey rulersNotes
Palas (Bengal, Bihar)Gopala (elected by nobles to end matsyanyaya — anarchy), Dharmapala (founded Vikramashila; placed Chakrayudha on Kannauj's throne), Devapala, Mahipala IPatrons of Mahayana and Vajrayana Buddhism; Nalanda, Somapura; links with Tibet and South-East Asia (Shailendras)
Gurjara-PratiharasNagabhata I, Vatsaraja, Nagabhata II (took Kannauj), Mihira Bhoja, MahendrapalaResisted Arabs of Sindh (R05)
RashtrakutasDhruva, Govinda III, Amoghavarsha, Indra IIIRaided the north repeatedly but did not stay (I11)

Why Kannauj? Prestige as Harsha's capital; control of the fertile Ganga–Yamuna doab and trade routes.

Result: the Pratiharas held Kannauj longest, but the struggle exhausted all three, leaving room for Rajput dynasties and making north India vulnerable to the Turks.

I15.2 Rajput dynasties of north India

DynastyRegionNotable
Chauhans (Chahamanas)Ajmer, DelhiVigraharaja IV, Prithviraj III — R06
ParamarasMalwa (Dhar)Bhoja (1010–1055): scholar-king, Bhojshala at Dhar, Bhojpur lake and temple, works on architecture (Samarangana Sutradhara)
ChandelasBundelkhand (Khajuraho, Mahoba, Kalinjar)Yashovarman, Dhanga; Khajuraho temples (Kandariya Mahadeva, Lakshmana) — UNESCO
GahadavalasKannauj, VaranasiGovindachandra; Jaichand (killed by Ghori at Chandawar, 1194)
Chaulukyas (Solankis)Gujarat (Anhilwara)Bhima I (Somnath attacked by Mahmud, 1025); Siddharaja Jayasimha; Kumarapala (Jain patron, Hemachandra); Modhera Sun temple
KalachurisTripuri (Jabalpur)Gangeyadeva, Lakshmikarna
TomarasDelhi (Dhillika)Founded Delhi (Lal Kot) c. 8th–11th c.; displaced by the Chauhans

For Rajput origins, Guhils and other Rajasthan dynasties, see R05–R07.

I15.3 Society, economy and the feudalism debate

  • Land grants to Brahmins, temples and officials multiplied; grantees received fiscal and judicial rights.
  • How new dynasties rose: subordinate chiefs (samantas) owed gifts, attendance at court and troops; as they grew strong they took titles like maha-samanta and maha-mandaleshvara and broke away. Some Brahmin families took to arms: Mayurasharman founded the Kadamba kingdom (Karnataka) and Harichandra the Gurjara-Pratihara line (Rajasthan). Dantidurga, the Rashtrakuta chief who overthrew his Chalukya overlord, performed the hiranyagarbha ("golden womb") ritual to be "reborn" a Kshatriya. New kings took titles such as maharajadhiraja and tribhuvana-chakravartin.
  • Jatis (sub-castes) ranked by occupation and power framed their own rules, enforced by a jati panchayat of elders.
  • Regional identities grew: in 1318 Amir Khusrau listed a separate language in each region (Sindhi, Lahori, Kashmiri, Dvarsamudri, Telangani, Gujari, Ma'bari, Gauri, Awadhi and Hindawi) and noted that Sanskrit belonged to no region and was known only to Brahmins.
  • Proliferation of castes; Kayasthas (scribes) rose as a group; new Rajput clans.
  • Temple building became central to kingship (Khajuraho, Modhera, Lingaraja).
  • Small towns grew from large villages from the 8th century: each had a mandapika (the later mandi) where villagers sold produce, market streets (hatta, later haat) and streets of artisans. A samanta often built a fortified palace nearby, taxed traders and artisans, and sometimes granted the right to collect those taxes to a temple; a 10th-century inscription from Rajasthan lists such dues, some in cash and some in kind.
  • Temple towns grew around endowed temples, e.g. Bhillasvamin (Vidisha), Somnath, Kanchipuram, Madurai and Tirupati; temples also financed trade and lending.
  • Regional languages and scripts emerged; Apabhramsha literature.
Debate

R.S. Sharma (Indian Feudalism, 1965) argued that land grants created a hierarchy of landed intermediaries, while trade, towns and coinage declined and peasants were tied to land — a feudal social formation. Harbans Mukhia ("Was there feudalism in Indian history?") argued that Indian peasants were never serfs. B.D. Chattopadhyaya argued that the period saw regional state formation, agrarian expansion into new areas and new urban centres, not decline. Burton Stein offered the segmentary model for the south. Present the debate rather than a verdict.

What historians say

See the debate box. Romila Thapar uses "early medieval" for the period to avoid both "dark age" and "feudal" labels. John S. Deyell (Living Without Silver) questioned the idea of a severe coin shortage, showing that coins continued in debased forms.

Prelims fact bank

  • Gopala was elected king to end matsyanyaya in Bengal.
  • Dharmapala founded Vikramashila and placed Chakrayudha on the throne of Kannauj.
  • Nagabhata II took Kannauj c. 816.
  • Rashtrakuta Indra III sacked Kannauj in 916.
  • The tripartite struggle was among the Palas, Pratiharas and Rashtrakutas.
  • Bhoja Paramara of Malwa ruled 1010–1055 from Dhar.
  • The Chandelas built the Khajuraho temples.
  • Kandariya Mahadeva is the largest temple at Khajuraho.
  • Jaichand Gahadavala was killed by Ghori at Chandawar (1194).
  • Kumarapala Chaulukya was a patron of the Jain scholar Hemachandra.
  • The Tomaras founded Lal Kot (Delhi).
  • R.S. Sharma wrote Indian Feudalism (1965).
Exam traps
  • Mandapika = market centre (later mandi); hatta = market street (later haat).
  • The tripartite struggle was for Kannauj and the Ganga valley, not the Kaveri valley (one guidebook mixes this up).
  • Nalanda was founded under the Guptas (Kumaragupta I); the Palas patronised it and founded Vikramashila.
  • Mihira Bhoja (Pratihara) ≠ Bhoja Paramara (Malwa).
  • Chaulukyas (Solankis, Gujarat) ≠ Chalukyas (Badami/Kalyani).
  • Chandawar (1194): Ghori vs Jaichand; Tarain (1191–92): Ghori vs Prithviraj.
  • Harbans Mukhia questioned Indian feudalism; R.S. Sharma proposed it.

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

What was the tripartite struggle? What were its consequences?

How to frame it: Parties → Cause → Result

Model answer (44 words)

From c. 750 to 900 the Palas, Gurjara-Pratiharas and Rashtrakutas fought for Kannauj, the prestige capital controlling the Ganga doab. The Pratiharas held it longest, but the struggle exhausted all three, fragmented north India among Rajput dynasties and left it vulnerable to Turkish invasions.

10 marks · 100 words

Discuss the debate on "Indian feudalism".

How to frame it: Sharma's thesis → Evidence → Critics → Assessment

Model answer (88 words)

R.S. Sharma argued that from the Gupta period, land grants to Brahmins, temples and officials created landed intermediaries with fiscal and judicial rights; trade, towns and coinage declined; peasants were burdened with forced labour and tied to land — a feudal formation. Critics disagree: Harbans Mukhia says Indian peasants were never serfs; B.D. Chattopadhyaya sees regional state formation and agrarian expansion; Deyell shows coins continued. Burton Stein proposed a segmentary model for the south. The debate shows the period was transformative, though the European "feudal" label fits poorly.

2 marks · 15 words

What was matsyanyaya?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (14 words)

"Law of the fishes": anarchy in Bengal before nobles elected Gopala king (c. 750).

Revision card

Remember
  • Tripartite: Palas (Gopala, Dharmapala, Devapala), Pratiharas (Nagabhata II, Mihira Bhoja), Rashtrakutas (Dhruva, Govinda III, Indra III).
  • Rajputs: Paramaras (Bhoja), Chandelas (Khajuraho), Gahadavalas (Jaichand, Chandawar 1194), Chaulukyas (Kumarapala), Tomaras (Delhi).
  • Feudalism: Sharma vs Mukhia, Chattopadhyaya, Stein, Deyell.

Part II · Unit I16Arab and Turkish Invasions712 – 1206 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Arab conquest of Sindh; Mahmud of Ghazni; Muhammad Ghori; battles of Tarain

Mains: GS-I: Turkish invasions — causes of Rajput defeat

"Why were the Rajputs defeated by the Turks?" is a classic Mains question; dates and battles (712, 1025, 1191, 1192, 1194) are Prelims staples.

The big picture

Islam reached India in three waves: Arab traders on the western coast (from the 7th century), the Arab conquest of Sindh (712), and the Turkish invasions from Afghanistan. Mahmud of Ghazni raided India seventeen times (1000–1027) for plunder, notably Somnath; Muhammad Ghori came to conquer, defeating Prithviraj Chauhan at Tarain (1192) and laying the foundation of the Delhi Sultanate (1206).

Exam focus

Explain the Turkish success through military organisation and political conditions on both sides — avoid simplistic "Rajput disunity" alone.

WhenWhat happened
712Muhammad bin Qasim conquers Sindh; Dahir killed
c. 725–740Arab raids repulsed by Nagabhata I and the Chalukyas
977Sabuktigin becomes ruler of Ghazni (later defeats the Hindushahi Jayapala, c. 986–991)
1001Mahmud defeats Jayapala (Hindushahi) at Peshawar
1025Mahmud sacks Somnath
1030Death of Mahmud
1175Ghori takes Multan
1178Ghori defeated by the Chaulukyas at Kasahrada (Mount Abu)
1191First battle of Tarain: Prithviraj wins
1192Second battle of Tarain: Ghori wins
1194Chandawar: Jaichand defeated
c. 1200–1204Bakhtiyar Khalji conquers Bihar and Bengal; Nalanda destroyed
1206Death of Ghori; Qutbuddin Aibak rules Delhi

Core notes

I16.1 The Arab conquest of Sindh

  • Cause: pirates near Debal seized Arab ships; Hajjaj, governor of Iraq, sent Muhammad bin Qasim.
  • 712: Raja Dahir killed; Sindh and Multan conquered.
  • Source: Chachnama (Persian translation, 13th c.).
  • Non-Muslims treated as dhimmis on payment of jizya.
  • Further Arab expansion was checked by the Pratiharas (Nagabhata I) and Chalukyas (Pulakeshi of Navsari).
  • Effects: limited politically; important culturally — Indian astronomy, mathematics (numerals), medicine and the Panchatantra reached Baghdad.

I16.2 Mahmud of Ghazni

  • Son of Sabuktigin; 17 raids between 1000 and 1027.
  • Defeated the Hindushahis (Jayapala, Anandapala — battle of Waihind, 1008); raided Nagarkot, Thanesar, Mathura, Kannauj (1018), Kalinjar, and Somnath (1025, against the Chaulukya Bhima I).
  • Aims: plunder of temple wealth to finance Central Asian wars; prestige as a champion of Islam; annexed only Punjab.
  • His court included Al-Biruni (Kitab-ul-Hind) and Firdausi (Shahnama); historian Utbi (Tarikh-i-Yamini).

I16.3 Muhammad Ghori

  • Shihabuddin (Muizzuddin) of the Ghurid dynasty; aimed at permanent conquest.
  • Took Multan (1175); defeated by the Chaulukyas near Mount Abu (Kasahrada, 1178 — under the boy-king Mularaja II and his mother, queen Naikidevi); took Punjab from the last Ghaznavids (Lahore, 1186).
  • Tarain I (1191): Prithviraj III won; Ghori wounded.
  • Tarain II (1192): Ghori won with mounted archers, feigned retreat and reserves; Prithviraj killed. Ajmer and Delhi occupied (R06).
  • Chandawar (1194): Jaichand Gahadavala defeated.
  • Bakhtiyar Khalji, a commander, overran Bihar (Nalanda, Vikramashila) and Bengal (Nadia, Lakshmana Sena), c. 1200–1204.
  • Ghori was killed in 1206 (by Khokhars, per most accounts); his slave Qutbuddin Aibak became ruler in India.

I16.4 Why the Turks succeeded

  1. Military: fast cavalry with mounted archers and Central Asian horses; unified command; tactics of feigned retreat and reserves; Indian armies relied on elephants and infantry and fought defensively.
  2. Political: rivalries among Rajput states (Chauhans, Gahadavalas, Chaulukyas, Chandelas); no common front; feudal armies loyal to local chiefs rather than the king.
  3. Organisation: the Turkish iqta and slave system produced loyal commanders; booty and religious zeal motivated troops.
  4. Social: caste restrictions limited recruitment; the warrior role was confined to certain groups.
  5. Strategic neglect of the north-west frontier after the Hindushahis fell.
Debate

Older writers blamed Indian "moral decline" or "Hindu fatalism"; modern historians (Satish Chandra, Simon Digby) emphasise military technology — the horse and the mounted archer — and political fragmentation.

What historians say

Mohammad Habib argued that the Ghaznavid raids were motivated by greed, not religion, and that the Ghurid conquest was accompanied by an "urban revolution". Simon Digby (War-horse and Elephant) stressed the cavalry advantage. Richard Eaton analysed temple desecration as political acts tied to defeated kings, not general religious policy.

Prelims fact bank

  • At Kasahrada (1178) the Chaulukya forces of Mularaja II, led by his mother Naikidevi, defeated Muhammad Ghori.
  • Muhammad bin Qasim conquered Sindh in 712 and killed Raja Dahir.
  • The Chachnama is the source for the Arab conquest of Sindh.
  • Mahmud of Ghazni raided India 17 times (1000–1027).
  • Mahmud sacked Somnath in 1025.
  • Al-Biruni wrote Kitab-ul-Hind; Firdausi wrote Shahnama at Mahmud's court.
  • Ghori was defeated by the Chaulukyas at Kasahrada near Mount Abu (1178).
  • Prithviraj won the first battle of Tarain (1191) and lost the second (1192).
  • Ghori defeated Jaichand at Chandawar (1194).
  • Bakhtiyar Khalji conquered Bihar and Bengal and destroyed Nalanda.
  • Ghori died in 1206; Qutbuddin Aibak became ruler in India.
Exam traps
  • Arabs conquered Sindh (712); Turks (Ghaznavids, Ghurids) conquered north India.
  • Mahmud annexed only Punjab; Ghori founded lasting rule.
  • Tarain I (1191) — Prithviraj won; Tarain II (1192) — Ghori won.
  • Ghori was defeated in Gujarat (1178) before Tarain.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Why were the Rajputs defeated by the Turks?

How to frame it: Military → Political → Organisation → Social → Conclusion

Model answer (91 words)

The Turks fielded fast cavalry with mounted archers and better horses, under unified command, using feigned retreat and reserves; Rajput armies depended on elephants and massed infantry and fought defensively. Rajput states — Chauhans, Gahadavalas, Chaulukyas — were rivals and formed no common front; feudal levies owed loyalty to local chiefs. The Turkish slave and iqta systems produced loyal commanders, motivated by booty and zeal. Caste limited Indian recruitment, and the north-west frontier was neglected after the Hindushahis fell. Military technology and political fragmentation, rather than moral decline, explain the defeat.

5 marks · 50 words

Distinguish between the invasions of Mahmud of Ghazni and Muhammad Ghori.

How to frame it: Aims → Results

Model answer (42 words)

Mahmud raided seventeen times for plunder and prestige, sacking temples such as Somnath, but annexed only Punjab. Ghori sought permanent conquest; after Tarain (1192) and Chandawar (1194) he occupied Delhi, Ajmer and the Ganga valley, laying the foundation of the Delhi Sultanate.

2 marks · 15 words

What is the Chachnama?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Persian chronicle of the Arab conquest of Sindh by Muhammad bin Qasim (712).

Revision card

Remember
  • Sindh 712: bin Qasim, Dahir, Chachnama.
  • Mahmud: 17 raids; Somnath 1025; Al-Biruni, Firdausi.
  • Ghori: Multan 1175; Kasahrada 1178 defeat; Tarain 1191/1192; Chandawar 1194; d. 1206.
  • Bakhtiyar Khalji: Bihar, Bengal, Nalanda.
  • Why Turks won: cavalry, unity, iqta/slaves; Rajput rivalry, elephants, caste.
