Part II · Unit I17Delhi Sultanate: Political History1206 – 1526

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Five dynasties of the Sultanate; Iltutmish, Balban, Alauddin Khalji, Muhammad bin Tughluq, Firoz Shah; Timur; Lodis

Mains: GS-I: Delhi Sultanate — consolidation, expansion, decline

Alauddin's reforms and Muhammad bin Tughluq's experiments are the most-asked topics. Sultanate relations with Rajasthan (Ranthambore, Chittor, Jalore) are in R06–R07.

The big picture

For 320 years five dynasties ruled from Delhi: the Mamluks ("Slave" dynasty, 1206–1290), Khaljis (1290–1320), Tughluqs (1320–1414), Sayyids (1414–1451) and Lodis (1451–1526). The Sultanate grew from a Turkish military occupation into an empire covering most of India under Muhammad bin Tughluq, then broke into regional kingdoms after the 1330s and Timur's sack of Delhi (1398). Babur's victory at Panipat (1526) ended it.

Three themes run through: the Sultan versus the nobility, defence against the Mongols, and the relationship between the Sultan and the ulema (religious scholars).

Exam focus

Learn each ruler through one theme: Iltutmish — consolidation; Balban — kingship; Alauddin — centralisation; Muhammad bin Tughluq — experiments; Firoz — welfare and decline.

WhenWhat happened
1206Qutbuddin Aibak, first Sultan (capital Lahore)
1210Death of Aibak (polo accident)
1211–1236Iltutmish; Delhi capital
1229Investiture from the Abbasid Caliph
1236–1240Razia Sultan
1266–1287Balban
1290Jalaluddin Khalji
1296–1316Alauddin Khalji
1301Ranthambore taken
1303Chittor taken; Mongol siege of Delhi
1306–1311Malik Kafur's campaigns to the Deccan and the south
1320Ghiyasuddin Tughluq
1325–1351Muhammad bin Tughluq
1327Capital shifted to Daulatabad
1336Vijayanagara founded
1347Bahmani kingdom founded
1351–1388Firoz Shah Tughluq
1398Timur sacks Delhi
1414Khizr Khan founds the Sayyid dynasty
1451Bahlul Lodi
1489–1517Sikandar Lodi; Agra founded (1504)
1526First battle of Panipat: Ibrahim Lodi killed by Babur

Core notes

I17.1 The Mamluk (Slave) dynasty, 1206–1290

Delhi before the Sultans: Delhi first became a royal capital under the Tomaras in the early 12th century; the Chauhans of Ajmer took it in the mid-12th century. Under both it was a trading town, with wealthy Jain merchants building temples, and its coins, the dehliwal, circulated widely. It became the seat of an empire only with the Sultans.

Actually three lines (Qutbi, Shamsi, Balbani), each founded by a former slave — hence "Mamluk" (owned).

RulerKey points
Qutbuddin Aibak (1206–10)Ghori's slave; ruled from Lahore; began Qutb Minar and Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque, Adhai Din ka Jhonpra (Ajmer); called Lakh Baksh for generosity
Iltutmish (1211–36)The real consolidator: made Delhi the capital; defeated rivals Yildiz and Qabacha; avoided Chingiz Khan by refusing refuge to Jalaluddin Mangbarni (1221); caliphal investiture (1229); organised the iqta system; created the Turkan-i-Chihalgani (the Forty); introduced silver tanka and copper jital; completed the Qutb Minar
Razia (1236–40)Only woman ruler of Delhi; nominated by Iltutmish; discarded purdah; promoted the Abyssinian Yaqut; overthrown by the Forty
Balban (1266–87)Theory of kingship (below); broke the Forty; sijda and paibos (prostration, kissing feet); Persian festival of Nauroz; diwan-i-arz (army department); spy system (barids); "blood and iron" against Mewatis; Mongol defence

Balban's theory of kingship: the king is Niyabat-i-Khudai (God's deputy) and Zil-i-Ilahi (shadow of God); kingship requires awe and distance; only nobles of high birth to hold office.

Razia in context: the chronicler Minhaj-us-Siraj accepted her ability but felt rule by a queen went against the social order of his day. Other women rulers of the period: Rudramadevi of the Kakatiyas (1262–89), who was shown as a man in some of her inscriptions, and Didda of Kashmir (980–1003).

I17.2 The Khaljis, 1290–1320

The "Khalji revolution": the Khaljis, though Turks, were treated as non-Turkish Afghans by the old nobility. Their rise opened office to non-Turks and Indian Muslims — power based on merit and loyalty, not race.

  • Jalaluddin (1290–96): mild ruler; killed by his nephew Alauddin.
  • Alauddin (1296–1316):
    • Conquests: Gujarat (1299), Ranthambore (1301, Hammir Deva), Chittor (1303, Ratan Singh), Malwa, Siwana, Jalore (1311, Kanhadadeva).
    • Deccan and south through Malik Kafur (a slave bought for 1,000 dinars — "hazar-dinari"): Devagiri (Yadavas), Warangal (Kakatiyas), Dwarasamudra (Hoysalas), Madurai (Pandyas) — tribute, not annexation.
    • Mongols: repulsed repeated invasions (1299, 1303, 1305–06); built Siri fort.
    • Reforms (market control, revenue, army) — see I18.
    • Four ordinances against noble conspiracy: confiscation of grants, spy network, ban on liquor, ban on nobles' social gatherings and marriages without permission.
    • Said "I do not know what is lawful or unlawful; I issue orders for the good of the state" (Barani) — state interest above the ulema.
    • Buildings: Alai Darwaza, Hauz-i-Khas, Siri.
  • After Alauddin: Malik Kafur, Mubarak Shah, Khusrau Khan (1320).

I17.3 The Tughluqs, 1320–1414

Ghiyasuddin Tughluq (1320–25): founded Tughluqabad; annexed Warangal; died when a pavilion collapsed.

Muhammad bin Tughluq (1325–51) — learned, ambitious, impatient. His experiments:

ExperimentAimOutcome
Transfer of capital to Daulatabad (Devagiri), 1327Central location to control the south; safer from MongolsElite and population moved with hardship; Delhi later restored; but it spread Muslim culture and Persian to the Deccan
Token currency (bronze/copper tokens for silver), c. 1329–30Silver shortage; following Chinese and Persian examplesEasily forged; withdrawn and redeemed in silver, at great cost
Taxation in the doabMore revenueCoincided with famine; peasant flight and revolt
Khurasan expedition (planned) and Qarachil (Kumaon) expeditionExpansionKhurasan army disbanded; Qarachil army destroyed in the hills
Agriculture department (Diwan-i-Kohi)Extend cultivation with state loans (sondhar)Failed
Mongol threatAlauddin KhaljiMuhammad bin Tughluq
ResponseDefensive: a large standing army, the new garrison town of SiriOffensive: defeated a Mongol army and planned campaigns beyond the frontier
Housing the armySiri built for the soldiersResidents of the old city (Dehli-i-Kuhna) moved out to Daulatabad and soldiers garrisoned there
Paying the armyCash salaries kept low by price controlExtra taxes and token currency

NCERT calls consolidating the land around the garrison towns (clearing forests in the Ganga–Yamuna doab) the internal frontier, and campaigns into unconquered regions the external frontier; the external push began under Alauddin and peaked under Muhammad bin Tughluq. He also appointed men of humble birth (Aziz Khummar, a wine-distiller; Firuz Hajjam, a barber; Manka Tabbakh, a cook; the gardeners Ladha and Pira), which Barani condemned.

Moroccan traveller Ibn Battuta (in India from 1333) described Delhi as the largest city in India and Daulatabad as its rival in size, and noted two postal relays: the horse-post (uluq), with royal horses every four miles, and the foot-post (dawa), with three stations a mile, which brought news from Sind in five days against fifty for ordinary travel. He served Muhammad bin Tughluq as qazi of Delhi and was sent as envoy to China (Rihla). Rebellions led to the loss of the south: Vijayanagara (1336), Madurai, Bahmani (1347).

Firoz Shah Tughluq (1351–88): conciliated nobles and ulema; made iqtas hereditary; abolished many taxes, levied only four sharia taxes (kharaj, khams, jizya, zakat) — jizya now also on Brahmins; canals (Yamuna); new towns (Firozabad, Hisar, Jaunpur — named after Muhammad bin Tughluq, whose earlier name was Jauna Khan); Diwan-i-Khairat (charity), Diwan-i-Bandagan (slaves — 1,80,000); employment bureau; hospitals (Dar-ul-Shifa); moved Ashokan pillars from Topra and Meerut to Delhi; wrote Futuhat-i-Firozshahi. His policies weakened central authority.

Timur's invasion (1398): sacked Delhi under Nasiruddin Mahmud; the Sultanate collapsed into regional kingdoms (Jaunpur, Malwa, Gujarat, Bengal, Mewar).

I17.4 Sayyids and Lodis, 1414–1526

  • Sayyids (1414–51): Khizr Khan was Timur's governor; ruled a small area.
  • Lodis — the first Afghan dynasty:
    • Bahlul Lodi (1451–89): conquered Jaunpur; treated Afghan nobles as equals.
    • Sikandar Lodi (1489–1517): founded Agra (1504); gaz-i-Sikandari (measure of land); wrote Persian poetry as "Gulrukhi"; orthodox.
    • Ibrahim Lodi (1517–26): alienated Afghan nobles; Daulat Khan Lodi (Punjab) and Rana Sanga invited or encouraged Babur; killed at Panipat (1526).

I17.5 Why the Sultanate declined

  1. No law of succession; frequent palace murders.
  2. Conflict between Sultans and nobles; Firoz's hereditary iqtas weakened control.
  3. Muhammad bin Tughluq's failed experiments and the size of the empire.
  4. Revolts in the provinces; rise of Vijayanagara and the Bahmanis.
  5. Timur's invasion (1398).
  6. Dependence on the personality of the ruler; narrow social base.
  7. Afghan tribal notions of equality under the Lodis; Babur's superior artillery and tactics.

What historians say

Ziauddin Barani (Tarikh-i-Firozshahi, Fatawa-i-Jahandari) is the main source for Balban to Firoz, with a strong aristocratic bias. Minhaj-us-Siraj (Tabaqat-i-Nasiri) covers the early Sultanate. Amir Khusrau's works (Khazain-ul-Futuh) describe Alauddin's campaigns. Ishwari Prasad and Agha Mahdi Husain reassessed Muhammad bin Tughluq; Mohammad Habib and K.A. Nizami stressed social change. Peter Jackson (The Delhi Sultanate) is the standard modern political history.

Debate

Was Muhammad bin Tughluq "a mixture of opposites" (Elphinstone), mad, or a visionary ahead of his time? Modern historians see sound ideas undone by poor execution, impatience and circumstances (famine, revolts).

Prelims fact bank

  • Malik Kafur was called "hazar-dinari" because he was bought for 1,000 dinars.
  • Jaunpur, founded by Firoz Shah Tughluq, was named after Jauna Khan (Muhammad bin Tughluq).
  • Tradition says Nizamuddin Auliya, threatened by Ghiyasuddin Tughluq, remarked "Hunuz Dilli dur ast" (Delhi is still far).
  • Qutbuddin Aibak was the first Sultan of Delhi (1206) and ruled from Lahore.
  • Iltutmish made Delhi the capital and received caliphal investiture in 1229.
  • Iltutmish introduced the silver tanka and copper jital.
  • Iltutmish formed the Turkan-i-Chihalgani (the Forty).
  • Razia was the only woman ruler of the Delhi Sultanate.
  • Balban introduced sijda and paibos.
  • Balban called the king Zil-i-Ilahi, the shadow of God.
  • Alauddin Khalji took Ranthambore in 1301 and Chittor in 1303.
  • Malik Kafur led Alauddin's campaigns in the Deccan and south.
  • Muhammad bin Tughluq moved the capital to Daulatabad in 1327.
  • Muhammad bin Tughluq introduced token currency.
  • Ibn Battuta served as qazi of Delhi under Muhammad bin Tughluq.
  • Ibn Battuta described the horse-post (uluq) and the foot-post (dawa).
  • Firoz Shah Tughluq made iqtas hereditary.
  • Firoz Shah moved Ashokan pillars from Topra and Meerut to Delhi.
  • Timur sacked Delhi in 1398.
  • Sikandar Lodi founded Agra in 1504.
  • Delhi first became a capital under the Tomaras, then passed to the Chauhans of Ajmer.
  • The dehliwal was the coin of pre-Sultanate Delhi.
  • Rudramadevi ruled the Kakatiya kingdom (1262–89).

  • The Lodis were the first Afghan dynasty of Delhi.
  • Ibrahim Lodi was killed at the first battle of Panipat (1526).
Exam traps
  • Aibak's capital was Lahore; Iltutmish made Delhi the capital.
  • The Forty were created by Iltutmish and broken by Balban.
  • Alauddin's southern campaigns took tribute; Muhammad bin Tughluq tried annexation.
  • Token currency was bronze/copper, not paper.
  • Jizya on Brahmins: Firoz Shah, not Alauddin.
  • Sayyids were Turkish/Arab; Lodis were Afghans.
  • Siri was Alauddin's garrison town; Jahanpanah was Muhammad bin Tughluq's city.
  • The first Mongol raid (1221, Chingiz Khan at the Indus) came under Iltutmish, not the Khaljis.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Evaluate the experiments of Muhammad bin Tughluq.

How to frame it: Capital → Currency → Doab tax → Expeditions → Agriculture → Assessment

Model answer (95 words)

Muhammad bin Tughluq moved the capital to Daulatabad (1327) to control the south, causing great hardship before Delhi was restored, though it spread Persian culture to the Deccan. His bronze token currency, meant to meet a silver shortage, was forged widely and withdrawn at heavy cost. Raised taxes in the doab coincided with famine and provoked revolt. The Khurasan plan was abandoned and the Qarachil army perished; the agriculture department failed. The ideas were often sound, but impatience, poor execution and bad timing caused revolts that cost him the south — Vijayanagara and the Bahmanis.

5 marks · 50 words

What was Balban's theory of kingship?

How to frame it: Divine → Awe → Practice

Model answer (44 words)

Balban held the king to be God's deputy (Niyabat-i-Khudai) and shadow (Zil-i-Ilahi). Kingship required awe and distance: he introduced sijda and paibos, Nauroz and a grave court, gave office only to high-born nobles, broke the Forty and used spies — restoring the Sultan's prestige.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the "Khalji revolution"?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (14 words)

Khalji rise (1290) ended Turkish aristocratic monopoly, opening office to non-Turks and Indian Muslims.

Revision card

  • Dynasties: Mamluk 1206, Khalji 1290, Tughluq 1320, Sayyid 1414, Lodi 1451–1526.
  • Iltutmish: Delhi capital, investiture 1229, tanka/jital, iqta, Forty.
  • Balban: Zil-i-Ilahi, sijda, paibos, diwan-i-arz.
  • Alauddin: Ranthambore 1301, Chittor 1303, Jalore 1311; Malik Kafur; Mongols; markets.
  • MbT: Daulatabad 1327, token currency, doab, Qarachil; Ibn Battuta.
  • Firoz: hereditary iqtas, four taxes, canals, pillars.
  • Timur 1398; Agra 1504; Panipat 1526.

Part II · Unit I18Delhi Sultanate: Administration, Economy and Society1206 – 1526

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Central and provincial administration; iqta; Alauddin's market and revenue reforms; Sultanate architecture and literature

Mains: GS-I: Sultanate administration, economy, society and culture

Alauddin's market control and the iqta system are frequent Mains questions; ministries and architecture are Prelims material.

The big picture

The Sultanate was a centralised military state. The Sultan was the head of the army, justice and administration; in theory he ruled under the sharia and recognised the Caliph. Ministers ran departments; the empire was divided into iqtas held by military officers (muqtis) who collected revenue and paid troops. The economy saw expanding cities, a money economy, new crafts (paper, the spinning wheel) and a new culture that blended Persian and Indian traditions — the Indo-Islamic style.

Exam focus

Know the four core ministries, how the iqta changed from Iltutmish to Firoz, and the logic of Alauddin's reforms (army → revenue → prices).

WhenWhat happened
c. 1220sIltutmish organises iqtas
c. 1300–1316Alauddin's revenue and market reforms
c. 1350s–80sFiroz Shah's hereditary iqtas and canals
c. 1357Barani writes Tarikh-i-Firozshahi

Core notes

I18.1 Central administration

Department / officeHeadFunction
Diwan-i-WizaratWazirFinance and revenue; chief minister
Diwan-i-ArzAriz-i-MumalikArmy: recruitment, muster, branding (est. by Balban)
Diwan-i-InshaDabir-i-KhasRoyal correspondence
Diwan-i-RisalatSadr-us-SudurReligious affairs, grants to scholars
JusticeQazi-ul-QuzatChief judge
Barid-i-Mumalik—Intelligence and posts
Diwan-i-Riyasat—Market control (Alauddin)
Diwan-i-Mustakhraj—Recovery of arrears (Alauddin)
Diwan-i-Kohi—Agriculture (Muhammad bin Tughluq)
Diwan-i-Khairat, Diwan-i-Bandagan—Charity; slaves (Firoz)

The "circle of justice" (Fakhr-i Mudabbir, 13th century): a king needs soldiers; soldiers need salaries; salaries come from peasants' revenue; peasants pay only when prosperous; and they prosper only when the king rules justly.

Provinces and below: iqta / wilayat under a muqti or wali; shiqs under shiqdars; parganas (amil); villages under headmen (muqaddam, chaudhari) and accountants (patwari).

I18.2 The iqta system

  • An iqta was a revenue assignment (not land ownership) given to an officer in return for military service and administration.
  • Iltutmish organised it as a means to centralise power, pay troops and integrate distant regions.
  • Balban and Alauddin tightened audit; Alauddin paid many soldiers in cash and transferred muqtis.
  • Muhammad bin Tughluq separated revenue collection from military command in some areas.
  • Firoz Shah made iqtas hereditary — weakening central control.
  • The iqta was the ancestor of the Mughal jagir.

I18.3 Alauddin Khalji's reforms

Why? To maintain a large standing army against the Mongols and for conquest, without bankrupting the treasury; and to curb the nobles and rural chiefs.

Revenue reforms

  • Land measured (masahat, by biswa); revenue fixed at half the produce in the doab.
  • Privileges of the khuts, muqaddams and chaudharis (rural intermediaries) curbed; they paid the same taxes.
  • House tax (ghari) and grazing tax (charai).
  • Diwan-i-Mustakhraj for arrears.

Market reforms (Barani)

  • Fixed prices for grain, cloth, horses, cattle and slaves; separate markets (mandi for grain, Sarai-i-Adl for cloth and luxuries).
  • Officials: Shahna-i-Mandi (market superintendent), Diwan-i-Riyasat, spies (munhiyans, barids).
  • State granaries; revenue in kind in the doab; merchants registered; harsh punishment for short-weighting.
  • Rationing during scarcity.

Army

  • Standing army paid in cash; dagh (branding of horses) and chehra (descriptive roll of soldiers).
Debate

Did market control apply to the whole empire or only Delhi? Most historians (following Barani) see it as confined to Delhi and its vicinity. Barani praises its efficiency but his account is idealised; the system ended with Alauddin's death. Irfan Habib sees the revenue reforms as a turning point in extracting surplus from the countryside.

I18.4 Economy and society

  • Taxes: kharaj (land tax), jizya, khams (one-fifth of war booty to the state; Alauddin and MbT took four-fifths), zakat.
  • Agriculture: wheat, rice, sugarcane, cotton; Firoz's canals.
  • Crafts and technology: the spinning wheel (charkha) and carder's bow, paper, the Persian wheel (araghatta improvements), arch and dome, gunpowder (late).
  • Towns and trade: growth of Delhi, Lahore, Multan, Cambay; Multani and Gujarati merchants; horses imported.
  • Society: Turkish, Persian, Afghan and Indian Muslim nobility; ulema; Hindu chiefs (rais, ranas) and intermediaries; peasants and artisans; slavery widespread.
  • Women: purdah among the elite; Razia an exception.

I18.5 Architecture and literature

FeatureExamples
Early buildings using temple material; corbelled archesQuwwat-ul-Islam mosque, Qutb Minar (Aibak, Iltutmish), Adhai Din ka Jhonpra (Ajmer)
Early tombsSultan Ghari (Iltutmish's son Nasiruddin Mahmud; regarded as the first Islamic tomb in India); Iltutmish's own tomb in the Qutb complex
Balban's tomb — first true archMehrauli
Alai Darwaza (1311) — first true dome; red sandstone, white marbleQutb complex
Tughluq style: sloping (battered) walls, austereTughluqabad, Ghiyasuddin's tomb, Firoz Shah Kotla
Mosques: the Quwwat-ul-Islam was enlarged by Iltutmish and again by Alauddin; Begumpuri mosque, main mosque of Jahanpanah (Muhammad bin Tughluq); Moth ki Masjid (built by Sikandar Lodi's minister); Jamali Kamali mosque (late 1520s)Delhi
Firoz Shah's tomb (1388), beside his madrasaHauz Khas, Delhi
Lodi style: octagonal tombs, double domeLodi Garden; Sikandar Lodi's tomb

Literature: Amir Khusrau ("Tuti-i-Hind", parrot of India) — Persian poetry, Hindavi verse, credited with qawwali and (by tradition) the sitar; works Khazain-ul-Futuh, Nuh Sipihr, Qiran-us-Sadain. Historians Minhaj, Barani, Isami (Futuh-us-Salatin), Afif. Sanskrit works translated into Persian under Firoz. Growth of regional languages and early Urdu/Hindavi.

What historians say

Irfan Habib analysed agrarian extraction and technology diffusion (spinning wheel, paper). K.A. Nizami discussed the relationship of state and religion, arguing the Sultanate was not a strict theocracy because state law (zawabit) operated alongside sharia. Mohammad Habib spoke of an "urban revolution" and "rural revolution" after the Turkish conquest.

Debate

Was the Sultanate a theocracy? Sultans recognised the Caliph and sharia, but Balban, Alauddin and Muhammad bin Tughluq framed laws (zawabit) on state interest. Most historians describe a military monarchy with Islamic legitimacy, not a theocracy.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Diwan-i-Arz (army department) was established by Balban.
  • The iqta was a revenue assignment, not ownership of land.
  • Firoz Shah made iqtas hereditary.
  • Alauddin fixed land revenue at half the produce in the doab.
  • Alauddin introduced dagh (branding of horses) and chehra (descriptive roll).
  • The Shahna-i-Mandi was Alauddin's market superintendent.
  • The Sarai-i-Adl was the market for cloth and luxuries under Alauddin.
  • The Diwan-i-Mustakhraj recovered revenue arrears.
  • The Diwan-i-Kohi was Muhammad bin Tughluq's agriculture department.
  • The Alai Darwaza (1311) has the first true dome in India.
  • Balban's tomb has the first true arch in India.
  • Amir Khusrau was called Tuti-i-Hind.
  • Sultan Ghari is regarded as the first Islamic tomb in India.
  • The Begumpuri mosque was the main mosque of Jahanpanah, Muhammad bin Tughluq's city.
  • Fakhr-i Mudabbir described the "circle of justice" linking king, army, revenue and peasants.
Exam traps
  • Iqta = revenue assignment; jagir (Mughal) is its successor.
  • First true arch: Balban's tomb; first true dome: Alai Darwaza.
  • Dagh and chehra: Alauddin (Akbar revived them).
  • Diwan-i-Arz: Balban; Diwan-i-Kohi: Muhammad bin Tughluq; Diwan-i-Riyasat: Alauddin.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Explain Alauddin Khalji's market control policy.

How to frame it: Purpose → Measures → Officials → Scope → Assessment

Model answer (92 words)

Alauddin needed a large cash-paid army against the Mongols without ruining the treasury, so he lowered the cost of living by fixing prices. Separate markets were set up for grain, for cloth and luxuries (Sarai-i-Adl), and for horses, cattle and slaves. The Shahna-i-Mandi and Diwan-i-Riyasat supervised them; merchants were registered, spies reported, state granaries held stocks and revenue in the doab was collected in kind. Short-weighting was harshly punished, and grain was rationed in scarcity. The system worked in and around Delhi but lapsed after his death; Barani's praise is somewhat idealised.

5 marks · 50 words

Describe the iqta system of the Delhi Sultanate.

How to frame it: Definition → Evolution

Model answer (44 words)

An iqta was a revenue assignment to a muqti in return for military service and administration. Iltutmish organised it to integrate the empire; Balban and Alauddin audited and transferred muqtis; Muhammad bin Tughluq separated revenue from command; Firoz made iqtas hereditary, weakening central control.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Alai Darwaza?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (14 words)

Gateway at the Qutb complex built by Alauddin (1311); first true dome in India.

Revision card

Remember
  • Ministries: Wazir (finance), Ariz (army), Dabir (letters), Sadr (religion), Qazi (justice), Barid (spies).
  • Iqta: Iltutmish → audit (Balban, Alauddin) → hereditary (Firoz).
  • Alauddin: half produce, masahat, curbed khuts/muqaddams; markets, Shahna-i-Mandi, Sarai-i-Adl; dagh, chehra.
  • Architecture: Qutb, arch (Balban), dome (Alai Darwaza), Tughluq sloping walls, Lodi octagonal tombs.
  • Amir Khusrau: Tuti-i-Hind.

    Show answer

    Half.

    Show answer

    Branding of horses and descriptive roll of soldiers.

  5. Who was the Shahna-i-Mandi?
    Show answer

    Alauddin's market superintendent.

  6. Which building has India's first true dome?
    Show answer

    Alai Darwaza.

  7. Who was called Tuti-i-Hind?
    Show answer

    Amir Khusrau.

  8. Which department did Muhammad bin Tughluq set up for agriculture?

Sources: Satish Chandra, Medieval India, vol. I, chs. 4–6.; Irfan Habib, "Economic History of the Delhi Sultanate", in Cambridge Economic History of India, vol. I.; Percy Brown, Indian Architecture (Islamic Period).; NCERT Class 7, Our Pasts II.

Part II · Unit I19Vijayanagara and the Bahmanis1336 – 1687

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Vijayanagara dynasties; Krishnadevaraya; Talikota; nayankara and ayagar systems; Bahmani kingdom; Mahmud Gawan; Deccan Sultanates; foreign travellers

Mains: GS-I: Vijayanagara administration and culture; Bahmani kingdom

Krishnadevaraya, the nayankara system and foreign travellers' accounts are frequent; Hampi links to art and culture.

The big picture

As Muhammad bin Tughluq's control over the south collapsed, two states arose: Vijayanagara (1336), founded by the brothers Harihara and Bukka of the Sangama family, and the Bahmani kingdom (1347) of Alauddin Hasan Bahman Shah. They fought for two centuries over the fertile Raichur doab (between the Krishna and Tungabhadra). The Bahmani kingdom split into five Deccan Sultanates, which combined to defeat Vijayanagara at Talikota (1565).

Exam focus

Vijayanagara is studied for its administration (nayankara), its capital Hampi (UNESCO) and the accounts of foreign visitors.

WhenWhat happened
1336Harihara I and Bukka found Vijayanagara (Sangama dynasty)
1347Bahman Shah founds the Bahmani kingdom (capital Gulbarga)
c. 1420Nicolo de Conti visits Vijayanagara
1422–1446Deva Raya II
1443Abdur Razzaq visits
1466–1481Mahmud Gawan prime minister of the Bahmanis
1485–86Saluva Narasimha (Saluva dynasty)
1505Tuluva dynasty
1509–1529Krishnadevaraya
1510Portuguese take Goa from Bijapur
c. 1518–1527Break-up of the Bahmani kingdom
1565Battle of Talikota (Rakshasa-Tangadi)
1570sAravidu dynasty, Penukonda / Chandragiri
1636Ahmadnagar annexed by the Mughals
1686, 1687Bijapur and Golconda annexed by Aurangzeb

Core notes

I19.1 Vijayanagara: four dynasties

DynastyRulers / events
Sangama (1336–1485)Harihara I and Bukka I (guided, per tradition, by the sage Vidyaranya); Harihara II; Deva Raya II (employed Muslim archers; Abdur Razzaq's visit)
Saluva (1485–1505)Saluva Narasimha
Tuluva (1505–1570)Vira Narasimha, Krishnadevaraya (1509–29), Achyuta Raya, Sadashiva Raya (with regent Rama Raya)
Aravidu (1570–c. 1646)Tirumala; capital moved to Penukonda, then Chandragiri; Venkata II

I19.2 Krishnadevaraya (1509–1529)

  • Defeated the Bahmani successors; took Raichur (1520) from Bijapur; campaigned against the Gajapatis of Odisha.
  • Friendly relations with the Portuguese (horse trade; Goa). Horses came first through Arab traders, later also through local kudirai chettis (horse merchants).
  • NCERT dates his gains as: Raichur doab 1512, Odisha's rulers subdued 1514, Bijapur's Sultan badly defeated 1520. He also backed claimants in the Sultanates and took the title "establisher of the Yavana kingdom".
  • Wrote Amuktamalyada (Telugu, on statecraft and devotion) and Sanskrit works (Jambavati Kalyanam).
  • His court had the Ashtadiggajas (eight Telugu poets) including Allasani Peddana ("Andhra Kavitapitamaha"); Tenali Ramakrishna is associated by tradition.
  • Built the Vitthala temple, Hazara Rama temple additions; tanks and the town of Nagalapuram.
  • Titles: Andhra Bhoja, Abhinava Bhoja.
  • Described by Domingo Paes and Fernão Nunes.

I19.3 Administration and society

  • King assisted by a council; empire divided into rajyas/mandalams, nadu, sthala, villages.
  • Nayankara system (NCERT: amara-nayaka): military chiefs (nayakas) held territories (amaram) in return for maintaining troops and paying tribute; they later became independent (Nayakas of Madurai, Thanjavur, Ikkeri).
  • Ayagar system: village functionaries (twelve ayagars) paid with tax-free land.
  • Land revenue about one-sixth (traditionally), though often higher; also taxes on trade, property, marriage.
  • Army: cavalry with imported horses (Arab, Persian, via Portuguese); artillery; forts.
  • Society: Brahmins prominent; temples as economic centres; devadasis; sati noted by travellers; women as wrestlers, accountants and palace guards (Nuniz).
  • Economy: irrigation tanks (Kamalapuram; the Anantaraja Sagar, with a 1.37 km earthen dam across the Maldevi river; a lake that Krishnadevaraya made by building an embankment between two hills), trade through western ports; coins — gold varaha (pagoda), pratapa, fanam.

I19.4 Art and architecture (Hampi)

  • Capital Hampi on the Tungabhadra — UNESCO World Heritage Site.
  • Legitimacy: the site was chosen for the shrines of Virupaksha and the local goddess Pampadevi; the kings ruled on Virupaksha's behalf and signed orders "Shri Virupaksha" in Kannada script.
  • City plan: Abdur Razzaq saw seven lines of fortification, with fields and gardens between them, so that a besieged city could feed itself; water came from tanks such as Kamalapuram and the Hiriya canal. The royal centre had over 60 temples and about 30 palace-like buildings; Chinese porcelain marks rich traders' quarters in the Muslim quarter of the urban core.
  • Mahanavami Dibba: a large platform (about 11,000 sq ft base, 40 ft high) used during the Mahanavami (Navaratri) festival, when the king received gifts from the nayakas and reviewed their armies.
  • The Vitthala temple's deity, a form of Vishnu worshipped in Maharashtra, shows how the rulers drew on several traditions; straight chariot streets lined with pillared shops ran from the gopurams.
  • Features: tall raya gopurams, kalyana mandapas (pillared marriage halls), carved pillars with yali (rearing horses), temple chariots.
  • Temples: Vitthala (stone chariot, musical pillars), Hazara Rama (Ramayana friezes), Virupaksha.
  • Secular: Lotus Mahal, Elephant Stables, Mahanavami Dibba (platform for the Mahanavami festival).
  • Literature in Telugu, Kannada, Tamil and Sanskrit; Sayana's commentaries on the Vedas.

I19.5 The Bahmani kingdom and the Deccan Sultanates

  • Founded by Alauddin Hasan Bahman Shah (1347); capital Gulbarga, later Bidar.
  • Firoz Shah Bahmani: encouraged Deccani culture; built an observatory near Daulatabad.
  • Mahmud Gawan (Persian, prime minister 1466–81): divided provinces (tarafs) from four to eight to curb governors; land survey; strengthened army; conquered Goa; founded the madrasa at Bidar. Executed in 1481 after a court conspiracy.
  • Conflict between Deccanis (local) and Afaqis/Pardesis (newcomers from Iran, Iraq) weakened the state.
  • Split into five: Bijapur (Adil Shahi), Ahmadnagar (Nizam Shahi), Golconda (Qutb Shahi), Berar (Imad Shahi), Bidar (Barid Shahi).
  • Bidri ware — inlay of silver (or brass) on a blackened alloy — is named after Bidar.
  • Monuments: Gol Gumbaz (Bijapur, tomb of Muhammad Adil Shah, among the largest domes), Charminar (Hyderabad, Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah, 1591), Ibrahim Rauza; Golconda fort.
  • Ibrahim Adil Shah II wrote Kitab-i-Nauras (songs in Dakhini); Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah — early Dakhini Urdu poet.

I19.6 The battle of Talikota (1565)

  • Also called Rakshasa-Tangadi or Bannihatti.
  • Cause: Rama Raya's interference in Sultanate rivalries; Bijapur, Ahmadnagar, Golconda and Bidar united (Berar stayed out).
  • Result: Rama Raya killed; Hampi sacked for months; the empire survived under the Aravidus but declined.

I19.7 Foreign travellers

TravellerCountryRuler visited
Ibn BattutaMoroccoTravelled in the south in the 1340s, in Harihara I's time (describes the region, not the later capital)
Nicolo de ContiItaly (Venice)Deva Raya I (c. 1420)
Abdur RazzaqPersia (envoy of Shah Rukh)Deva Raya II (1443)
Afanasy NikitinRussiaBahmani kingdom (c. 1470)
Duarte BarbosaPortugalKrishnadevaraya
Domingo PaesPortugalKrishnadevaraya (c. 1520)
Fernão Nunes (Nuniz)PortugalAchyuta Raya (c. 1535)

What historians say

Robert Sewell (A Forgotten Empire, 1900) translated Paes and Nunes and portrayed Vijayanagara as a Hindu bulwark against Islam. Burton Stein rejected this and called it a segmentary state with ritual sovereignty; Noboru Karashima analysed nayaka land relations. Phillip Wagoner showed Vijayanagara kings adopted Islamicate dress and titles (Hindu-raya-suratrana, "Sultan among Hindu kings"). Richard Eaton (A Social History of the Deccan) studies the Deccan Sultanates.

Debate

Was Vijayanagara a Hindu state defending dharma against Islam (Sewell, Nilakanta Sastri), or a pragmatic regional power that shared a Persianate court culture with the Sultanates (Wagoner, Eaton)? Evidence: Muslim soldiers in its army, alliances across religious lines, the title "Hindu-raya-suratrana".

Prelims fact bank

  • Vijayanagara was founded in 1336 by Harihara I and Bukka of the Sangama family.
  • The Bahmani kingdom was founded in 1347 by Alauddin Hasan Bahman Shah.
  • The first Bahmani capital was Gulbarga, later Bidar.
  • The Raichur doab lies between the Krishna and Tungabhadra rivers.
  • Krishnadevaraya ruled 1509–1529 and wrote Amuktamalyada in Telugu.
  • Royal orders of Vijayanagara were signed 'Shri Virupaksha'.
  • Hampi was declared a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1986.
  • Kudirai chettis were Vijayanagara's local horse merchants.
  • Bidri inlay metalwork takes its name from Bidar.
  • Krishnadevaraya's court had the Ashtadiggajas, eight Telugu poets.
  • Nayakas were military chiefs holding territories in Vijayanagara.
  • The ayagar system paid village servants with tax-free land.
  • Mahmud Gawan founded a madrasa at Bidar.
  • The battle of Talikota was fought in 1565.
  • Abdur Razzaq visited Vijayanagara in 1443 under Deva Raya II.
  • Afanasy Nikitin, a Russian, visited the Bahmani kingdom.
  • The Gol Gumbaz is at Bijapur.
  • The Charminar was built by Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah in 1591.
  • The gold coin of Vijayanagara was the varaha (pagoda).
Exam traps
  • Talikota (1565): Bijapur, Ahmadnagar, Golconda and Bidar fought; Berar stayed out (NCERT VII also lists Berar).
  • Raichur: NCERT dates the doab's acquisition to 1512; the famous battle of Raichur against Bijapur was in 1520.
  • Nayankara (Vijayanagara military chiefs) ≠ ayagar (village servants).
  • Talikota: four Sultanates joined; Berar did not.
  • Nikitin visited the Bahmanis, not Vijayanagara.
  • Paes described Krishnadevaraya; Nuniz described Achyuta Raya's time.
  • Gol Gumbaz: Bijapur; Charminar: Hyderabad (Golconda).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Describe the administration of the Vijayanagara empire.

How to frame it: King and council → Provinces → Nayankara → Ayagar → Revenue → Army

Model answer (91 words)

The king ruled with a council of ministers; the empire was divided into rajyas, nadus and villages. Under the nayankara system, military chiefs (nayakas) held territories (amaram), maintained troops and paid tribute — strengthening defence but later producing independent Nayaka states. Village administration rested on the ayagar system, with twelve functionaries paid in tax-free land. Land revenue was the main income, supplemented by trade and property taxes. A strong cavalry using imported horses, artillery and forts defended the realm. The system was effective but decentralised, which weakened the empire after Talikota.

5 marks · 50 words

Assess the achievements of Krishnadevaraya.

How to frame it: Military → Culture → Administration

Model answer (41 words)

Krishnadevaraya took Raichur from Bijapur and defeated the Gajapatis, keeping friendly ties with the Portuguese for horses. He wrote Amuktamalyada, patronised the Ashtadiggajas and built the Vitthala and Hazara Rama temples and irrigation tanks. Paes praised his justice and prosperous capital.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the significance of the battle of Talikota?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

1565: Deccan Sultanates killed Rama Raya and sacked Hampi; Vijayanagara declined thereafter.

Revision card

Remember
  • Vijayanagara 1336 (Harihara, Bukka); Bahmani 1347 (Bahman Shah, Gulbarga → Bidar).
  • Dynasties: Sangama, Saluva, Tuluva, Aravidu.
  • Krishnadevaraya: Raichur 1520, Amuktamalyada, Ashtadiggajas, Paes.
  • Nayankara, ayagar, varaha.
  • Mahmud Gawan: tarafs 4→8, Bidar madrasa.
  • Five Sultanates; Talikota 1565.
  • Travellers: Conti, Razzaq, Nikitin, Paes, Nuniz, Barbosa.

Part II · Unit I20Bhakti and Sufi Movementsc. 6th – 17th century

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Alvars and Nayanars; Acharyas; Nirguna and Saguna saints; Kabir, Nanak, Mirabai, Tulsidas; Sufi silsilas

Mains: GS-I: Bhakti and Sufi movements — teachings and social impact; medieval religious synthesis

One of the highest-yield medieval topics for both papers. Rajasthan saints (Mira, Dadu, Jambhoji, Ramdev, Pipa) are in the art and culture notebook; core Rajasthan links appear here.

The big picture

Bhakti — loving devotion to a personal god — began with the Tamil Alvars (Vishnu) and Nayanars (Shiva) between the 6th and 9th centuries, was given a philosophical base by the Acharyas (Ramanuja, Madhva and others), and spread north from the 14th century. Northern bhakti had two streams: nirguna (God without attributes — Kabir, Nanak, Dadu, Ravidas) and saguna (God with form — Rama and Krishna devotees such as Tulsidas, Surdas, Mirabai, Chaitanya).

Sufism — Islamic mysticism — came with Persian and Central Asian saints. Organised in orders (silsilas), the Chishtis and Suhrawardis spread in India from the 12th–13th centuries.

Both used regional languages, criticised ritualism and caste or orthodoxy, and stressed love of God and a guru or pir.

Exam focus

Questions ask for: common features, social impact, comparisons (nirguna vs saguna; bhakti vs Sufi), and individual saints.

WhenWhat happened
c. 6th–9th c.Alvars and Nayanars in Tamil Nadu
c. 788–820Shankaracharya (Advaita) — traditional dates
c. 1017–1137Ramanuja (Vishishtadvaita)
12th c.Basavanna and the Lingayats (Karnataka)
13th c.Madhva (Dvaita); Jnaneshwar (Maharashtra)
1236Death of Muinuddin Chishti at Ajmer
1325Death of Nizamuddin Auliya
c. 1440–1518Kabir (dates debated)
1469–1539Guru Nanak
1486–1533Chaitanya Mahaprabhu
c. 1498–1547Mirabai (traditional dates)
1574Tulsidas begins Ramcharitmanas

Core notes

I20.1 Southern bhakti: Alvars, Nayanars, Acharyas

  • Alvars (12, devotees of Vishnu) — hymns compiled in the Nalayira Divya Prabandham; Andal was a woman Alvar.
  • Nayanars (63, devotees of Shiva) — hymns in the Tevaram (part of Tirumurai); Appar, Sambandar, Sundarar, Manikkavachakar; Karaikkal Ammaiyar, a woman Nayanar.
  • They came from all castes, including groups treated as "untouchable" such as the Pulaiyar and Panar, travelled between temples and sang in Tamil. Manikkavachakar's hymns form the Tiruvachakam; the Divya Prabandham was called the "Tamil Veda", claimed equal to the four Vedas.
  • Link with the state: from the 10th to 12th centuries Chola and Pandya kings built large temples at shrines the saints had sung of (Chidambaram, Thanjavur, Gangaikondacholapuram), installed images of the saints and had the Tevaram hymns sung in temples. The hymns' chief targets were Buddhists and Jains, rivals for royal patronage.
AcharyaPhilosophyRegion
ShankaracharyaAdvaita (non-dualism); jnana; four mathasKerala (Kaladi)
RamanujaVishishtadvaita (qualified non-dualism); bhakti to VishnuSriperumbudur, Srirangam
MadhvaDvaita (dualism)Udupi, Karnataka
NimbarkaDvaitadvaita; Radha–KrishnaVrindavan; associated with Salemabad (Rajasthan) seat
VallabhacharyaShuddhadvaita; Pushti Marg; Krishna (Shrinathji)Vrindavan; Nathdwara (Rajasthan)

Basavanna (12th c., Karnataka): Virashaiva/Lingayat movement; rejected caste, idol worship and Brahmin rituals; vachanas in Kannada; Anubhava Mantapa. Companions: Allama Prabhu and Akka Mahadevi. Lingayats bury their dead (believing the devotee joins Shiva and is not reborn), and accepted post-puberty marriage and widow remarriage.

Nathpanthis, Siddhas and Yogis criticised ritual and the social order, taught renunciation and meditation on a formless reality through yoga and breath control, drew followers from artisan and "low" castes, and prepared the ground for nirguna bhakti in the north.

I20.2 Maharashtra (Warkari tradition)

  • Devotion to Vitthala (Vithoba) of Pandharpur.
  • Jnaneshwar (13th c.): Jnaneshwari, Marathi commentary on the Gita.
  • Namdev (14th c.): tailor; his verses are in the Guru Granth Sahib.
  • Eknath (16th c.) and Tukaram (17th c., abhangas).
  • Ramdas (17th c.): Dasbodh; associated with Shivaji.
  • Women and "untouchable" saints too: Sakhubai, and the family of Chokhamela (a Mahar).
  • The Varkari sect centres on the annual pilgrimage to Pandharpur. These saints rejected renunciation, lived as householders and saw bhakti as sharing others' pain; the Gujarati saint Narsi Mehta defined a Vaishnava as one who understands others' pain.

I20.3 Northern nirguna bhakti

SaintKey points
Ramananda (14th–15th c.)Bridge between south and north; preached Rama bhakti in Hindi; disciples from all castes — traditionally Kabir, Ravidas, Pipa, Sain, Dhanna
KabirWeaver of Banaras; formless God (Rama, Allah, Hari); attacked idol worship, caste, ritual of both faiths; dohas, sakhis, ramainis; compiled in the Bijak, Kabir Granthavali, and the Guru Granth Sahib. The Bijak is preserved by the Kabirpanth (Varanasi), the Granthavali by the Dadupanth (Rajasthan). He wrote in the "sant bhasha" of nirguna poets and in ulatbansi (upside-down sayings). That Ramananda was his guru is a later Vaishnava claim, hard to establish
Ravidas (Raidas)Leather worker; Mirabai is traditionally regarded as his disciple
Guru Nanak (1469–1539)Born at Talwandi (Nankana Sahib); one formless God; nam japna, kirat karna, vand chhakna; langar, sangat; founded Kartarpur; the basis of Sikhism (I25)
Dadu Dayal (1544–1603)Gujarat-born, worked in Rajasthan (Naraina); Dadu Panth; Dadu Vani

I20.4 Northern saguna bhakti

SaintKey points
Tulsidas (1532–1623)Ramcharitmanas (Awadhi); Vinay Patrika; Rama as maryada purushottam
SurdasBlind poet of Krishna's childhood; Sursagar (Braj); follower of Vallabhacharya; one of the Ashtachhap
MirabaiRathore princess of Merta, married into the Mewar royal house; devotion to Krishna (Girdhar Gopal); padas in Rajasthani and Braj; defied family and social norms
Chaitanya (1486–1533)Bengal; Gaudiya Vaishnavism; kirtan; Radha–Krishna; settled at Puri
Shankaradeva (1449–1568)Assam; Ekasarana dharma; namghars, sattras; Ankiya Naat

I20.5 Sufism

  • Key ideas: love of God, surrender, the pir–murid (master–disciple) relationship, khanqah (hospice), sama (devotional music), wahdat-ul-wujud (unity of being, Ibn Arabi), dargah (shrine), urs (death anniversary), silsila (chain of succession).
  • Ba-shara (following sharia) and be-shara (not bound by sharia, e.g. qalandars).
SilsilaFounder in India / key saintsFeatures
ChishtiMuinuddin Chishti (Ajmer, d. 1236); Bakhtiyar Kaki (Delhi); Baba Farid (Pakpattan, verses in the Guru Granth); Nizamuddin Auliya (Delhi); Nasiruddin Chiragh-i-Delhi; Salim Chishti (Fatehpur Sikri)Kept away from the state; poverty; sama; open to all; most popular
SuhrawardiOrder of Shihabuddin Umar Suhrawardi (Baghdad); in India Bahauddin Zakariya (Multan, 13th c.); Jalaluddin Tabrizi (Bengal); Qazi Hamiduddin Nagauri (Delhi)Strict observance of the sharia; accepted state grants, posts and wealth; kept large khanqahs; mainly in Sindh, Multan and Punjab, also Bengal
NaqshbandiKhwaja Baqi Billah; Shaikh Ahmad Sirhindi (Mujaddid)Orthodox; opposed Akbar's policies; wahdat-ul-shuhud (unity of appearance)
QadiriShah Niamatullah; Miyan Mir (laid foundation of the Golden Temple, by tradition); Dara Shikoh was a disciple of Mulla ShahLiberal
Firdausi, Shattari, Rishi (Kashmir — Nuruddin Rishi)—Regional

Amir Khusrau was a disciple of Nizamuddin Auliya.

  • Chishti khanqah life: Nizamuddin's hospice in Delhi ran an open kitchen (langar) on unasked-for gifts (futuh); visitors included Amir Hasan Sijzi, Amir Khusrau and Ziyauddin Barani; bowing before the shaikh, shaving initiates' heads and yogic exercises show local influence. His disciples called him sultan-ul-mashaikh.
  • Ziyarat (pilgrimage to a saint's tomb) sought the saint's grace (barakat). At Ajmer, Muhammad bin Tughluq was the first Sultan to visit, Sultan Ghiyasuddin Khalji of Malwa funded the earliest building, and Akbar came fourteen times.
  • Language: Chishtis in Delhi spoke Hindavi; Baba Farid's verses entered the Guru Granth Sahib; Chishti Sufis around Bijapur wrote short poems in Dakhani, influenced by Kannada vachanas. Other Deccan Chishti: Bandanawaz Gisudaraz of Gulbarga.
  • Sufis and the state: Sultans set up charitable trusts (auqaf) and gave tax-free land (inam); kings sought burial near shrines; but both sides expected ritual deference, which caused friction.

I20.6 Common features and impact

  1. Devotion to one God; personal relationship; guru/pir.
  2. Criticism of ritualism, priests and (in many saints) caste.
  3. Use of regional languages: Hindi, Marathi, Bengali, Punjabi, Kannada — boosting their literature.
  4. Participation of lower castes and women (Andal, Akka Mahadevi, Mira, Lal Ded).
  5. Fostered religious tolerance; Hindu–Muslim syncretism (Kabir, Nanak, Sufi shrines visited by all).
  6. New sects: Sikhism, Kabirpanth, Dadu Panth, Gaudiya Vaishnavism.
  7. Limits: did not overthrow the caste system; saguna bhakti often worked within Brahmanical norms; later sects themselves became institutionalised.

What historians say

Tara Chand (Influence of Islam on Indian Culture) argued Islam influenced bhakti; most historians now trace bhakti to Tamil roots predating Islam in India, while accepting interaction in the north. Irfan Habib linked nirguna saints to artisan groups in growing towns. David Lorenzen and Charlotte Vaudeville studied Kabir and the nirguna tradition. S.A.A. Rizvi wrote the standard history of Sufism in India. Richard Eaton studied Sufis in Bijapur and Bengal and the frontier of conversion.

Debate

Was bhakti a "movement"? Some scholars (e.g. J.S. Hawley) argue the idea of a single bhakti movement is a later construction; it was many local traditions linked over centuries.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Alvars were devotees of Vishnu; the Nayanars of Shiva.
  • Andal was a woman Alvar.
  • Lingayats bury their dead rather than cremate them.
  • Chokhamela, a Mahar, was a Varkari saint of Maharashtra.
  • The Kabir Bijak is preserved by the Kabirpanth; the Kabir Granthavali by the Dadupanth.
  • Akbar visited the Ajmer dargah fourteen times.
  • Bandanawaz Gisudaraz was a Chishti saint of Gulbarga.
  • The Nalayira Divya Prabandham is the compilation of Alvar hymns.
  • Ramanuja propounded Vishishtadvaita.
  • Madhva propounded Dvaita.
  • Vallabhacharya founded the Pushti Marg and Shuddhadvaita.
  • Basavanna founded the Lingayat (Virashaiva) movement.
  • Jnaneshwar wrote the Jnaneshwari in Marathi.
  • Kabir was a weaver of Banaras.
  • Guru Nanak was born at Talwandi in 1469.
  • Dadu Dayal's main seat is at Naraina in Rajasthan.
  • Tulsidas wrote the Ramcharitmanas in Awadhi.
  • Surdas wrote the Sursagar.
  • Chaitanya founded Gaudiya Vaishnavism.
  • Shankaradeva founded namghars in Assam.
  • Muinuddin Chishti's dargah is at Ajmer.
  • The Suhrawardi silsila accepted state patronage.
  • Shaikh Ahmad Sirhindi belonged to the Naqshbandi silsila.
  • Amir Khusrau was a disciple of Nizamuddin Auliya.
Exam traps
  • Two Hamiduddin Nagauris: Qazi Hamiduddin Nagauri was a Suhrawardi in Delhi; Sufi Hamiduddin (Suwali) Nagauri of Nagaur was a Chishti disciple of Muinuddin.
  • Tiruvachakam = Manikkavachakar (Shaiva); Divya Prabandham = Alvars (Vaishnava).
  • Bijak = Kabirpanth (Varanasi); Kabir Granthavali = Dadupanth (Rajasthan).
  • Advaita (Shankara) ≠ Vishishtadvaita (Ramanuja) ≠ Dvaita (Madhva) ≠ Shuddhadvaita (Vallabha) ≠ Dvaitadvaita (Nimbarka).
  • Kabir and Nanak: nirguna; Tulsidas, Surdas, Mira: saguna.
  • Chishtis avoided the state; Suhrawardis accepted it.
  • Wahdat-ul-wujud (Ibn Arabi) vs wahdat-ul-shuhud (Sirhindi).
  • Tevaram = Nayanar hymns; Divya Prabandham = Alvar hymns.

RAS Mains questions are quoted as printed. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Discuss the social impact of the Bhakti movement.

How to frame it: Caste → Women → Languages → Harmony → New sects → Limits

Model answer (92 words)

Bhakti saints taught that devotion, not birth or ritual, leads to God. Many came from artisan and lower castes — Kabir, Ravidas, Namdev — and challenged Brahmin priesthood and caste. Women such as Andal, Akka Mahadevi and Mirabai found a religious voice. Saints wrote in Hindi, Marathi, Bengali and Punjabi, enriching regional literature. Kabir and Nanak promoted Hindu–Muslim harmony, and new sects such as Sikhism and the Dadu Panth arose. Yet caste survived, saguna bhakti often accepted Brahmanical norms, and sects later became institutions — the impact was cultural more than structural.

5 marks · 50 words

Compare the Chishti and Suhrawardi silsilas.

How to frame it: Attitude to state → Practice → Region

Model answer (46 words)

The Chishtis (Muinuddin, Nizamuddin) kept away from the court, refused grants, lived in poverty, used sama and welcomed all. The Suhrawardis (Bahauddin Zakariya, Multan) accepted state patronage and office, kept wealth and followed stricter sharia. Chishtis spread across north India; Suhrawardis mainly in Punjab and Sindh.

2 marks · 15 words

Who were the Alvars?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Twelve Tamil Vishnu devotees (6th–9th c.) whose hymns form the Divya Prabandham.

2 marks · 15 words

Describe the Suhrawardi Sufi silsila.

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (18 words)

Sufi order brought to Multan by Bahauddin Zakariya; followed the sharia strictly and accepted state grants and posts.

5 marks · 50 words

Explain the socio-religious significance of the Nayanar and Alvar bhakti saints.

How to frame it: Devotion over ritual → Caste and gender → Tamil → Temples and kings → Legacy

Model answer (64 words)

The Nayanars (Shiva) and Alvars (Vishnu) of the 6th–9th centuries put devotion above ritual. They came from all castes, including Nandanar and Tiruppan Alvar of "untouchable" groups, and women such as Andal and Karaikkal Ammaiyar. Singing in Tamil, they made it a sacred language (Tevaram, Divya Prabandham). Pallava and Chola kings built temples around them, checking Buddhism and Jainism and shaping later all-India bhakti.

10 marks · 100 words

Underline the similarities between the 6th-century BCE religious movements and the nirguna bhakti movement in terms of the varna system and prevalent evils.

How to frame it: Context → Varna → Ritual and priests → Language and followers → Limits

Model answer (103 words)

Both rose against Brahmanical dominance. On varna, the Buddha and Mahavira denied that birth decided worth and admitted all to the sangha (Upali was a barber); Kabir, Ravidas and Nanak, a weaver, a leather-worker and a Khatri, rejected caste and the Brahmin's role as mediator. On evils, the early movements condemned costly Vedic sacrifice and animal killing; the nirguna saints attacked idol worship, pilgrimage, rituals and hypocrisy in both Hindu and Muslim practice. Both preached ethical living and inner purity, spoke the people's languages (Pali and Prakrit; Hindavi and Punjabi), and drew artisans and traders. Neither ended caste, which absorbed them over time.

Revision card

Remember
  • South: Alvars (Vishnu, Divya Prabandham, Andal); Nayanars (Shiva, Tevaram).
  • Acharyas: Shankara (Advaita), Ramanuja (Vishishtadvaita), Madhva (Dvaita), Nimbarka (Dvaitadvaita), Vallabha (Shuddhadvaita).
  • Basavanna — Lingayats; Maharashtra — Jnaneshwar, Namdev, Tukaram.
  • Nirguna: Kabir, Ravidas, Nanak, Dadu. Saguna: Tulsidas, Surdas, Mira, Chaitanya.
  • Sufi: Chishti (Ajmer, Nizamuddin), Suhrawardi (Multan), Naqshbandi (Sirhindi), Qadiri (Miyan Mir).

    Show answer

    Show answer

    Vishishtadvaita.

  3. Who founded the Pushti Marg?
    Show answer

    Vallabhacharya.

  4. Who wrote the Jnaneshwari?
    Show answer

    Jnaneshwar.

  5. What were Nanak's three principles?
    Show answer

    Nam japna, kirat karna, vand chhakna.

  6. Where is Dadu Dayal's seat?
    Show answer

    Naraina (Rajasthan).

  7. Where is Muinuddin Chishti's dargah?
    Show answer

    Ajmer.

  8. Which silsila accepted state patronage?
    Show answer

  9. Which Naqshbandi saint opposed Akbar?
    Show answer

Sources: NCERT Class 12, Themes in Indian History II, ch. 6 ("Bhakti-Sufi Traditions").; Satish Chandra, Medieval India, vol. I, ch. 10.; S.A.A. Rizvi, A History of Sufism in India.; Charlotte Vaudeville, Kabir.

Part II · Unit I21The Mughals I: Babur to Akbar1526 – 1605

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Babur's battles; Humayun and Sher Shah; Sur administration; Akbar — conquests, Rajput policy, religious policy (Sulh-i-kul, Din-i-Ilahi)

Mains: GS-I: Mughal empire — establishment, consolidation; Akbar's policies

Akbar's Rajput and religious policies and Sher Shah's administration are the highest-yield topics; battles and dates are Prelims staples. Rajasthan angles (Sanga, Pratap, Haldighati, Amber) are in R08–R11.

The big picture

Babur, a Timurid from Ferghana, won north India with artillery and cavalry tactics at Panipat (1526) and Khanwa (1527). His son Humayun lost the empire to the Afghan Sher Shah Sur, whose short reign (1540–45) laid administrative foundations. Humayun returned in 1555; his son Akbar (1556–1605) built a lasting empire through conquest, alliance with the Rajputs, a centralised administration (I23) and a policy of religious tolerance (sulh-i-kul).

Exam focus

The key analytical point: Akbar turned a foreign conquest state into an Indian empire by broadening its ruling class (Rajputs, Iranis, Indian Muslims) and its ideology.

WhenWhat happened
1526First battle of Panipat: Babur defeats Ibrahim Lodi
1527Battle of Khanwa: Babur defeats Rana Sanga
1528Chanderi: Babur defeats Medini Rai
1529Battle of the Ghaghra: Babur defeats the Afghans

Core notes

I21.1 Babur (1526–1530)

  • Descended from Timur (father's side) and Chingiz Khan (mother's side); lost Samarkand to the Uzbeks; ruled Kabul from 1504.
  • Invited/encouraged by Daulat Khan Lodi and (per Babur) Rana Sanga.
  • Panipat (1526): used the tulughma (flanking) tactic and the Ottoman (Rumi) method — carts linked as a barrier (araba) with matchlockmen and cannon behind; gunners Ustad Ali and Mustafa.
  • Khanwa (1527): declared jihad, broke his wine cups, remitted tamgha for Muslims; defeated Sanga's Rajput confederacy; took title Ghazi.
  • Chanderi (1528) and Ghaghra (1529).
  • Memoirs: Tuzuk-i-Baburi / Baburnama in Chaghatai Turkish — candid observations of India.
  • Called himself Padshah (rather than Sultan).

I21.2 Humayun and the Sur interlude

  • Humayun (1530–40, 1555–56): divided the empire among brothers (Kamran, Askari, Hindal); fought Bahadur Shah of Gujarat; defeated by Sher Khan at Chausa (1539) and Kannauj (1540); exile in Persia (Shah Tahmasp); returned in 1555; died falling from the stairs of his library (Sher Mandal). Gulbadan Begum wrote the Humayun-nama. Tomb in Delhi built by his widow Haji Begum.

Sher Shah Sur (1540–1545)

  • Farid, son of a jagirdar of Sasaram; took title Sher Khan after killing a tiger.
  • Battles: Chausa, Kannauj; Sammel (1544) against Maldeo — "I nearly lost the empire of Hindustan for a handful of millet"; killed by a gunpowder explosion at Kalinjar (1545).
  • Administration:
    • Land measured; revenue fixed at one-third of average produce; patta (title deed) and qabuliyat (agreement) with peasants; Raja Todar Mal worked under him.
    • Silver rupiya (178 grains) and copper dam — basis of later currency.
    • Sarkars and parganas; shiqdar, amin, munsif.
    • Roads: rebuilt the Grand Trunk road (Sonargaon to Peshawar) and others; sarais every 2 kos, also used for posts (dak).
    • Revived dagh and chehra; local responsibility for crime (muqaddams).
  • Monuments: tomb at Sasaram, Purana Qila (Qila-i-Kuhna mosque).
  • Successors: Islam Shah; then Afghan collapse; Hemu (Hemchandra Vikramaditya), Adil Shah's minister, took Delhi in 1556.

I21.3 Akbar (1556–1605): consolidation and conquests

  • Crowned at Kalanaur (1556) aged 13; regent Bairam Khan; second Panipat (1556) against Hemu.
  • 1560–64: "petticoat government" (influence of Maham Anaga and Adham Khan) — a term of older historians.
  • Conquests: Malwa (Baz Bahadur, 1561), Gondwana (Rani Durgavati, 1564), Chittor (1568), Ranthambore and Kalinjar (1569), Gujarat (1572–73) — Buland Darwaza commemorates it, Bengal and Bihar (1576), Kabul (1581), Kashmir (1586), Sindh (1591), Odisha (1592), Kandahar (1595), Berar, Ahmadnagar (Chand Bibi), Asirgarh (1601) — his last conquest.
  • Revolts of the Uzbek nobles and Mirzas suppressed; Mirza Hakim of Kabul (1581).

I21.4 Akbar's Rajput policy

  • Matrimonial alliances: Bharmal of Amber (1562; mother of Jahangir — Harkha Bai, called Mariam-uz-Zamani); later Bikaner, Jaisalmer, Marwar (Mota Raja Udai Singh).
  • Rajputs given high mansabs: Man Singh and Bhagwant Das of Amber, Raja Todar Mal (Khatri), Rai Singh of Bikaner.
  • Rajput rulers kept their watan jagirs (hereditary lands) and internal autonomy.
  • Abolition of pilgrim tax and jizya won wider support.
  • Those who resisted (Mewar — Udai Singh, Maharana Pratap at Haldighati 1576) were attacked; Mewar never submitted in Akbar's time.
  • Nagaur Durbar (1570): Bikaner and Jaisalmer submitted.
  • Significance: a loyal martial class balanced Turani and Irani nobles, secured Rajasthan and the routes to Gujarat, and gave the empire Indian roots.

I21.5 Akbar's religious policy

  1. Early influences: Sufi (Chishti, Salim Chishti), Bhakti, liberal tutors (Abdul Latif); Rajput wives; tolerant Timurid tradition.
  2. 1562: ban on enslavement of war prisoners; 1563: pilgrim tax abolished; 1564: jizya abolished.
  3. 1575: Ibadat Khana (house of worship) at Fatehpur Sikri — first only for Sunni divines, from 1578 open to Shias, Hindus (Purushottam, Devi), Jains (Hiravijaya Suri), Zoroastrians (Dastur Meherji Rana), Christians (Jesuits Aquaviva, Monserrate).
  4. 1579: Mahzar — drafted by Shaikh Mubarak; made Akbar the final arbiter (Imam-i-Adil) among conflicting interpretations of Islamic law by the ulema.
  5. 1582: Tauhid-i-Ilahi (Din-i-Ilahi) — "divine monotheism", an order of discipleship (murid) rather than a new religion; few members (Birbal was one Hindu member; Man Singh declined).
  6. Sulh-i-kul (universal peace): principle of the state treating all religions equally.
  7. Translation bureau (Maktab Khana): Mahabharata as Razmnama; Ramayana, Atharvaveda, Lilavati translated.
  8. Ilahi calendar (1584), jharokha darshan, sijda revived.
Debate

Was Din-i-Ilahi a new religion? Badauni (hostile) said Akbar left Islam; Abul Fazl presented it as a spiritual discipleship; modern historians (S.R. Sharma, Iqtidar Alam Khan, Irfan Habib) see it as a political-ideological device for loyalty around a divinely-illumined emperor — not a religion. V.A. Smith called it "a monument of Akbar's folly", a view now rejected.

What historians say

Abul Fazl (Akbarnama, Ain-i-Akbari) presents Akbar as the ideal ruler with divine light (farr-i-izadi); Badauni (Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh) is critical; Nizamuddin Ahmad (Tabaqat-i-Akbari) is more neutral. Satish Chandra and Iqtidar Alam Khan analysed the composition of the nobility. Irfan Habib and M. Athar Ali studied the Mughal ruling class.

Gondwana: the Gond kingdom of Garha Katanga was divided into garhs (each under a Gond clan), then units of 84 villages (chaurasi) and of 12 villages (barhot). Its raja Aman Das took the title Sangram Shah; his son Dalpat married Durgavati, daughter of the Chandela ruler of Mahoba. Ruling for her young son Bir Narayan, she resisted Akbar's general Asaf Khan and chose death over surrender (1564). The Gond chiefs' wish to be counted as Rajputs and their land grants to Brahmins show how tribal states adopted caste society.

Chronicles and books: Abul Fazl wrote the Akbarnama (1589–1602) in three volumes; Henry Beveridge translated it into English. Gulbadan Begum began the Humayun-nama in 1587; Babur's Turkish memoirs were rendered into Persian in 1589. Manuscripts were made in the imperial kitabkhana (scriptorium); Akbar's favourite script was nastaliq. The translation bureau (maktab khana) at Fatehpur Sikri produced the illustrated Razmnama (Mahabharata). The first Jesuit mission reached Fatehpur Sikri in 1580. The Mughals divided inheritance among all sons (coparcenary) rather than following primogeniture.

Prelims fact bank

  • Babur defeated Ibrahim Lodi at the first battle of Panipat in 1526.
  • Babur defeated Rana Sanga at Khanwa in 1527.
  • Babur used the tulughma tactic and the Rumi (Ottoman) cart method.
  • Babur wrote the Baburnama in Chaghatai Turkish.
  • Sher Shah defeated Humayun at Chausa (1539) and Kannauj (1540).
  • Sher Shah fought Maldeo at Sammel in 1544.
  • Sher Shah died at Kalinjar in 1545.
  • Sher Shah introduced the silver rupiya and copper dam.
  • Sher Shah issued patta and took qabuliyat from peasants.
  • Sher Shah's tomb is at Sasaram.
  • Gulbadan Begum wrote the Humayun-nama.
  • Rani Durgavati, a Chandela princess of Mahoba, ruled Garha Katanga as regent for her son Bir Narayan.
  • The first Jesuit mission reached Akbar's court at Fatehpur Sikri in 1580.
  • Nastaliq was Akbar's favourite style of calligraphy.
  • Akbar was crowned at Kalanaur in 1556.
  • The second battle of Panipat (1556) was fought against Hemu.
  • Akbar abolished jizya in 1564.
  • The Ibadat Khana was built in 1575 at Fatehpur Sikri.
  • The Mahzar was issued in 1579.
  • Din-i-Ilahi (Tauhid-i-Ilahi) was announced in 1582.
  • Hiravijaya Suri was the Jain scholar at Akbar's court.
  • The Razmnama is the Persian translation of the Mahabharata.
  • Asirgarh (1601) was Akbar's last conquest.
  • The Buland Darwaza commemorates the conquest of Gujarat.
Exam traps
  • Durgavati died fighting Asaf Khan in 1564 (NCERT Class VII says 1565).
  • Panipat I (1526, Babur–Ibrahim Lodi); Panipat II (1556, Akbar/Bairam–Hemu).
  • Khanwa (1527) — Sanga; Chanderi (1528) — Medini Rai; Ghaghra (1529) — Afghans.
  • Chausa (1539) before Kannauj (1540).
  • The Mahzar (1579) preceded Din-i-Ilahi (1582).
  • Ibadat Khana opened to non-Muslims only in 1578.
  • Haldighati (1576) did not end Mewar's resistance.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Evaluate Akbar's Rajput policy.

How to frame it: Measures → Motives → Results → Limits

Model answer (93 words)

Akbar combined marriage alliances (Amber, 1562; later Bikaner, Jaisalmer, Marwar), high mansabs (Man Singh, Bhagwant Das, Rai Singh), retention of watan jagirs and internal autonomy, and the abolition of pilgrim tax and jizya. The motives were political: a loyal martial class to balance Turani and Irani nobles, control of Rajasthan and the Gujarat routes, and wider legitimacy. The Rajputs fought for the empire from Kabul to Bengal. Mewar resisted (Haldighati, 1576) and was never subdued in his time. The policy became a pillar of the empire until Aurangzeb's strains with Marwar and Mewar.

5 marks · 50 words

Assess the administrative reforms of Sher Shah Sur.

How to frame it: Revenue → Currency → Roads → Legacy

Model answer (48 words)

Sher Shah measured land, fixed revenue at one-third of average produce and issued patta and qabuliyat. He introduced the silver rupiya and copper dam, rebuilt the Grand Trunk road with sarais and dak, revived dagh and chehra and fixed local responsibility for crime. Akbar built on these foundations.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Mahzar of 1579?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (10 words)

Declaration making Akbar final arbiter (Imam-i-Adil) among ulema's conflicting interpretations.

Revision card

  • Babur: Panipat 1526 (tulughma, Rumi carts), Khanwa 1527, Chanderi 1528, Ghaghra 1529; Baburnama.
  • Humayun: Chausa 1539, Kannauj 1540; return 1555.
  • Sher Shah: Sammel 1544, Kalinjar 1545; one-third, patta/qabuliyat, rupiya/dam, GT road, sarais.
  • Akbar: Kalanaur 1556; Panipat II; Amber 1562; jizya 1564; Chittor 1568; Gujarat 1572; Ibadat Khana 1575; Haldighati 1576; Mahzar 1579; Din-i-Ilahi 1582; Asirgarh 1601.
  • Sulh-i-kul; Razmnama; Abul Fazl vs Badauni.

Part II · Unit I22The Mughals II: Jahangir to Aurangzeb and the Decline Debate1605 – 1761

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Jahangir, Nur Jahan; Shah Jahan — architecture, war of succession; Aurangzeb — religious and Deccan policy; later Mughals; Nadir Shah, Abdali

Mains: GS-I: Mughal empire — Aurangzeb's policies; causes of decline (historiographical debate)

"Causes of decline of the Mughal empire" with the historiographical schools is a classic Mains question. Rajput relations under Aurangzeb (Marwar, Mewar, Durgadas) are in R08–R09.

The big picture

The empire reached its greatest extent under Aurangzeb (1658–1707) but began to break up within decades of his death. Jahangir (1605–27) and Shah Jahan (1628–58) continued Akbar's system; Shah Jahan's reign is remembered for architecture. Aurangzeb's long Deccan wars, his strains with Rajputs, Marathas, Jats and Sikhs, and a crisis in the jagir system preceded rapid decline after 1707. Nadir Shah's sack of Delhi (1739) exposed the empire's weakness.

Exam focus

For decline, examiners expect the schools of thought: the "Aurangzeb's policy" view, the "agrarian/jagirdari crisis" view (Aligarh school) and the "regional/revisionist" view.

WhenWhat happened

1761Third battle of Panipat

Core notes

I22.1 Jahangir (1605–1627)

  • Prince Salim; revolted against Akbar; had Abul Fazl killed (1602, by Bir Singh Bundela).
  • Revolt of son Khusrau (1606); execution of Guru Arjan for supporting him.
  • Chain of justice (zanjir-i-adl) at Agra; memoir Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri.
  • Nur Jahan (Mehrunnisa, m. 1611): coins and farmans in her name; her "junta" (her father Itimad-ud-Daula, brother Asaf Khan, Prince Khurram) — a term from older historiography; later rivalry with Khurram.
  • Mewar treaty (1615): Amar Singh accepted Mughal suzerainty with honour (not required to attend court).
  • Kandahar lost to Persia (1622); revolt of Khurram (1622–25) and Mahabat Khan's coup (1626).
  • Europeans: William Hawkins (1608), Sir Thomas Roe (1615–19, ambassador of James I) — English factory rights at Surat.
  • Peak of Mughal painting: Ustad Mansur (birds, animals), Abul Hasan; Itimad-ud-Daula's tomb (pietra dura) built by Nur Jahan.

I22.2 Shah Jahan (1628–1658)

  • Revolts: Khan Jahan Lodi, Bundelas (Jujhar Singh).
  • Deccan: Ahmadnagar annexed (1636); Bijapur and Golconda accepted suzerainty (Deed of Submission); Aurangzeb as viceroy of the Deccan.
  • Central Asian campaign (Balkh, Badakhshan, 1646–47) failed; Kandahar recovered (1638) and lost again (1649).
  • Architecture: Taj Mahal (architect Ustad Ahmad Lahori), Red Fort and Jama Masjid of Shahjahanabad, Moti Masjid (Agra), Peacock Throne (Takht-i-Taus), Diwan-i-Khas.
  • Court historians: Abdul Hamid Lahori (Padshahnama), Inayat Khan.
  • Travellers: Bernier, Tavernier, Manucci (reign of Shah Jahan to Aurangzeb).
  • François Bernier, a French physician, was in India from 1656 to 1668, attached to Dara Shukoh and then to the noble Danishmand Khan. He dedicated his main work to Louis XIV and set India up as the inverse of Europe: crown ownership of land, he argued, discouraged investment and ruined agriculture. He called Mughal cities "camp towns" that depended on the imperial camp. His books (1670–71) were quickly translated across Europe.
  • War of succession (1657–58) among Dara Shikoh, Shuja, Aurangzeb and Murad: Bahadurgarh (Shuja defeated by Dara's son), Dharmat (Aurangzeb and Murad defeat Jaswant Singh), Samugarh (Dara defeated), Khajwa (Shuja), Deorai (Dara, 1659). Shah Jahan imprisoned in Agra Fort till death (1666).
  • Dara Shikoh: translated 52 Upanishads as Sirr-i-Akbar; wrote Majma-ul-Bahrain ("mingling of two oceans").

I22.3 Aurangzeb (1658–1707)

Religious policy

  • Ended jharokha darshan, Nauroz, court music (though music continued privately); banned new temples, ordered destruction of some (Vishvanath, Banaras; Keshava Rai, Mathura, 1669–70); appointed muhtasibs.
  • Jizya reimposed (1679).
  • Compiled Fatawa-i-Alamgiri (Hanafi law).
  • Yet: Hindus in the nobility rose in number (Athar Ali: about 31.6% of high mansabdars, 1679–1707, mainly due to Marathas); grants to some temples continued.

Revolts and wars

  • Jats (Gokula, 1669; Rajaram, Churaman); Satnamis (Narnaul, 1672); Sikhs (Guru Tegh Bahadur executed 1675; Guru Gobind Singh — Khalsa 1699); Afghans (Yusufzai).
  • Ahoms (Assam): Mir Jumla defeated them in 1662–63, but Mughal control did not last; the Ahoms won at Saraighat (1671) and rebelled again in the 1680s. The Ahom state, founded by migrants from Myanmar in the 13th century, ran on the paik system (rotational labour and military service, based on a census), used firearms from the 1530s, and kept chronicles called buranjis, first in Ahom and later in Assamese.
  • Rajputs: on Jaswant Singh's death (1678) Marwar was occupied; Durgadas Rathore defended infant Ajit Singh; Mewar (Raj Singh) joined; Prince Akbar's revolt (1681) — R09.
  • Deccan (1681–1707): annexed Bijapur (1686) and Golconda (1687); captured and executed Sambhaji (1689); but Maratha resistance under Rajaram and Tarabai continued; the war drained the treasury and the emperor died at Ahmednagar (1707).
Debate

Was Aurangzeb a religious bigot who ruined the empire (J.N. Sarkar), or a ruler whose policies were politically motivated and whose problems were structural (Satish Chandra, Athar Ali)? Audrey Truschke argues his temple destructions were few and political. Present both; avoid moral verdicts.

I22.4 The later Mughals and external invasions

  • Bahadur Shah I (1707–12): conciliatory; called "Shah-i-Bekhabar" by Khafi Khan.
  • Jahandar Shah (1712–13): ijara (revenue farming) spread.
  • Farrukhsiyar (1713–19): puppet of the Sayyid brothers (Abdullah and Husain Ali — "kingmakers"); farman of 1717 gave the English duty-free trade in Bengal; abolished jizya again (by the Sayyids, 1713).
  • Muhammad Shah (1719–48): Sayyids overthrown (1720); provinces broke away — Hyderabad (Nizam-ul-Mulk, 1724), Bengal (Murshid Quli Khan), Awadh (Saadat Khan).
  • Court factions: nobles split into Iranis and Turanis, and later emperors became their puppets: Farrukhsiyar (1713–19) and Alamgir II (1754–59) were killed; Ahmad Shah (1748–54) and Shah Alam II were blinded.

The successor states (NCERT's three groups)

  1. Old Mughal provinces — Awadh, Bengal, Hyderabad — founded by high nobles who kept formal ties with the emperor.
    • Hyderabad: Nizam-ul-Mulk Asaf Jah, governor of the Deccan, controlled its administration by 1720–22 and ruled independently from 1724; he fought the Marathas and Telugu nayakas, and the British checked him on the Coromandel coast.
    • Awadh: Burhan-ul-Mulk Saadat Khan (from 1722) held subadari, diwani and faujdari together; he cut the number and size of Mughal jagirs, reassessed revenue, and took Rajput zamindaris and Rohilkhand lands.
    • Bengal: Murshid Quli Khan, first the deputy (naib) and diwan, moved Mughal jagirdars to Orissa, reassessed revenue and collected it strictly in cash, so many zamindars borrowed or sold land; under Alivardi Khan (1740–56) the banking house of Jagat Seth prospered.
    • Common features: distrust of the jagir system; revenue farming (ijara), with bankers guaranteeing the revenue farmers' payments, which brought bankers and moneylenders into state finance.
  2. Watan jagirs — the Rajput states (Amber, Jodhpur), which already had wide autonomy (see R11).
  3. States won by armed struggle — Marathas, Sikhs, Jats.
  • Nadir Shah (Persia) defeated the Mughals at Karnal (1739), sacked Delhi, took the Peacock Throne and Koh-i-Noor.
  • Ahmad Shah Abdali invaded repeatedly (1748–67); third Panipat (1761) against the Marathas (I24).
  • Shah Alam II — Buxar (1764), Allahabad treaty (1765); under Maratha then British protection; Bahadur Shah Zafar — last emperor (1857).

I22.5 Causes of decline: the debate

SchoolArgumentHistorians
Personal / religious policyAurangzeb's orthodoxy alienated Rajputs, Marathas, Sikhs and Jats; weak successorsJadunath Sarkar, Stanley Lane-Poole
Jagirdari crisisToo many mansabdars for too few productive jagirs (be-jagiri), especially after Deccan nobles were absorbed; nobles fought for good jagirs; factionalism (Irani, Turani, Hindustani)Satish Chandra
Agrarian crisisExcessive revenue demand and jagir transfers led jagirdars to squeeze peasants; peasant flight and revolts led by zamindars (Jats, Satnamis, Marathas)Irfan Habib
Nobility and politicsInclusion of Deccani and Maratha nobles broke the cohesion of the ruling classM. Athar Ali
Revisionist / regionalNot "decline" but decentralisation: regional economies prospered; regional elites (zamindars, bankers, nobles) built successor statesMuzaffar Alam, C.A. Bayly, Richard Barnett, Chetan Singh
ExternalNadir Shah, Abdali; the European companies—

Synthesis for answers: structural strains (jagir crisis, agrarian pressure, factions) + Aurangzeb's long Deccan wars and alienation of key groups + weak successors and court factions + external blows = rapid decentralisation into regional states, which the British exploited.

What historians say

See the decline table. Sources: Khafi Khan (Muntakhab-ul-Lubab), Saqi Mustaid Khan (Maasir-i-Alamgiri), Bhimsen (Nuskha-i-Dilkusha); official histories: Abdul Hamid Lahori's Badshahnama (first two decades of Shah Jahan, revised by the wazir Sadullah Khan; Muhammad Waris continued it), and Muhammad Kazim's Alamgirnama (Aurangzeb's first ten years). An illustrated Badshahnama, gifted by the Nawab of Awadh to George III in 1799, is in the Royal Collection at Windsor; European travellers Bernier (who argued there was no private property in land — a view later used by Marx's "Asiatic mode"), Tavernier, Manucci.

Exam traps
  • Shah Alam II reigned 1759–1806 (blinded 1788), not to 1816.
  • Ahmadnagar: NCERT Class VII gives 1632 and Daulatabad fell in 1633; the Nizam Shahi state was finally ended in 1636.
  • NCERT Class XII says Aurangzeb gave the title Mirza Raja to Jai Singh and Jaswant Singh; Jai Singh I is usually said to have received it from Shah Jahan (our R11).
  • Dharmat (1658) before Samugarh (1658); Deorai (1659) ended Dara.
  • Jizya: abolished by Akbar (1564), reimposed by Aurangzeb (1679), abolished again (1713/1720).
  • Karnal (1739) — Nadir Shah; Panipat III (1761) — Abdali.
  • Satish Chandra = jagirdari crisis; Irfan Habib = agrarian crisis; Athar Ali = nobility.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Discuss the causes of the decline of the Mughal empire.

How to frame it: Aurangzeb → Jagir crisis → Agrarian crisis → Successors → External → Revisionist view

Model answer (88 words)

Jadunath Sarkar blamed Aurangzeb's orthodoxy for alienating Rajputs, Marathas, Sikhs and Jats; his long Deccan wars drained resources. Satish Chandra identified a jagirdari crisis — too many mansabdars for too few productive jagirs — which bred factionalism. Irfan Habib stressed an agrarian crisis: heavy demands and jagir transfers drove peasants and zamindars to revolt. Weak successors, the Sayyid brothers and court factions followed, and Nadir Shah's sack (1739) exposed the empire. Revisionists such as Muzaffar Alam and C.A. Bayly see decentralisation into prosperous regional states rather than collapse.

5 marks · 50 words

What was the war of succession of 1657–58?

How to frame it: Contestants → Battles → Result

Model answer (43 words)

When Shah Jahan fell ill, his sons Dara, Shuja, Aurangzeb and Murad fought for the throne. Aurangzeb and Murad won at Dharmat and Samugarh; Shuja lost at Khajwa and Dara at Deorai (1659). Aurangzeb imprisoned Shah Jahan, killed his brothers and became emperor.

2 marks · 15 words

Who were the Sayyid brothers?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (10 words)

Abdullah and Husain Ali, "kingmakers" who controlled Mughal emperors, 1713–1720.

  • Shah Jahan: Ahmadnagar 1636, Taj, Shahjahanabad, Kandahar 1649, Peacock Throne; succession war 1657–59.
  • Aurangzeb: temples, jizya 1679, Jats, Satnamis, Sikhs, Rathore war, Deccan 1681–1707.
  • Later: Sayyids, farman 1717, Hyderabad 1724, Karnal 1739, Panipat 1761.
  • Decline: Sarkar (policy), Chandra (jagir), Habib (agrarian), Athar Ali (nobility), Alam/Bayly (regional).
