Part II · Unit I23Mughal Administration: Mansab, Jagir and Revenue1556 – 1707

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Mansabdari system (zat, sawar); jagirdari; zabt / dahsala; central and provincial officers; Mughal architecture and painting

Mains: GS-I: Mughal administration and economy; art and architecture

Mansabdari and Todar Mal's dahsala system are among the most-asked medieval Mains topics.

The big picture

Akbar created an administrative structure that lasted, in parts, into British times. Its three pillars were: the mansabdari system (ranks for nobles and officers, defining pay and troops), the jagirdari system (assignment of revenue in lieu of cash salary) and the zabt land revenue system based on measurement and average yields (Todar Mal's dahsala). The emperor stood at the top as a divinely illumined ruler; ministers and provincial governors were transferable officials, not hereditary lords.

Exam focus

Know the meaning of zat and sawar, how the mansab linked to the jagir, and how the dahsala was computed.

WhenWhat happened
1573–74Mansabdari system organised; dagh revived
1574Karori experiment (karoris in north India)
1580Dahsala (ain-i-dahsala) system introduced
1580Empire divided into 12 subas
c. 1595Sawar rank introduced (date debated)
1595–1602Ain-i-Akbari completed

Core notes

I23.1 Central administration

OfficerFunction
VakilPrime minister (powerful under Bairam Khan; reduced by Akbar)
Wazir / Diwan-i-KulFinance and revenue
Mir BakhshiMilitary department; mansabdars' records, intelligence
Mir Saman (Khan-i-Saman)Royal household and karkhanas (workshops)
Sadr-us-SudurReligious affairs, charity, grants (suyurghal / madad-i-maash)
Qazi-ul-QuzatChief justice
MuhtasibPublic morals (prominent under Aurangzeb)

Provinces (subas): 12 under Akbar (later 15; 21 under Aurangzeb). Officers: subedar (governor), diwan (revenue — reporting to the central diwan, a check on the subedar), bakhshi, sadr, qazi, kotwal (towns), waqia-navis (news writer).

Below: sarkar (faujdar — law and order; amalguzar — revenue), pargana (shiqdar, amil, qanungo — record keeper), village (muqaddam, patwari).

I23.2 The mansabdari system

  • Every officer held a mansab (rank) expressed in numbers, from 10 to 10,000 (princes higher; later raised).
  • Zat: personal rank — determined status and personal salary.
  • Sawar: number of cavalrymen (and horses) the officer had to maintain.
  • Three classes by sawar relative to zat: first class (sawar = zat), second (sawar at least half of zat), third (sawar less than half).
  • Mansabdars were paid in cash (naqdi) or, more often, by jagir.
  • Dagh (branding) and chehra (descriptive roll) checked fraud.
  • Appointment, promotion and dismissal by the emperor; not hereditary — property escheated (zabti) at death.
  • Later rules: du-aspa sih-aspa (Jahangir — extra troopers without raising zat); month scales (Shah Jahan — jagir yield calculated as fraction of a year).
  • Akbar's nobility was a mix of Turanis, Iranis, Afghans, Indian Muslims (Shaikhzadas) and Rajputs.
Debate

Was the mansabdari system derived from Mongol decimal organisation, or an original Akbari system? Most historians see Central Asian roots but an Akbari synthesis. The date of the sawar rank (1595 vs earlier) is debated (A.J. Qaisar, Shireen Moosvi).

I23.3 The jagirdari system

  • Jagir: assignment of the right to collect revenue from a territory in lieu of salary — not ownership of land.
  • Types: tankha jagir (in lieu of salary), mashrut (conditional on office), watan jagir (hereditary homeland of zamindars/Rajput chiefs, non-transferable), inam (free of obligations).
  • Jagirdars were transferred every few years to prevent local roots; this tempted them to squeeze peasants.
  • Land not assigned was khalisa — revenue went directly to the treasury (Akbar about one-fourth to one-fifth).
  • Crisis under Aurangzeb: more mansabdars than available jagirs (be-jagiri).

I23.4 Land revenue: from zabt to dahsala

  1. Sher Shah: measurement, one-third of produce, schedule of crop rates (rai).
  2. Akbar's early experiments: annual assessment; then karoris (1574) in charge of areas yielding one crore dams.
  3. Dahsala / Ain-i-Dahsala (1580), devised by Raja Todar Mal:
    • Land measured with the bamboo jarib (iron-linked) in bighas (ilahi gaz).
    • Average produce of each crop over the previous ten years calculated; state share one-third.
    • Converted into cash using average prices — dastur-ul-amal (cash rates) for each revenue circle (dastur).
    • Applied to the core provinces (Lahore to Allahabad, Malwa, Gujarat).
  4. Land classification by continuity of cultivation: polaj (cultivated every year), parauti (left fallow briefly), chachar (fallow 3–4 years), banjar (fallow 5+ years).
  5. Other methods: batai / ghalla-bakhshi (crop-sharing), kankut (estimation), nasaq (based on past records; in Bengal).
  6. Remissions in famine; taqavi loans for seed and cattle.

Zamindars — hereditary holders of rights over land revenue — collected revenue and kept a share (nankar/malikana); ranged from autonomous chiefs to small intermediaries.

I23.5 Economy and culture (summary)

  • Banjaras, the leading trader-nomads, moved grain in caravans (tanda) of bullocks: Alauddin used them to supply Delhi's markets, Jahangir described them, and they fed Mughal armies on campaign.
  • Agriculture: cash crops (cotton, indigo, sugarcane); new crops from the Americas (tobacco, maize, chilli, potato).
  • Silver flowed in through trade; rupee standard; banking (sarrafs, hundis).
  • Karkhanas (royal workshops); textiles exported (Gujarat, Bengal, Coromandel).
  • Architecture: Humayun's tomb (Persian charbagh, double dome), Fatehpur Sikri (Buland Darwaza, Panch Mahal, Diwan-i-Khas), Agra Fort; Sikandra (Akbar's tomb); Itimad-ud-Daula (pietra dura); Taj Mahal; Red Fort; Badshahi Mosque (Lahore); Bibi ka Maqbara (Aurangabad).
  • Painting: Mir Sayyid Ali and Abdus Samad (Persian masters under Humayun); Hamzanama; Daswant, Basawan under Akbar; peak under Jahangir (Mansur, Abul Hasan, Bishandas); decline under Aurangzeb led to provincial (Rajput, Pahari) schools.

I23.6 Agrarian society: peasants, villages and zamindars

Sources: the Ain-i-Akbari (a view from the top), revenue records from Gujarat, Maharashtra and Rajasthan, and East India Company records for eastern India.

  • Peasants (raiyat, plural riaya; also muzarian, kisan, asami) were of two kinds: khud-kashta, who lived in the village where they held land, and pahi-kashta, who cultivated in other villages on contract, by choice (better terms) or under distress (famine).
  • Crops: two seasons, kharif and rabi; most areas grew at least two crops a year (do-fasla). The Ain lists 39 crops for Agra, 43 for Delhi and 50 varieties of rice for Bengal. The state encouraged jins-i-kamil ("perfect crops") such as cotton and sugarcane, which paid more revenue. The Shah Nahr canal in Punjab was repaired under Shah Jahan.
  • Village: the panchayat of elders was headed by a muqaddam or mandal; each caste also had a jati panchayat, which in Rajasthan settled civil disputes, land claims and marriage questions. Records from Rajasthan and Maharashtra preserve petitions against excess taxes and forced labour (begar).
  • Artisans made up as much as a quarter of village households in Marathi records; in Maharashtra the land given to them became their miras or watan. Payment in goods for services was later called the jajmani system. The British picture of the village as a "little republic" hid sharp caste and gender inequality.
  • Women sowed, weeded, threshed and spun; peasant and artisan communities paid bride-price and allowed widows and divorced women to remarry; women petitioned panchayats; in Bengal there were women zamindars.
  • Forests: forest people lived by gathering, hunting and shifting cultivation, e.g. the Bhils' seasonal round. For the state the forest was a refuge (mawas) for rebels; peshkash from forest chiefs often included elephants. The Ahom kings used paiks, who did military service in return for land.
  • Zamindars held personal land (milkiyat) that they could sell or mortgage, did service (khidmat) for the state, kept forts (qilachas) and armed men, settled new land and set up markets (haats). Caste lineages built zamindaris, e.g. Rajputs and Jats in the north, Sadgops in Bengal.
  • Revenue terms: jama was the amount assessed, hasil the amount collected; the amil-guzar (amalguzar) collected it, in cash by preference.

I23.7 The court, nobility and information

  • The Ain-i-Akbari is the third book of Abul Fazl's Akbarnama. It has five daftars: manzil-abadi (household), sipah-abadi (army and civil administration, with notes on mansabdars and learned men), mulk-abadi (revenue and the "Account of the Twelve Provinces": sarkars, parganas, measured area, jama, suyurghal, zamindars' castes); the last two cover religion, literature and Akbar's sayings.
  • Sovereignty as a contract (Abul Fazl): the emperor protects his subjects' life (jan), property (mal), honour (namus) and faith (din) in return for obedience and a share of resources. The lion lying beside the lamb was its visual symbol.
  • Court ritual: status was shown by nearness to the throne and the depth of the salutation (sijda the deepest; NCERT says Shah Jahan replaced it with chahar taslim and zaminbos). Akbar began jharoka darshan; then came the diwan-i-am (public business) and the diwan-i-khas (private audience).
  • Honours: the khilat (a robe once worn by the emperor), the sarapa (head-to-foot set), and the jewelled lotus (padma murassa) in rare cases. A courtier offered a small sum (nazr) or a large one (peshkash).
  • Nobility: Turanis and Iranis first, then Rajputs and Indian Muslims (Shaikhzadas) from the 1560s; Iranians rose under Nur Jahan; Marathas were numerous under Aurangzeb. Holders of 1,000 zat or more were umara. Akbar had 29 mansabdars of 5,000 zat; Aurangzeb had 79. A candidate was recommended through a noble's tajwiz; nobles posted at court (tainat-i-rakab) formed a reserve.
  • Information: court writers (waqia-navis) recorded proceedings; nobles' agents sent home the akhbarat-i-darbar-i-mualla (news of the exalted court); relays of runners (qasid, pathmar) carried papers in bamboo tubes.
  • Pargana level: three semi-hereditary officers — the qanungo (revenue records), the chaudhuri (collection) and the qazi. Persian was the language of administration, but village accounts were kept in local languages.

What historians say

The Ain-i-Akbari (Abul Fazl) is the basic source. W.H. Moreland (The Agrarian System of Moslem India), Irfan Habib (The Agrarian System of Mughal India), Shireen Moosvi (The Economy of the Mughal Empire c. 1595) and M. Athar Ali are standard. J.F. Richards argued the system was centralised; revisionists such as Frank Perlin and André Wink (fitna — negotiated sovereignty) stress negotiation with local power holders.

Prelims fact bank

  • Zat denoted personal rank and salary; sawar denoted cavalry to be maintained.
  • Mansabs ranged from 10 to 10,000 under Akbar.
  • Jahangir introduced the du-aspa sih-aspa rule.
  • Khalisa land's revenue went directly to the imperial treasury.
  • Watan jagirs were hereditary and non-transferable.
  • The dahsala system was introduced in 1580 by Raja Todar Mal.
  • Dahsala used the average produce and prices of the previous ten years.
  • Polaj land was cultivated every year; banjar was fallow for five or more years.
  • The Mir Bakhshi headed the military department.
  • The Mir Saman was in charge of the royal household and karkhanas.
  • Under Akbar the empire was divided into 12 subas in 1580.
  • Taqavi loans were given to peasants for seed and cattle.
  • Ustad Mansur was a painter of birds and animals under Jahangir.
  • Khud-kashta peasants lived in the village where they held land; pahi-kashta peasants cultivated elsewhere on contract.
  • Jins-i-kamil ('perfect crops') such as cotton and sugarcane paid more revenue.
  • Jama was the revenue assessed; hasil the revenue collected.
  • Milkiyat was a zamindar's personal land, which could be sold or mortgaged.
  • The Ain-i-Akbari is the third book of the Akbarnama and has five daftars.
  • Abul Fazl described sovereignty as protection of jan, mal, namus and din.
  • The akhbarat-i-darbar-i-mualla were newsletters of court proceedings.
Exam traps
  • Zat ≠ sawar; mansab ≠ jagir (rank vs revenue assignment).
  • Jagir = right to revenue, not land ownership.
  • Dahsala is not a ten-year settlement; it is assessment based on ten-year averages.
  • Mir Bakhshi (military) ≠ Mir Saman (household) ≠ Diwan (finance).
  • Khud-kashta = resident cultivator; pahi-kashta = non-resident, contract cultivator.
  • Nazr = small offering; peshkash = large offering (also tribute from chiefs).
  • Du-aspa sih-aspa: Jahangir; month scales: Shah Jahan.

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Explain the mansabdari system of the Mughals.

How to frame it: Meaning → Zat and sawar → Payment → Controls → Later changes → Significance

Model answer (92 words)

Akbar gave every noble and officer a mansab, a numerical rank from 10 to 10,000. Zat fixed personal status and salary; sawar fixed the cavalry to be maintained, with three classes according to the zat–sawar ratio. Mansabdars were paid in cash or, usually, by transferable jagirs. Dagh and chehra prevented fraud; appointment and promotion lay with the emperor, and estates escheated at death. Jahangir added du-aspa sih-aspa; Shah Jahan used month scales. The system bound a multi-ethnic nobility to the throne, but by Aurangzeb's time a shortage of jagirs created a crisis.

5 marks · 50 words

Describe Todar Mal's dahsala system.

How to frame it: Measurement → Average → Cash rates → Classification

Model answer (50 words)

Introduced in 1580, it measured land with the bamboo jarib, calculated each crop's average produce over ten years and fixed one-third as the state share, converted into cash rates (dastur-ul-amal) for each revenue circle. Land was classed as polaj, parauti, chachar and banjar. It gave certainty to peasants and state.

2 marks · 15 words

What was a watan jagir?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Hereditary, non-transferable homeland of a zamindar or Rajput chief, held as jagir.

Revision card

Remember
  • Centre: Vakil, Wazir/Diwan, Mir Bakhshi, Mir Saman, Sadr, Qazi.
  • Suba (subedar, diwan), sarkar (faujdar, amalguzar), pargana (shiqdar, qanungo).
  • Mansab: zat + sawar; dagh, chehra; du-aspa sih-aspa; month scales.
  • Jagir types: tankha, watan, mashrut, inam; khalisa.
  • Dahsala 1580: jarib, 10-year average, one-third, dastur-ul-amal; polaj/parauti/chachar/banjar.

Self-test

  1. What did zat denote?
    Show answer

    Personal rank and salary.

  2. What did sawar denote?
    Show answer

    The number of cavalry to be maintained.

  3. What was khalisa?
    Show answer

    Land whose revenue went directly to the treasury.

  4. Who devised the dahsala?
    Show answer

    Raja Todar Mal (1580).

  5. What was polaj land?
    Show answer

    Land cultivated every year.

  6. Who headed the Mughal military department?
    Show answer

    The Mir Bakhshi.

  7. Who introduced du-aspa sih-aspa?
    Show answer

    Jahangir.

  8. What were taqavi loans?
    Show answer

    State loans to peasants for seed and cattle.

Sources: Irfan Habib, The Agrarian System of Mughal India.; Shireen Moosvi, The Economy of the Mughal Empire c. 1595.; Satish Chandra, Medieval India, vol. II.; NCERT Class 12, Themes in Indian History II, ch. 8 ("Peasants, Zamindars and the State").

Part II · Unit I24The Marathas1630 – 1818

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Shivaji — conquests, administration (Ashtapradhan, chauth, sardeshmukhi); Peshwas; third battle of Panipat; Maratha confederacy

Mains: GS-I: Rise of the Marathas; Shivaji's administration; Panipat III

Shivaji's administration and Panipat III are classic questions. Maratha intervention in Rajasthan (chauth, Holkar–Scindia) is in R15.

The big picture

The Marathas rose in the western Deccan, in the hilly Maval region, where peasant-soldiers and local landholders (deshmukhs) had served the Deccan Sultanates. Shivaji (1630–1680) built an independent kingdom (crowned 1674) by guerrilla warfare against Bijapur and the Mughals. After his son Sambhaji's execution, the Marathas resisted Aurangzeb for two decades. In the 18th century, under the Peshwas, they became the strongest power in India, collecting chauth from Rajasthan to Bengal, until the defeat at Panipat (1761) and three wars with the British (1775–1818).

Exam focus

Explain both Shivaji's state-building and why the Marathas, despite their power, failed to replace the Mughals or resist the British.

WhenWhat happened
1630Birth of Shivaji at Shivneri
1646Capture of Torna
1659Afzal Khan killed at Pratapgarh
1663Night attack on Shaista Khan at Pune
1664First sack of Surat
1665Treaty of Purandar with Jai Singh
1666Shivaji at Agra; escape
1670Second sack of Surat
1674Coronation at Raigad
1677–78Karnataka campaign (Jinji, Vellore)
1680Death of Shivaji
1689Sambhaji executed
1700–07Tarabai leads resistance
1707Shahu released; civil war with Tarabai
1713Balaji Vishwanath made Peshwa
1719Mughal grant of chauth and sardeshmukhi of the Deccan
1720–1740Baji Rao I
1728Battle of Palkhed (Nizam defeated)
1737Raid on Delhi
1738Bhopal: Nizam defeated
1740–1761Balaji Baji Rao (Nana Saheb)
1750Sangola agreement: Peshwa supreme
1761Third battle of Panipat
1761–1772Madhav Rao I restores power
1775–82First Anglo-Maratha War; Treaty of Salbai
1802Treaty of Bassein
1803–05Second Anglo-Maratha War
1817–18Third Anglo-Maratha War; Peshwaship abolished

Core notes

I24.1 Rise: factors

  • Geography: Sahyadri hills and forts suited guerrilla warfare.
  • Experience of Maratha chiefs in Bijapur and Ahmadnagar service (Shahji Bhonsle, Shivaji's father).
  • Bhakti movement (Tukaram, Ramdas) created regional identity in Marathi.
  • Decline of Ahmadnagar and Bijapur; Mughal overstretch.
  • Leadership of Shivaji; mother Jijabai and guardian Dadaji Kondadev.

I24.2 Shivaji's career

  • Forts captured from Bijapur: Torna, Raigad, Kondana, Purandar.
  • Afzal Khan killed at Pratapgarh (1659).
  • Night attack on Mughal governor Shaista Khan at Pune (1663); sack of Surat (1664, 1670).
  • Treaty of Purandar (1665) with Raja Jai Singh of Amber: surrendered 23 of 35 forts; agreed to serve the Mughals.
  • Visit to Agra (1666): placed among lower-rank mansabdars; house arrest; escape.
  • Recovered forts; coronation at Raigad (1674) by Gaga Bhatt; title Chhatrapati, Haindava Dharmoddharak.
  • Karnataka expedition (1677–78): Jinji, Vellore.
  • Built a navy (Sindhudurg, Vijaydurg).

I24.3 Shivaji's administration

Ashtapradhan (council of eight ministers) — advisory, not a cabinet; posts were not hereditary:

MinisterFunction
Peshwa (Mukhya Pradhan)Prime minister, general administration
Amatya (Majumdar)Finance, accounts
Mantri (Waqia-navis)Royal records, correspondence of daily affairs
Sachiv (Surunavis)Royal correspondence
Sumant (Dabir)Foreign affairs
Senapati (Sar-i-Naubat)Army
PanditraoReligious affairs, charity
NyayadhishJustice
  • All except the Panditrao and Nyayadhish held military commands.
  • Revenue: land measured with the kathi; revenue initially two-fifths (after cesses were abolished), on the model of Malik Ambar; officials paid in cash; discouraged jagirs (mokasas) and curbed deshmukhs.
  • Chauth (one-fourth of revenue) and sardeshmukhi (additional one-tenth) levied on neighbouring territories in return for protection from raids.
  • Army: cavalry (bargirs — equipped by the state; shiledars — self-equipped), infantry (Mavalis), forts under three officers (havaldar, sabnis, sarnobat) of different castes as a check.
  • Provinces (prants) under subedars.

I24.4 After Shivaji: resistance to Aurangzeb

  • Sambhaji (1680–89): sheltered Prince Akbar; captured and executed (1689).
  • Rajaram (1689–1700): fled to Jinji; Maratha chiefs (Santaji Ghorpade, Dhanaji Jadhav) fought guerrilla war.
  • Tarabai (1700–07): regent for her son Shivaji II; carried war into Mughal territory.
  • Release of Shahu (1707) by the Mughals caused civil war with Tarabai; battle of Khed (1707); two branches — Satara (Shahu) and Kolhapur.

I24.5 The Peshwas

PeshwaAchievements
Balaji Vishwanath (1713–20)Secured Shahu's position; deal with the Sayyid brothers — Mughal sanad of 1719 recognised Shahu and granted chauth and sardeshmukhi of the six Deccan provinces
Baji Rao I (1720–40)Policy of northern expansion ("strike at the trunk, the branches will fall"); Palkhed (1728) — Nizam defeated; Malwa, Bundelkhand (help to Chhatrasal; Mastani); raid on Delhi (1737); Bhopal (1738); Bassein from the Portuguese (1739, by Chimaji Appa)
Balaji Baji Rao (Nana Saheb, 1740–61)Sangola agreement (1750) made the Peshwa the real power; Maratha power to Punjab, Rajasthan, Bengal, Odisha; defeat at Panipat
Madhav Rao I (1761–72)Restored Maratha power; Mahadji Scindia restored Shah Alam II to Delhi (1771)
Narayan Rao (murdered 1773); Raghunath Rao; Nana Phadnavis (regent, Barbhai council)First Anglo-Maratha War
Baji Rao II (1796–1818)Treaty of Bassein (1802); last Peshwa; pensioned at Bithur

Maratha rule and the economy: areas raided were usually made to pay tribute rather than annexed; once rule was secure, revenue was assessed to local conditions. Cities did well: Ujjain under Sindhia and Indore under Holkar; Chanderi silk found a market in Poona; Burhanpur's trade reached Poona, Nagpur, Lucknow and Allahabad.

Maratha confederacy: Peshwa (Pune), Bhonsles (Nagpur), Gaekwads (Baroda), Holkars (Indore), Scindias (Gwalior).

I24.6 Third battle of Panipat (14 January 1761)

  • Cause: Maratha expansion into Punjab (1758, Raghunath Rao) clashed with Ahmad Shah Abdali, who had claims there; Rohillas (Najib-ud-Daula) and Shuja-ud-Daula of Awadh allied with Abdali.
  • Maratha army: led by Sadashiv Rao Bhau, with Vishwas Rao (Peshwa's son); Ibrahim Khan Gardi's artillery.
  • Causes of defeat: no allies (Jats under Suraj Mal, Rajputs and Sikhs alienated by Maratha demands); supply lines cut, army starving; non-combatants and pilgrims slowed the army; Bhau abandoned guerrilla tactics; Abdali's superior cavalry and leadership.
  • Results: Bhau and Vishwas Rao killed; the Peshwa died soon after; Maratha northern expansion checked for a decade; Abdali gained little; the British, free of rivals, consolidated in Bengal.

I24.7 Why the Marathas fell

  1. Confederacy loose; rivalry among Scindia, Holkar, Bhonsle and the Peshwa.
  2. Chauth-based raiding alienated other Indian powers.
  3. Weak economy and administration in conquered areas.
  4. Outdated military: reliance on cavalry; artillery dependent on foreign officers.
  5. British diplomacy (Subsidiary Alliance, Bassein) and superior organisation.

What historians say

G.S. Sardesai (New History of the Marathas) and Jadunath Sarkar (Shivaji and His Times) are classic. M.G. Ranade (Rise of the Maratha Power) saw the rise as a national movement shaped by the bhakti saints. André Wink (Land and Sovereignty in India) explained Maratha expansion through fitna (negotiated sedition). Stewart Gordon (The Marathas 1600–1818) stresses continuity with Deccan Sultanate institutions.

Debate

Was Shivaji's state a "Hindu" national kingdom (Ranade, Sarkar) or a regional state built on Deccani institutions and local landholders (Gordon, Wink)? Note Muslim soldiers and officers in his service and his use of Persianate administrative practices.

Prelims fact bank

  • Shivaji was born at Shivneri in 1630.
  • Afzal Khan was killed at Pratapgarh in 1659.
  • Shivaji sacked Surat in 1664 and 1670.
  • The Treaty of Purandar (1665) was signed with Raja Jai Singh.
  • Shivaji was crowned at Raigad in 1674.
  • The Ashtapradhan was Shivaji's council of eight ministers.
  • Chauth was one-fourth of revenue; sardeshmukhi an additional one-tenth.
  • Sambhaji was executed by Aurangzeb in 1689.
  • Rajaram moved to Jinji.
  • Balaji Vishwanath obtained the Mughal sanad of 1719.
  • Baji Rao I defeated the Nizam at Palkhed in 1728.
  • The Sangola agreement (1750) made the Peshwa supreme.
  • The third battle of Panipat was fought on 14 January 1761.
  • Sadashiv Rao Bhau led the Marathas at Panipat in 1761.
  • The Gaekwads ruled Baroda; the Holkars Indore; the Scindias Gwalior; the Bhonsles Nagpur.
  • Mobile peasant-pastoralists (kunbis) formed the backbone of Shivaji's army.
  • Ujjain grew under Sindhia's patronage and Indore under Holkar's.
Exam traps
  • Ashtapradhan was advisory, not hereditary under Shivaji (became hereditary later).
  • Purandar (1665) — Shivaji and Jai Singh; Purandar (1776) — British and Marathas.
  • Panipat III leader: Sadashiv Rao Bhau, not the Peshwa.
  • Bargirs were state-equipped; shiledars self-equipped.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Describe the administrative system of Shivaji.

How to frame it: Ashtapradhan → Revenue → Chauth → Army → Forts → Assessment

Model answer (92 words)

Shivaji ruled with an advisory Ashtapradhan of eight non-hereditary ministers headed by the Peshwa. Land was measured with the kathi and revenue assessed on Malik Ambar's model; officials were paid in cash and jagirs were discouraged to curb deshmukhs. Chauth and sardeshmukhi were levied on neighbouring areas. The army combined state-equipped bargirs, self-equipped shiledars and Mavali infantry; forts were held by three officers of different castes as a check, and a navy guarded the coast. The system was centralised and efficient, though its reliance on chauth later strained relations with other powers.

5 marks · 50 words

Why were the Marathas defeated at Panipat in 1761?

How to frame it: Isolation → Supplies → Tactics → Abdali

Model answer (45 words)

The Marathas had no allies — Jats, Rajputs and Sikhs had been alienated by their demands. Supplies were cut and the army starved; pilgrims and non-combatants slowed it. Bhau abandoned guerrilla tactics, while Abdali, allied with the Rohillas and Awadh, had superior cavalry and leadership.

2 marks · 15 words

What were chauth and sardeshmukhi?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Maratha levies: one-fourth and an additional one-tenth of revenue of other territories.

Revision card

Remember
  • Shivaji: Shivneri 1630, Pratapgarh 1659, Surat 1664/1670, Purandar 1665, Agra 1666, Raigad 1674, d. 1680.
  • Ashtapradhan; chauth 1/4, sardeshmukhi 1/10; bargir/shiledar.
  • Sambhaji 1689; Rajaram (Jinji); Tarabai; Shahu 1707.
  • Peshwas: Balaji Vishwanath (1719 sanad), Baji Rao I (Palkhed 1728), Nana Saheb (Sangola 1750, Panipat 1761), Madhav Rao.
  • Confederacy: Gaekwad, Holkar, Scindia, Bhonsle.

  5. Which Peshwa obtained the sanad of 1719?

Part II · Unit I25The Sikhs1469 – 1849

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Ten Gurus; Guru Granth Sahib; Khalsa; Banda Bahadur; misls; Ranjit Singh; Anglo-Sikh wars

Mains: GS-I: Rise of Sikh power; Ranjit Singh

Mostly Prelims (Gurus and their contributions); Ranjit Singh and the transformation of the Sikh community into a political power suit Mains.

The big picture

Sikhism began with the teachings of Guru Nanak (1469–1539), a nirguna bhakti saint (I20). Under nine successor Gurus the community developed institutions — langar, scripture, gurdwaras, the masand system — and, after clashes with the Mughals, a military identity, culminating in Guru Gobind Singh's Khalsa (1699). After Banda Bahadur's uprising and decades of struggle, Sikh confederacies (misls) controlled Punjab; Ranjit Singh united them into a kingdom (1799–1839), annexed by the British in 1849.

Exam focus

Track the change from a devotional community to a political and military power — and the reasons (Mughal persecution, Jat peasant base, collapse of Mughal authority in Punjab).

WhenWhat happened
1469Birth of Guru Nanak at Talwandi
1539Guru Angad
1552Guru Amar Das
1574Guru Ram Das (founds Ramdaspur / Amritsar)
1581Guru Arjan
1604Adi Granth compiled
1606Guru Arjan executed; Guru Hargobind
1644Guru Har Rai
1661Guru Har Krishan
1664Guru Tegh Bahadur
1675Guru Tegh Bahadur executed in Delhi
1699Khalsa founded at Anandpur (Baisakhi)
1708Death of Guru Gobind Singh at Nanded
1710Banda Bahadur takes Sirhind
1716Banda executed
1748Dal Khalsa organised
1799Ranjit Singh takes Lahore
1809Treaty of Amritsar with the British
1839Death of Ranjit Singh
1845–46First Anglo-Sikh War; Treaty of Lahore
1848–49Second Anglo-Sikh War; Punjab annexed

Core notes

I25.1 The ten Gurus

GuruKey contributions
1. Nanak (1469–1539)Founder; one God; langar, sangat; settled at Kartarpur
2. AngadGurmukhi script (standardised); collected Nanak's hymns; Khadur
3. Amar DasStrengthened langar ("pehle pangat, phir sangat"); manji system (22 seats); opposed sati and purdah; Goindwal baoli
4. Ram DasFounded Ramdaspur (Amritsar) on land granted (by tradition) by Akbar; began the tank
5. ArjanCompiled the Adi Granth (1604); built Harmandir Sahib; masand system and voluntary tithe (dasvandh); executed by Jahangir (1606)
6. HargobindMiri and piri (temporal and spiritual authority, two swords); Akal Takht; armed followers; fought Mughal forces; Kiratpur
7. Har RaiKept peace; supported Dara Shikoh
8. Har KrishanChild Guru; died of smallpox in Delhi (Bangla Sahib)
9. Tegh BahadurFounded Anandpur; defended Kashmiri Pandits; executed in Delhi by Aurangzeb (1675) — Sis Ganj gurdwara
10. Gobind SinghKhalsa (1699); five Ks; Dasam Granth; Zafarnama (letter to Aurangzeb); ended the line of personal Gurus — the Granth became the Guru; died at Nanded (1708)

I25.2 The Khalsa

  • Founded at Anandpur on Baisakhi, 1699; initiation (khande di pahul) of the Panj Pyare (five beloved).
  • Five Ks: kesh (uncut hair), kangha (comb), kara (steel bracelet), kachera (undergarment), kirpan (sword).
  • Members took the names Singh (men) and Kaur (women); rejected caste distinctions; abolished the masand system, which had become corrupt.
  • Created a disciplined community for armed resistance; Guru Gobind Singh's sons died (Chamkaur; the younger two at Sirhind).

I25.3 Banda Bahadur and the misls

  • Banda Singh Bahadur (Lachhman Das), commissioned by Guru Gobind Singh: took Sirhind (1710), struck coins in the Gurus' name, abolished zamindari in areas he held; capital at Lohgarh; captured and executed in Delhi (1716).
  • Persecution under the Mughal governors of Lahore; the Sikhs organised in bands (jathas).
  • Dal Khalsa (1748) under Jassa Singh Ahluwalia; sarbat khalsa and gurmata (collective decisions at Amritsar).
  • Rakhi system: protection in return for a share of produce (one-fifth).
  • 12 misls (e.g. Sukerchakia, Bhangi, Ahluwalia, Ramgarhia, Kanhaiya, Phulkian) controlled Punjab after Abdali's invasions.

I25.4 Ranjit Singh (1780–1839)

  • Head of the Sukerchakia misl; took Lahore (1799) — Afghan ruler Zaman Shah confirmed him as governor; proclaimed Maharaja (1801).
  • Took Amritsar (1805), Multan (1818), Kashmir (1819), Peshawar (1834); the Koh-i-Noor from Shah Shuja.
  • Treaty of Amritsar (1809) with the British (Metcalfe): Sutlej fixed as boundary; cis-Sutlej states under British protection.
  • Army: modernised on European lines with French and Italian officers (Ventura, Allard, Avitabile, Court); the Fauj-i-Khas; artillery foundry at Lahore.
  • Administration: secular; ministers included the Dogra brothers (Gulab Singh, Dhian Singh) and Fakir Azizuddin (foreign affairs); revenue through kardars; did not call himself Guru — ruled in the name of the Khalsa (Sarkar-i-Khalsa).
  • Covered the Harmandir Sahib with gold (hence "Golden Temple"). Called "Sher-i-Punjab".

I25.5 Anglo-Sikh wars and annexation

  • After 1839: weak successors (Kharak Singh, Nau Nihal Singh, Sher Singh); infant Dalip Singh with Rani Jindan; the army (Khalsa panchayats) dominant.
  • First Anglo-Sikh War (1845–46): Mudki, Ferozeshah, Aliwal, Sobraon; Treaty of Lahore (1846) — Jalandhar doab ceded; Kashmir sold to Gulab Singh (Treaty of Amritsar, 1846); British Resident at Lahore; Treaty of Bhairowal (1846) — Council of Regency under the Resident.
  • Second Anglo-Sikh War (1848–49): revolt of Mulraj at Multan; Chillianwala, Gujrat; Punjab annexed (1849) by Lord Dalhousie; Dalip Singh pensioned and sent to England.

What historians say

J.S. Grewal (The Sikhs of the Punjab, New Cambridge History) and Khushwant Singh (A History of the Sikhs) are standard. W.H. McLeod analysed the growth of Sikh identity; Harjot Oberoi (The Construction of Religious Boundaries) argued that sharp Sikh identity markers were consolidated in the 19th century (Singh Sabha). Irfan Habib linked the Khalsa's rise to the Jat peasantry's agrarian grievances.

Prelims fact bank

  • Guru Angad standardised the Gurmukhi script.
  • Guru Amar Das introduced the manji system.
  • Guru Ram Das founded Ramdaspur (Amritsar).
  • Guru Arjan compiled the Adi Granth in 1604.
  • Guru Arjan was executed by Jahangir in 1606.
  • Guru Hargobind built the Akal Takht and adopted miri and piri.
  • Guru Tegh Bahadur was executed by Aurangzeb in 1675.
  • Guru Gobind Singh founded the Khalsa at Anandpur in 1699.
  • Guru Gobind Singh died at Nanded in 1708.
  • Banda Bahadur captured Sirhind in 1710 and was executed in 1716.
  • Guru Nanak named his follower Lehna as successor (Guru Angad), who wrote Nanak's compositions in Gurmukhi.
  • Guru Gobind Singh authenticated the scripture in 1706, adding Guru Tegh Bahadur's hymns; it became the Guru Granth Sahib.
  • Under the rakhi system, cultivators paid the Sikh misls about a fifth of their produce for protection.
  • The Khalsa struck its own coin again in 1765, with the inscription used in Banda's time.
  • Ranjit Singh belonged to the Sukerchakia misl.
  • Ranjit Singh took Lahore in 1799.
  • The Treaty of Amritsar (1809) fixed the Sutlej as the boundary with the British.
  • Punjab was annexed by Lord Dalhousie in 1849.
Exam traps
  • Adi Granth compiled by Guru Arjan (1604); Granth declared Guru by Guru Gobind Singh (1708).
  • Treaty of Amritsar 1809 (Ranjit Singh–British) ≠ Treaty of Amritsar 1846 (British–Gulab Singh, Kashmir).
  • Miri-piri: Guru Hargobind, not Gobind Singh.
  • Manji: Amar Das; masand: Arjan (abolished by Gobind Singh).

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Trace the transformation of the Sikhs into a military power.

How to frame it: Early community → Institutions → Mughal conflict → Khalsa → Banda and misls → Ranjit Singh

Model answer (90 words)

Guru Nanak founded a devotional community; his successors built institutions — Gurmukhi, langar, the manji and masand systems, Amritsar and the Adi Granth. Guru Arjan's execution (1606) led Guru Hargobind to adopt miri-piri and arm followers. After Guru Tegh Bahadur's execution (1675), Guru Gobind Singh created the Khalsa (1699) — a disciplined, casteless fighting brotherhood. Banda Bahadur's uprising and the persecution that followed produced jathas, the Dal Khalsa and twelve misls, aided by Mughal collapse and Abdali's invasions. Ranjit Singh united them into a kingdom (1799) with a modern army.

5 marks · 50 words

Assess Ranjit Singh as a ruler.

How to frame it: Conquests → Army → Administration → Limits

Model answer (51 words)

Ranjit Singh united the misls, took Lahore, Multan, Kashmir and Peshawar, and built a European-trained army. His administration was secular, employing Dogras and Fakir Azizuddin. But the Treaty of Amritsar (1809) conceded the cis-Sutlej region, and power rested on his person — the kingdom fell within ten years of his death.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the miri-piri doctrine?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Guru Hargobind's combination of temporal (miri) and spiritual (piri) authority — two swords.

Revision card

Remember
  • Gurus: Nanak, Angad (Gurmukhi), Amar Das (manji), Ram Das (Amritsar), Arjan (Adi Granth 1604, d. 1606), Hargobind (miri-piri, Akal Takht), Har Rai, Har Krishan, Tegh Bahadur (d. 1675), Gobind Singh (Khalsa 1699, d. 1708).
  • Banda: Sirhind 1710, d. 1716; Dal Khalsa 1748; misls; rakhi.
  • Ranjit Singh: Lahore 1799, Amritsar treaty 1809, Multan 1818, Kashmir 1819, Peshawar 1834.
  • Anglo-Sikh wars 1845–46, 1848–49; annexation 1849.

Self-test

  1. Which Guru standardised Gurmukhi?
    Show answer

    Guru Angad.

  2. Who compiled the Adi Granth?
    Show answer

    Guru Arjan (1604).

  3. Who built the Akal Takht?
    Show answer

    Guru Hargobind.

  4. When and where was the Khalsa founded?
    Show answer

    Anandpur, Baisakhi 1699.

  5. Name the five Ks.
    Show answer

    Kesh, kangha, kara, kachera, kirpan.

  6. Which city did Banda Bahadur capture in 1710?
    Show answer

    Sirhind.

  7. What did the Treaty of Amritsar (1809) fix?
    Show answer

    The Sutlej as the British–Sikh boundary.

  8. When was Punjab annexed?
    Show answer

    1849, by Dalhousie.

Sources: J.S. Grewal, The Sikhs of the Punjab.; Khushwant Singh, A History of the Sikhs, vol. I.; Satish Chandra, Medieval India, vol. II.; Bipan Chandra, History of Modern India.

Part IIIModern India (to 1947)

17 units54 verified PYQs141 self-test questions

Part III · Unit I26The European Companies1498 – 1763

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Portuguese, Dutch, English, Danish, French companies; Carnatic wars

Mains: GS-I: Advent of Europeans; Anglo-French rivalry

Dates of arrival and factories are Prelims staples; "Why did the English succeed against the French?" is a standard Mains question.

The big picture

After Vasco da Gama reached Calicut (1498), the Portuguese dominated Indian Ocean trade through naval power and the cartaz (pass) system. In the 17th century the Dutch, English and French East India Companies — chartered joint-stock trading companies — arrived for spices, textiles, indigo and saltpetre. The Dutch concentrated on the spice islands of Indonesia; the English and French fought for supremacy in the three Carnatic wars (1746–63). English victory set the stage for territorial conquest beginning in Bengal.

Exam focus

The English–French contest is the analytical heart of this unit.

WhenWhat happened
1498Vasco da Gama reaches Calicut (Zamorin)
1505–09Francisco de Almeida, first Portuguese viceroy (blue water policy)
1510Albuquerque takes Goa from Bijapur
1600English East India Company chartered (31 December)
1602Dutch VOC formed
1608Hawkins at Jahangir's court
1612English defeat the Portuguese at Swally; Surat factory (1613)
1616Danish company
1623Amboyna massacre: English driven from the spice islands
1639Madras (Fort St George) granted
1661Bombay given to Charles II as dowry; leased to the Company (1668)
1664French company (Colbert)
1674Pondicherry founded
1690Job Charnock at Sutanuti; Calcutta (1698: three villages)
1717Farrukhsiyar's farman
1746–48First Carnatic War
1749–54Second Carnatic War
1756–63Third Carnatic War
1759Battle of Bedara: Dutch defeated by the English
1760Battle of Wandiwash

Core notes

I26.1 The Portuguese

  • Vasco da Gama reached Calicut (1498), guided from Malindi; the Zamorin (Samudri) received him.
  • Almeida: "blue water policy" — control of the sea, not land.
  • Albuquerque: took Goa (1510), Malacca (1511), Hormuz (1515); encouraged marriages with Indian women; abolished sati in Goa.
  • Cartaz system: passes for Asian ships.
  • Possessions: Goa, Daman, Diu, Salsette, Bassein, Hughli (taken by Shah Jahan, 1632).
  • Contributions: tobacco, potato, chilli, pineapple, printing press (Goa, 1556), Gothic churches.
  • Decline: religious intolerance (Inquisition, 1560), corruption, small home population, union with Spain (1580), rise of the Dutch and English. Goa freed only in 1961.

I26.2 The Dutch, English, Danes and French

CompanyKey settlementsNotes
Dutch (VOC, 1602)Masulipatnam (1605), Pulicat, Surat, Chinsura, Cochin, NagapattinamFocus on Indonesia; defeated by the English at Bedara (1759)
English (EIC, 1600)Surat (1613), Masulipatnam (1611), Madras (1639), Bombay (1668), Calcutta (1690)Fort William (Calcutta), Fort St George (Madras)
Danish (1616)Tranquebar (Tamil Nadu), Serampore (Bengal)Missionary activity at Serampore; sold to the British in 1845
French (1664)Surat (1668), Masulipatnam (1669), Pondicherry (1674), Chandernagore, Mahe, KaraikalState-controlled company; Pondicherry under Francois Martin

English privileges: Jahangir's permission (Roe, 1615); Golden Farman of Golconda (1632); Shah Shuja's nishan (1651) for Bengal trade; Farrukhsiyar's farman (1717) — duty-free trade in Bengal on payment of Rs 3,000 a year, the right to issue dastaks — called the "Magna Carta" of the Company.

I26.3 The Carnatic wars

WarEuropean linkEventsTreaty
First (1746–48)Austrian SuccessionFrench under Dupleix took Madras; battle of St Thome (Adyar) — small French force defeated the Nawab Anwaruddin's armyAix-la-Chapelle (1748): Madras returned
Second (1749–54)None (Indian succession disputes)Rival claimants in Hyderabad and Carnatic; Battle of Ambur (1749) — Anwaruddin killed; Dupleix backed Chanda Sahib and Muzaffar Jang; Robert Clive seized Arcot (1751); Muhammad Ali became Nawab of CarnaticTreaty of Pondicherry (1754); Dupleix recalled
Third (1756–63)Seven Years' WarCount de Lally; Wandiwash (1760) — Sir Eyre Coote defeated the French; Pondicherry taken (1761)Treaty of Paris (1763): French factories returned unfortified

I26.4 Why the English won

  1. Company structure: the English company was private, with commercial motives and steady finances; the French company was state-controlled and dependent on the crown.
  2. Naval supremacy of Britain — reinforcements and supplies.
  3. Bases: Bombay, Madras and Calcutta; after Plassey (1757), Bengal's revenue financed the war.
  4. Leadership: Clive, Coote, Lawrence vs. quarrels among Dupleix, La Bourdonnais, Lally.
  5. French home policy: preoccupation with continental wars in Europe; recall of Dupleix.

What historians say

Indian ports and merchants (17th century): Surat, the Mughal emporium of western trade, was called the "gate to Mecca" for its pilgrim ships; the English writer Ovington (1689) counted about a hundred ships in port at a time; its zari-bordered textiles sold in West Asia, Africa and Europe; its bankers' hundis were honoured at Cairo, Basra and Antwerp. It declined with the Mughals, Portuguese control of the sea routes and the rise of Bombay (Company headquarters from 1687). Masulipatnam on the Krishna delta drew Golconda nobles, Persian merchants, Telugu Komati Chettis and the Companies; the Qutb Shahis imposed royal monopolies, Mir Jumla played the Dutch and English against each other, and the port faded after Aurangzeb took Golconda (1687). Indian shipowners such as Mulla Abdul Ghafur and Virji Vora competed with the Companies until European naval power made Indian traders their agents. Weavers increasingly worked on Company advances, to Company designs. In Madras and Calcutta, Indian merchants and artisans lived in the "Black Town", Europeans in the fort area.

K.N. Chaudhuri (The Trading World of Asia and the English East India Company) and Om Prakash (Dutch trade) are standard. Ashin Das Gupta studied Indian merchants of Surat and their decline. Holden Furber (Rival Empires of Trade) compares the companies.

Prelims fact bank

  • The English Company's first factory on the Hugli dates to 1651; it began fortifying the settlement in 1696.
  • The Second Carnatic War opened with the Battle of Ambur (1749), in which Nawab Anwaruddin was killed.
  • Vasco da Gama reached Calicut in 1498.
  • Albuquerque captured Goa in 1510.
  • The Portuguese issued cartaz passes to Asian ships.
  • The English East India Company was chartered on 31 December 1600.
  • The Dutch VOC was formed in 1602.
  • The English established a factory at Surat in 1613.
  • Virji Vora and Mulla Abdul Ghafur were leading Indian merchants of 17th-century Surat.
  • Fort St George was built at Madras.
  • Bombay was given to Charles II as dowry in 1661.
  • Job Charnock founded the settlement at Sutanuti in 1690.
  • The French company was formed in 1664 under Colbert.
  • Farrukhsiyar's farman of 1717 is called the Magna Carta of the Company.
  • The Danes had settlements at Tranquebar and Serampore.
  • Clive seized Arcot in 1751.
  • Sir Eyre Coote defeated the French at Wandiwash in 1760.
  • The English defeated the Dutch at Bedara in 1759.
Exam traps
  • The first Dutch factory was at Masulipatnam (1605); Pulicat came in 1610.
  • Bombay was leased to the Company in 1668; NCERT says the headquarters moved there the same year, but the presidency seat moved from Surat only in 1687.
  • Order of arrival: Portuguese, Dutch, English, Danes, French (company formation: English 1600, Dutch 1602).
  • Second Carnatic War had no European war behind it.
  • Bedara (1759) — Dutch; Wandiwash (1760) — French.
  • Aix-la-Chapelle (1748), Pondicherry (1754), Paris (1763).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Why did the English succeed against the French in India?

How to frame it: Company structure → Navy → Bases and Bengal → Leadership → Home policy

Model answer (86 words)

The English company was a private commercial body with sound finances, while the French company depended on a crown preoccupied with European wars. British naval supremacy kept English armies supplied and cut French reinforcements. The English held Bombay, Madras and Calcutta, and after Plassey Bengal's revenue financed the war. Clive and Coote were able commanders, while Dupleix was recalled and Lally quarrelled with colleagues. Hence Wandiwash (1760) and the fall of Pondicherry (1761) ended French ambitions; the Treaty of Paris (1763) left them unfortified trading posts.

5 marks · 50 words

What was the significance of Farrukhsiyar's farman of 1717?

How to frame it: Terms → Consequences

Model answer (42 words)

It granted the English duty-free trade in Bengal for Rs 3,000 a year, the right to issue dastaks and to rent land around Calcutta. Misuse of dastaks for private trade drained Bengal's revenue and caused conflict with the Nawabs, leading to Plassey.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the cartaz system?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Portuguese system of passes that Asian ships had to buy to sail safely.

Revision card

Remember
  • Portuguese: 1498 Calicut, 1510 Goa, cartaz, Almeida (blue water), Albuquerque.
  • Dutch 1602 (Masulipatnam, Pulicat); English 1600 (Surat 1613, Madras 1639, Bombay 1668, Calcutta 1690); Danes 1616; French 1664 (Pondicherry 1674).
  • Farman 1717 = Magna Carta.
  • Carnatic wars: 1746–48 (St Thome), 1749–54 (Arcot), 1756–63 (Wandiwash 1760).

  4. Which farman is called the Company's Magna Carta?
    Show answer

    Farrukhsiyar's farman of 1717.

  5. Where were the Danish settlements?
    Show answer

    Tranquebar and Serampore.

  6. Who seized Arcot in 1751?
    Show answer

    Robert Clive.

  7. Which battle ended French ambitions?

    Show answer

Part III · Unit I27Bengal: Plassey to Buxar1717 – 1772

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Nawabs of Bengal; Plassey; Mir Jafar, Mir Qasim; Buxar; Treaty of Allahabad; Dual government

Mains: GS-I: Establishment of British power in Bengal

"Plassey was a conspiracy, Buxar a battle" — compare their significance. Dual government is frequently asked.

The big picture

Bengal was the richest Mughal province. Its Nawabs — Murshid Quli Khan, Alivardi Khan and Siraj-ud-Daula — were virtually independent. Disputes over the Company's misuse of trade privileges and fortification led to Plassey (1757), won by Clive mostly through conspiracy. The British then made and unmade Nawabs (Mir Jafar, Mir Qasim). Buxar (1764), a real military victory over the combined forces of Bengal, Awadh and the Mughal emperor, brought the Diwani of Bengal, Bihar and Odisha (1765) — the start of British territorial rule.

Exam focus

The analytical question is always the comparison of Plassey and Buxar and the evils of the dual government.

WhenWhat happened
1717Murshid Quli Khan: virtually independent Nawab; capital Murshidabad
1740Alivardi Khan (battle of Giria)
1742–51Maratha raids on Bengal (bargis)
1756Siraj-ud-Daula; takes Calcutta; "Black Hole" incident
1757Clive retakes Calcutta; Plassey (23 June)
1757–60Mir Jafar
1760–63Mir Qasim (capital Munger)
1764Battle of Buxar (22 October)
1765Treaty of Allahabad; Diwani granted
1765–72Dual government
1770Bengal famine
1772Warren Hastings ends dual government

Core notes

I27.1 The Nawabs and the conflict

  • Murshid Quli Khan (Diwan from 1700; Nawab 1717–27): revenue farming (ijaradari), taqavi loans; capital Murshidabad.
  • Shujauddin, Sarfaraz Khan; Alivardi Khan (1740–56): defended Bengal against Maratha raids; did not allow European fortifications.
  • Siraj-ud-Daula (1756–57): causes of conflict —
    1. Misuse of dastaks by Company servants for private trade.
    2. Fortification of Calcutta without permission.
    3. Shelter to Krishna Das (Krishna Ballabh), who had fled with treasure.
    4. The English did not send customary presents on his accession; intrigues with his rivals (Shaukat Jang, Ghasiti Begum).
  • Siraj took Kasimbazar and Calcutta (June 1756). The "Black Hole" story (Holwell) of 146 prisoners in a small room — numbers widely disputed by historians.

I27.2 Battle of Plassey (23 June 1757)

  • Clive and Admiral Watson retook Calcutta (January 1757); Treaty of Alinagar; took Chandernagore from the French.
  • Conspiracy: Mir Jafar (commander), Rai Durlabh, Jagat Seth (banker), Omichund (Amir Chand) and Yar Lutf Khan; Clive forged a treaty to deceive Omichund.
  • Most of the Nawab's army under Mir Jafar did not fight; Mir Madan and Mohan Lal fought loyally.
  • Siraj fled, was captured and killed by Miran (Mir Jafar's son).
  • Results: Mir Jafar made Nawab; the Company got the zamindari of the 24 Parganas; Clive received a jagir; huge sums paid to the Company and its officials; free trade confirmed.

I27.3 Mir Jafar, Mir Qasim and Buxar

  • Mir Jafar (1757–60) could not meet Company demands; the Dutch attempt (Bedara, 1759) failed; replaced.
  • Mir Qasim (1760–63): gave the Company Burdwan, Midnapore and Chittagong; moved the capital to Munger; reorganised army and finances; when the Company's servants abused dastaks, he abolished all internal duties for everyone (1763) — the Company saw this as an attack on its privileges; war followed (battles of Katwa, Giria, Udaynala).
  • Buxar (22 October 1764): Mir Qasim, Shuja-ud-Daula (Awadh) and Mughal emperor Shah Alam II against the Company under Hector Munro; the allies were defeated.
  • Treaty of Allahabad (1765), negotiated by Clive:
    • With Shah Alam II: Company received the Diwani (revenue rights) of Bengal, Bihar and Odisha for an annual tribute of Rs 26 lakh; the emperor got Kara and Allahabad.
    • With Shuja-ud-Daula: Awadh returned for Rs 50 lakh; Kara and Allahabad ceded (to the emperor); defensive alliance — Awadh became a buffer state.

I27.4 Plassey versus Buxar

Plassey (1757)Buxar (1764)
Won by conspiracy and treachery, little fightingA real military contest
Against the Nawab of Bengal aloneAgainst Bengal, Awadh and the Mughal emperor combined
Made the Company kingmaker in BengalMade the Company the dominant power in north India; Diwani gave legal authority and revenue
Beginning of political controlConfirmation and expansion — the real foundation of British rule

I27.5 Dual government (1765–1772)

  • Devised by Clive: the Company held the Diwani (revenue) and through the Nawab's deputy controlled the Nizamat (police, justice) — power without responsibility.
  • Indian deputies: Muhammad Reza Khan (Bengal), Raja Shitab Rai (Bihar).
  • Evils: revenue farmed out to the highest bidder; oppression of peasants; collapse of administration and justice; decline of crafts as Company servants forced weavers; drain of wealth.
  • Bengal famine of 1770: about one-third of the population died; revenue collection continued.
  • Abolished by Warren Hastings (1772); the Company took direct control of revenue; treasury moved to Calcutta.

What historians say

P.J. Marshall (Bengal: The British Bridgehead) argued the conquest resulted from internal Bengal politics and the private trade interests of Company servants — a "sub-imperialism" rather than London's design. Rajat Kanta Ray stressed the role of the Bengal bankers and the Nawab's elite in inviting the British. N.K. Sinha wrote on the economic history of Bengal. Older nationalist writing emphasised plunder and treachery.

Debate

Accidental empire or planned conquest? Marshall and C.A. Bayly see local collaboration and private interests drawing the Company in; nationalist historians stress deliberate aggression and plunder.

Prelims fact bank

  • Murshid Quli Khan moved the capital of Bengal to Murshidabad.
  • Alivardi Khan did not allow European fortifications in Bengal.
  • The battle of Plassey was fought on 23 June 1757.
  • Mir Jafar, Rai Durlabh, Jagat Seth and Omichund conspired against Siraj-ud-Daula.
  • After Plassey the Company received the zamindari of the 24 Parganas.
  • Mir Qasim moved his capital to Munger.
  • Mir Qasim abolished all internal duties in 1763.
  • The battle of Buxar was fought on 22 October 1764.
  • Hector Munro commanded the Company at Buxar.
  • The Treaty of Allahabad (1765) gave the Company the Diwani of Bengal, Bihar and Odisha.
  • The Company paid Shah Alam II Rs 26 lakh a year for the Diwani.
  • The dual government lasted from 1765 to 1772.
  • The Bengal famine occurred in 1770.
  • Warren Hastings ended the dual government in 1772.
Exam traps
  • Plassey 1757 (Clive); Buxar 1764 (Munro).
  • Diwani = revenue; Nizamat = police and justice.
  • Treaty of Allahabad was two treaties — with Shah Alam II and Shuja-ud-Daula.
  • Mir Qasim abolished duties for all, not only for the English.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

ExamQuestionMarks
UPSC Pre 2018What were the staple exports of the English East India Company from Bengal in the mid-18th century? — Answer: cotton, silk, saltpetre and opium.—

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

"Plassey was a conspiracy, Buxar a battle." Compare their significance.

How to frame it: Plassey → Buxar → Comparison → Conclusion

Model answer (99 words)

At Plassey (1757) Clive won through a conspiracy with Mir Jafar, Jagat Seth, Rai Durlabh and Omichund; most of Siraj-ud-Daula's army did not fight. It made the Company kingmaker in Bengal and brought it the 24 Parganas and great wealth. At Buxar (1764) Munro defeated the combined armies of Mir Qasim, Shuja-ud-Daula and Shah Alam II in a real battle. The Treaty of Allahabad gave the Company the Diwani of Bengal, Bihar and Odisha and made Awadh a buffer. Plassey began political control; Buxar confirmed British military supremacy and gave legal authority — the real foundation of British rule.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the defects of the dual government in Bengal?

How to frame it: System → Effects

Model answer (48 words)

Under Clive's system the Company held revenue rights while the Nawab's deputies kept nominal police and justice — power without responsibility. Revenue farming oppressed peasants, administration and crafts collapsed, wealth was drained, and the 1770 famine killed about a third of the population. Hastings ended it in 1772.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Diwani?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Right to collect revenue; granted to the Company for Bengal, Bihar, Odisha (1765).

Revision card

Remember
  • Nawabs: Murshid Quli (Murshidabad), Alivardi, Siraj-ud-Daula.
  • Plassey 23 June 1757: Clive; Mir Jafar, Jagat Seth, Rai Durlabh, Omichund; 24 Parganas.
  • Mir Qasim: Munger; abolished duties 1763.
  • Buxar 22 October 1764: Munro vs Mir Qasim, Shuja, Shah Alam II.
  • Allahabad 1765: Diwani for Rs 26 lakh; Awadh buffer.
  • Dual government 1765–72; famine 1770; Hastings.

    Show answer

    Hector Munro.

    Mir Qasim, Shuja-ud-Daula, Shah Alam II.

  6. What did the Company get by the Treaty of Allahabad?
    Show answer

    The Diwani of Bengal, Bihar and Odisha.

Sources: P.J. Marshall, Bengal: The British Bridgehead.; Bipan Chandra, History of Modern India, ch. 4.; Sekhar Bandyopadhyay, From Plassey to Partition, ch. 1.

Part III · Unit I28British Expansion: Mysore, Marathas, Sikhs, Subsidiary Alliance, Lapse1765 – 1856

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Anglo-Mysore wars; Anglo-Maratha wars; Subsidiary Alliance; Doctrine of Lapse; annexation of Awadh; Governors-General

Mains: GS-I: Expansion of British paramountcy — policies and instruments

Subsidiary Alliance and Doctrine of Lapse are frequently asked; the Rajasthan treaties of 1817–18 are in R15.

The big picture

From Bengal, the Company extended control over India in about ninety years through war, diplomacy and annexation. The main opponents were Mysore (Haidar Ali, Tipu Sultan), the Marathas and the Sikhs. The key instruments were war, the Subsidiary Alliance (Wellesley), the policy of paramountcy (Hastings) and the Doctrine of Lapse and annexation for "misgovernment" (Dalhousie).

Exam focus

Learn by Governor-General: Hastings and Cornwallis (Mysore), Wellesley (Subsidiary Alliance), Lord Hastings (Marathas, paramountcy), Dalhousie (Lapse, Punjab, Awadh).

WhenWhat happened
1767–69First Anglo-Mysore War; Treaty of Madras
1775–82First Anglo-Maratha War; Treaty of Salbai (1782)
1780–84Second Anglo-Mysore War; Treaty of Mangalore
1790–92Third Anglo-Mysore War; Treaty of Seringapatam
1798Wellesley; Subsidiary Alliance with Hyderabad
1799Fourth Anglo-Mysore War; Tipu killed
1802Treaty of Bassein
1803–05Second Anglo-Maratha War (Assaye, Laswari)
1814–16Anglo-Nepal War; Treaty of Sugauli
1817–18Third Anglo-Maratha War; Pindaris suppressed; Rajput treaties
1824–26First Anglo-Burmese War
1839–42First Anglo-Afghan War
1843Sindh annexed
1845–49Anglo-Sikh wars; Punjab annexed (1849)
1848–56Dalhousie: Doctrine of Lapse
1856Awadh annexed

Core notes

I28.1 Mysore: Haidar Ali and Tipu Sultan

WarEventsTreaty
First (1767–69)Haidar Ali vs English, Nizam and Marathas; Haidar reached MadrasMadras (1769): mutual restitution, defensive alliance
Second (1780–84)Haidar defeated Baillie; defeated by Eyre Coote at Porto Novo (1781); Haidar died (1782); Tipu continuedMangalore (1784): mutual restitution
Third (1790–92)Cornwallis with Marathas and Nizam; Seringapatam besiegedSeringapatam (1792): Tipu lost half his kingdom, paid indemnity, two sons as hostages
Fourth (1799)Wellesley; Tipu killed defending SeringapatamWodeyars restored under Subsidiary Alliance

Tipu Sultan: modernised army with French help; rockets; new calendar, coins, weights; planted a "Tree of Liberty" and joined the Jacobin club (per tradition); sought help from France, Afghanistan and the Ottomans; trade monopolies; called "Tiger of Mysore".

I28.2 The Anglo-Maratha wars

  • First (1775–82): Company backed Raghunath Rao (Treaty of Surat, 1775); Treaty of Purandar (1776); Maratha victory at Wadgaon (1779); Treaty of Salbai (1782) — status quo; Mahadji Scindia mediated; twenty years of peace.
  • Second (1803–05): after Baji Rao II signed the Treaty of Bassein (1802) accepting a Subsidiary Alliance, Scindia and Bhonsle fought; Arthur Wellesley won at Assaye, Lake at Laswari; treaties of Deogaon (Bhonsle), Surji-Arjangaon (Scindia), Rajghat (Holkar).
  • Third (1817–18): Lord Hastings suppressed the Pindaris and defeated the Peshwa (Kirkee, Koregaon), Bhonsle (Sitabaldi) and Holkar (Mahidpur); Peshwaship abolished, Baji Rao II pensioned to Bithur. Rajput states signed subsidiary treaties (1817–18).

I28.3 Subsidiary Alliance (Wellesley, 1798–1805)

Terms:

  1. The Indian state accepted a British force on its territory and paid for it (cash or ceded territory).
  2. A British Resident at the court.
  3. No Europeans of other nations employed without British consent.
  4. No alliances or wars without British permission — foreign relations surrendered.
  5. In return, the Company protected the state against external attack and (nominally) did not interfere in internal affairs.

States: Hyderabad (1798, first), Mysore (1799), Tanjore (1799), Awadh (1801), Peshwa (1802), Bhonsle and Scindia (1803), later Rajput states.

Effects: states lost independence; armies disbanded (soldiers unemployed — some became Pindaris); rulers neglected governance under British protection; Company got a large army at others' expense, and territory in lieu of payment.

Note: the idea predates Wellesley — Dupleix used Indian rulers to pay for French troops; Wellesley made it a system.

I28.4 Paramountcy and Doctrine of Lapse

  • Lord Hastings (1813–23): policy of paramountcy — the Company as the supreme power, entitled to intervene.
  • Kittur (Karnataka): when the Company moved to take over this small state, Rani Channamma led an armed resistance; she was captured in 1824 and died in prison in 1829. Sangolli Rayanna, a village watchman, carried on the fight until he was hanged in 1830.
  • Doctrine of Lapse (Dalhousie, 1848–56): a dependent state whose ruler died without a natural heir would pass to the Company; adoption not recognised for succession to the state.
  • States annexed: Satara (1848), Jaitpur and Sambalpur (1849), Baghat (1850), Udaipur (Chhattisgarh, 1852), Jhansi (1853), Nagpur (1854).
  • Also: pension of Nana Saheb (adopted son of Baji Rao II) stopped; titles of the Nawab of Carnatic and Raja of Tanjore abolished.
  • Awadh (1856) annexed on grounds of "misgovernment" (Nawab Wajid Ali Shah) — not by lapse.
  • Punjab (1849) and Lower Burma (1852) by conquest.
  • By 1857 the Company ruled directly about 63% of the subcontinent's territory and 78% of its population.

I28.5 Other wars on the frontiers

  • Anglo-Nepal War (1814–16): Treaty of Sugauli — Kumaon, Garhwal, Shimla region to the British.
  • Anglo-Burmese wars: 1824–26 (Yandabo — Assam, Arakan, Tenasserim), 1852 (Lower Burma), 1885 (Upper Burma).
  • First Anglo-Afghan War (1839–42): disastrous retreat from Kabul.
  • Sindh annexed (1843) by Charles Napier.
  • Anglo-Sikh wars — see I25.

What historians say

C.A. Bayly (Indian Society and the Making of the British Empire) emphasised Indian collaborators — bankers, merchants, revenue farmers — and the "military fiscalism" of Indian and British states alike. P.J. Marshall stressed the "men on the spot". Kate Brittlebank and Irfan Habib (ed., Confronting Colonialism) studied Tipu Sultan as a modernising ruler, against the older British image of a tyrant.

Prelims fact bank

  • Rani Channamma of Kittur resisted the Company and was captured in 1824.
  • In 1785 Tipu Sultan stopped the export of sandalwood, pepper and cardamom through his ports.
  • The Treaty of Madras (1769) ended the First Anglo-Mysore War.
  • Eyre Coote defeated Haidar Ali at Porto Novo in 1781.
  • The Treaty of Mangalore (1784) ended the Second Anglo-Mysore War.
  • By the Treaty of Seringapatam (1792) Tipu lost half his kingdom.
  • Tipu Sultan was killed in 1799.
  • The Treaty of Salbai (1782) ended the First Anglo-Maratha War.
  • Baji Rao II signed the Treaty of Bassein in 1802.
  • Arthur Wellesley won the battle of Assaye in 1803.
  • Hyderabad was the first state to accept the Subsidiary Alliance (1798).
  • The Peshwaship was abolished in 1818.
  • The Treaty of Sugauli ended the Anglo-Nepal War.

  • Jhansi was annexed in 1853 and Nagpur in 1854.

  • Sindh was annexed in 1843.
Exam traps
  • Udaipur annexed by lapse (1852) is Udaipur in Chhattisgarh, not Mewar.
  • Lapse dates: Jhansi 1853 and Nagpur 1854 in standard lists; NCERT Class VIII reverses them (Nagpur 1853, Jhansi 1854). The rulers died in 1853; both states were taken over in 1854.
  • Awadh: annexed for misgovernment, not lapse.
  • Hyderabad — first Subsidiary Alliance; Satara — first lapse.
  • Treaty of Bassein 1802; Salbai 1782; Seringapatam 1792.
  • Lord Hastings (paramountcy, 1813–23) ≠ Warren Hastings (1772–85).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Explain the Subsidiary Alliance and its effects on Indian states.

How to frame it: Terms → States → Effects on states → Gains for the Company

Model answer (90 words)

Wellesley's Subsidiary Alliance required an Indian ruler to station a British force and pay for it in cash or territory, accept a Resident, employ no other Europeans and conduct no foreign relations without British consent; the Company promised protection. Hyderabad (1798), Mysore, Awadh, the Peshwa (Bassein, 1802), Scindia and Bhonsle joined. States lost independence and territory; disbanded soldiers swelled the Pindaris; rulers, secure under British protection, neglected governance. The Company gained a large army paid for by others, territory and control of Indian diplomacy — a decisive step towards paramountcy.

5 marks · 50 words

What was the Doctrine of Lapse?

How to frame it: Meaning → States → Effect

Model answer (44 words)

Dalhousie's policy that a dependent state whose ruler died without a natural heir would pass to the Company, adoption being ignored. Satara, Jhansi, Nagpur, Sambalpur and others were annexed. It alarmed princes and contributed to the Revolt of 1857, after which it was abandoned.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Treaty of Bassein?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

1802 treaty by which Peshwa Baji Rao II accepted the Subsidiary Alliance.

  • Subsidiary Alliance: Wellesley; Hyderabad first (1798).
  • Paramountcy: Lord Hastings. Lapse: Dalhousie — Satara 1848, Jhansi 1853, Nagpur 1854; Awadh 1856 (misgovernment).
  • Frontiers: Sugauli, Yandabo, Sindh 1843, Punjab 1849.

Part III · Unit I29Constitutional Development, 1773–19471773 – 1947

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Regulating Act to Indian Independence Act — key provisions

Mains: GS-I/II: Constitutional development under British rule; background of the Indian Constitution

Very high yield for Prelims (provisions) and frequently a Mains question (Government of India Act 1935, Morley-Minto, Montford). Links to Polity.

The big picture

British constitutional development moved through two phases. Under the Company (1773–1858), Parliament gradually asserted control over the Company and centralised Indian administration. Under the Crown (1858–1947), Acts slowly introduced Indian representation, elections, communal electorates, provincial autonomy and finally independence — each step usually a response to nationalist pressure, and each designed to retain British control.

Exam focus

For each Act know: what changed at the centre, in the provinces, in representation — and what the nationalists said about it.

WhenWhat happened
1773Regulating Act
1784Pitt's India Act
1793, 1813, 1833, 1853Charter Acts
1858Government of India Act (Crown rule)
1861Indian Councils Act
1892Indian Councils Act
1909Morley–Minto Reforms
1919Montagu–Chelmsford Reforms
1935Government of India Act
1947Indian Independence Act

Core notes

I29.1 Company period

ActKey provisions
Regulating Act, 1773First parliamentary control over the Company; Governor of Bengal became Governor-General of Bengal (Warren Hastings) with a council of four; Bombay and Madras subordinated; Supreme Court at Calcutta (1774) — Chief Justice Elijah Impey; servants barred from private trade and presents
Act of Settlement, 1781Amended the Regulating Act after clashes between Hastings's council and the Supreme Court: Governor-General in Council exempted from the Court's jurisdiction for official acts; revenue matters outside the Court; personal laws of Hindus and Muslims to apply
Pitt's India Act, 1784Board of Control (political affairs) alongside the Court of Directors (commerce) — dual control; Company territories called "British possessions in India"
Act of 1786Governor-General could override his council (Cornwallis); also commander-in-chief
Charter Act, 1793Company charter renewed for 20 years
Charter Act, 1813Company's trade monopoly ended except tea and trade with China; Rs 1 lakh a year for education; missionaries allowed
Charter Act, 1833Governor-General of Bengal became Governor-General of India (William Bentinck); legislative centralisation; Company ceased commercial activities; Law Member added (Macaulay); open competition proposed (not implemented); first Law Commission
Charter Act, 1853Separated legislative and executive functions — a 12-member Legislative Council (Indian Legislative Council); open competition for civil services (from 1854, Macaulay Committee); charter not renewed for a fixed period

I29.2 Crown period

ActKey provisionsAssessment
Government of India Act, 1858Company rule ended; Secretary of State for India with a 15-member India Council; Governor-General became Viceroy (Canning); Queen's Proclamation (Nov 1858)Change of master, little change in administration
Indian Councils Act, 1861Indians nominated as non-official members (Canning nominated the Raja of Benaras, Maharaja of Patiala, Sir Dinkar Rao); portfolio system (Lord Canning); restored legislative powers to Bombay and Madras; Viceroy could issue ordinancesBeginning of representative institutions — nominated, advisory
Indian Councils Act, 1892Councils enlarged; members could discuss the budget and ask questions (no supplementaries); indirect election introduced through recommendation by bodies (universities, municipalities)Congress called it inadequate
Indian Councils Act, 1909 (Morley–Minto)Enlarged councils; elected members (indirect); supplementary questions, resolutions on budget; separate electorates for Muslims; first Indian in the Viceroy's Executive Council (Satyendra Prasanna Sinha, Law Member)"Father of communal electorate": Minto; divided Indians
Government of India Act, 1919 (Montagu–Chelmsford)Based on Montagu's August Declaration (1917) — "responsible government"; dyarchy in provinces (transferred subjects under Indian ministers; reserved under the Governor); bicameral central legislature (Council of State, Legislative Assembly); direct elections on limited franchise; separate electorates extended to Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians, Europeans; separation of central and provincial budgets; Public Service Commission (1926); High Commissioner in London; review after ten yearsCongress rejected it; dyarchy failed
Government of India Act, 1935See below"Charter of slavery" (Nehru); "a machine with strong brakes but no engine"
Indian Independence Act, 1947Two dominions, India and Pakistan, from 15 August 1947; Constituent Assemblies sovereign; Secretary of State abolished; paramountcy lapsed; Governor-General as constitutional head; Crown's title "Emperor of India" droppedEnd of British rule

I29.3 Government of India Act, 1935 (in detail)

  1. All-India Federation of provinces and princely states (states joining voluntarily) — never came into being because the princes did not join.
  2. Three lists: federal, provincial, concurrent; residuary powers with the Viceroy.
  3. Provincial autonomy: dyarchy abolished in provinces; responsible government with ministers answerable to the legislature — basis of the 1937 elections.
  4. Dyarchy at the centre (reserved and transferred subjects) — not implemented.
  5. Bicameral legislatures in six provinces.
  6. Communal electorates extended to depressed classes (by the Poona Pact, reserved seats), women and labour.
  7. Federal Court (1937); Reserve Bank of India (1935); Federal, Provincial and Joint Public Service Commissions.
  8. Franchise extended to roughly 10–14% of the population (estimates vary).
  9. Burma separated from India; Sindh and Odisha created as provinces.
  10. Governor and Governor-General retained special responsibilities and discretionary powers.

Legacy: much of the Constitution of India (1950) — the federal scheme, lists, emergency provisions, Governor's role, public service commissions — was drawn from this Act.

What historians say

B.R. Tomlinson and R.J. Moore argued British reforms aimed to give Indians limited power at the periphery while keeping control at the centre — "collaboration" politics. Nationalist historians (Bipan Chandra) saw the reforms as concessions to pressure, designed to divide (separate electorates). Anil Seal and the "Cambridge school" emphasised how reforms shaped Indian political competition.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Act of Settlement (1781) limited the jurisdiction of the Calcutta Supreme Court created by the Regulating Act.
  • The Regulating Act of 1773 made the Governor of Bengal the Governor-General of Bengal.
  • The Supreme Court at Calcutta was set up in 1774.
  • Pitt's India Act (1784) created the Board of Control.
  • The Charter Act of 1813 ended the Company's trade monopoly except in tea and China trade.
  • The Charter Act of 1813 provided Rs 1 lakh a year for education.
  • The Charter Act of 1833 created the post of Governor-General of India.
  • The Charter Act of 1853 introduced open competition for the civil services.
  • The Government of India Act of 1858 created the post of Secretary of State for India.
  • Lord Canning introduced the portfolio system under the Indian Councils Act of 1861.
  • The Indian Councils Act of 1892 introduced indirect election.
  • The Morley-Minto Reforms (1909) introduced separate electorates for Muslims.
  • S.P. Sinha was the first Indian in the Viceroy's Executive Council.
  • The Government of India Act of 1919 introduced dyarchy in the provinces.
  • The Government of India Act of 1935 introduced provincial autonomy.
  • The Federal Court was set up in 1937 under the 1935 Act.
  • The Indian Independence Act was passed in July 1947.
Exam traps
  • Dyarchy: provinces in 1919; proposed at the centre in 1935 (not implemented).
  • Governor-General of Bengal (1773) → of India (1833) → Viceroy (1858).
  • Separate electorates: Muslims 1909; extended to Sikhs etc. 1919.
  • The 1935 federation never came into force; provincial autonomy did.
  • Open competition: proposed 1833, introduced 1853.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Discuss the main provisions of the Government of India Act, 1935.

How to frame it: Federation → Lists → Provincial autonomy → Central dyarchy → Institutions → Assessment

Model answer (93 words)

The Act proposed an All-India Federation of provinces and princely states, which never materialised because the princes stayed out. Powers were divided into federal, provincial and concurrent lists. Provinces received autonomy — dyarchy was abolished and ministers became responsible to elected legislatures, leading to the 1937 elections. Dyarchy was proposed at the centre. It created the Federal Court and the RBI, extended separate electorates and widened the franchise, and separated Burma. Governors kept wide discretionary powers, so Nehru called it a "charter of slavery", yet much of the 1950 Constitution drew on it.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the main features of the Montagu–Chelmsford Reforms?

How to frame it: Dyarchy → Centre → Franchise

Model answer (41 words)

The 1919 Act introduced dyarchy in provinces — transferred subjects under ministers, reserved subjects under the Governor; a bicameral central legislature; direct elections on limited franchise; separate electorates extended to Sikhs and others; separate budgets; and a review after ten years.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Board of Control?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Body created by Pitt's India Act (1784) to control the Company's political affairs.

Revision card

Remember
  • 1773 Regulating (GG Bengal, Supreme Court); 1784 Pitt (Board of Control).
  • Charters: 1813 (monopoly ends, Rs 1 lakh), 1833 (GG of India), 1853 (open competition).
  • 1858 (Secretary of State, Viceroy); 1861 (nominated Indians, portfolio); 1892 (indirect election).
  • 1909 (separate electorates); 1919 (provincial dyarchy, bicameral centre); 1935 (federation, provincial autonomy, Federal Court, RBI); 1947 (independence).

  5. Which Act introduced separate electorates for Muslims?

  6. Which Act introduced provincial dyarchy?
    Show answer
