Part III · Unit I30British Administrative Policies1772 – 1947

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Land revenue settlements; civil services; judiciary; police; army; education policy; press laws; policy towards princely states

Mains: GS-I: British administrative structure; land revenue systems; education policy

The three land revenue systems and education milestones (1813, 1835, 1854, 1882, 1904, 1917, 1944) are high-yield for both papers.

The big picture

British rule rested on a set of institutions built between Warren Hastings and Curzon: a land revenue system to extract the surplus, a civil service to run the state, a judiciary and codified law, a police and an army to maintain order, and an education policy to train subordinate staff and spread Western ideas. The guiding aims were revenue, order and the security of British rule — with liberal and utilitarian ideas (Bentinck, Macaulay) and paternalism (after 1857) shaping the style.

Exam focus

Questions ask you to compare land revenue systems and to trace education policy by milestone.

WhenWhat happened
1772Warren Hastings: five-year farming of revenue; district collectors
1793Permanent Settlement (Cornwallis); Cornwallis Code
1813Charter Act: Rs 1 lakh for education
1820Ryotwari in Madras (Munro)
1822Mahalwari begins (Holt Mackenzie)
1833Mahalwari revised by R.M. Bird (Regulation IX)
1835Macaulay's Minute; English Education Act
1854Wood's Despatch
1857Universities of Calcutta, Bombay, Madras
1860–62IPC (1860), CrPC; Police Act (1861); High Courts (1862)
1878Vernacular Press Act
1882Hunter Commission
1904Indian Universities Act
1917Sadler Commission
1937Wardha scheme (Basic Education)
1944Sargent Plan

Core notes

I30.1 Land revenue systems

Permanent Settlement (Zamindari)RyotwariMahalwari
Year / by1793, Cornwallis (John Shore)1820, Thomas Munro (Alexander Read, Baramahal 1792)1822, Holt Mackenzie; revised 1833 by R.M. Bird and James Thomason
AreasBengal, Bihar, Odisha, Varanasi, north Madras — about 19% of British IndiaMadras, Bombay, Assam, Coorg — about 51%North-West Provinces, Punjab, Central Provinces — about 30%
Settled withZamindars as ownersIndividual cultivators (ryots)Village community / mahal (estate), jointly
RevenueFixed permanently: 10/11 to the state, 1/11 to the zamindarRevised every 20–30 years; very high (45–55% initially)Periodically revised; high (66% initially, reduced to 50%)
FeaturesSunset law — estates auctioned if revenue unpaid by sunset of due dateDirect relation between state and peasantVillage headmen (lambardars) collected

Effects: Permanent Settlement secured revenue and created a loyal landlord class, but the state lost future increases, absentee landlordism and sub-infeudation (patnidari) grew, and peasants became tenants-at-will. Ryotwari and Mahalwari made peasants owners on paper but high, rigid demands drove them to moneylenders, causing indebtedness and land alienation. All three commercialised land.

Bengal after 1793 (NCERT Class XII): the revenue was fixed high while prices were low in the 1790s, the zamindars' troops and courts were taken away, and many defaulted. At the Burdwan auction of 1797 most buyers were the raja's own agents, and over 95% of the sale was fictitious: zamindars kept their estates through benami purchases, transfers to women of the family and endless re-bidding. In the villages, rich peasants called jotedars (also haoladars, gantidars or mandals) controlled land, trade and credit, worked land through sharecroppers (adhiyars or bargadars) and often withheld rent from the zamindars. The Fifth Report (1813) to Parliament, with petitions, collectors' reports and revenue tables, shaped later views of rural Bengal.

Why later settlements were not permanent: rising prices after 1810 benefited only the Bengal zamindars, and officials influenced by Ricardo held that a landowner should keep only the "average rent", the state taxing the surplus. Ryotwari demands of the 1820s were so high that peasants fled; prices fell after 1832 and the famine of 1832–34 drove ryots into debt.

I30.2 Civil services, judiciary, police, army

  • Civil service: Cornwallis — "father of the civil services" — separated commercial and administrative functions, raised salaries, banned private trade; Fort William College (Wellesley, 1800); Haileybury College (1806); open competition (1853); Satyendranath Tagore first Indian ICS (1863); maximum age lowered to 19 (1876, Lytton), leading to protests; Statutory Civil Service (1879); Aitchison Commission (1886); Islington Commission (1912); Lee Commission (1924); exam held simultaneously in India from 1922.
  • Judiciary: Warren Hastings — district diwani and faujdari adalats (1772), with the Sadar Diwani and Sadar Nizamat Adalats at Calcutta as appeal courts; maulvis and pandits advised the European judges; eleven pandits compiled a digest of Hindu law (1775), translated by N.B. Halhed, and a digest of Muslim law followed (1778); Supreme Court (1774); Cornwallis Code (1793) — separation of revenue and judicial functions; Bentinck abolished provincial circuit courts and used Indian languages in lower courts; IPC (1860, drafted by Macaulay's Law Commission); High Courts (1862, Calcutta, Bombay, Madras); Federal Court (1937). Ilbert Bill (1883) controversy over Indian judges trying Europeans.
  • Police: Cornwallis — thanas under daroghas; superintendent of police post (Bentinck); Police Act of 1861; Police Commission (Fraser, 1902, under Curzon) — CID set up.
  • Army: after 1857 the European proportion raised; artillery kept British; "martial races" theory; "divide and rule" in regiments (by caste, region); Peel Commission (1859); Indianisation slow.

I30.3 Education policy

MilestoneContent
Calcutta Madrasa (1781, Warren Hastings); Asiatic Society (1784, William Jones); Sanskrit College, Benaras (1791, Jonathan Duncan)Orientalist phase
Charter Act 1813Rs 1 lakh annually for education
Orientalist–Anglicist controversyOrientalists (H.T. Prinsep) vs Anglicists
Macaulay's Minute (1835); English Education Act (Bentinck)English as medium; Western learning; downward filtration theory — educate the upper classes, who would educate the masses
Thomason (NWP, 1843–53)Vernacular education in villages
Wood's Despatch (1854) — "Magna Carta of English education in India"Education departments in provinces; universities on the London model (1857); grants-in-aid; vernacular primary schools; teacher training; women's education; rejected downward filtration in principle
Hunter Commission (1882–83)Primary education to local bodies (district and municipal boards); emphasis on primary and secondary education; women's education
Indian Universities Act (1904), CurzonRaleigh Commission (1902); more government control of universities; seen as a move against nationalism
Government resolution 1913Policy of removing illiteracy; but refused compulsory education (Gokhale's Bill of 1911 rejected)
Sadler Commission (1917–19)Calcutta University; 12-year school course; Intermediate stage; Board of secondary education
Montford Reforms (1919)Education transferred to provincial ministers
Hartog Committee (1929)Warned of wastage and stagnation; consolidation of primary education
Wardha Scheme / Basic Education (1937)Gandhi's Nai Talim: education through crafts in the mother tongue; Zakir Hussain Committee
Sargent Plan (1944)Universal free compulsory education for ages 6–14 within 40 years
  • Orientalists: William Jones, H.T. Colebrooke and N.B. Halhed studied Sanskrit and Persian texts; the Asiatic Society published Asiatick Researches. They held that India's civilisation had been great in the past and had declined. Anglicists: James Mill wanted "useful" Western science; Macaulay called Oriental colleges "temples of darkness".
  • Missionaries: the Company kept them out of its territory until 1813, so William Carey and others worked from Danish Serampore (press 1800, college 1818). After 1857 the government avoided direct support for missionary schools.
  • William Adam's reports (1830s) on Bengal and Bihar found over 1 lakh pathshalas teaching over 20 lakh children: no fixed fees, printed books, buildings or timetable, and closed at harvest so peasant children could attend. After 1854 government pandits supervised four or five schools each and grants went only to pathshalas that accepted timetables, textbooks, examinations and fees — which pushed out poor children who worked at harvest.
  • Nationalist alternatives: Gandhi held that colonial education bred inferiority and wanted teaching in Indian languages, joined to handwork; Tagore founded Santiniketan (1901) for creative learning in nature, adding science and arts; Aurobindo (Bombay speech, 1908) asked for national education in the vernacular, using modern science.

I30.4 Press and policy towards princely states

  • Press laws: Censorship Act (1799, Wellesley); Licensing Regulations (1823, John Adam); Metcalfe — "liberator of the Indian press" (1835); Licensing Act (1857); Registration Act (1867); Vernacular Press Act (1878, Lytton) — "gagging act", repealed by Ripon (1882); Newspapers (Incitement to Offences) Act (1908); Indian Press Act (1910); Press Act of 1931 (during Civil Disobedience).
  • Print in India (NCERT Class X): the first press came to Goa with Portuguese missionaries in the mid-16th century (Jesuits printed tracts in Konkani). From 1780 James Augustus Hickey edited the weekly Bengal Gazette, which printed gossip about Company officials. Reform debates moved into print: Rammohun Roy's Sambad Kaumudi (1821) was answered by the orthodox Samachar Chandrika; the ulama used cheap lithographic presses for Urdu and Persian religious texts. Tilak's writing in Kesari led to his imprisonment in 1908.
  • Princely states: ring-fence (Warren Hastings) → subordinate isolation (Lord Hastings) → annexation (Dalhousie) → after 1858, subordinate union — no more annexation, adoption recognised, but intervention for "misrule"; Chamber of Princes (1921); Butler Committee (1927) — paramountcy supreme.

I30.5 Reference: Governors-General and Viceroys, 1773–1947

Prelims often asks "during whose tenure" an Act, war or reform took place. Only the main events are listed.

After independence: Mountbatten (1947–48), then C. Rajagopalachari (1948–50), the only Indian Governor-General.

I30.6 Colonial cities and hill stations

  • Three presidency cities: Madras (1639), Calcutta (1690) and Bombay (given to the Company 1661 and leased 1668) grew at the expense of older towns, first as export depots, then as entry points for British goods. Railways (from 1853) made railway towns such as Jamalpur, Waltair and Bareilly.
  • Bombay: the commercial capital, half of India's trade by 1900 (including Malwa opium to China); after the Suez Canal (1869) it called itself Urbs Prima in Indis; the one-room tenement, the chawl, was its typical housing.
  • After 1857: "Civil Lines" were built as safe European enclaves; sanitary rules from the 1860s–70s sharpened the White Town/Black Town divide.
  • Hill stations: Shimla was founded during the Gurkha War (1815–16) and became the summer seat of government in 1864 (John Lawrence); British interest in Mount Abu followed the Maratha war of 1818; Darjeeling was taken from Sikkim in 1835.

What historians say

Ranajit Guha (A Rule of Property for Bengal) traced the Physiocratic ideas behind the Permanent Settlement. Eric Stokes (The English Utilitarians and India) showed the utilitarian influence on Bentinck, Macaulay and the ryotwari. Gauri Viswanathan (Masks of Conquest) analysed English education as an instrument of rule. B.B. Misra wrote on the bureaucracy.

Prelims fact bank

  • James Augustus Hickey's Bengal Gazette (1780) was the first newspaper in India.
  • William Adam's reports of the 1830s counted over 1 lakh pathshalas in Bengal and Bihar.
  • Tagore founded Santiniketan in 1901.
  • Shimla became the official summer capital in 1864 under John Lawrence.
  • Jotedars were rich peasants of Bengal who controlled land, trade and moneylending in the villages.
  • The Fifth Report on the Company's administration was submitted to Parliament in 1813.
  • N.B. Halhed translated the digest of Hindu law compiled by eleven pandits (1775).
  • Edmund Burke led the impeachment of Warren Hastings, which lasted seven years.
  • Amherst was Governor-General during the First Anglo-Burmese War (Treaty of Yandabo, 1826).
  • Auckland was Governor-General during the First Anglo-Afghan War (1839–42); Ellenborough annexed Sindh (1843).
  • Lansdowne was Viceroy when the Indian Councils Act (1892) was passed and the Durand Line drawn (1893).
  • The Permanent Settlement was introduced by Cornwallis in 1793.
  • Under the Permanent Settlement the zamindar kept 1/11 of the revenue.
  • The sunset law applied under the Permanent Settlement.
  • Thomas Munro introduced the ryotwari system in Madras in 1820.
  • Holt Mackenzie devised the Mahalwari system in 1822.
  • Cornwallis is called the father of the civil services in India.
  • Satyendranath Tagore was the first Indian to join the ICS (1863).
  • The Indian Penal Code was enacted in 1860.
  • The High Courts of Calcutta, Bombay and Madras were set up in 1862.
  • Macaulay's Minute on education was written in 1835.
  • Wood's Despatch (1854) is called the Magna Carta of English education in India.
  • The universities of Calcutta, Bombay and Madras were founded in 1857.
  • The Hunter Commission was appointed in 1882.
  • The Indian Universities Act was passed in 1904 under Curzon.
  • The Sadler Commission (1917) examined Calcutta University.
  • The Sargent Plan was presented in 1944.
  • Lytton passed the Vernacular Press Act in 1878.
  • Metcalfe is called the liberator of the Indian press.
Exam traps
  • Sanskrit College, Benaras: Jonathan Duncan (1791), not William Jones; Fort William College: Richard Wellesley, not Arthur Wellesley.
  • Permanent (Cornwallis 1793); Ryotwari (Munro 1820); Mahalwari (Mackenzie 1822, Bird 1833).
  • Downward filtration: Macaulay; Wood's Despatch moved away from it.
  • Vernacular Press Act: Lytton (1878), repealed by Ripon (1882).
  • Hunter Commission: primary education; Sadler: university; Raleigh: university (1902).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Compare the Permanent Settlement, Ryotwari and Mahalwari systems.

How to frame it: Settled with → Revenue → Areas → Effects

Model answer (87 words)

The Permanent Settlement (Cornwallis, 1793) in Bengal and Bihar made zamindars owners and fixed revenue permanently, with the sunset law for defaulters. Ryotwari (Munro, 1820) in Madras and Bombay settled directly with cultivators, with revenue revised every 20–30 years. Mahalwari (Mackenzie, 1822; Bird, 1833) in the North-West Provinces and Punjab settled with village communities jointly. Zamindari secured fixed revenue and a loyal landlord class but caused absentee landlordism and sub-infeudation; ryotwari and mahalwari imposed high, rigid demands, pushing peasants into debt and land loss. All commercialised land.

5 marks · 50 words

Why is Wood's Despatch called the Magna Carta of English education?

How to frame it: Provisions

Model answer (32 words)

Wood's Despatch (1854) created a comprehensive system: provincial education departments, universities at Calcutta, Bombay and Madras (1857), grants-in-aid, vernacular primary schools, teacher training and women's education — shaping Indian education for decades.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the sunset law?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Zamindar's estate was auctioned if revenue was unpaid by sunset of due date.

Revision card

Remember
  • Land: Permanent 1793 (Cornwallis), Ryotwari 1820 (Munro), Mahalwari 1822/1833.
  • Civil service: Cornwallis; Fort William College 1800; open competition 1853; Tagore 1863.
  • Judiciary: Cornwallis Code 1793; IPC 1860; High Courts 1862; Ilbert Bill 1883.
  • Education: 1813, 1835 (Macaulay), 1854 (Wood), 1882 (Hunter), 1904 (Universities), 1917 (Sadler), 1937 (Wardha), 1944 (Sargent).
  • Press: Metcalfe 1835, VPA 1878, Press Act 1910.

    Show answer

  4. What was the downward filtration theory?
    Show answer

    Educate the upper classes, who would educate the masses.

  5. Which document is the Magna Carta of English education?
    Show answer

    Wood's Despatch (1854).

  6. Which commission handed primary education to local bodies?
    Show answer

    Hunter Commission (1882).

  7. Who passed the Vernacular Press Act?
    Show answer

    Lord Lytton (1878).

  8. Who was the first Indian ICS officer?
    Show answer

    Satyendranath Tagore.

Part III · Unit I31Economic Impact of British Rule1757 – 1947

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Drain of wealth; deindustrialisation; commercialisation of agriculture; famines; railways

Mains: GS-I: Economic impact of colonial rule; economic critique of the early nationalists

Drain theory (Naoroji, R.C. Dutt) and deindustrialisation are classic Mains questions.

The big picture

British rule transformed India's economy in three phases, as described by R. Palme Dutt: mercantile (1757–1813, monopoly trade and plunder), industrial capital / free trade (1813–1858, India as a market for British manufactures and a supplier of raw materials) and finance capital (after 1858, British investment in railways, plantations, banks). The results were the drain of wealth, deindustrialisation, commercialisation of agriculture with peasant impoverishment, recurrent famines, and limited modern industry. The early nationalists' economic critique became the intellectual foundation of Indian nationalism.

Exam focus

Use the nationalist critique (Naoroji, Dutt, Ranade) and modern historians' qualifications (Tirthankar Roy) for balance.

WhenWhat happened
1757–1813Mercantile phase
1813Company trade monopoly ends; free trade
1853First railway, Bombay to Thane (16 April)
1854First cotton mill, Bombay (C.N. Davar)
1855First jute mill, Rishra (Bengal)
1867Naoroji first presents the drain idea ("England's Debt to India")
1876–78Great Famine (south and west India)
1880First Famine Commission (Strachey)
1901Poverty and Un-British Rule in India (Naoroji)
1901–03R.C. Dutt, The Economic History of India
1907TISCO founded (Jamshedpur)
1943Bengal famine

Core notes

I31.1 Drain of wealth

  • Meaning: the portion of India's national product transferred to Britain without an equivalent economic, commercial or material return.
  • Components: Home Charges (salaries, pensions of British officials, India Office expenses, interest on public debt, army costs abroad), profits of British firms, interest on investment (guaranteed railway returns), "invisible" payments (shipping, banking, insurance).
  • Proponents: Dadabhai Naoroji (Poverty and Un-British Rule in India, 1901), R.C. Dutt (Economic History of India), M.G. Ranade, G.V. Joshi, G. Subramania Iyer.
  • Naoroji estimated per capita income at Rs 20 a year (1867–68).
  • Effects: capital lost for Indian investment; export surplus used to pay Britain; poverty.
  • The drain theory showed that British rule was exploitative — "the evil of all evils" (Naoroji).

I31.2 Deindustrialisation

  • Decline of traditional handicrafts, especially textiles, in the 19th century.
  • Causes: one-way free trade (British goods entered duty-free, Indian goods faced high duties in Britain); competition from machine-made goods; loss of royal and aristocratic patronage; Company coercion of weavers (Bengal); railways opened interior markets to British goods.
  • Effects: artisans pushed back to agriculture — increased pressure on land, ruralisation; decline of towns such as Dhaka, Murshidabad, Surat.
  • Indian textiles before the fall: around 1750 India was the world's largest producer of cotton cloth. European words record the trade: muslin (from Mosul, where Europeans first met fine Indian cotton), calico (from Calicut), chintz (from chhint, small flowered prints, e.g. from Masulipatnam), bandanna (from bandhna, tie-and-dye, mostly Rajasthan and Gujarat). Other famous weaves: patola (Surat, Ahmedabad, Patan; prized in Indonesia) and jamdani (Dacca, Lucknow). Weaving communities included the tanti (Bengal), julaha or momin (north India) and the sale, kaikollar and devang (south India); dyers were rangrez and block printers chhipigar.
  • How the trade was captured: England's wool and silk makers won the Calico Act (1720) banning printed cotton (chintz) in England, and English calico printing grew behind it. In Bengal the Company used paid agents (gomasthas) and advances to tie weavers to itself. By the 1830s British cloth flooded India; by the 1880s two-thirds of the cotton cloth Indians wore was British.
  • Survival of handlooms: machines could not make fine bordered saris or the coarse cloth of the poor; new centres grew at Sholapur and Madura; by 1941 over 35% of handlooms used the fly shuttle.
Debate

Was deindustrialisation real? Nationalists (Dutt) and Amiya Bagchi (Bihar census data) said yes. Morris D. Morris questioned it, arguing handloom output survived; Tirthankar Roy argues handicrafts adapted and some specialised crafts survived. Most accept a sharp decline in the 19th century with regional variation.

I31.3 Agriculture, famines and modern industry

  • Commercialisation of agriculture: cash crops (cotton, jute, indigo, tea, opium) grown for export, often under compulsion; benefited traders and moneylenders more than peasants; food crops neglected.
  • High revenue demands, moneylenders, absentee landlords, fragmentation — rural indebtedness.
  • Famines: major famines in 1770, 1837–38, 1860–61, 1866 (Odisha), 1876–78, 1896–97, 1899–1900, 1943 (Bengal). Famine Commissions: Strachey (1880) → Famine Code (1883); Lyall (1898); MacDonnell (1901). Amartya Sen: the 1943 famine was caused by a collapse in entitlements, not a food shortage alone.
  • Railways (Dalhousie, 1853): served British commerce and military needs; rates favoured ports; guaranteed 5% interest to British investors; but also integrated markets and enabled famine relief and national unity.
  • Cotton boom: when the American Civil War (1861) cut supplies, over 90% of Britain's cotton imports came from India by 1862; when it ended (1865) credit dried up, which led to the Deccan riots (I33).
  • Mills: the first cotton mill was a spinning mill in Bombay (1854); Ahmedabad's first mill 1861; the Elgin Mill at Kanpur in the 1860s; Madras 1874; Bombay had over 84 mills by 1900, many founded by Parsi and Gujarati traders enriched by the China trade. Early entrepreneurs: Dwarkanath Tagore (six joint-stock companies, 1830s–40s), Dinshaw Petit, Jamsetji Tata; Seth Hukumchand set up the first Indian-owned jute mill in Calcutta (1917).
  • Modern industry: cotton (Bombay, Ahmedabad — Indian capital), jute (Bengal — British capital), coal, plantations; TISCO (1907; steel from 1912 at Jamshedpur on the Subarnarekha; by 1919 the government bought 90% of its steel); growth during the World Wars; discriminating protection (1920s); dominated by managing agencies.
  • Emergence of a working class, an Indian capitalist class (Tata, Birla, Walchand) and a modern middle class.

I31.4 Nationalist economic critique

  1. Drain of wealth (Naoroji, Dutt).
  2. Deindustrialisation and the need for protection (Ranade).
  3. Heavy land revenue; demand for permanent settlement everywhere (Dutt).
  4. High military and civil expenditure (Home Charges).
  5. Demand for Indianisation of services to reduce the drain.

The critique undercut the moral claim of British rule and fed the Swadeshi idea (I37).

What historians say

R. Palme Dutt (India Today) gave the three-phase framework. Bipan Chandra (Rise and Growth of Economic Nationalism) analysed the nationalist critique. Irfan Habib and Amiya Bagchi emphasise exploitation; Tirthankar Roy (The Economic History of India 1857–1947) gives a more mixed assessment; Utsa Patnaik estimated the drain in very large figures (contested). Mike Davis (Late Victorian Holocausts) linked famines to colonial policy.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Calico Act (1720) banned the use of printed cotton chintz in England.
  • Gomasthas were paid Company agents who supervised weavers.
  • By the 1880s two-thirds of the cotton cloth worn in India was made in Britain.
  • Seth Hukumchand set up the first Indian-owned jute mill in Calcutta (1917).
  • Dadabhai Naoroji wrote Poverty and Un-British Rule in India (1901).
  • R.C. Dutt wrote The Economic History of India.
  • Naoroji estimated India's per capita income at Rs 20 a year.
  • Home Charges included pensions, salaries and interest paid in Britain.
  • The first railway ran from Bombay to Thane on 16 April 1853.
  • The first cotton mill was set up in Bombay in 1854 by C.N. Davar.
  • The first jute mill was set up at Rishra in 1855.
  • TISCO was founded in 1907.
  • The first Famine Commission was headed by Richard Strachey (1880).
  • The Famine Code was framed in 1883.
  • Amartya Sen explained the 1943 Bengal famine by entitlement failure.
  • R. Palme Dutt described three phases of colonialism.
Exam traps
  • Spinning jenny: James Hargreaves (1764); flying shuttle: John Kay (1733); water frame: Arkwright (1769); improved steam engine: James Watt. NCERT-based guides sometimes misassign these.
  • TISCO was founded in 1907 but began producing steel in 1912.
  • Drain = unrequited transfer, not all trade.
  • Cotton mills mainly Indian capital; jute mills mainly British capital.
  • Strachey (1880) → Famine Code (1883); Lyall (1898); MacDonnell (1901).

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Explain the drain of wealth theory.

How to frame it: Meaning → Components → Proponents → Effects → Significance

Model answer (93 words)

The drain was the part of India's national product transferred to Britain without an equivalent return. It consisted of Home Charges — salaries, pensions, interest on debt, India Office and army costs — together with profits of British firms, guaranteed interest on railway capital and payments for shipping, banking and insurance. Dadabhai Naoroji (Poverty and Un-British Rule, 1901), R.C. Dutt and Ranade argued it deprived India of capital and caused poverty. The theory exposed the exploitative character of British rule, undermined its moral claim and became the foundation of economic nationalism and Swadeshi.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the causes of deindustrialisation in India?

How to frame it: Trade policy → Competition → Patronage → Railways

Model answer (40 words)

One-way free trade admitted British goods freely while Indian goods faced duties in Britain; cheap machine goods undercut handicrafts; courts that patronised artisans disappeared; the Company coerced weavers; railways opened the interior to British goods. Artisans were pushed into agriculture.

2 marks · 15 words

What were Home Charges?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Expenses paid in Britain from Indian revenue: pensions, salaries, interest, India Office costs.

Revision card

Remember
  • Phases: mercantile, free trade, finance capital (Palme Dutt).
  • Drain: Home Charges, profits, interest; Naoroji, Dutt, Ranade.
  • Deindustrialisation: free trade, machine goods, patronage lost; debate (Morris, Roy).
  • Railways 1853; cotton mill 1854; jute 1855; TISCO 1907.
  • Famines: 1770, 1876–78, 1943; Strachey, Famine Code 1883.

Self-test

  1. Who wrote Poverty and Un-British Rule in India?
    Show answer

    Dadabhai Naoroji.

  2. What was the drain of wealth?
    Show answer

    Transfer of India's wealth to Britain without equivalent return.

  3. Name two components of the drain.
    Show answer

    Home Charges; profits and interest on British capital.

  4. When did the first railway run?
    Show answer

    16 April 1853 (Bombay–Thane).

  5. Where was the first jute mill?
    Show answer

    Rishra (1855).

  6. Who headed the first Famine Commission?
    Show answer

    Richard Strachey (1880).

  7. Which historian questioned deindustrialisation?
    Show answer

    Morris D. Morris (also Tirthankar Roy).

  8. What did Amartya Sen argue about the 1943 famine?
    Show answer

    It was caused by a collapse of entitlements.

Sources: Bipan Chandra, The Rise and Growth of Economic Nationalism in India.; Tirthankar Roy, The Economic History of India 1857–1947.; NCERT Class 12 (old), Bipan Chandra, Modern India, ch. 9.

Part III · Unit I32The Revolt of 18571857–1859

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: British imperialism and resistance — Revolt of 1857; British political, economic and administrative policies

Mains: GS-I Part B: British policies and their impact — political, economic and administrative; GS-I Part B: Indian National Movement — its various stages (1857 as the great precursor); Rajasthan side of the revolt: Rajasthan History unit R16

Asked in RAS Mains at least three times (2013, 2016 Special, 2021 — see section 6). Prelims questions usually test leaders and centres, dates, and the 1858 changes.

The big picture

By 1856 the East India Company ruled or controlled almost all of India. A hundred years of conquest had created a long list of people with grievances: dethroned rulers, dispossessed landlords, over-taxed peasants, ruined artisans, and sepoys who felt their pay, status and religion were under threat. In May 1857 a dispute over rifle cartridges at Meerut set this dry wood alight.

For about a year the revolt shook British rule across the Gangetic plain and central India — Delhi, Kanpur, Lucknow, Jhansi, Bihar and Rohilkhand — before being crushed with great brutality. It failed, but it ended Company rule, changed how Britain governed India, and became the founding memory of Indian nationalism.

Exam focus

For Mains you need three things from this unit: why it happened (causes), why it failed, and what kind of event it was (the "nature" debate) — plus the changes of 1858.

WhenWhat happened
1848–1856Dalhousie annexes Satara, Jhansi, Nagpur (lapse) and Awadh (misgovernance, 1856)
1856General Service Enlistment Act; Enfield rifle introduced
29 Mar 1857Mangal Pandey attacks officers at Barrackpore (hanged 8 April)
10 May 1857Sepoys rise at Meerut
11 May 1857Rebels reach Delhi; Bahadur Shah II proclaimed leader
Jun–Jul 1857Revolt spreads: Kanpur, Lucknow, Jhansi, Bihar, Rohilkhand
Sep 1857British recapture Delhi
Mar–Jun 1858Lucknow retaken; Jhansi falls; Rani Lakshmibai killed near Gwalior (June)
2 Aug 1858Government of India Act: Crown takes over
1 Nov 1858Queen Victoria's Proclamation read at Allahabad
Apr 1859Tatya Tope captured and hanged

Core notes

I32.1 Why it happened: the causes

Historians group the causes into four kinds. In an answer, always show how they combined — no single cause explains a revolt of this size.

1. Political.

  • Subsidiary alliances had already reduced many rulers to dependants.
  • Doctrine of Lapse (Dalhousie): states whose rulers died without a natural heir were annexed — Satara (1848), Jaitpur and Sambalpur (1849), Udaipur in central India (1852), Jhansi (1853) and Nagpur (1854).
  • Awadh (1856) was annexed on the charge of misgovernance, not lapse. This mattered enormously: a large share of the Bengal army's sepoys came from Awadh.
  • Pensions and titles were withdrawn: Nana Saheb, adopted son of the last Peshwa Baji Rao II, was refused his father's pension, and the Mughal emperor was told his successors would lose the royal title and the Red Fort.

2. Economic.

  • High land revenue demands under the various settlements drove peasants into debt and land sales.
  • In Awadh the 1856 summary settlement stripped many taluqdars (big landholders) of villages.
  • The Inam Commission (Bombay, 1852) confiscated thousands of rent-free estates.
  • Cheap British manufactures ruined Indian artisans, especially weavers.

3. Social and religious. Reforms such as the abolition of sati (1829) and the Widow Remarriage Act (1856), the Religious Disabilities Act (1850) (which let converts to Christianity inherit property), open missionary activity, and even railways and telegraphs were read by many as an attack on religion and caste.

4. Military. Sepoys were paid far less than British soldiers and could not rise above junior ranks. The General Service Enlistment Act (1856) required new recruits to serve overseas, which high-caste sepoys feared would cost them caste (in 1824 sepoys at Barrackpore who refused to go to Burma by sea had been severely punished). Allowances for service outside one's home region (bhatta) were cut.

The spark: the greased cartridge. The new Enfield rifle used paper cartridges whose end had to be bitten off; rumours spread that they were greased with cow and pig fat, offending both Hindus and Muslims. On 29 March 1857 Mangal Pandey of the 34th Bengal Native Infantry attacked officers at Barrackpore; he was hanged on 8 April. At Meerut, 85 troopers of the 3rd Light Cavalry who refused the cartridges were publicly humiliated and jailed on 9 May. On 10 May their comrades rose, freed them, killed officers and marched to Delhi.

I32.2 How it spread: centres, leaders and suppression

On 11 May the Meerut sepoys reached Delhi and proclaimed the aged Mughal emperor Bahadur Shah II (Zafar) their leader. The emperor's name gave the scattered risings a common symbol. Real military command at Delhi passed to Bakht Khan, who came with the Bareilly troops.

CentreIndian leadersBritish commandersOutcome
DelhiBahadur Shah II, Bakht KhanJohn Nicholson, William HodsonRecaptured September 1857; Bahadur Shah exiled to Rangoon (died 1862); Hodson shot the princes
KanpurNana Saheb (based at Bithoor), Tatya Tope, Azimullah KhanHavelock, Colin CampbellRetaken July 1857; Tatya Tope fought on in central India until April 1859
LucknowBegum Hazrat Mahal (for her son Birjis Qadr)Havelock, Outram, Colin CampbellResidency besieged; city retaken March 1858; the Begum escaped to Nepal
Jhansi and GwaliorRani Lakshmibai (with Tatya Tope)Hugh RoseJhansi taken April 1858; the Rani killed fighting at Kotah-ki-Serai near Gwalior, June 1858
Bihar (Jagdishpur)Kunwar SinghWilliam Taylor, Vincent EyreKunwar Singh, about 80, fought across Bihar and UP; died April 1858
BareillyKhan Bahadur KhanColin CampbellRetaken 1858
FaizabadMaulvi Ahmadullah Shah—Killed 1858
AllahabadLiaqat AliColonel NeillSuppressed with mass executions
RajasthanThakur Kushal Singh (Auwa); Kota rebelsGeorge Lawrence (AGG)See unit R16

Where it did not spread. Punjab (where the Sikh princes helped the British), most of Bengal, the Bombay and Madras presidencies, and the major princely states — Gwalior's Scindia, Indore's Holkar, Hyderabad (under minister Salar Jung) and the Rajputana rulers — stayed loyal or neutral. The revolt was concentrated in north and central India.

Popular participation. In Awadh and western UP the revolt became a wide rural rising: taluqdars, peasants and artisans joined the sepoys, and village leaders such as Shah Mal in the Baghpat region organised whole groups of villages.

  • Shah Mal, of a Jat cultivating clan of pargana Barout, moved at night through the chaurasi des (84 villages), attacked moneylenders' houses and government buildings, cut the Meerut–headquarters communications and sent supplies to the rebels in Delhi.
  • Gonoo, a tribal cultivator of Singhbhum (Chotanagpur), led the Kols; Rani Avantibai Lodhi of Ramgarh (Mandla, MP) raised about 4,000 men and, surrounded after an early win at Kheri, chose death over capture.
  • Maulvi Ahmadullah Shah of Faizabad, called "Danka Shah" (the maulvi with the drum), prophesied the end of British rule; at Kanpur Nana Saheb called himself Peshwa and a governor under Bahadur Shah; at Lucknow Birjis Qadr also accepted the emperor's suzerainty.
  • Signals and rumours: chapatis passed from village to village; rumours said cow and pig bone dust had been mixed into flour. They spread because they fitted fears built up since Bentinck's reforms, annexations and missionary activity. Charles Ball noted nightly sepoy panchayats at Kanpur.
  • Awadh, "the nursery of the Bengal army", had been called by Dalhousie "a cherry that will drop into our mouth one day" (1851). Officer Forsyth estimated that three-fourths of Awadh's adult men were in revolt. The Azamgarh Proclamation (25 August 1857) is a main source on what the rebels wanted.
  • Indian eyewitness sources: Vishnubhatt Godse's Marathi travelogue Majha Pravas; Subedar Sitaram Pande's From Sepoy to Subedar.

Suppression was savage on both sides: the killings of British civilians at Kanpur (Satichaura Ghat and Bibighar) were followed by mass hangings, burning of villages and "blowing from guns" by British forces.

I32.3 Why the revolt failed

  1. Limited spread. It never became all-India: Punjab, the south, most of Bengal and the big princely states stayed out, and many Indian soldiers (Sikh, Gurkha and others) fought for the British.
  2. Divided leadership and no common plan. Each centre fought its own war; leaders such as Nana Saheb, the Rani and Bahadur Shah coordinated little.
  3. No forward-looking programme. Rebels mostly aimed to restore the old order — the Mughal emperor, the Peshwa, the Nawab of Awadh — which could not unite all classes.
  4. Loyalty of key groups. Most princes, big zamindars outside Awadh, merchants and the new English-educated middle class did not join.
  5. British resources. Control of the telegraph and railways, money, modern weapons, and troops brought in from outside India gave the British the long-run advantage.

I32.4 What changed: consequences

1. Crown rule. The Government of India Act, 2 August 1858, ended Company rule. A Secretary of State for India, with a 15-member Council of India, took control in London; the Governor-General also became the Viceroy (Canning was the first). The Company was finally dissolved in 1874.

2. Queen Victoria's Proclamation, read by Canning at Allahabad on 1 November 1858:

  • no further annexations; treaties with princes would be honoured (the Doctrine of Lapse was dropped, and Canning later issued adoption sanads);
  • no interference in religion;
  • equal protection of law and access to office "so far as may be";
  • pardon for rebels, except those involved in killing British subjects.

3. Army reorganisation (Peel Commission, 1859): more British soldiers relative to Indian ones (about 1:2 in the Bengal army), artillery kept in British hands, and units mixed by caste and region to prevent unity — later justified through the "martial races" idea.

4. A new ruling strategy. The British now relied on princes and landlords as allies (Awadh taluqdars got their estates back), slowed social reform, and increasingly played communities against each other. Racial distance between rulers and ruled widened.

5. Legacy. The Mughal dynasty ended. The revolt became a powerful memory for later nationalists — most famously in V.D. Savarkar's The Indian War of Independence (1909).

What historians say

"What was 1857?" is the classic Mains question. Know these positions and who held them:

ViewWhoArgument
A sepoy mutinyContemporary British officials; J.R. SeeleyA military revolt over service grievances, without popular support. Seeley called it a "wholly unpatriotic and selfish sepoy mutiny".
A Muslim conspiracySome British officials (e.g. Outram)An attempt to restore Mughal rule. Ignores the many Hindu leaders.
A national revolt / war of independenceKarl Marx (newspaper articles, 1857–58); V.D. Savarkar (1909)Savarkar called it the "First War of Indian Independence", a planned national rising.
Neither first, nor national, nor a war of independenceR.C. MajumdarLarge parts of India and many classes stayed out; leaders fought for their own interests.
Began as a mutiny, became a war of independenceS.N. Sen, Eighteen Fifty-Seven (1957, official centenary history)"What began as a fight for religion ended as a war of independence."
A peasant and popular revoltEric Stokes, The Peasant Armed; Rudrangshu Mukherjee (on Awadh); subaltern historiansIn Awadh and western UP it was a broad rural rising led by taluqdars and peasants with their own grievances.

A balanced Mains conclusion: it began as a sepoy mutiny, became a widespread civil rising in north and central India with strong Hindu–Muslim cooperation, but lacked an all-India national programme. Bipan Chandra calls it the first great struggle of Indians against British rule, which laid the ground for the national movement.

  • Rani Avantibai Lodhi of Ramgarh (Mandla) led about 4,000 men against the British in 1857.

  • Doctrine of Lapse annexations: Satara 1848, Jaitpur and Sambalpur 1849, Udaipur (central India) 1852, Jhansi 1853, Nagpur 1854.
  • Awadh annexed in February 1856 on grounds of misgovernance, under Dalhousie.
  • Religious Disabilities Act 1850 allowed converts to inherit ancestral property.
  • General Service Enlistment Act 1856 (Canning): new recruits liable for overseas service.
  • Inam Commission, Bombay, 1852.
  • Mangal Pandey: 34th Bengal Native Infantry, Barrackpore, 29 March 1857; hanged 8 April 1857.
  • Meerut rising: 10 May 1857, began with the 3rd Light Cavalry.
  • Delhi seized 11 May 1857; Bahadur Shah II proclaimed Emperor of Hindustan.
  • Bakht Khan commanded the rebel forces at Delhi.
  • Delhi recaptured by the British in September 1857; Nicholson died of wounds.
  • Bahadur Shah II exiled to Rangoon; died there in 1862.
  • Nana Saheb (Dhondu Pant) was the adopted son of Peshwa Baji Rao II.
  • Tatya Tope's real name: Ramachandra Pandurang; captured through betrayal and hanged at Shivpuri, April 1859.
  • Begum Hazrat Mahal led at Lucknow for her son Birjis Qadr.
  • Rani Lakshmibai (Manikarnika) died fighting near Gwalior in June 1858; Hugh Rose was her opponent.
  • Kunwar Singh of Jagdishpur (Bihar) was about 80 years old; died April 1858.
  • Khan Bahadur Khan led at Bareilly; Maulvi Ahmadullah Shah at Faizabad; Liaqat Ali at Allahabad.
  • Maniram Dewan (Assam) was hanged for his role.
  • Governor-General during the revolt: Lord Canning, who became the first Viceroy.
  • Government of India Act: 2 August 1858; created the Secretary of State and a 15-member Council of India.
  • Queen's Proclamation read at Allahabad on 1 November 1858.
  • Peel Commission (1859) on army reorganisation.
  • S.N. Sen wrote the centenary history "Eighteen Fifty-Seven" (1957).
  • V.D. Savarkar: "The Indian War of Independence" (1909).
Exam traps
  • Mangal Pandey attacked his officers on 29 March 1857 and was hanged on 8 April; NCERT Class VIII wrongly implies he was hanged on 29 March.
  • Mangal Pandey was at Barrackpore (Bengal), not Meerut. The general revolt began at Meerut.
  • Awadh was NOT annexed under the Doctrine of Lapse — it was annexed for alleged misgovernance.
  • Bakht Khan led at Delhi; he came from Bareilly. Khan Bahadur Khan led at Bareilly.
  • Rani Lakshmibai died near Gwalior, not at Jhansi.
  • Canning was Governor-General when the revolt began; he became Viceroy only in 1858.
  • The GoI Act was passed on 2 August 1858; the Queen's Proclamation was read on 1 November 1858. Do not swap them.
  • R.C. Majumdar denied it was a national war; S.N. Sen called it a war of independence that began as a fight for religion.
  • Tatya Tope was hanged in 1859, after the main revolt had ended in 1858.

Questions as listed by RajRAS (rajras.in, "Indian History and Culture PYQs"). Model answers to all three are in section 7. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

Discuss the nature of the 1857 revolt.

How to frame it: Name the main views with their historians → Give your balanced judgement

Model answer (51 words)

British officials saw a sepoy mutiny; Savarkar a "First War of Independence"; R.C. Majumdar denied it was national. S.N. Sen saw a fight for religion turning into a war of independence. Balanced view: a mutiny that became a wide civil rising in north India, with Hindu–Muslim unity but no all-India programme.

5 marks · 50 words

In what way was the revolt of 1857 a turning point in Indian history?

How to frame it: Crown rule (1858 Act) → Proclamation and new policy towards princes → Army reorganisation → Legacy for nationalism

Model answer (50 words)

It ended Company rule: the 1858 Act brought Crown rule through a Secretary of State and Viceroy. The Queen's Proclamation stopped annexations and promised religious non-interference. The army was reorganised to prevent unity, princes and landlords became British allies, reform slowed, and the revolt became an inspiration for later nationalism.

5 marks · 50 words

Throw light on the activities of Kunwar Singh in Bihar during the Revolt of 1857.

How to frame it: Who he was → Arrah and guerrilla campaign → Last victory and death → Significance

Model answer (50 words)

Kunwar Singh, the roughly 80-year-old zamindar of Jagdishpur, led the Danapur sepoys and besieged Arrah (1857). He then waged a mobile guerrilla campaign across Bihar and eastern UP, defeated British forces, and returned to Jagdishpur, where he died in April 1858. He symbolised landed and popular participation in the revolt.

10 marks · 100 words

Why did the Revolt of 1857 fail?

How to frame it: Geographical and social limits → Leadership and programme → Loyal groups → British resources → One-line conclusion

Model answer (97 words)

The revolt stayed confined to north and central India; Punjab, the south, most of Bengal and the major princely states kept out, and many Indian soldiers fought for the British. Its leaders — Bahadur Shah, Nana Saheb, the Rani, Kunwar Singh — fought separate wars without a common plan or command. Their aim of restoring the old order offered nothing to many classes, so most princes, merchants and the educated middle class stayed loyal. The British commanded the telegraph, railways, finance, modern arms and reinforcements from abroad. Yet the revolt ended Company rule and inspired later nationalism.

Revision card

Remember
  • Causes = political (lapse, Awadh 1856) + economic (revenue, taluqdars, artisans) + socio-religious + military; spark = Enfield cartridges.
  • Barrackpore 29 Mar (Mangal Pandey) → Meerut 10 May → Delhi 11 May (Bahadur Shah II; Bakht Khan).
  • Kanpur: Nana Saheb, Tatya Tope · Lucknow: Hazrat Mahal · Jhansi: Lakshmibai · Bihar: Kunwar Singh · Bareilly: Khan Bahadur Khan.
  • Failure: limited spread, divided leaders, backward-looking aims, loyal princes and middle class, British resources.
  • 1858: GoI Act (2 Aug) → Crown rule, Secretary of State, Viceroy; Proclamation (1 Nov, Allahabad) → no annexation, religious non-interference.
  • Army: Peel Commission 1859, more British troops, mixed units.
  • Nature: mutiny (Seeley) · war of independence (Savarkar) · not national (Majumdar) · religion to independence (S.N. Sen) · peasant revolt (Stokes).

Self-test

  1. Name three states annexed under the Doctrine of Lapse.
    Show answer

    Any three of: Satara (1848), Jaitpur, Sambalpur (1849), Udaipur in central India (1852), Jhansi (1853), Nagpur (1854).

  2. On what ground was Awadh annexed, and why did this matter for the army?
    Show answer

    Misgovernance (1856). Many Bengal army sepoys came from Awadh, so the annexation hit them and their families directly.

  3. Where and when did Mangal Pandey revolt, and which regiment was he in?
    Show answer

    Barrackpore, 29 March 1857; 34th Bengal Native Infantry.

  4. Who commanded the rebel army at Delhi?
    Show answer

    Bakht Khan.

  5. Who led the revolt at Lucknow, and on whose behalf?
    Show answer

    Begum Hazrat Mahal, for her son Birjis Qadr.

  6. Where did Rani Lakshmibai die, and who was the British commander against her?
    Show answer

    Near Gwalior, June 1858; Hugh Rose.

  7. Give two provisions of the Government of India Act 1858.
    Show answer

    Crown took over from the Company; created a Secretary of State for India with a 15-member Council of India (and the Governor-General became Viceroy).

  8. Give two promises of Queen Victoria's Proclamation.
    Show answer

    No further annexation (princes' treaties honoured) and non-interference in religion; also pardon for most rebels.

  9. Which historian said the revolt was "neither first, nor national, nor a war of independence"?
    Show answer

    R.C. Majumdar.

  10. What did the Peel Commission recommend?
    Show answer

    A higher proportion of British to Indian troops, artillery in British hands, and mixing of castes and regions in units.

Part III · Unit I33Peasant Movements1783 – 1947

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Indigo revolt; Pabna; Deccan riots; Champaran, Kheda; Moplah; Bardoli; Kisan Sabha; Tebhaga; Telangana

Mains: GS-I: Peasant movements under colonial rule — phases and character

Frequently asked in both papers. Rajasthan peasant movements (Bijolia, Begun, Shekhawati) are in R17.

The big picture

Colonial land revenue, landlords, planters and moneylenders made peasants the most exploited class under British rule. Peasant resistance passed through three stages: early revolts mixed with religious or tribal elements (Sanyasi-Fakir, Faraizi, Moplah outbreaks); post-1857 movements focused on specific grievances and fought largely within the law (Indigo, Pabna, Deccan riots); and 20th-century movements linked to the national movement and organised by Kisan Sabhas and the Left (Champaran, Kheda, Bardoli, AIKS, Tebhaga, Telangana).

Exam focus

Learn each movement by: place, year, against whom, leaders, result.

WhenWhat happened
1763–1800Sanyasi and Fakir revolts (Bengal)
1783Rangpur dhing (Bengal) against Devi Singh
1817Paika rebellion, Khurda (Odisha) — Bakshi Jagabandhu
1820s–1850sFaraizi movement (Haji Shariatullah, Dudu Miyan)
1836–1921Moplah (Mappila) outbreaks, Malabar
1859–60Indigo revolt (Bengal)
1873–76Pabna agrarian leagues
1875Deccan riots (Poona, Ahmednagar)
1917Champaran
1918Kheda
1918–20Kisan Sabha in UP (Baba Ramchandra, Eka movement 1921)
1921Moplah rebellion
1928Bardoli satyagraha

1946–47Tebhaga (Bengal)
1946–51Telangana movement

Core notes

I33.1 Nineteenth-century movements

MovementDetails
Sanyasi–Fakir (1763–1800)Bengal; after the 1770 famine; restrictions on pilgrimage; described in Bankim's Anandamath
Paika rebellion (1817)Odisha (Khurda); Paikas — the hereditary militia of the Khurda king — lost their rent-free lands after the British conquest (1803); led by Bakshi Jagabandhu, the deposed king's hereditary military commander. A higher revenue demand added to the anger; the rising began at Banpur on 29 March 1817, the rebels with Kandh tribals took Puri in April, and the British imposed martial law. Jagabandhu held out until he surrendered in 1825. Afterwards salt prices were cut, corrupt officials dismissed and some lands restored
FaraiziEast Bengal; Haji Shariatullah (religious reform), Dudu Miyan — against zamindars and indigo planters; "land belongs to God"
Moplah outbreaksMalabar; Muslim tenants against Hindu jenmis (landlords) and the British
Indigo revolt (1859–60)Bengal (Nadia — Digambar and Bishnu Biswas); against European planters forcing indigo under the tinkathia-like dadan system; supported by intelligentsia — Dinabandhu Mitra's Nil Darpan (1860), Harish Chandra Mukherji (Hindoo Patriot); Indigo Commission (1860) — cultivation not compulsory. Background: India's share of Britain's indigo imports rose from about 30% (1788) to 95% (1810). Planters grew indigo either on land they controlled (nij, under a quarter of the area) or, mostly, through ryoti contracts (satta) that tied advances to indigo on at least a quarter of a ryot's holding, often on the best rice land. In March 1859 ryots refused to sow; magistrate Ashley Eden's notice that ryots could not be compelled to accept contracts was read as support. The Commission blamed the planters; indigo collapsed in Bengal and planters moved to Bihar
Pabna (1873–76)East Bengal; agrarian leagues against zamindars' illegal enhancement of rent; legal methods; supported by Bankim Chandra, R.C. Dutt, Indian Association; led to the Bengal Tenancy Act (1885)
Deccan riots (1875)Poona and Ahmednagar; ryots against Marwari and Gujarati moneylenders after the cotton boom collapsed (the customary rule that interest could not exceed the principal had broken down, and the Limitation Law of 1859, which made loan bonds valid for three years, led moneylenders to make ryots sign fresh bonds with interest added to principal); the Deccan Riots Commission recorded the abuses (the revolt began at Supa, Poona district, on 12 May 1875, where ryots seized and burned the moneylenders' account books (bahi khatas) and bonds; ruined peasants had been made to sign "deeds of hire" for land and cattle that had become the moneylender's. The Commission blamed the moneylenders, not the revenue demand); social boycott and seizure of bonds; Deccan Agriculturists Relief Act (1879)

Features: targeted immediate enemies (planters, zamindars, moneylenders), not colonialism as such; limited territorial reach; no long-term programme; but built peasant consciousness.

I33.2 Twentieth-century movements

MovementDetails
Champaran (1917)Bihar; against European planters' tinkathia system (indigo on 3/20 of land); Raj Kumar Shukla invited Gandhi; Rajendra Prasad, J.B. Kripalani, Mazharul Haq, Mahadev Desai; inquiry committee; tinkathia abolished; Gandhi's first satyagraha in India
Kheda (1918)Gujarat; crop failure; demand for remission of revenue; Gandhi and Vallabhbhai Patel; partial relief
UP Kisan Sabha (1918–21)Gauri Shankar Mishra, Indra Narayan Dwivedi, Madan Mohan Malaviya; Baba Ramchandra in Awadh; Awadh Kisan Sabha (1920); Eka movement (1921–22, Madari Pasi)
Moplah rebellion (1921)Malabar; linked to Khilafat; turned communal in places; brutally suppressed; Wagon tragedy
Bardoli (1928)Gujarat; against a 22% (later reduced to 6.03%) revenue hike; led by Vallabhbhai Patel — women gave him the title "Sardar"; Maxwell–Broomfield inquiry; success
All India Kisan Sabha (1936)Lucknow; president Swami Sahajanand Saraswati, secretary N.G. Ranga; Kisan Manifesto (1936) — abolition of zamindari; linked with the Congress Socialists and Communists
Tebhaga (1946–47)Bengal; sharecroppers (bargadars) demanded two-thirds of the crop instead of half; Bengal Provincial Kisan Sabha; Floud Commission recommendation
Telangana (1946–51)Hyderabad state; against jagirdars, deshmukhs and the Nizam's Razakars; Communist-led; largest peasant guerrilla war of modern India; called off after the police action and in 1951
OthersPunjab (Kirti Kisan), Kerala (Punnapra-Vayalar, 1946), Bakasht movement (Bihar), Warli revolt (Maharashtra)

What historians say

Ranajit Guha (Elementary Aspects of Peasant Insurgency) and the Subaltern Studies group saw peasant movements as autonomous, with their own consciousness, not merely led by elites. Bipan Chandra and Sumit Sarkar analysed their links with the national movement. Kathleen Gough classified peasant revolts; D.N. Dhanagare (Peasant Movements in India) discussed which strata of peasants led them.

Prelims fact bank

  • Indigo planters' two systems were nij (own land, hired labour) and ryoti (contract cultivation by ryots).
  • The Paika rebellion (1817, Khurda, Odisha) was led by Bakshi Jagabandhu.
  • Dinabandhu Mitra wrote Nil Darpan (1860).
  • The Indigo Commission was appointed in 1860.
  • The Pabna movement led to the Bengal Tenancy Act of 1885.
  • The Deccan riots of 1875 were against moneylenders.
  • The Deccan Agriculturists Relief Act was passed in 1879.
  • The tinkathia system required indigo cultivation on 3/20 of the land.
  • Raj Kumar Shukla invited Gandhi to Champaran.
  • Vallabhbhai Patel led the Kheda and Bardoli movements.
  • Bardoli's women gave Vallabhbhai Patel the title Sardar.
  • Baba Ramchandra led the Awadh peasant movement.
  • Madari Pasi led the Eka movement.
  • The All India Kisan Sabha was founded in 1936 at Lucknow.
  • Swami Sahajanand Saraswati was the first president of the All India Kisan Sabha.
  • The Tebhaga movement demanded two-thirds of the crop for sharecroppers.
  • The Telangana movement was against the Nizam and the jagirdars.
Exam traps
  • The Paika rebellion is not officially the "first war of independence": that was proposed in 2017, but in 2021 the Union Culture Ministry told Parliament it would be treated as an early revolt, not the first war of independence.
  • Pabna (rent, zamindars) ≠ Deccan riots (moneylenders).
  • Indigo revolt (Bengal, 1859) ≠ Champaran (Bihar, 1917).
  • Kheda (1918) and Bardoli (1928) — both Patel.
  • AIKS president: Sahajanand; secretary: N.G. Ranga.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

ExamQuestionMarks
UPSC Pre 2020Why had indigo cultivation in India declined by the beginning of the 20th century? — Answer: it became unprofitable in the world market because of new inventions (synthetic dyes).—

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Trace the changing character of peasant movements in colonial India.

How to frame it: Early revolts → Post-1857 → 20th century → Assessment

Model answer (95 words)

Early revolts such as the Sanyasi-Fakir, Faraizi and Moplah outbreaks combined peasant grievances with religious leadership and were violent and local. After 1857 movements — the Indigo revolt, Pabna and the Deccan riots — targeted planters, zamindars and moneylenders, often using legal methods and winning laws such as the Bengal Tenancy Act. In the 20th century, peasant struggles joined the national movement: Champaran, Kheda and Bardoli under Gandhi and Patel, Kisan Sabhas in UP, the AIKS (1936) demanding zamindari abolition, and Left-led Tebhaga and Telangana. Peasants thus moved from local protest to organised, political action.

5 marks · 50 words

What was the significance of the Indigo revolt?

How to frame it: Cause → Support → Result

Model answer (44 words)

In 1859–60 Bengal peasants refused to grow indigo for European planters. The revolt was disciplined and won intelligentsia support — Nil Darpan, the Hindoo Patriot. The Indigo Commission (1860) ruled cultivation voluntary. It showed peasants could resist colonial interests and inspired later nationalist politics.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the tinkathia system?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Champaran planters forced peasants to grow indigo on 3/20 of their land.

Revision card

Remember
  • 19th c.: Sanyasi-Fakir, Faraizi, Moplah; Indigo 1859–60 (Nil Darpan); Pabna 1873–76 (BTA 1885); Deccan 1875 (DARA 1879).
  • 20th c.: Champaran 1917, Kheda 1918, UP Kisan Sabha, Eka, Moplah 1921, Bardoli 1928.
  • AIKS 1936 (Sahajanand, Ranga); Tebhaga 1946; Telangana 1946–51.

Self-test

  1. Who wrote Nil Darpan?
    Show answer

    Dinabandhu Mitra.

  2. Which law followed the Pabna movement?
    Show answer

    Bengal Tenancy Act, 1885.

  3. Against whom were the Deccan riots directed?
    Show answer

    Moneylenders.

  4. What was the tinkathia system?
    Show answer

    Compulsory indigo on 3/20 of land.

  5. Who led the Bardoli satyagraha?
    Show answer

    Vallabhbhai Patel (1928).

  6. Who led the Eka movement?
    Show answer

    Madari Pasi.

  7. When was the AIKS founded?
    Show answer

    1936, Lucknow.

  8. What did the Tebhaga movement demand?
    Show answer

    Two-thirds of the crop for sharecroppers.

Sources: Bipan Chandra et al., India's Struggle for Independence, chs. 4, 14.; Spectrum, A Brief History of Modern India, peasant movements chapter.; D.N. Dhanagare, Peasant Movements in India 1920–1950.

Part III · Unit I34Tribal Movements1774 – 1947

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Paharia, Kol, Santhal, Munda (Birsa), Bhil, Khond, Rampa, Naga, Tana Bhagat revolts

Mains: GS-I: Tribal uprisings — causes and character

Santhal and Munda (Birsa) revolts are the most asked. Rajasthan tribal movements (Govind Guru, Bhil, Meena, Eki) are in R18.

The big picture

Tribal communities lived in forests and hills with their own land systems, chiefs and customs. Colonial rule brought outsiders — dikus (moneylenders, traders, landlords), revenue demands, forest laws and missionaries — which destroyed their autonomy, land and access to the forest. Tribal revolts were among the most frequent and violent in colonial India, often led by charismatic leaders who promised a golden age.

Exam focus

Explain the common causes (land, forest, dikus, missionaries) and use two or three examples in detail.

WhenWhat happened
1774–79Halba rebellion (Bastar)
1778Paharia rising (Rajmahal hills)
1820–37Ho and Munda risings
1831–32Kol uprising (Chotanagpur)
1817–46Bhil uprisings (Khandesh)
1837–56Khond uprisings (Odisha, Chakra Bisoi)
1855–56Santhal hul
1879–80Rampa rebellion (first)
1899–1900Ulgulan (Birsa Munda)
1913Mangarh massacre (Govind Guru, Bhils)
1914Tana Bhagat movement
1922–24Rampa rebellion (Alluri Sitarama Raju)
1927–32Naga movement (Jadonang, Rani Gaidinliu)

Core notes

I34.1 Causes

  1. Land: introduction of private property and revenue settlements; land passed to outsiders (dikus).
  2. Forest laws: Forest Acts (1865, 1878, 1927) restricted shifting cultivation (jhum), grazing and collection of forest produce; forced labour (begar).
  3. Moneylenders and traders exploited tribals through debt.
  4. Missionaries undermined customs (though some aided education).
  5. Loss of chiefs' authority and imposition of courts and police.

I34.2 Major revolts

RevoltRegionLeaders / details
PahariaRajmahal hillsAgainst British expansion into their territory (1778)
Kol uprising (1831–32)Chotanagpur, Ranchi, SinghbhumBuddho Bhagat; against transfer of land to outsiders
BhilKhandesh (western ghats)Against Company rule (1817–19, 1825, 1831, 1846)
Khond (1837–56)Odisha hillsChakra Bisoi; against suppression of human sacrifice (meriah), taxes, outsiders
Santhal hul (1855–56)Rajmahal hills, Damin-i-Koh (Bihar–Bengal)Brothers Sidhu and Kanhu (also Chand, Bhairav); against zamindars, moneylenders and police; declared end of Company rule; suppressed by the army; Santhal Parganas district created (1855) with special laws
Munda ulgulan (1899–1900)Chotanagpur, south of RanchiBirsa Munda ("Dharti Aba"); against the destruction of khuntkatti (joint landholding) by jagirdars, thikadars and moneylenders, and missionaries; aimed at Munda raj; Birsa died in Ranchi jail (1900); led to the Chotanagpur Tenancy Act (1908); 15 November observed as Janjatiya Gaurav Divas
Tana Bhagat (1914)Chotanagpur (Oraons)Jatra Bhagat; religious reform; later joined Gandhian satyagraha
RampaGodavari hills (Andhra)1879; 1922–24 under Alluri Sitarama Raju against forest laws; killed in 1924
Naga / ZeliangrongManipur, NagalandJadonang; Rani Gaidinliu (arrested 1932; Nehru called her "Rani")
KukiManipur1917–19, against labour recruitment in World War I
Bhil (Rajasthan)Banswara, DungarpurGovind Guru, Mangarh (1913); Motilal Tejawat's Eki movement — R18

Other protests NCERT names: Songram Sangma's revolt in Assam (1906), the Bastar rebellion (1910), the forest satyagraha of the 1930s in the Central Provinces and the Warli revolt in Maharashtra (1940).

Paharias and Santhals ("the hoe and the plough"): the Paharias of the Rajmahal hills lived by shifting cultivation and forest produce and raided the plains. The British tried extermination in the 1770s; in the 1780s Augustus Cleveland proposed pacification, paying allowances to Paharia chiefs. Failing to settle the Paharias, the British brought in Santhals to clear and plough the lower slopes; by 1832 the Damin-i-Koh was marked out as Santhal land. Heavy taxes and moneylenders' takeovers led to the hul of 1855–56, after which the Santhal Parganas (about 5,500 square miles, from Bhagalpur and Birbhum districts) were created.

Birsa Munda's path: he went to a mission school and also spent time with a Vaishnav preacher, wearing the sacred thread. His movement first sought reform (no liquor, no witchcraft) and a return to a golden age (satyug); he was jailed 1895–97, then urged followers to destroy "Ravana" (the dikus and Europeans). They attacked police stations, churches and moneylenders' property under a white flag of Birsa Raj. He died in 1900 (officially of cholera).

I34.3 Character

  • Violent, armed with traditional weapons; easily crushed by modern armies.
  • Strong ethnic solidarity; outsiders, including Indian dikus, as enemies.
  • Messianic leaders (Birsa claimed divine mission); millenarian hopes of a golden age.
  • Results: protective laws (Santhal Parganas Tenancy, Chotanagpur Tenancy Act 1908), "excluded" and "partially excluded" areas (1935) — the base for the Fifth and Sixth Schedules of the Constitution.

What historians say

K.S. Singh (Birsa Munda and His Movement) is the standard study. Ranajit Guha used the Santhal hul in his theory of peasant insurgency. David Hardiman (The Coming of the Devi) studied Adivasi assertion in western India. Ramachandra Guha (The Unquiet Woods) linked tribal protest to colonial forestry.

Prelims fact bank

  • Augustus Cleveland proposed a policy of pacifying the Paharias in the 1780s.
  • The Damin-i-Koh was demarcated as Santhal land by 1832.
  • Birsa Munda's followers raised a white flag as the symbol of Birsa Raj.
  • Sidhu and Kanhu led the Santhal hul of 1855–56.
  • The Santhal Parganas district was created after the hul.
  • Birsa Munda led the ulgulan of 1899–1900.
  • Birsa Munda was called Dharti Aba.
  • The Chotanagpur Tenancy Act was passed in 1908.
  • Khuntkatti was the Munda system of joint landholding.
  • Buddho Bhagat led the Kol uprising of 1831–32.
  • Chakra Bisoi led the Khond uprising.
  • Alluri Sitarama Raju led the Rampa rebellion of 1922–24.
  • Rani Gaidinliu led the Naga movement.
  • Jatra Bhagat started the Tana Bhagat movement.
  • 15 November, Birsa Munda's birth anniversary, is observed as Janjatiya Gaurav Divas.
Exam traps
  • Santhal hul (1855) — Sidhu-Kanhu; Munda ulgulan (1899) — Birsa.
  • Rampa (Andhra) — Alluri Sitarama Raju; not Bastar.
  • Kol (1831) ≠ Khond (1837).

UPSC Pre 2018After the Santhal uprising: (1) the Santhal Parganas were created; (2) transfer of land by a Santhal to a non-Santhal became illegal. Which are correct? — Answer: both.—
UPSC Pre 2011What was the common factor behind 19th-century tribal insurrections? — Answer: the complete disruption of the old agrarian order of the tribal communities.—

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

What were the causes and nature of tribal revolts in colonial India?

How to frame it: Causes → Examples → Character → Results

Model answer (92 words)

Colonial land settlements transferred tribal land to outsiders; Forest Acts restricted jhum, grazing and forest produce; moneylenders, traders and contractors (dikus) exploited tribals; missionaries and courts undermined custom and chiefs. Revolts followed — the Kol (1831), Khond, Santhal hul under Sidhu and Kanhu (1855), Birsa Munda's ulgulan (1899) and the Rampa revolt under Alluri Sitarama Raju. They were violent, based on ethnic solidarity and often led by messianic figures, and were crushed by superior arms. Yet they produced protective laws such as the Chotanagpur Tenancy Act (1908) and the later Scheduled Areas.

5 marks · 50 words

Describe the Munda ulgulan.

How to frame it: Cause → Leader → Result

Model answer (41 words)

In 1899–1900 Birsa Munda led the Mundas of Chotanagpur against jagirdars, moneylenders and missionaries who destroyed the khuntkatti system, aiming at Munda raj. It was suppressed and Birsa died in jail (1900), but the Chotanagpur Tenancy Act (1908) protected tribal land.

2 marks · 15 words

Who were the dikus?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Outsiders — moneylenders, traders, landlords — who exploited tribals in colonial India.

Revision card

Remember
  • Causes: land, forest laws, dikus, missionaries.
  • Kol 1831 (Buddho Bhagat); Khond (Chakra Bisoi); Santhal 1855 (Sidhu-Kanhu); Munda 1899 (Birsa, CNT Act 1908).
  • Rampa 1922–24 (Alluri); Naga (Gaidinliu); Tana Bhagat 1914.

    Chotanagpur Tenancy Act, 1908.

  4. Who led the Rampa rebellion of 1922?
    Show answer

    Alluri Sitarama Raju.

  5. Who led the Naga movement and was called Rani by Nehru?
    Show answer

    Rani Gaidinliu.

  6. Who led the Kol uprising?
    Show answer

    Buddho Bhagat.

  7. What were dikus?
    Show answer

Part III · Unit I35Socio-Religious Reform Movements1815 – 1947

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Brahmo Samaj, Prarthana Samaj, Arya Samaj, Ramakrishna Mission, Theosophical Society, Aligarh, Satyashodhak, SNDP; social legislation

Mains: GS-I: 19th-century renaissance — reform movements, their nature and limitations

One of the most-asked modern topics. Rajasthan links (Dayanand at Udaipur and Ajmer; Paropkarini Sabha; Walterkrit Sabha) are in R15.

The big picture

In the 19th century educated Indians, influenced by Western rationalism and humanism and by pride in India's past, sought to reform society and religion. Two broad trends emerged: reformist (Brahmo Samaj, Prarthana Samaj, Aligarh — reform through reason and adaptation) and revivalist (Arya Samaj, Deoband — return to a purified original tradition). Reformers attacked sati, child marriage, caste, purdah, idolatry and the ban on widow remarriage, and promoted education. Lower-caste movements (Phule, Narayana Guru, Ambedkar) challenged caste hierarchy itself.

Exam focus

Examiners ask for the contribution of individual reformers, the character of the "Indian Renaissance" and its limitations.

WhenWhat happened
1815Atmiya Sabha (Rammohun Roy)
1828Brahmo Sabha (Brahmo Samaj)
1829Sati abolished (Bentinck, Regulation XVII)
1828–31Young Bengal (Derozio)
1856Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act
1866Split: Brahmo Samaj of India (Keshab)
1867Prarthana Samaj (Bombay)
1872Native Marriage Act (Brahmo Marriage)
1873Satyashodhak Samaj (Phule)
1875Arya Samaj (Bombay); Theosophical Society (New York); MAO School, Aligarh
1878Sadharan Brahmo Samaj
1891Age of Consent Act
1897Ramakrishna Mission
1903SNDP Yogam
1924–25Vaikom satyagraha
1925Self-Respect movement (Periyar)
1929Sarda Act (Child Marriage Restraint Act)

Core notes

I35.1 Bengal: Rammohun Roy and the Brahmo Samaj

  • Raja Rammohun Roy (1772–1833): "father of the Indian Renaissance"; monotheism based on the Upanishads; Tuhfat-ul-Muwahhidin (Persian), Precepts of Jesus; campaigned against sati (abolished 1829), polygamy, idolatry; for women's property rights and English education (Hindu College 1817 support, Vedanta College 1825); newspapers Sambad Kaumudi, Mirat-ul-Akhbar; title "Raja" from Mughal emperor Akbar II; died at Bristol (1833).
  • Brahmo Samaj (1828): Debendranath Tagore (Tattvabodhini Sabha, 1839) revived it; Keshab Chandra Sen spread it; split in 1866 (Adi Brahmo Samaj vs Brahmo Samaj of India); Keshab's daughter's under-age marriage to the Cooch Behar prince led to the Sadharan Brahmo Samaj (1878).
  • Young Bengal: Henry Vivian Derozio of Hindu College — radical rationalism.
  • Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar: widow remarriage (Act of 1856), girls' schools, Bengali prose (Barna Parichay), against polygamy and child marriage.

I35.2 Western India

  • Prarthana Samaj (1867, Bombay): Atmaram Pandurang; M.G. Ranade, R.G. Bhandarkar; inter-caste dining, widow remarriage, women's education. Ranade founded the Widow Remarriage Association and the Deccan Education Society (with Tilak, Agarkar).
  • Jyotiba Phule: Satyashodhak Samaj (1873) — against Brahmin dominance; Gulamgiri (1873); girls' school at Pune (1848) with Savitribai Phule.
  • Gopal Hari Deshmukh ("Lokahitawadi"); D.K. Karve — widow home, SNDT women's university (1916); Pandita Ramabai — Arya Mahila Samaj, Sharada Sadan.
  • Behramji Malabari: campaign leading to the Age of Consent Act (1891).
  • Parsi reform: Rahnumai Mazdayasnan Sabha (1851; Dadabhai Naoroji, Naoroji Furdonji, S.S. Bengalee).

I35.3 Arya Samaj, Ramakrishna Mission, Theosophy

  • Swami Dayanand Saraswati: Arya Samaj (1875, Bombay; headquarters later Lahore); "Back to the Vedas"; rejected idolatry, ritualism, caste by birth, child marriage; Satyarth Prakash (first edition 1875; revised edition prepared at Udaipur, 1882); shuddhi (reconversion); DAV schools (Lahore, 1886; Lala Hansraj); split (1893) into the College group and the Gurukul group (Swami Shraddhanand, Gurukul Kangri, 1902). Died at Ajmer (1883); Paropkarini Sabha.
  • Ramakrishna Paramahamsa: priest at Dakshineswar; all religions lead to God.
  • Swami Vivekananda: Chicago Parliament of Religions (1893); Ramakrishna Mission (1897) — social service; "practical Vedanta"; inspired nationalism.
  • Theosophical Society: Madame Blavatsky and Colonel Olcott (New York, 1875); headquarters Adyar (1886); Annie Besant — Central Hindu School, Benaras (1898), later BHU; Home Rule League (I37).

I35.4 Muslim, Sikh and other reform

  • Sir Syed Ahmad Khan: Aligarh movement; Scientific Society (1864); MAO College (1875, Aligarh; university 1920); journal Tahzib-ul-Akhlaq; urged Muslims to adopt Western education and loyalty to the British; later opposed the Congress.
  • Deoband (1866, Muhammad Qasim Nanautavi, Rashid Ahmad Gangohi): orthodox revivalist; anti-British; supported the Congress.
  • Ahmadiya (1889, Mirza Ghulam Ahmad, Qadian).
  • Wahabi/Waliullahi movement (Syed Ahmad of Rae Bareli) — anti-British.
  • Singh Sabha (1873, Amritsar) — Sikh reform; Khalsa College; Akali movement (1920s, gurdwara reform; Sikh Gurdwaras Act 1925 — SGPC).

I35.5 Lower-caste and southern movements

  • Sree Narayana Guru (Kerala): SNDP Yogam (1903) for Ezhavas; "one caste, one religion, one God for man"; consecrated a Shiva idol at Aruvippuram (1888).
  • Vaikom satyagraha (1924–25): right to use roads near the temple; T.K. Madhavan, K.P. Kesava Menon; Gandhi visited; Guruvayur satyagraha (1931–32, K. Kelappan).
  • Periyar E.V. Ramasamy: Self-Respect movement (1925); Justice Party (1916).
  • Ayyankali (Kerala) — Sadhu Jana Paripalana Sangham; Pulaya rights.
  • B.R. Ambedkar: Bahishkrit Hitakarini Sabha (1924); Mahad satyagraha (1927); burned the Manusmriti; Independent Labour Party (1936); All India Scheduled Castes Federation (1942).
  • Gandhi: Harijan Sevak Sangh (1932).
  • Others in NCERT: Ghasidas founded the Satnami movement among leatherworkers in central India; Haridas Thakur's Matua sect worked among Chandala cultivators in east Bengal; Veerasalingam Pantulu formed a widow remarriage association in the Telugu areas; the Veda Samaj (Madras, 1864) followed the Brahmo model; the Paramhans Mandali (Bombay, 1840) worked against caste; Mumtaz Ali reread the Qur'an to argue for women's education.
  • Phule dedicated Gulamgiri (1873) to the Americans who fought to free slaves. Ambedkar led three temple-entry movements between 1927 and 1935. Periyar left the Congress after seeing caste-based seating at a nationalist feast.
  • Orthodox reaction: the Sanatan Dharma Sabhas, the Bharat Dharma Mahamandal and the Brahman Sabha (Bengal) defended caste as scriptural.

I35.6 Social legislation

LawYearAssociated with
Abolition of sati1829Bentinck, Rammohun Roy
Female infanticide banned1795, 1804, 1870—
Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act1856Dalhousie/Canning, Vidyasagar
Native Marriage Act1872Keshab Chandra Sen
Age of Consent Act1891Malabari
Sarda Act (Child Marriage Restraint)1929Harbilas Sarda (Ajmer) — girls 14, boys 18

I35.7 Assessment

Positive: rational and humanist outlook; better status for women; education; challenge to caste; pride in heritage; base for nationalism.

Limitations: confined to the urban educated upper castes; reliance on colonial legislation; revivalism sometimes sharpened communal identities (shuddhi, Aligarh's separatism); lower-caste critiques showed the limits of upper-caste reform.

What historians say

Sumit Sarkar critiqued the idea of a "Bengal Renaissance" as limited and dependent on colonial structures. Kenneth Jones classified movements as "transitional" and "acculturative". Gail Omvedt and Rosalind O'Hanlon studied Phule and anti-caste movements. Uma Chakravarti and Lata Mani (on sati debates) showed that women were often the site rather than the subject of reform debates.

Prelims fact bank

  • Ghasidas founded the Satnami movement among leatherworkers of central India.
  • Haridas Thakur's Matua sect worked among the Chandalas of east Bengal.
  • The Veda Samaj was founded in Madras in 1864.
  • Rammohun Roy founded the Atmiya Sabha in 1815 and the Brahmo Sabha in 1828.
  • Sati was abolished in 1829 by Lord William Bentinck.
  • Derozio led the Young Bengal movement.
  • Vidyasagar's campaign led to the Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act of 1856.
  • The Sadharan Brahmo Samaj was formed in 1878.
  • The Prarthana Samaj was founded in 1867 in Bombay.
  • Jyotiba Phule founded the Satyashodhak Samaj in 1873 and wrote Gulamgiri.
  • Dayanand Saraswati founded the Arya Samaj in 1875 in Bombay.
  • Dayanand Saraswati wrote Satyarth Prakash.
  • Dayanand Saraswati died at Ajmer in 1883.
  • Vivekananda founded the Ramakrishna Mission in 1897.
  • The Theosophical Society's headquarters is at Adyar.
  • Sir Syed Ahmad Khan founded the MAO College at Aligarh in 1875.
  • The Deoband school was founded in 1866.
  • Sree Narayana Guru founded the SNDP Yogam in 1903.
  • The Vaikom satyagraha took place in 1924–25.
  • Periyar started the Self-Respect movement in 1925.
  • Ambedkar led the Mahad satyagraha in 1927.
  • The Sarda Act of 1929 was proposed by Harbilas Sarda of Ajmer.
  • The Age of Consent Act was passed in 1891.
Exam traps
  • Phule's Gulamgiri was published in 1873, not 1871 as some NCERT-based guides print.
  • Sarda Act (1929): girls 14, boys 18 — not 16 for girls, as some NCERT-based guides print.
  • William Carey, of the Serampore mission, was an English Baptist, not Scottish (William Adam was the Scottish missionary).
  • Arya Samaj founded at Bombay (1875), not Lahore.
  • Theosophical Society founded at New York (1875); Adyar from 1886.
  • Prarthana Samaj (Bombay) ≠ Brahmo Samaj (Calcutta).
  • Aligarh (reformist, pro-British) ≠ Deoband (orthodox, anti-British).
  • Satyarth Prakash: Dayanand; Gulamgiri: Phule.
