No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Evaluate the contribution of Swami Dayanand Saraswati to Indian society.

How to frame it: Ideas → Social reform → Education → Nationalism → Limits

Model answer (85 words)

Dayanand founded the Arya Samaj (1875) with the call "Back to the Vedas", rejecting idolatry, ritualism, caste by birth and Brahmin monopoly. He opposed child marriage and supported widow remarriage and women's education; his Satyarth Prakash set out these ideas. His followers built DAV schools and the Gurukul Kangri. By asserting Vedic self-respect and swadeshi, he inspired nationalists such as Lajpat Rai. His work in Rajasthan — Udaipur, Ajmer, the Paropkarini Sabha — influenced princes. Yet shuddhi and polemics against other faiths sharpened communal boundaries.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the limitations of the 19th-century reform movements?

How to frame it: Social base → Dependence → Communal side

Model answer (44 words)

Reform was confined to urban, educated upper castes and barely touched peasants or lower castes; it relied on colonial laws; revivalist movements sometimes sharpened communal identities; and women were often objects of the debates rather than participants. Phule and Narayana Guru exposed these limits.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Satyashodhak Samaj?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (11 words)

Jyotiba Phule's society (1873) against Brahmin dominance and for lower-caste rights.

Revision card

Remember
  • Bengal: Rammohun (Brahmo 1828, sati 1829), Derozio, Vidyasagar (1856), Keshab, Sadharan 1878.
  • West: Prarthana 1867 (Ranade), Phule (Satyashodhak 1873), Karve, Ramabai, Malabari (1891).
  • Arya Samaj 1875 (Dayanand, Satyarth Prakash); RK Mission 1897; Theosophy 1875 (Besant).
  • Muslim: Aligarh 1875, Deoband 1866, Ahmadiya 1889. Sikh: Singh Sabha 1873, Akali.
  • Caste: SNDP 1903, Vaikom 1924, Self-Respect 1925, Mahad 1927.
  • Laws: 1829, 1856, 1872, 1891, 1929.

Part III · Unit I36Early Nationalism and the Moderates1836 – 1905

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Pre-Congress associations; foundation of the INC; Moderate leaders, methods and demands

Mains: GS-I: Rise of nationalism; foundation of the Congress (safety-valve debate); assessment of the Moderates

The safety-valve theory and the assessment of the Moderates are classic Mains questions.

The big picture

Modern Indian nationalism arose from the contradictions of colonial rule — economic exploitation, racial discrimination, administrative unification, modern education, the press and the reform movements. Political associations in the presidency towns (from the 1830s) led to the Indian National Congress (1885). Its early leaders, the Moderates (1885–1905), believed in British justice, used constitutional methods (petitions, resolutions, speeches) and built the economic critique of colonialism.

Exam focus

Assess the Moderates fairly: limited immediate gains but vital foundations — political education, economic critique, a national platform.

WhenWhat happened
1836Bangabhasha Prakasika Sabha
1838Zamindari Association (Landholders' Society)
1851British Indian Association (Calcutta)
1852Madras Native Association; Bombay Association
1866East India Association, London (Naoroji)
1870Poona Sarvajanik Sabha (Ranade)
1876Indian Association (S.N. Banerjea, A.M. Bose)
1876–80Lytton: Vernacular Press Act, Arms Act, Delhi Durbar during famine
1883Ilbert Bill controversy; National Conference
1884Madras Mahajan Sabha
1885Bombay Presidency Association; INC founded (28 December, Bombay)
1892Indian Councils Act
1905Partition of Bengal

Core notes

I36.1 Causes of the rise of nationalism

  1. Colonial economic exploitation affected all classes.
  2. Political and administrative unification; railways, telegraph, post.
  3. Western education and ideas of liberty, democracy, nationalism.
  4. Press and literature (Amrita Bazar Patrika, The Hindu, Kesari, Bangadarshan).
  5. Rediscovery of India's past (Orientalist scholarship).
  6. Socio-religious reform movements.
  7. Racial arrogance (Ilbert Bill, 1883) and reactionary policies of Lytton (VPA, Arms Act 1878, lowering ICS age, Delhi Durbar 1877 during famine, removal of cotton import duties).

I36.2 Pre-Congress associations

  • Bengal: Bangabhasha Prakasika Sabha (1836), Landholders' Society (1838, Dwarkanath Tagore), British India Society (1843), British Indian Association (1851), Indian Association (1876, Surendranath Banerjea, Ananda Mohan Bose) — the most important; agitated on ICS age; held the Indian National Conference (1883, 1885).
  • Bombay: Bombay Association (1852); Poona Sarvajanik Sabha (1870, M.G. Ranade, G.V. Joshi); Bombay Presidency Association (1885, Pherozeshah Mehta, Badruddin Tyabji, K.T. Telang).
  • Madras: Madras Native Association (1852); Madras Mahajan Sabha (1884, M. Viraraghavachari, G. Subramania Iyer, P. Ananda Charlu).
  • London: East India Association (1866, Dadabhai Naoroji).

I36.3 Foundation of the Congress

  • Founded on 28 December 1885 at Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College, Bombay (shifted from Poona due to cholera); 72 delegates; first president W.C. Bonnerjee; A.O. Hume, retired ICS officer, as organiser and first general secretary.
  • Viceroy Dufferin initially welcomed it, later called it a "microscopic minority".
  • Early presidents: Dadabhai Naoroji (1886, Calcutta; also 1893, 1906), Badruddin Tyabji (1887, Madras — first Muslim president), George Yule (1888 — first English president), Annie Besant (1917 — first woman), Sarojini Naidu (1925 — first Indian woman).
Debate

Safety-valve theory: Lala Lajpat Rai (Young India, 1916) and later R.P. Dutt argued that Hume founded the Congress, with Dufferin's blessing, as a safety valve to release Indian discontent and prevent revolt (Hume's "seven volumes" of secret reports). Bipan Chandra rejects this as a myth: Indian leaders used Hume as a "lightning conductor" to avoid official hostility, and the Congress was the culmination of a political awakening already under way. The "Cambridge school" (Anil Seal) saw it as a product of elite competition for patronage.

I36.4 The Moderates (1885–1905)

Leaders: Dadabhai Naoroji ("Grand Old Man of India"), Pherozeshah Mehta, Surendranath Banerjea ("Rashtraguru", "Surrender-not"), Gopal Krishna Gokhale (Servants of India Society, 1905; political guru of Gandhi), M.G. Ranade, Dinshaw Wacha, Badruddin Tyabji, R.C. Dutt, W.C. Bonnerjee, Madan Mohan Malaviya.

Methods: constitutional — petitions, prayers, protests ("3 Ps" — a later critics' label); resolutions, memoranda, meetings; British Committee of the INC (1889) and journal India in London.

Demands:

  • Economic: reduce land revenue; end the drain; protect Indian industry; reduce military expenditure; famine relief.
  • Constitutional: expansion and reform of councils; elected members; Indian control over the budget — self-government within the empire later (Naoroji's "swaraj" at Calcutta, 1906).
  • Administrative: Indianisation of services, simultaneous ICS exams, separation of judiciary and executive, repeal of the Arms Act, expansion of education.
  • Civil rights: freedom of speech, press and association.

Achievements: economic critique (drain theory); political education and a national platform; Indian Councils Act (1892); a resolution in the House of Commons (1893) for simultaneous ICS exams; Welby Commission (1895). Naoroji became the first Indian member of the British House of Commons (1892, Finsbury Central).

Limitations: narrow social base (urban educated middle class); faith in British justice; no mass mobilisation; little immediate success; led to the rise of the Extremists.

I36.5 Reference: important Congress sessions, 1885–1948

What historians say

Bipan Chandra (India's Struggle for Independence) defends the Moderates' achievement in building an anti-colonial ideology. Anil Seal (The Emergence of Indian Nationalism) presented early nationalism as competition among regional elites. S.R. Mehrotra (The Emergence of the Indian National Congress) traced the Congress's roots in earlier associations.

  • A.O. Hume was the first general secretary of the INC.
  • The first session of the INC had 72 delegates.

Exam traps
  • First session: Bombay (not Poona — shifted due to cholera).
  • Safety-valve theory: Lajpat Rai, R.P. Dutt; rejected by Bipan Chandra ("lightning conductor").
  • First woman president: Annie Besant (1917); first Indian woman: Sarojini Naidu (1925).
  • Indian Association (1876) ≠ British Indian Association (1851).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Assess the role of the Moderates in the national movement.

How to frame it: Leaders and methods → Demands → Achievements → Limitations → Conclusion

Model answer (91 words)

Moderates such as Naoroji, Gokhale, Banerjea and Ranade used petitions, resolutions and speeches, trusting British justice. They demanded lower land revenue, an end to the drain, Indianisation of services, expanded councils and civil liberties. Their drain theory exposed colonial exploitation; they gave Indians political education and a national platform, and won the Indian Councils Act (1892). But their base was the urban educated class, they avoided mass mobilisation and achieved little concrete success, which produced the Extremist reaction. Still, they laid the ideological foundation on which later mass movements were built.

5 marks · 50 words

Examine the safety-valve theory of the foundation of the Congress.

How to frame it: Theory → Critique

Model answer (46 words)

Lajpat Rai and R.P. Dutt argued that Hume founded the Congress with Dufferin's approval to release Indian discontent safely. Bipan Chandra rejects this: Indian leaders used Hume as a "lightning conductor", and the Congress grew out of a political awakening already under way in earlier associations.

2 marks · 15 words

Who founded the Indian Association?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (9 words)

Surendranath Banerjea and Ananda Mohan Bose, in Calcutta, 1876.

Revision card

Remember
  • Causes: exploitation, unification, education, press, reform, Lytton, Ilbert Bill.
  • Associations: BIA 1851, East India Assoc. 1866, Poona Sarvajanik 1870, Indian Assoc. 1876, Madras Mahajan 1884.
  • INC: 28 Dec 1885, Bombay, 72 delegates, Bonnerjee, Hume.
  • Safety valve: Lajpat Rai vs Bipan Chandra.
  • Moderates: methods, demands, 1892 Act; limits.

Part III · Unit I37Swadeshi and the Extremists1905 – 1918

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Partition of Bengal; Swadeshi and boycott; Surat split; Muslim League; Morley-Minto; Lucknow Pact; Home Rule Leagues

Mains: GS-I: Swadeshi movement; Extremists vs Moderates; Home Rule movement

Swadeshi movement and the Surat split are high-yield; the Lucknow Pact and Home Rule are frequent Prelims topics.

The big picture

Dissatisfaction with Moderate methods, Curzon's policies and international events produced the Extremists — Lal, Bal, Pal (Lajpat Rai, Tilak, Bipin Chandra Pal) and Aurobindo Ghosh — who demanded swaraj and used mass methods. Curzon's partition of Bengal (1905) triggered the Swadeshi movement, the first mass movement of modern India. The Surat split (1907), repression and the Morley-Minto reforms followed. During the First World War, the Lucknow Pact (1916) reunited the Congress and brought it together with the Muslim League, and the Home Rule Leagues revived agitation.

Exam focus

Explain why the Extremists emerged, what Swadeshi achieved and why it declined.

WhenWhat happened
1899–1905Curzon Viceroy
1904Indian Universities Act; Official Secrets Act
1905Partition of Bengal announced (July); effective 16 October; boycott resolution at Calcutta Town Hall (7 August)
1905Benaras Congress (Gokhale)
1906Muslim League founded at Dacca (30 December); Calcutta Congress — swaraj (Naoroji)
1907Surat split
1908Tilak sentenced to six years (Mandalay)
1909Morley-Minto Reforms
1911Partition annulled; capital to Delhi announced (Delhi Durbar)
1914Tilak released; First World War
1916Home Rule Leagues; Lucknow Pact; Congress reunited
1917Montagu's August Declaration

Core notes

I37.1 Rise of the Extremists

  1. Failure of Moderate methods to win concessions.
  2. Curzon's reactionary policies: Calcutta Corporation Act (1899), Official Secrets Act (1904), Indian Universities Act (1904), partition of Bengal (1905).
  3. Famines and plague (1896–1900) and harsh plague measures in Pune (Rand's murder, 1897; Chapekar brothers).
  4. International: Japan's defeat of Russia (1905), Ethiopia's defeat of Italy (Adwa, 1896), nationalist movements in Ireland, Egypt, Turkey, Persia.
  5. Growth of self-confidence and pride in Indian culture (Vivekananda, Dayanand, Bankim).

Ideas: swaraj as the goal; self-reliance; mass participation; passive resistance; boycott and swadeshi; national education. Tilak: "Swaraj is my birthright and I shall have it"; Ganapati (1893) and Shivaji (1895) festivals; papers Kesari and Mahratta.

I37.2 Partition of Bengal and the Swadeshi movement

  • Announced July 1905 by Curzon; effective 16 October 1905: Eastern Bengal and Assam (capital Dacca), Muslim majority; western Bengal with Bihar and Odisha — Bengali speakers became a minority.
  • Official reason: administrative convenience; real motive (Risley's notes): "Bengal united is a power; Bengal divided will pull in several different ways" — weaken Bengali nationalism and divide Hindus and Muslims.
  • Boycott resolution at the Calcutta Town Hall, 7 August 1905.
  • 16 October observed as a day of mourning; raksha bandhan as a symbol of unity; Amar Sonar Bangla (Tagore).
  • Methods: boycott of British goods, swadeshi industries (Bengal Chemicals of P.C. Ray, Banga Lakshmi cotton mills, swadeshi match and soap factories), national education (Bengal National College, 1906, Aurobindo principal; National Council of Education), samitis (Swadesh Bandhab Samiti of Ashwini Kumar Dutta, Barisal), social boycott, traditional festivals and folk theatre (jatras), songs (Tagore, Rajanikanta Sen, Mukunda Das).
  • Spread: Tilak (Bombay, Poona), Lajpat Rai and Ajit Singh (Punjab), Syed Haider Raza (Delhi), V.O. Chidambaram Pillai (Swadeshi Steam Navigation Company, Tuticorin).
  • Decline (after 1908): repression; Surat split; lack of an effective organisation; failure to involve peasants and Muslims (the Muslim peasantry of east Bengal was largely alienated; Nawab Salimullah backed partition); leaders arrested or exiled (Tilak to Mandalay; Aurobindo withdrew to Pondicherry; Pal retired).
  • Significance: first mass movement; brought students, women and some workers into politics; boost to indigenous industry and culture; tested methods (boycott, non-cooperation, passive resistance) later used by Gandhi.

I37.3 Surat split (1907) and after

  • At Calcutta (1906), under Naoroji, compromise resolutions on swaraj, swadeshi, boycott and national education.
  • At Surat (1907): Extremists wanted Lajpat Rai or Tilak as president; Moderates chose Rash Behari Ghosh; fear that the four resolutions would be dropped; the session broke up in chaos.
  • The government repressed the Extremists (Seditious Meetings Act 1907, Newspapers Act 1908, Explosive Substances Act, Tilak sentenced 1908) and conciliated the Moderates with the Morley-Minto Reforms (1909) — "rally the Moderates".
  • Muslim League (Dacca, 30 December 1906): Aga Khan led the Simla deputation (1 October 1906) to Minto seeking separate electorates; founded under Nawab Salimullah of Dacca, Aga Khan first president; Morley-Minto granted separate electorates (1909).
  • Annulment of partition and transfer of the capital to Delhi announced at the Delhi Durbar (12 December 1911) of George V.

I37.4 Lucknow Pact and the Home Rule movement

  • Lucknow session (1916), president A.C. Majumdar: Moderates and Extremists reunited.
  • Lucknow Pact (Congress–League): joint demand for self-government and reforms; Congress accepted separate electorates for Muslims; Tilak and Jinnah played key roles. Gave the League political legitimacy.
  • Home Rule Leagues (1916), on the Irish model:
    • Tilak's League (April 1916, Belgaum): Maharashtra (except Bombay city), Karnataka, Central Provinces, Berar.
    • Annie Besant's League (September 1916, Madras): the rest of India; papers New India and Commonweal; George Arundale, B.P. Wadia, C.P. Ramaswami Aiyar.
    • Aim: home rule (self-government within the empire); methods: meetings, newspapers, libraries, pamphlets.
    • Besant interned (June 1917), released after protest; elected Congress president (1917).
    • Decline after Montagu's August Declaration (1917) — "gradual development of self-governing institutions, with a view to the progressive realisation of responsible government".

What historians say

Sumit Sarkar (The Swadeshi Movement in Bengal 1903–1908) identified four trends — moderate, constructive swadeshi, political extremism, revolutionary terrorism — and analysed why the movement failed to reach the Muslim peasantry. Bipan Chandra stressed its role in creating mass politics. Hugh Owen and H.F. Owen studied the Home Rule movement.

Prelims fact bank

  • The partition of Bengal came into effect on 16 October 1905.
  • The boycott resolution was passed at the Calcutta Town Hall on 7 August 1905.
  • Tagore composed Amar Sonar Bangla during the Swadeshi movement.
  • P.C. Ray founded Bengal Chemicals.
  • V.O. Chidambaram Pillai founded the Swadeshi Steam Navigation Company at Tuticorin.
  • Ashwini Kumar Dutta founded the Swadesh Bandhab Samiti in Barisal.
  • The Muslim League was founded at Dacca on 30 December 1906.
  • The Aga Khan led the Simla deputation of 1 October 1906.
  • At the 1906 Calcutta session Dadabhai Naoroji declared swaraj as the goal.
  • The Congress split at Surat in 1907.
  • Rash Behari Ghosh presided over the Surat session.
  • Tilak was sentenced to six years in Mandalay jail in 1908.
  • The partition of Bengal was annulled in 1911.
  • Moderates and Extremists reunited at Lucknow in 1916.
  • Tilak's Home Rule League was founded in April 1916 at Belgaum.
  • Annie Besant's Home Rule League was founded in September 1916 at Madras.
  • Montagu's August Declaration was made in 1917.
Exam traps
  • Partition announced July 1905, effective 16 October 1905, annulled 1911.
  • Surat (1907) split; Lucknow (1916) reunion and pact.
  • Lucknow Pact: Congress accepted separate electorates.
  • Tilak's League first (April 1916); Besant's (September 1916).
  • Muslim League first president: Aga Khan; founded at Dacca.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Discuss the methods and significance of the Swadeshi movement.

How to frame it: Cause → Methods → Spread → Decline → Significance

Model answer (93 words)

Curzon's partition of Bengal (1905), meant to divide Bengali nationalists, sparked the Swadeshi movement. Its methods were boycott of British goods, swadeshi industries such as Bengal Chemicals, national education, samitis, festivals, songs and social boycott; it spread to Maharashtra, Punjab, Delhi and Tamil Nadu. It declined after 1908 because of repression, the Surat split, weak organisation and the failure to draw in peasants and Muslims. Yet it was the first mass movement, brought students and women into politics, encouraged indigenous industry and culture, and tested boycott and passive resistance later used by Gandhi.

5 marks · 50 words

Why did the Congress split at Surat in 1907?

How to frame it: Differences → Immediate cause → Result

Model answer (49 words)

Moderates and Extremists differed on goals and methods — reform versus swaraj, petitions versus boycott and mass action. At Surat the Moderates chose Rash Behari Ghosh as president and seemed set to dilute the Calcutta resolutions; the session broke up. The government repressed the Extremists and wooed the Moderates.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Lucknow Pact?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (9 words)

1916 Congress–League agreement; joint demands; Congress accepted separate electorates.

Revision card

Remember
  • Extremists: Lal-Bal-Pal, Aurobindo; causes; swaraj.
  • Partition: July 1905, 16 Oct 1905; boycott 7 Aug 1905; annulled 1911.
  • Swadeshi methods; spread; decline; significance.
  • Muslim League 1906 (Dacca, Aga Khan); Surat 1907; Morley-Minto 1909.
  • Lucknow Pact 1916; Home Rule: Tilak (April 1916), Besant (Sept 1916); August Declaration 1917.

Self-test

    Show answer

    16 October 1905.

  2. Who was the Viceroy at the time of partition?
    Show answer

    Lord Curzon.

  3. Where was the boycott resolution passed?
    Show answer

    Calcutta Town Hall, 7 August 1905.

  4. Where was the Muslim League founded?
    Show answer

    Dacca (1906).

  5. Who presided over the Surat session?
    Show answer

    Rash Behari Ghosh.

  6. When was the partition annulled?
    Show answer

    1911.

  7. What did the Congress accept in the Lucknow Pact?
    Show answer

    Separate electorates for Muslims.

  8. Which newspapers did Annie Besant run?
    Show answer

    New India and Commonweal.

Part III · Unit I38Revolutionary Nationalism1897 – 1934

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Anushilan, Jugantar; Abhinav Bharat; Ghadar; HRA/HSRA; Kakori; Bhagat Singh; Chittagong; revolutionaries abroad

Mains: GS-I: Revolutionary movements — phases, ideology, contribution

Ghadar, HSRA and Bhagat Singh are the most-asked. Rajasthan revolutionaries (Barhat family, Arjun Lal Sethi, Rao Gopal Singh Kharwa) are in R19.

The big picture

Revolutionary nationalism developed in two waves. The first wave (1905–1918) grew out of the Swadeshi movement's frustration: secret societies in Bengal and Maharashtra used assassination and dacoity to finance activities, while revolutionaries abroad (India House, Ghadar Party) tried to organise armed revolt, especially during the First World War. The second wave (1920s–1934), after the withdrawal of Non-Cooperation, produced the HRA/HSRA in north India — with a socialist ideology under Bhagat Singh — and the Chittagong armoury raid in Bengal.

Exam focus

Assess their contribution: they failed militarily but inspired patriotism and pushed national politics towards complete independence and socialism.

WhenWhat happened
1897Chapekar brothers kill Rand (Poona)
1902Anushilan Samiti (Calcutta, Midnapore)
1904Abhinav Bharat (V.D. Savarkar)
1905India House, London (Shyamji Krishna Varma)
1906Jugantar (paper and group)
1908Muzaffarpur bomb (Khudiram Bose, Prafulla Chaki); Alipore conspiracy case
1909Madan Lal Dhingra kills Curzon Wyllie (London); Jackson killed (Nasik)
1912Bomb on Lord Hardinge in Delhi (Rash Behari Bose)
1913Ghadar Party (San Francisco)
1914Komagata Maru incident
1915Ghadar plan for February revolt; Singapore mutiny; Provisional Government at Kabul
1924Hindustan Republican Association (Kanpur)
1925Kakori train robbery (9 August)
1928HSRA (Feroz Shah Kotla, Delhi); Saunders killed (Lahore)
1929Bomb in the Central Legislative Assembly (8 April)
1930Chittagong armoury raid (18 April)
1931Bhagat Singh, Rajguru, Sukhdev executed (23 March); Azad killed (27 February)

Core notes

I38.1 First wave in India

  • Maharashtra: Chapekar brothers (Damodar and Balkrishna) killed plague commissioner Rand (1897); Abhinav Bharat (1904, V.D. and Ganesh Savarkar); Nasik conspiracy (Jackson's murder, 1909).
  • Bengal: Anushilan Samiti (Pramathanath Mitra; Barindra Kumar Ghosh, Aurobindo's brother); Jugantar (paper, 1906); Dacca Anushilan (Pulin Behari Das).
  • Muzaffarpur (1908): Khudiram Bose and Prafulla Chaki threw a bomb meant for judge Kingsford; Khudiram hanged.
  • Alipore (Maniktala) bomb case (1908): Aurobindo acquitted (defended by C.R. Das); Barindra sentenced.
  • Delhi conspiracy (1912): bomb on Viceroy Hardinge during the state entry into Delhi — Rash Behari Bose, Sachindranath Sanyal.
  • Jatin Mukherjee (Bagha Jatin): planned a German-aided uprising (1915); killed at Balasore.

I38.2 Revolutionaries abroad

  • India House, London (1905, Shyamji Krishna Varma; journal Indian Sociologist); Savarkar wrote The Indian War of Independence of 1857; Madan Lal Dhingra killed Curzon Wyllie (1909).
  • Paris and Europe: Madame Bhikaji Cama (unfurled an Indian flag at Stuttgart, 1907), S.R. Rana; Virendranath Chattopadhyay; Berlin Committee (1915).
  • Ghadar Party (1913, San Francisco): Lala Har Dayal, Sohan Singh Bhakna (president), Ramchandra, Barkatullah, Kartar Singh Sarabha, Bhai Parmanand; weekly Ghadar (from 1 November 1913); mostly Punjabi Sikh peasants and ex-soldiers on the west coast of North America.
  • Komagata Maru (1914): Japanese ship carrying Indian emigrants (Gurdit Singh) refused entry at Vancouver; on return, clash at Budge Budge (Calcutta).
  • Ghadar plan (1915): armed revolt fixed for 21 February 1915; betrayed; Lahore conspiracy cases; Kartar Singh Sarabha hanged. Singapore mutiny (5th Light Infantry, February 1915).
  • Provisional Government of India at Kabul (1915): Raja Mahendra Pratap (president), Barkatullah (prime minister), Ubaidullah Sindhi.

I38.3 Second wave: HRA/HSRA

  • Hindustan Republican Association (October 1924, Kanpur): Sachindranath Sanyal (author of Bandi Jeevan), Jogesh Chatterjee, Ram Prasad Bismil; aim — federal republic of the United States of India.
  • Kakori train robbery (9 August 1925): government treasury looted near Lucknow; Bismil, Ashfaqulla Khan, Roshan Singh and Rajendra Lahiri hanged (1927); Chandrashekhar Azad escaped.
  • HSRA (September 1928, Feroz Shah Kotla, Delhi): Chandrashekhar Azad, Bhagat Singh, Sukhdev, Bhagwati Charan Vohra; "Socialist" added — aim: socialist republic; Naujawan Bharat Sabha (1926, Lahore).
  • Saunders killing (December 1928, Lahore): revenge for the death of Lala Lajpat Rai after the lathi charge during the Simon Commission protest (meant for Scott).
  • Assembly bomb (8 April 1929): Bhagat Singh and Batukeshwar Dutt threw harmless bombs in the Central Legislative Assembly against the Public Safety Bill and Trade Disputes Bill — "to make the deaf hear"; courted arrest; used the trial for propaganda ("Inquilab Zindabad").
  • Lahore conspiracy case: Bhagat Singh, Rajguru, Sukhdev hanged on 23 March 1931 (Hussainiwala). Jatin Das died after a 64-day hunger strike (1929).
  • Bhagwati Charan Vohra wrote The Philosophy of the Bomb (reply to Gandhi's Cult of the Bomb).
  • Bhagat Singh's Why I am an Atheist; he declared revolution means the overthrow of exploitation, not just violence.
  • Azad died in Alfred Park, Allahabad (27 February 1931).

I38.4 Bengal in the 1930s

  • Chittagong armoury raid (18 April 1930): Surya Sen ("Masterda"), Indian Republican Army; Jalalabad hill battle; Surya Sen hanged (1934).
  • Women revolutionaries: Pritilata Waddedar (Pahartali European Club attack, 1932), Kalpana Dutt, Bina Das (fired at the Governor, 1932), Santi Ghosh and Suniti Chaudhury (shot a magistrate in Comilla, 1931).
  • Writers' Building attack (1930, Binoy, Badal, Dinesh).

I38.5 Assessment

Why they failed: no mass base; small secret groups; ruthless repression; lack of arms; reliance on individual action.

Contribution: sacrifice inspired youth; pushed Congress towards purna swaraj; Bhagat Singh introduced socialist ideas; kept nationalism alive in periods of lull.

What historians say

Bipan Chandra and Manmathnath Gupta wrote on HSRA ideology; S. Irfan Habib (To Make the Deaf Hear) analysed Bhagat Singh's socialism. Peter Heehs (The Bomb in Bengal) studied early Bengal revolutionaries. Harish Puri wrote on the Ghadar movement. Older official records (Rowlatt/Sedition Committee Report, 1918) described revolutionaries as terrorists — a term avoided by most Indian historians today.

Prelims fact bank

  • Sachindranath Sanyal, a founder of the HRA, wrote Bandi Jeevan.
  • The Chapekar brothers killed Rand in 1897.
  • V.D. Savarkar founded Abhinav Bharat in 1904.
  • Shyamji Krishna Varma founded India House in London in 1905.
  • Khudiram Bose and Prafulla Chaki threw the Muzaffarpur bomb in 1908.
  • Madan Lal Dhingra killed Curzon Wyllie in London in 1909.
  • Madame Bhikaji Cama unfurled an Indian flag at Stuttgart in 1907.
  • Rash Behari Bose planned the bomb attack on Lord Hardinge in 1912.
  • The Ghadar Party was founded in 1913 in San Francisco.
  • Lala Har Dayal was the leading figure of the Ghadar Party.
  • The Komagata Maru incident occurred in 1914.
  • Raja Mahendra Pratap headed the Provisional Government at Kabul in 1915.
  • The Kakori train robbery took place on 9 August 1925.
  • Ram Prasad Bismil and Ashfaqulla Khan were hanged in the Kakori case.
  • The HSRA was founded at Feroz Shah Kotla in 1928.
  • Bhagat Singh and Batukeshwar Dutt threw bombs in the Central Legislative Assembly on 8 April 1929.
  • Bhagat Singh, Rajguru and Sukhdev were hanged on 23 March 1931.

  • Surya Sen led the Chittagong armoury raid on 18 April 1930.
  • Pritilata Waddedar attacked the Pahartali European Club in 1932.
Exam traps
  • HRA (1924, Kanpur) → HSRA (1928, Delhi) — "Socialist" added.
  • Saunders was killed by mistake for Scott (Lajpat Rai's death).
  • Assembly bomb was not meant to kill.
  • Philosophy of the Bomb: Bhagwati Charan Vohra.
  • Ghadar: San Francisco; India House: London.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Assess the contribution of Bhagat Singh and the HSRA to the national movement.

How to frame it: Origins → Actions → Ideology → Impact

Model answer (95 words)

The HSRA (1928), founded by Azad, Bhagat Singh and Sukhdev, renamed the HRA to aim at a socialist republic. After Lajpat Rai's death it killed Saunders; on 8 April 1929 Bhagat Singh and B.K. Dutt threw harmless bombs in the Assembly "to make the deaf hear" and used their trial for propaganda. Jatin Das's fast and the executions of 23 March 1931 roused the country. Bhagat Singh saw revolution as the end of exploitation, not mere violence, and introduced socialist thought. Though militarily ineffective, the HSRA inspired youth and radicalised the struggle for purna swaraj.

5 marks · 50 words

What was the Ghadar movement?

How to frame it: Founding → Plan → Result

Model answer (47 words)

Founded in San Francisco (1913) by Har Dayal, Sohan Singh Bhakna and others among Punjabi emigrants, the Ghadar Party planned an armed revolt in India for 21 February 1915 during the war. Betrayed, it was crushed in the Lahore conspiracy cases, but it spread secular, radical nationalism.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Komagata Maru incident?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (15 words)

1914: ship of Indian emigrants refused at Vancouver; returning passengers fired on at Budge Budge.

  • First wave: Chapekars 1897, Abhinav Bharat 1904, Anushilan, Jugantar, Muzaffarpur 1908, Alipore, Hardinge 1912.
  • Abroad: India House 1905, Dhingra 1909, Cama 1907, Ghadar 1913, Komagata Maru 1914, Kabul 1915.
  • Second wave: HRA 1924, Kakori 1925, HSRA 1928, Saunders, Assembly bomb 1929, 23 March 1931.
  • Chittagong 1930 (Surya Sen); Pritilata, Kalpana Dutt, Bina Das.

Part III · Unit I39Gandhi's Early Movements and Non-Cooperation1915 – 1922

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Gandhi in South Africa; Champaran, Ahmedabad, Kheda; Rowlatt satyagraha; Jallianwala Bagh; Khilafat; Non-Cooperation; Chauri Chaura

Mains: GS-I: Gandhian methods; Non-Cooperation and Khilafat — significance and withdrawal

Non-Cooperation, its withdrawal, and Gandhian satyagraha are among the most-asked modern topics.

The big picture

Mohandas Karamchand Gandhi (1869–1948) developed satyagraha — non-violent resistance based on truth — in South Africa (1893–1914). Returning to India in 1915, he tested it locally (Champaran, Ahmedabad, Kheda), then nationally against the Rowlatt Act (1919). After Jallianwala Bagh and the Khilafat issue, he led the Non-Cooperation Movement (1920–22), the first all-India mass movement, uniting Hindus and Muslims, and transforming the Congress into a mass organisation. He withdrew it after Chauri Chaura (February 1922).

Exam focus

Know Gandhi's method (satyagraha, ahimsa, constructive work, struggle–truce–struggle) and the debate over the withdrawal of Non-Cooperation.

WhenWhat happened
1893Gandhi in South Africa; Pietermaritzburg incident
1894Natal Indian Congress
1904Phoenix Settlement; Indian Opinion (1903)
1906Satyagraha against the Asiatic registration ordinance
1909Hind Swaraj
1910Tolstoy Farm
9 January 1915Return to India (Pravasi Bharatiya Divas)
1917Champaran
1918Ahmedabad mill strike; Kheda
1919Rowlatt Act (March); satyagraha (6 April); Jallianwala Bagh (13 April)
1919Khilafat Committee; Hunter Committee
1920Non-Cooperation launched (1 August); Calcutta special session (September); Nagpur session (December)
1921Prince of Wales's visit boycotted (November); Moplah rebellion
5 February 1922Chauri Chaura
12 February 1922Bardoli resolution: NCM withdrawn
March 1922Gandhi sentenced to six years

Core notes

I39.1 Gandhi in South Africa and his method

  • Went to South Africa as a lawyer (Dada Abdullah's case, 1893); thrown off a train at Pietermaritzburg.
  • Founded the Natal Indian Congress (1894), Indian Opinion (1903), Phoenix Settlement (1904), Tolstoy Farm (1910).
  • Satyagraha against the registration certificates (passes), the £3 tax and non-recognition of Indian marriages; Smuts–Gandhi agreement (1914).
  • Influences: the Gita, Jainism, Tolstoy (The Kingdom of God is Within You), Ruskin (Unto This Last), Thoreau (civil disobedience).
  • Satyagraha: truth, non-violence, readiness to suffer, conversion (not coercion) of the opponent.
  • Strategy: mass mobilisation including peasants and women; struggle–truce–struggle (S–T–S, Bipan Chandra's term); constructive programme (khadi, Hindu–Muslim unity, removal of untouchability, prohibition, basic education).
  • Hind Swaraj (1909): critique of modern civilisation.

I39.2 Early satyagrahas in India

MovementIssueResult
Champaran (1917)Tinkathia indigo systemInquiry; tinkathia abolished; 25% refund
Ahmedabad (1918)Mill workers' plague bonus dispute; Gandhi's first hunger strike; Anasuya Sarabhai (mill owner Ambalal's sister) supported workers35% wage increase via tribunal
Kheda (1918)Revenue remission in crop failure; Patel, Indulal YagnikRevenue collected only from those able to pay

Gandhi also campaigned for army recruitment in 1918 (in hope of post-war self-rule) — criticised later.

I39.3 Rowlatt satyagraha and Jallianwala Bagh

  • Rowlatt Act (Anarchical and Revolutionary Crimes Act, March 1919), based on the Sedition Committee (Justice Rowlatt) report: detention without trial up to two years; trial without jury — "no vakil, no dalil, no appeal".
  • Gandhi formed the Satyagraha Sabha; nationwide hartal on 6 April 1919 — first all-India mass protest; some violence; Gandhi called it a "Himalayan blunder" to launch it before people were trained.
  • Arrest of Dr Saifuddin Kitchlew and Dr Satyapal in Amritsar.
  • Jallianwala Bagh, 13 April 1919 (Baisakhi): General Reginald Dyer ordered firing on an unarmed crowd in an enclosed ground; official toll 379 (Congress estimate about 1,000). Lt-Governor Michael O'Dwyer; martial law in Punjab.
  • Reactions: Tagore renounced his knighthood; Gandhi returned the Kaiser-i-Hind medal (1920); Sankaran Nair resigned from the Viceroy's council.
  • Hunter Committee (official) censured Dyer mildly; the House of Lords praised him; Congress inquiry committee (Motilal Nehru, C.R. Das, Gandhi and others).
  • Udham Singh shot O'Dwyer at Caxton Hall, London (13 March 1940).

I39.4 Khilafat and Non-Cooperation

  • Khilafat issue: after World War I, the Ottoman Sultan (Caliph) was humiliated by the Treaty of Sèvres; Ali brothers (Muhammad Ali, Shaukat Ali), Maulana Azad, Hakim Ajmal Khan, Hasrat Mohani formed the Khilafat Committee (1919); Gandhi saw an opportunity for Hindu–Muslim unity.
  • Khilafat Committee adopted non-cooperation (June 1920); launched 1 August 1920 (the day Tilak died).
  • Congress approved it at the special session at Calcutta (September 1920, Lala Lajpat Rai) and confirmed it at Nagpur (December 1920, C. Vijayaraghavachariar), where the goal became "swaraj by peaceful and legitimate means"; Congress reorganised — linguistic Provincial Congress Committees, Congress Working Committee, four-anna membership.
  • Programme: surrender of titles; boycott of government schools, colleges, law courts, legislatures (1920 elections boycotted) and foreign cloth; national schools (Jamia Millia Islamia, Kashi Vidyapith, Gujarat Vidyapith, Bihar Vidyapith); swadeshi and khadi; Tilak Swaraj Fund (Rs 1 crore); later non-payment of taxes.
  • Participation: lawyers (Motilal Nehru, C.R. Das, Rajendra Prasad, Patel), students, peasants (Awadh, Bengal, Andhra — Chirala-Perala, Pedanandipadu no-tax), workers, tribals; Akali movement (Punjab); Gandhi promised "swaraj within a year".
  • Regional forms (NCERT): in Awadh the sanyasi Baba Ramchandra led tenants against taluqdars' high rents, cesses and begar; panchayats organised nai–dhobi bandhs (denying landlords barbers and washermen); the Oudh Kisan Sabha (October 1920, with Jawaharlal Nehru) took up their grievances; in 1921 taluqdars' houses and grain stores were attacked. In Guntur (Andhra) tribals and poor peasants held forest satyagrahas, grazing cattle without fees. In Assam tea-garden workers, bound by the Inland Emigration Act (1859), which barred them from leaving without permission, left the plantations for home shouting "Gandhi Maharaj ki Jai". In Madras the non-Brahman Justice Party did not boycott the council elections.
  • Boycott of the Prince of Wales's visit (November 1921).
  • Chauri Chaura (Gorakhpur district, 5 February 1922): a crowd burned a police station, killing 22 policemen; Gandhi withdrew the movement (Bardoli, 12 February 1922).

I39.5 Withdrawal: the debate and significance

Debate

Critics (Motilal Nehru, C.R. Das, Subhas Bose, Jawaharlal Nehru) felt the withdrawal came at the peak and demoralised the movement. Bipan Chandra argues it was sound: the movement was showing fatigue, violence would invite crushing repression, and mass movements must have phases of rest (S–T–S strategy). Some Marxist historians (R.P. Dutt) saw it as Gandhi protecting propertied interests.

Significance: first all-India mass movement; spread nationalism to villages, peasants, workers and women; Hindu–Muslim unity (though short-lived — Khilafat collapsed when Turkey abolished the Caliphate in 1924); Congress became a mass organisation with a new constitution; fear of the British was broken.

What historians say

Judith Brown (Gandhi's Rise to Power) explained Gandhi's rise through "sub-contractors" — local leaders who mobilised regions. Shahid Amin ("Gandhi as Mahatma") showed how peasants in Gorakhpur created their own image of Gandhi with miraculous powers; his Event, Metaphor, Memory studies Chauri Chaura. Gail Minault studied the Khilafat movement. Bipan Chandra analysed the S–T–S strategy.

Prelims fact bank

  • Baba Ramchandra, a sanyasi, led the Awadh peasants during the Non-Cooperation years.
  • Under the Inland Emigration Act (1859) Assam tea workers could not leave the gardens without permission.
  • Gandhi founded the Natal Indian Congress in 1894.
  • Gandhi wrote Hind Swaraj in 1909.
  • Gandhi returned to India on 9 January 1915.
  • Raj Kumar Shukla persuaded Gandhi to go to Champaran.
  • Gandhi's first hunger strike in India was during the Ahmedabad mill strike (1918).
  • The Rowlatt Act was passed in March 1919.
  • The Jallianwala Bagh massacre took place on 13 April 1919.
  • General Dyer ordered the firing at Jallianwala Bagh.
  • Tagore renounced his knighthood after Jallianwala Bagh.
  • The Hunter Committee inquired into the Jallianwala Bagh massacre.
  • The Ali brothers led the Khilafat movement.
  • The Non-Cooperation Movement was launched on 1 August 1920.
  • The Calcutta special session of September 1920 approved non-cooperation.
  • The Nagpur session of December 1920 reorganised the Congress on linguistic lines.
  • The Tilak Swaraj Fund aimed to raise Rs 1 crore.
  • The Chauri Chaura incident occurred on 5 February 1922.
  • Udham Singh shot Michael O'Dwyer in London in 1940.
Exam traps
  • Non-cooperation was launched on 1 August 1920 and approved at Calcutta (September) and Nagpur (December 1920); NCERT Class VIII says it "began in January 1921", meaning when it took off.
  • Calcutta special session (Sept 1920) approved NCM; Nagpur (Dec 1920) ratified and reorganised.
  • Gandhi's "Himalayan blunder" refers to the Rowlatt satyagraha (1919).
  • Dyer ordered the firing; O'Dwyer was the Lt-Governor; Udham Singh killed O'Dwyer.
  • Kheda: revenue; Ahmedabad: wages; Champaran: indigo.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Evaluate the significance of the Non-Cooperation Movement.

How to frame it: Background → Programme → Participation → Withdrawal → Significance

Model answer (92 words)

The Rowlatt Act, Jallianwala Bagh and the Khilafat grievance led Gandhi to launch non-cooperation in August 1920, approved at Calcutta and Nagpur. Indians surrendered titles and boycotted schools, courts, councils and foreign cloth; national schools and khadi spread; peasants, workers, students and women joined. After Chauri Chaura (1922) Gandhi withdrew it. Though swaraj was not won, it was the first all-India mass movement: it carried nationalism to villages, forged Hindu–Muslim unity for a time, turned the Congress into a mass organisation with a new constitution and removed the fear of British power.

5 marks · 50 words

Why did Gandhi withdraw the Non-Cooperation Movement?

How to frame it: Event → Reasons → Debate

Model answer (46 words)

After a crowd killed 22 policemen at Chauri Chaura (February 1922), Gandhi withdrew the movement, fearing violence would invite repression and betray non-violence. Critics like Nehru and Bose felt it was the movement's peak; Bipan Chandra argues the movement was already tiring and needed a pause.

How to frame it: Answer

1919 law allowing detention without trial; "no vakil, no dalil, no appeal".

  • Khilafat (Ali brothers); NCM 1 Aug 1920; Calcutta (Sept) and Nagpur (Dec) 1920.
  • Chauri Chaura 5 Feb 1922; withdrawn 12 Feb 1922.

Part III · Unit I40The 1920s: Swarajists, Simon Commission, Nehru Report1922 – 1929

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Swaraj Party; no-changers; Simon Commission; Nehru Report; Jinnah's 14 points; Lahore session and purna swaraj; left-wing, youth and workers' movements

Mains: GS-I: National movement 1922–29; Nehru Report and communal question

The Nehru Report, Simon boycott and the Lahore session are high-yield.

The big picture

After Non-Cooperation, the Congress split over the next step: the Swarajists (C.R. Das, Motilal Nehru) wanted to enter the councils and obstruct them from within, while the no-changers (Patel, Rajendra Prasad, Rajagopalachari) favoured Gandhi's constructive programme. Communal riots grew. The all-white Simon Commission (1927) united Indian parties in protest; the Nehru Report (1928) was the Indian answer, but it failed to resolve the communal question. The decade ended with the Lahore Congress (December 1929) declaring purna swaraj. Socialist ideas, youth leagues, trade unions and the Communist movement also took shape.

Exam focus

Show how the period was not a lull: council politics, constructive work, new ideologies and the radicalisation that led to Civil Disobedience.

WhenWhat happened
1920AITUC founded (Bombay; Lala Lajpat Rai president)
1922Gaya Congress; pro-changers vs no-changers
January 1923Swaraj Party (Congress-Khilafat Swarajya Party) founded
1923Delhi special session allows council entry
1924Kanpur Bolshevik conspiracy case
1925Death of C.R. Das; CPI (Kanpur conference, December); RSS (Nagpur)
1926Swarajists decline after elections
November 1927Simon Commission announced
1927Madras Congress: complete independence resolution
3 February 1928Simon Commission arrives; boycott
August 1928Nehru Report
November 1928Lajpat Rai injured in Lahore lathi charge; dies 17 November
1928Bardoli; Independence for India League
March 1929Jinnah's 14 points; Meerut conspiracy case
31 October 1929Irwin Declaration (dominion status)
December 1929Lahore Congress: purna swaraj

Core notes

I40.1 Swarajists and no-changers

  • Swaraj Party founded January 1923 by C.R. Das (president) and Motilal Nehru (secretary) after the Gaya session (December 1922) rejected council entry.
  • Aim: enter legislatures to obstruct and expose the "hollowness" of the 1919 reforms.
  • 1923 elections: majority in the Central Provinces, strong in Bengal and the Central Legislative Assembly; Vithalbhai Patel elected President (Speaker) of the Central Legislative Assembly (1925).
  • Achievements: defeated government budgets and bills; Public Safety Bill (1928) defeated; exposed the reforms.
  • Decline: death of C.R. Das (1925); split (Responsivists — Lajpat Rai, Malaviya, N.C. Kelkar); communalism; lure of office; walked out of legislatures (1926, 1930).
  • No-changers: constructive work — khadi, national schools, Hindu–Muslim unity, untouchability removal, ashrams (Sabarmati), Gandhi's All India Spinners' Association (1925); prepared cadres for later mass movements.
  • Gandhi's mediation kept the two groups within the Congress.

I40.2 New forces of the 1920s

  • Socialist ideas: Jawaharlal Nehru and Subhas Bose (Independence for India League, 1928); youth leagues.
  • Communists: M.N. Roy (CPI at Tashkent, 1920); CPI at Kanpur conference (December 1925); Kanpur Bolshevik (1924) and Meerut conspiracy (1929) cases.
  • Workers: Bombay Mill Hands Association (1890, N.M. Lokhande) — the first organised workers' body; AITUC (1920; Lala Lajpat Rai first president, N.M. Joshi); Trade Unions Act (1926); Trade Disputes Act (1929); Girni Kamgar Union strikes (Bombay, 1928).
  • Peasants: Bardoli (1928), Kisan sabhas.
  • Revolutionaries: HRA/HSRA (I38).
  • Communal organisations: Hindu Mahasabha (1915), RSS (1925), Tabligh and Tanzim movements; riots (Kohat 1924).
  • Caste movements: Self-Respect (1925), Ambedkar's Mahad satyagraha (1927).

I40.3 Simon Commission

  • Appointed November 1927 (two years early, under the 1919 Act's ten-year review) by the Conservative government — Birkenhead Secretary of State; chaired by Sir John Simon; seven British MPs, no Indian (Clement Attlee among them).
  • Congress (Madras session, 1927, president M.A. Ansari) decided to boycott it; also passed a resolution for complete independence; the Muslim League (Jinnah faction) and Hindu Mahasabha joined.
  • Landed at Bombay on 3 February 1928: hartal, black flags, "Simon Go Back".
  • Lala Lajpat Rai was injured in a lathi charge at Lahore (30 October 1928) and died on 17 November; "every blow on my body will be a nail in the coffin of British rule". Nehru and Pant were beaten in Lucknow.
  • Report (1930): abolish dyarchy, establish representative government in provinces, federation, retain separate electorates — basis of the 1935 Act; no mention of dominion status.
  • Birkenhead challenged Indians to produce an agreed constitution → the Nehru Report.

I40.4 Nehru Report and Jinnah's 14 points

  • All Parties Conference (1928) appointed a committee under Motilal Nehru (members included Tej Bahadur Sapru, Subhas Bose, Ali Imam, M.S. Aney, Mangal Singh, Shuaib Qureshi; Jawaharlal Nehru as secretary).
  • Recommendations (August 1928):
    1. Dominion status (not complete independence).
    2. Joint electorates with reservation of seats for Muslims at the centre and in provinces where they were a minority (not where a majority).
    3. Linguistic provinces.
    4. 19 fundamental rights, including equal rights for women and adult suffrage.
    5. Responsible government at the centre and in provinces; federal structure with residuary powers at the centre.
    6. Secular state.
  • Opposition: the young radicals (Nehru, Bose) wanted complete independence; Jinnah's amendments (one-third seats in the central legislature, residuary powers to provinces, reservation in Punjab and Bengal) were rejected at the Calcutta convention (December 1928) under Hindu Mahasabha pressure.
  • Jinnah's 14 points (March 1929): federal constitution with residuary powers to provinces, provincial autonomy, one-third Muslim representation at the centre, separate electorates, Muslim majorities in Punjab, Bengal and NWFP, safeguards for religion and culture.
  • The Calcutta Congress (1928) gave the government one year to accept the Nehru Report or face civil disobedience.

I40.5 The road to purna swaraj

  • Irwin Declaration (31 October 1929): dominion status as the "natural issue" of India's progress; a Round Table Conference promised. Delhi Manifesto by Indian leaders demanded assurances; Irwin could not give them.
  • Lahore session (December 1929), president Jawaharlal Nehru:
    • Purna swaraj (complete independence) declared as the goal.
    • Round Table Conference to be boycotted; Congress members to resign from legislatures.
    • CWC authorised to launch civil disobedience.
    • Tricolour hoisted on the banks of the Ravi at midnight of 31 December 1929.
    • 26 January 1930 celebrated as Independence Day (Purna Swaraj Day) — later chosen as Republic Day.

What historians say

Bipan Chandra sees the 1920s as a phase of consolidation preparing for the next struggle. Sumit Sarkar (Modern India) emphasises new social forces (workers, peasants, Left). Ayesha Jalal (The Sole Spokesman) argues Jinnah sought a strong Muslim position within a federation and that the Nehru Report's rejection of his amendments was a turning point.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Swaraj Party was founded in January 1923 by C.R. Das and Motilal Nehru.
  • C.R. Das was the first president of the Swaraj Party.
  • Vithalbhai Patel became President of the Central Legislative Assembly in 1925.
  • The AITUC was founded in 1920 with Lala Lajpat Rai as president.
  • The CPI was formally founded at the Kanpur conference in December 1925.
  • The Meerut conspiracy case began in 1929.
  • The Simon Commission was appointed in November 1927 and had no Indian member.
  • Clement Attlee was a member of the Simon Commission.

Exam traps
  • Nehru Report: Motilal Nehru (chair); dominion status, not independence.
  • Madras (1927) passed an independence resolution; Lahore (1929) made purna swaraj the official goal.
  • Simon Commission had seven British MPs, no Indians.
  • Swaraj Party: C.R. Das (president), Motilal Nehru (secretary).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Discuss the recommendations of the Nehru Report and the reasons for its failure.

How to frame it: Context → Recommendations → Opposition → Result

Model answer (94 words)

In reply to Birkenhead's challenge and the Simon Commission, an All Parties committee under Motilal Nehru proposed (1928) dominion status, responsible government, joint electorates with reserved seats for minorities, linguistic provinces, nineteen fundamental rights, adult suffrage and a secular state. Young radicals wanted complete independence, while Jinnah's amendments — one-third central seats and residuary powers to provinces — were rejected under Hindu Mahasabha pressure. Jinnah responded with his 14 points. The report thus failed to settle the communal question, but it set out a democratic constitutional framework and led to the purna swaraj demand.

5 marks · 50 words

Why was the Simon Commission boycotted?

How to frame it: Composition → Reaction

Model answer (45 words)

Appointed in 1927 to review the 1919 reforms, it had seven British MPs and no Indian — an insult to Indian self-respect. Congress, the Jinnah League and others boycotted it with hartals and "Simon Go Back"; Lajpat Rai died after a lathi charge in Lahore.

2 marks · 15 words

What was decided at the Lahore session of 1929?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Purna swaraj as the goal; civil disobedience authorised; 26 January 1930 Independence Day.

  • Irwin Declaration 31 Oct 1929; Lahore Dec 1929 — purna swaraj; 26 Jan 1930.

Part III · Unit I41Civil Disobedience to the 1937 Ministries1930 – 1939

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Dandi March; Civil Disobedience; Round Table Conferences; Gandhi-Irwin Pact; Karachi session; Communal Award; Poona Pact; 1937 elections; Tripuri crisis

Mains: GS-I: Civil Disobedience — features and comparison with NCM; Poona Pact; Congress ministries

Civil Disobedience (and its comparison with NCM), the Gandhi-Irwin Pact and the Poona Pact are very frequent.

The big picture

After the Lahore declaration, Gandhi launched Civil Disobedience with the Dandi March (March–April 1930). Unlike Non-Cooperation, it involved deliberately breaking laws — salt, forest, tax laws. Mass participation, including women, was larger than ever. The British combined repression with negotiation: the Round Table Conferences, the Gandhi-Irwin Pact (1931) and, after the second phase, the Communal Award (1932), which Gandhi resisted by a fast, leading to the Poona Pact. The 1935 Act followed; in the 1937 elections the Congress formed ministries in most provinces and governed for 28 months.

Exam focus

Common questions: why salt? CDM vs NCM; Gandhi-Irwin Pact's merits; Poona Pact; record of the Congress ministries.

WhenWhat happened
31 January 1930Gandhi's 11 demands to Irwin
12 March – 6 April 1930Dandi March (Sabarmati to Dandi, 78 volunteers)
April 1930Peshawar: Khudai Khidmatgars; Garhwali soldiers refuse to fire; Chittagong raid; Rajaji's Vedaranyam march
May 1930Dharasana salt works raid (Sarojini Naidu)
November 1930 – January 1931First Round Table Conference
5 March 1931Gandhi-Irwin Pact (Delhi Pact)
23 March 1931Bhagat Singh, Sukhdev, Rajguru executed
March 1931Karachi session: fundamental rights and economic policy
September – December 1931Second Round Table Conference
January 1932Civil Disobedience resumed; Gandhi arrested
16 August 1932Communal Award (Ramsay MacDonald)
September 1932Gandhi's fast; Poona Pact (24 September)
November – December 1932Third Round Table Conference
1934CDM formally withdrawn; Congress Socialist Party founded
1936Lucknow and Faizpur sessions
1937Elections; Congress ministries (July)
1938Haripura (Bose)
1939Tripuri crisis; Bose resigns, forms Forward Bloc; ministries resign (October–November)

Core notes

I41.1 Dandi March and Civil Disobedience

  • 11 demands (January 1930) to Irwin — included halving land revenue, abolishing the salt tax, reducing military expenditure, releasing political prisoners, protective tariffs — ignored.
  • Why salt? A tax on a basic necessity touching every Indian, especially the poor; easy to break; non-communal; symbol of injustice.
  • Dandi March: 12 March 1930 from Sabarmati Ashram with 78 volunteers, 240 miles (about 385 km) to Dandi; broke the salt law on 6 April 1930.
  • Spread:
    • Tamil Nadu: C. Rajagopalachari — Trichinopoly to Vedaranyam.
    • Malabar: K. Kelappan (Calicut to Payyanur).
    • NWFP: Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan ("Frontier Gandhi") — Khudai Khidmatgars (Red Shirts); Garhwal Rifles under Chandra Singh Garhwali refused to fire on the crowd at Peshawar.
    • Dharasana (Gujarat): Sarojini Naidu, Imam Saheb and Manilal Gandhi led non-violent raid on the salt works; brutal beating reported by the American journalist Webb Miller.
    • Sholapur (textile workers' parallel government); Manipur and Nagaland (Rani Gaidinliu); no-chowkidari tax (Bihar, Bengal); forest satyagraha (Maharashtra, Karnataka, Central Provinces); no-revenue (Gujarat, UP).
  • Features: breaking of laws; large participation of women; merchants and business groups; peasants; weaker Muslim participation than in NCM (except NWFP); boycott of foreign cloth and liquor.
  • Who joined and why (NCERT): rich peasants (Patidars of Gujarat, Jats of UP), hit by the Depression's price fall, fought against revenue and were let down when the movement was suspended in 1931 without revenue cuts; poor tenants wanted unpaid rent remitted. Business wanted protection from imports and a rupee–sterling ratio that discouraged them; it had organised in the Indian Industrial and Commercial Congress (1920) and FICCI (1927); Purshottamdas Thakurdas and G.D. Birla backed the movement. Workers joined in large numbers mainly around Nagpur (railway strikes 1930, dock strikes 1932), but the Congress kept workers' demands off its programme to avoid alienating industrialists. Kamaladevi Chattopadhyay persuaded Gandhi to let women join the salt protests.
  • Limits: Dalit groups (especially in Maharashtra and Nagpur) kept away, as did most Muslim organisations after the Khilafat–Non-Cooperation alliance ended.

I41.2 Round Table Conferences and Gandhi-Irwin Pact

ConferenceCongress?Outcome
First (Nov 1930 – Jan 1931)BoycottedPrinces agreed in principle to federation; little else
Second (Sept – Dec 1931)Gandhi as sole Congress representative (Sarojini Naidu attended separately); Ambedkar demanded separate electorates for depressed classesDeadlock on the communal question; Gandhi returned empty-handed
Third (Nov – Dec 1932)BoycottedLed to the White Paper (1933) and the 1935 Act

Gandhi-Irwin Pact (5 March 1931):

  • Government: release political prisoners not convicted of violence; return confiscated property not yet sold; lenient treatment of resigned officials; allow making salt for consumption in coastal villages; permit peaceful picketing; withdraw emergency ordinances.
  • Congress: suspend Civil Disobedience; attend the Second Round Table Conference.
  • Not granted: inquiry into police excesses; commutation of Bhagat Singh's death sentence (criticised by the youth).
  • Significance: the government negotiated with the Congress as an equal.

Karachi session (March 1931, president Vallabhbhai Patel): endorsed the pact; resolutions on Fundamental Rights and National Economic Programme (drafted with Nehru) — free speech, equality, universal adult franchise, free primary education, rent and revenue reduction, state control of key industries.

I41.3 Second phase, Communal Award and Poona Pact

  • Willingdon's repression; CDM resumed (January 1932); Gandhi arrested; Congress banned; movement withdrawn (April 1934).
  • Communal Award (16 August 1932) by Prime Minister Ramsay MacDonald: separate electorates for Muslims, Europeans, Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians — and the depressed classes (who also got a vote in general constituencies).
  • Gandhi saw separate electorates for depressed classes as dividing Hindu society; began a fast unto death in Yerwada jail (20 September 1932).
  • Poona Pact (24 September 1932) between Gandhi (through Malaviya and others) and Ambedkar: separate electorates abandoned; joint electorate with reserved seats for depressed classes — 148 seats in provincial legislatures (instead of 71 under the Award); 18% of general seats in the central legislature; primary election by depressed class voters for a panel of four candidates.
  • Gandhi then launched an all-India anti-untouchability campaign: Harijan Sevak Sangh (1932), weekly Harijan (1933).

I41.4 CDM compared with NCM

AspectNon-Cooperation (1920–22)Civil Disobedience (1930–34)
GoalSwaraj (undefined), Khilafat, Punjab wrongsPurna swaraj
MethodNon-cooperation — withdrawal (boycott)Deliberate violation of laws
MuslimsLarge participation (Khilafat)Limited (except NWFP)
Middle class (lawyers, students)StrongLess
Women, peasants, businessSomeMuch larger
WorkersSignificantLess
EndingWithdrawn after Chauri ChauraPact (1931), then repression and withdrawal (1934)

I41.5 1937 elections and Congress ministries

  • Congress Socialist Party (1934): Jayaprakash Narayan, Acharya Narendra Dev, Minoo Masani.
  • Lucknow (1936, Nehru) and Faizpur (1936 — first village session) sessions; Congress decided to contest elections under the 1935 Act.
  • 1937 elections: Congress won absolute majorities in five provinces (Madras, Bihar, Orissa, Central Provinces, United Provinces) and was the largest party in Bombay; later formed ministries in eight (including NWFP and Assam). The Muslim League did poorly even in Muslim seats.
  • Work of the ministries (28 months): civil liberties restored; political prisoners released; agrarian laws (tenancy, debt relief); prohibition; basic education (Wardha); improvements in health; National Planning Committee (1938, Nehru).
  • Limitations: limited powers under the Governors; caution on radical agrarian reform; tensions with the Left and kisan sabhas.
  • Jinnah's League attacked the ministries (Pirpur Report, 1938); celebrated "Day of Deliverance" (22 December 1939) when they resigned.
  • Haripura (1938) and Tripuri (1939): Subhas Bose re-elected (defeating Pattabhi Sitaramayya, Gandhi's candidate); Gandhi: "Pattabhi's defeat is my defeat"; Bose resigned and formed the Forward Bloc (May 1939).
  • Ministries resigned (October–November 1939) in protest against India being declared a party to World War II without consultation.

What historians say

Judith Brown (Gandhi and Civil Disobedience) studied the movement's regional variations. Sumit Sarkar highlighted business support and its limits. Bipan Chandra defended the Gandhi-Irwin Pact as a truce in the S–T–S strategy, not a surrender. Eleanor Zelliot and Gail Omvedt analysed Ambedkar's position on the Poona Pact, which he later criticised.

  • Khan Abdul Ghaffar Khan organised the Khudai Khidmatgars.

  • The Gandhi-Irwin Pact was signed on 5 March 1931.

  • Vallabhbhai Patel presided over the Karachi session.

Exam traps

  • Poona Pact: joint electorate with reserved seats (148), not separate electorates.
  • Karachi (1931) president: Patel; Lahore (1929): Nehru; Tripuri (1939): Bose.
  • Congress absolute majority in five provinces, ministries in eight.
  • Gandhi-Irwin Pact did not save Bhagat Singh.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

How did the Civil Disobedience Movement differ from the Non-Cooperation Movement?

How to frame it: Goal → Method → Participation → Ending → Conclusion

Model answer (90 words)

Non-Cooperation (1920–22) sought an undefined swaraj and redress of Khilafat and Punjab wrongs; Civil Disobedience (1930–34) aimed at purna swaraj. NCM withdrew cooperation through boycotts; CDM deliberately broke laws — salt, forest and tax laws. Muslim participation was high in NCM because of Khilafat but limited in CDM except in the NWFP; lawyers and students were prominent in NCM, while CDM drew far more women, peasants and business groups. NCM was withdrawn after Chauri Chaura; CDM ended in the Gandhi-Irwin Pact and then repression. CDM was broader and more defiant.

5 marks · 50 words

What was the Poona Pact? Why was it significant?

How to frame it: Background → Terms → Significance

Model answer (44 words)

Against the Communal Award's separate electorates for depressed classes, Gandhi fasted in Yerwada; Ambedkar agreed (24 September 1932) to joint electorates with 148 reserved provincial seats. It kept Hindu society electorally united, increased depressed class representation and began the reserved-seat model of the Constitution.

2 marks · 15 words

Why did Gandhi choose salt?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Salt tax hit every Indian, especially the poor; easy, non-communal law to break.

Revision card

Remember
  • 11 demands; Dandi 12 Mar – 6 Apr 1930; Vedaranyam, Peshawar, Dharasana.
  • RTCs: I (boycott), II (Gandhi), III (boycott).
  • Gandhi-Irwin Pact 5 Mar 1931; Karachi 1931 (Patel; FR resolution).
  • Communal Award 16 Aug 1932; Poona Pact 24 Sept 1932 (148 seats).
  • CSP 1934; 1937 elections; ministries; Tripuri 1939; Forward Bloc; resignations 1939.

Part III · Unit I421939–1947: War, Quit India, INA, Partition1939 – 1947

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: August Offer; individual satyagraha; Cripps Mission; Quit India; INA; Wavell Plan; RIN mutiny; Cabinet Mission; Direct Action; Mountbatten Plan; Independence Act

Mains: GS-I: Quit India — significance; INA; Cabinet Mission; causes of partition

Quit India, Cripps and Cabinet Mission, and "causes of partition" are among the most-asked questions of modern history.

The big picture

The Second World War changed everything. When the Viceroy declared India at war without consultation, the Congress ministries resigned. British offers (August Offer, Cripps Mission) fell short; Gandhi launched the Quit India Movement (August 1942), the most intense mass uprising of the freedom struggle. Abroad, Subhas Bose led the Indian National Army. After the war, the INA trials, the RIN mutiny and mass unrest convinced Britain to leave. The Cabinet Mission (1946) tried to keep India united, but Congress–League deadlock, Direct Action and communal violence led to the Mountbatten Plan and partition on 15 August 1947.

Exam focus

The two analytical questions: significance of Quit India, and why partition happened.

9 December 1946Constituent Assembly first meets
20 February 1947Attlee announces British withdrawal by June 1948
3 June 1947Mountbatten Plan
18 July 1947Indian Independence Act receives royal assent
15 August 1947Independence; partition

Core notes

I42.1 War, August Offer, individual satyagraha, Cripps

  • Congress position (Wardha, September 1939): support the war against fascism if India is declared free and a Constituent Assembly promised; Linlithgow refused — ministries resigned.
  • Lahore Resolution (March 1940, Muslim League): "independent states" for Muslim-majority areas in the north-west and east — later called the Pakistan Resolution. (The name "Pakistan" was coined by Choudhry Rahmat Ali, 1933.)
  • August Offer (8 August 1940, Linlithgow): dominion status as the objective (undated); expansion of the Viceroy's Executive Council; a body to frame a constitution after the war; minorities' consent for any future constitution (a veto to the League). Congress rejected it.
  • Individual satyagraha (October 1940): limited, symbolic; anti-war speech; Vinoba Bhave first, Nehru second, Brahma Datt third; "Delhi Chalo".
  • Cripps Mission (March–April 1942), after Japan's advance (fall of Singapore, Rangoon):
    • Dominion status after the war; constitution-making body elected by provincial legislatures and nominated by princes; provinces could opt out and form a separate union; defence with Britain during the war.
    • Rejected by Congress (no immediate transfer, princes nominated, provincial option threatened unity; Gandhi is said to have called it "a post-dated cheque" — the addition "on a crashing bank" is of doubtful origin) and by the League (Pakistan not explicitly accepted).

I42.2 Quit India Movement (1942)

  • Causes: failure of Cripps; rising prices and shortages; Japanese threat and fear of a "scorched earth" policy; discrimination in evacuating Indians from Malaya and Burma; loss of faith in British rule.
  • Resolution passed by the AICC at Gowalia Tank, Bombay, on 8 August 1942; Gandhi's mantra "Do or Die" (Karo ya Maro).
  • Operation Zero Hour: Gandhi and all top leaders arrested early on 9 August; Congress banned.
  • Leaderless phase: spontaneous uprisings; attacks on railways, telegraph lines, police stations; underground leaders — Jayaprakash Narayan, Ram Manohar Lohia, Aruna Asaf Ali (hoisted the flag at Gowalia Tank), Usha Mehta (Congress Radio), Achyut Patwardhan, Sucheta Kripalani.
  • Parallel governments: Ballia (Chittu Pandey, August 1942), Tamluk (Midnapore — Jatiya Sarkar, 1942–44; Matangini Hazra killed), Satara (Prati Sarkar, 1943–46, Y.B. Chavan, Nana Patil; volunteer corps (seba dals) and village units (tufan dals), people's courts; mainly kunbi peasants with Dalit support, it lasted until the 1946 elections despite later Congress disapproval).
  • Repression: over 1 lakh arrested; firing, aerial strafing; collective fines.
  • Attitudes: Muslim League, Hindu Mahasabha and Communists (after the USSR joined, "People's War") stayed away; Ambedkar opposed it; princes supported the British.
  • Gandhi's 21-day fast (February 1943) against government blame for violence.
  • Significance: demand for independence became non-negotiable; youth, students, peasants and women participated massively; showed that British rule could not continue without consent; though crushed by 1944, it made withdrawal a question of time.

I42.3 Subhas Bose and the INA

  • Bose escaped house arrest (January 1941), reached Berlin via Kabul; Free India Centre and radio; Indian Legion.
  • First INA (1942): Mohan Singh with Japanese help from Indian prisoners of war; Rash Behari Bose's Indian Independence League (Tokyo, Bangkok conference 1942).
  • Bose reached Singapore (July 1943); took command of the Azad Hind Fauj; slogans "Delhi Chalo", "Jai Hind", "Give me blood and I will give you freedom".
  • Provisional Government of Free India (21 October 1943, Singapore); Andaman and Nicobar islands handed over (renamed Shaheed and Swaraj); Rani Jhansi Regiment (Captain Lakshmi Swaminathan).
  • INA brigades (Gandhi, Nehru, Azad, Subhas) fought in the Imphal–Kohima campaign (1944); flag hoisted at Moirang (Manipur); retreat after Japanese defeat.
  • Bose reportedly died in an air crash at Taipei (18 August 1945).
  • INA trials (Red Fort, November 1945): Shah Nawaz Khan, Prem Kumar Sahgal, Gurbaksh Singh Dhillon — Hindu, Sikh and Muslim; defended by Bhulabhai Desai, Tej Bahadur Sapru, Nehru, Asaf Ali, K.N. Katju; nationwide agitation; sentences remitted.

I42.4 Post-war upsurge

  • Wavell Plan / Simla Conference (June 1945): Executive Council of Indians except the Viceroy and Commander-in-Chief; parity of caste Hindus and Muslims; failed over Jinnah's claim to nominate all Muslim members.
  • Labour Party won in Britain (July 1945, Attlee).
  • Elections (1945–46): Congress swept general seats; League won most Muslim seats — polarisation.
  • RIN mutiny (18 February 1946, HMIS Talwar, Bombay): against racial discrimination and poor food; spread to 78 ships and 20 shore establishments; supported by strikes; called off on Patel and Jinnah's advice. Also RIAF strikes and Jabalpur army unrest.
  • Peasant and worker struggles: Tebhaga, Telangana, Punnapra-Vayalar; postal strike; princely state movements.

I42.5 Cabinet Mission (1946)

  • Members: Pethick-Lawrence (Secretary of State), Stafford Cripps, A.V. Alexander.
  • Plan of 16 May 1946:
    1. Rejected a full Pakistan.
    2. Union of India (provinces and states) with control over defence, foreign affairs and communications; all other powers to provinces.
    3. Grouping of provinces: Group A (Hindu-majority — Madras, Bombay, UP, Bihar, CP, Orissa), Group B (Punjab, NWFP, Sindh), Group C (Bengal, Assam); groups could frame their own constitutions.
    4. Constituent Assembly elected by provincial assemblies (389 members: 292 provinces, 4 chief commissioners' provinces, 93 princely states).
    5. Interim Government of Indian leaders.
  • Disagreement on grouping: Congress saw it as optional; the League as compulsory. Nehru's statement (July 1946) that the Congress was not bound by the plan led the League to withdraw acceptance.
  • Direct Action Day (16 August 1946): Calcutta killings; violence spread to Noakhali (Gandhi's peace mission), Bihar, Garhmukteshwar, Punjab.
  • Interim Government (2 September 1946) under Nehru; the League joined in October (Liaquat Ali Khan — finance) but obstructed. Constituent Assembly met on 9 December 1946; League boycotted.

I42.6 Mountbatten Plan and partition

  • Attlee's statement (20 February 1947): British would leave by June 1948.
  • Mountbatten as Viceroy (March 1947); 3 June Plan: partition; provincial assemblies of Punjab and Bengal to vote on division; referendum in NWFP and Sylhet; Sindh to decide; princely states free to join either (paramountcy lapses); boundary commission; date advanced to 15 August 1947.
  • Radcliffe boundary awards (published 17 August 1947).
  • Indian Independence Act (royal assent 18 July 1947) — see I29.
  • Partition violence: an estimated 0.5–1 million killed (estimates vary up to 2 million); 10–15 million displaced.
  • About 30,000 abducted women were "recovered" (22,000 Muslim women in India, 8,000 Hindu and Sikh women in Pakistan) in an operation that ran until 1954.
  • Gandhi spent 15 August in Calcutta (Beliaghata), fasting for peace; assassinated on 30 January 1948.

I42.7 Why partition happened

  1. British policy of divide and rule: separate electorates (1909), Communal Award, recognising the League as the sole Muslim voice (August Offer, Cripps, Simla veto).
  2. Growth of communalism: Hindu and Muslim communal organisations; two-nation theory.
  3. Congress limitations: failure to win over Muslim masses after the 1930s; rejection of coalitions in UP (1937); underestimation of the League.
  4. Jinnah's leadership and the League's mass mobilisation after 1937; the Lahore Resolution; 1946 election victory.
  5. Direct Action and communal violence made a united India seem unworkable; Congress leaders accepted partition to obtain a strong centre and quick transfer of power.
  6. British haste to leave (Attlee's deadline; Mountbatten's advanced date).
  7. Numbers NCERT uses: in 1937 (franchise about 10–12% of the population) the League won only 4.4% of the Muslim vote; in 1946 the Congress took 91.3% of the non-Muslim vote and the League 86.6% of the Muslim vote. Iqbal's League presidential address (Allahabad, 1930) spoke of a "North-West Indian Muslim state", still within a loose federation. In December 1938 the Congress Working Committee barred its members from the Hindu Mahasabha (founded 1915). In Punjab the cross-community landholders' Unionist Party was strong from 1923 to 1947.
Debate

Ayesha Jalal argues Jinnah used the Pakistan demand as a bargaining counter for parity in a federal India and that the Congress's insistence on a strong centre made partition unavoidable. Bipan Chandra stresses colonial communal policy and the failure to contain communalism. Anita Inder Singh and Yasmin Khan (The Great Partition) emphasise the contingency of 1946–47 and the role of violence.

What historians say

See the debate box. Sumit Sarkar and Gyanendra Pandey (Remembering Partition) study the violence. Urvashi Butalia (The Other Side of Silence) and Ritu Menon recorded women's experiences. Leonard Gordon wrote a standard biography of the Bose brothers; Peter Fay studied the INA.

Prelims fact bank

  • In 1946 the League won 86.6% of the Muslim vote; the Congress 91.3% of the non-Muslim vote.
  • The recovery of abducted women after Partition continued until 1954.
  • Linlithgow declared India at war on 3 September 1939.
  • The Muslim League passed the Lahore Resolution in March 1940.
  • The August Offer was made on 8 August 1940.
  • Vinoba Bhave was the first individual satyagrahi.
  • The Cripps Mission came to India in March 1942.
  • The Quit India resolution was passed on 8 August 1942 at Gowalia Tank, Bombay.
  • Gandhi gave the slogan Do or Die.
  • Aruna Asaf Ali hoisted the Congress flag at Gowalia Tank on 9 August 1942.
  • Usha Mehta ran the underground Congress Radio.
  • Chittu Pandey set up a parallel government at Ballia.
  • The Satara parallel government (Prati Sarkar) lasted from 1943 to 1946.
  • Mohan Singh organised the first INA.
  • Bose formed the Provisional Government of Free India on 21 October 1943.
  • Lakshmi Swaminathan commanded the Rani Jhansi Regiment.
  • The INA trials began at the Red Fort in November 1945.
  • Shah Nawaz Khan, P.K. Sahgal and G.S. Dhillon were tried in the first INA trial.
  • The Wavell Plan led to the Simla Conference in June 1945.
  • The RIN mutiny began on 18 February 1946 on HMIS Talwar.
  • The Cabinet Mission consisted of Pethick-Lawrence, Cripps and A.V. Alexander.
  • The Cabinet Mission plan was announced on 16 May 1946.
  • Direct Action Day was observed on 16 August 1946.
  • The Interim Government was formed on 2 September 1946.
  • The Constituent Assembly first met on 9 December 1946.
  • Attlee announced on 20 February 1947 that the British would leave by June 1948.
  • The Mountbatten Plan was announced on 3 June 1947.
  • Gandhi was assassinated on 30 January 1948.
Exam traps
  • The Muslim League accepted the Cabinet Mission plan on 6 June 1946; the Mission's interim-government statement was 16 June. NCERT's timeline swaps the two dates.
  • 1937 was not the first provincial election: councils had been elected under the 1919 Act from 1920. It was the first under the 1935 Act with provincial autonomy (NCERT says "for the first time").
  • Both the League (June 1946) and the Congress initially accepted the Cabinet Mission plan; the League withdrew its acceptance in July 1946. Statements that neither accepted it are wrong.
  • Cripps (1942): provincial option; Cabinet Mission (1946): grouping, rejected Pakistan.
  • Quit India resolution 8 August; arrests 9 August (August Kranti Day).
  • First INA: Mohan Singh; Bose took over in 1943.
  • Wavell Plan (1945) failed at Simla over League's nomination claim.
  • Independence Act royal assent 18 July 1947.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Assess the significance of the Quit India Movement.

How to frame it: Causes → Course → Parallel governments → Repression → Significance

Model answer (100 words)

After the Cripps failure, wartime hardship and the Japanese threat, the Congress passed the Quit India resolution on 8 August 1942; Gandhi gave the call "Do or Die". Leaders were arrested next morning, and the movement became a spontaneous uprising: railways and police stations were attacked, underground leaders such as JP, Lohia and Aruna Asaf Ali organised resistance, and parallel governments arose in Ballia, Tamluk and Satara. It was crushed by 1944 with massive repression. Yet it made independence the only acceptable demand, involved youth, peasants and women on an unprecedented scale and showed Britain could not rule without consent.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the main proposals of the Cabinet Mission Plan?

How to frame it: Union → Grouping → Assembly → Interim government

Model answer (44 words)

The 1946 plan rejected Pakistan and proposed a Union handling defence, foreign affairs and communications, with residuary powers to provinces grouped into A, B and C; a Constituent Assembly elected by provincial assemblies; and an interim government. Disputes over grouping led to its collapse.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the RIN mutiny?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

February 1946 revolt of Indian naval ratings in Bombay against discrimination; widely supported.

Revision card

Remember
  • War 1939; resignations; Lahore Resolution 1940; August Offer; individual satyagraha (Vinoba).
  • Cripps 1942 (provincial option); Quit India 8 Aug 1942; Do or Die; Ballia, Tamluk, Satara.
  • INA: Mohan Singh → Bose; Azad Hind govt 21 Oct 1943; Imphal 1944; Red Fort trials 1945.
  • Wavell/Simla 1945; RIN mutiny Feb 1946; Cabinet Mission May 1946; Direct Action 16 Aug 1946.
  • Interim govt 2 Sept 1946; CA 9 Dec 1946; Attlee 20 Feb 1947; 3 June Plan; 15 Aug 1947.

    Show answer

Part IVIndependent India (to 2000)
