Part IV · Unit I43Integration of the Princely States1947 – 1950

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Instrument of Accession; Standstill Agreement; Patel and V.P. Menon; Junagadh, Hyderabad, Kashmir; Goa, Pondicherry

Mains: GS-I: Post-independence consolidation — integration of princely states

Patel's role and the three problem states are standard questions. The integration of Rajasthan (seven stages) is in R21.

The big picture

At independence there were about 565 princely states covering roughly two-fifths of the subcontinent's area. With the lapse of paramountcy (Indian Independence Act, 1947), each was legally free to join India or Pakistan or remain independent. Through the diplomacy of Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel and V.P. Menon (States Department, July 1947), almost all acceded before 15 August 1947 by signing the Instrument of Accession (surrendering defence, external affairs and communications). Junagadh, Hyderabad and Kashmir were the exceptions. A second stage (1948–50) merged or integrated the states into unions and provinces, and democratised them.

Exam focus

Explain the method (persuasion, popular pressure, and force when needed), the three problem cases and the two-stage process.

WhenWhat happened
July 1947States Department under Patel; V.P. Menon secretary
25 July 1947Mountbatten addresses the Chamber of Princes
15 August 1947Most states have acceded
September 1947Junagadh accedes to Pakistan
20 February 1948Junagadh plebiscite
22 October 1947Tribal raiders invade Kashmir
26 October 1947Hari Singh signs the Instrument of Accession
1 January 1948India refers Kashmir to the UN
November 1947Standstill Agreement with Hyderabad
13–17 September 1948Operation Polo (Hyderabad)
1948–1949Unions: Saurashtra, Matsya, Vindhya Pradesh, Madhya Bharat, PEPSU, Rajasthan, Travancore-Cochin
1950Constitution: Part A, B, C, D states
1954French settlements transferred (de jure 1962)
1961Goa liberated (Operation Vijay)
1971Privy purses abolished (26th Amendment)

Core notes

I43.1 The approach

  • States Department (5 July 1947): Patel as minister, V.P. Menon secretary.
  • Instrument of Accession: states surrendered only defence, external affairs and communications; other matters remained with rulers initially.
  • Standstill Agreement: existing administrative arrangements (customs, posts, railways) to continue.
  • Patel's appeal to patriotism and to the princes' self-interest, with assurances of privy purses, titles and private property; Mountbatten's influence with the princes.
  • Popular movements (praja mandals, States Peoples' Conference) pressured reluctant rulers.
  • States such as Travancore (C.P. Ramaswami Aiyar — declared independence briefly), Bhopal and Jodhpur hesitated before acceding.

I43.2 The three problem states

StateSituationResolution
JunagadhHindu majority, Muslim Nawab (Mahabat Khan III); acceded to Pakistan (September 1947); not contiguous to PakistanPopular revolt (Samaldas Gandhi's provisional government, Arzi Hukumat); Indian troops; Nawab fled; plebiscite (February 1948) — overwhelming vote for India
HyderabadLargest state; Hindu majority; Nizam Osman Ali Khan wanted independence; Standstill Agreement (November 1947); Razakars (Kasim Razvi, Ittehad-ul-Muslimeen) terrorised the population; Telangana peasant struggleOperation Polo ("police action", 13–17 September 1948); Nizam acceded; became Rajpramukh (1950)
Jammu and KashmirMuslim majority, Hindu ruler Hari Singh; sought independence (standstill with Pakistan); National Conference under Sheikh Abdullah opposed the ruler and the Muslim LeagueTribal raiders backed by Pakistan invaded (22 October 1947); Hari Singh signed the Instrument of Accession (26 October 1947); Indian troops airlifted; India referred the matter to the UN (1 January 1948); ceasefire (1 January 1949) left part of the state under Pakistan's control; Article 370

I43.3 Second stage: merger and democratisation

  • Merger of small states into neighbouring provinces (e.g. Orissa and Chhattisgarh states, 1948).
  • Unions of states: Saurashtra (1948), Matsya (1948), Vindhya Pradesh, Madhya Bharat, PEPSU (Patiala and East Punjab States Union), Rajasthan (in stages, completed 1949; Ajmer added 1956), Travancore-Cochin (1949).
  • Some centrally administered (Himachal Pradesh, Tripura, Manipur, Bhopal, Kutch, Bilaspur).
  • Rulers became Rajpramukhs of unions; given privy purses (guaranteed under Article 291) — abolished by the 26th Amendment (1971) after the Supreme Court struck down a 1970 presidential order (Madhav Rao Scindia case).
  • The 1950 Constitution: Part B states (former princely unions) under Rajpramukhs; ended by the States Reorganisation Act (1956).

I43.4 French and Portuguese territories

  • French: Chandernagore by plebiscite (1949), merged with West Bengal (1954); Pondicherry, Karaikal, Mahe, Yanam transferred de facto in 1954, de jure in 1962.
  • Portuguese: Dadra and Nagar Haveli liberated by volunteers (1954); Goa, Daman and Diu — Goa Liberation satyagraha (1955); Operation Vijay (18–19 December 1961); merged into India.

What historians say

V.P. Menon (The Story of the Integration of the Indian States) is the insider account. Ramachandra Guha (India after Gandhi) and Bipan Chandra, Mridula Mukherjee, Aditya Mukherjee (India since Independence) give balanced narratives. Ian Copland (The Princes of India in the Endgame of Empire) analysed the princes' strategies.

Prelims fact bank

  • There were about 565 princely states at independence.
  • The States Department was set up in July 1947 under Sardar Patel.
  • V.P. Menon was secretary of the States Department.
  • The Instrument of Accession covered defence, external affairs and communications.
  • The Nawab of Junagadh acceded to Pakistan in September 1947.
  • A plebiscite in Junagadh in February 1948 favoured India.
  • Maharaja Hari Singh signed the Instrument of Accession on 26 October 1947.
  • India referred the Kashmir issue to the UN on 1 January 1948.
  • The Razakars were led by Kasim Razvi.
  • Operation Polo against Hyderabad took place in September 1948.
  • PEPSU stood for Patiala and East Punjab States Union.
  • Privy purses were abolished by the 26th Amendment in 1971.
  • Goa was liberated by Operation Vijay in December 1961.
  • Pondicherry was transferred de facto in 1954 and de jure in 1962.
Exam traps
  • Accession (3 subjects) ≠ merger/integration (full).
  • Junagadh — plebiscite; Hyderabad — Operation Polo; Kashmir — accession + UN.
  • Privy purses abolished 1971 (26th Amendment), not 1950.
  • Chandernagore by plebiscite (1949); Pondicherry de jure 1962.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. The history sections of the RAS Prelims 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers had no question on this unit (checked against RPSC final answer keys; 2018 not checked, key unavailable).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Evaluate Sardar Patel's role in the integration of princely states.

How to frame it: Problem → Method → Problem states → Second stage → Assessment

Model answer (96 words)

With paramountcy lapsing, about 565 states could choose independence. Patel, with V.P. Menon at the States Department, persuaded nearly all to sign the Instrument of Accession before 15 August 1947, offering privy purses and titles while popular movements pressed reluctant rulers. Junagadh was secured through popular revolt and plebiscite, Hyderabad through Operation Polo (1948), and Kashmir acceded under tribal invasion. In a second stage he merged states into provinces and unions such as Rajasthan and Saurashtra and democratised them. His blend of persuasion and firmness prevented the Balkanisation of India — hence "Iron Man of India".

5 marks · 50 words

How was Hyderabad integrated?

How to frame it: Nizam → Razakars → Action

Model answer (35 words)

The Nizam sought independence and signed only a Standstill Agreement (1947), while Kasim Razvi's Razakars terrorised the people. When negotiations failed, India launched Operation Polo (13–17 September 1948); the Nizam surrendered, acceded and became Rajpramukh.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Instrument of Accession?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Document by which states ceded defence, external affairs and communications to India.

Revision card

Remember
  • 565 states; States Dept July 1947 (Patel, V.P. Menon).
  • Instrument of Accession (3 subjects); Standstill Agreement.
  • Junagadh (plebiscite Feb 1948); Hyderabad (Polo, Sept 1948); Kashmir (26 Oct 1947, UN).
  • Unions: Saurashtra, Matsya, Rajasthan, PEPSU, Travancore-Cochin; Part B states; privy purses ended 1971.
  • French (1954/1962), Goa (1961).

    Show answer

    26 October 1947.

  6. What was Operation Polo?
    Show answer

Sources: V.P. Menon, The Story of the Integration of the Indian States.; Bipan Chandra et al., India since Independence, ch. 6.; Ramachandra Guha, India after Gandhi, chs. 3–4.; NCERT Class 12, Politics in India since Independence, ch. 1.

Part IV · Unit I44Linguistic Reorganisation of States1948 – 1987

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Dhar Commission; JVP Committee; Potti Sriramulu; States Reorganisation Commission; creation of new states

Mains: GS-I: Linguistic reorganisation — process, rationale and effect on national unity

Frequently asked as an analytical question ("Did linguistic states weaken or strengthen national unity?").

The big picture

The Congress had accepted linguistic provinces since the Nagpur session (1920). After independence and partition, leaders feared that linguistic states would encourage division. The Dhar Commission and the JVP Committee (1948–49) advised caution. Popular agitation — especially the death of Potti Sriramulu after a fast for Andhra (1952) — forced the creation of Andhra (1953) and the States Reorganisation Commission (1953–55). The States Reorganisation Act (1956) created 14 states and 6 union territories, largely on linguistic lines. Further reorganisation followed (Bombay, Punjab, the north-east, and later Jharkhand, Chhattisgarh, Uttarakhand, Telangana).

Exam focus

Conclude with the accepted view: linguistic states, by accommodating diversity, strengthened democracy and national unity.

WhenWhat happened
1920Nagpur Congress accepts linguistic provincial committees
1936Orissa created on a linguistic basis
1948Linguistic Provinces Commission (S.K. Dhar)
1948–49JVP Committee (Nehru, Patel, Pattabhi Sitaramayya)
19 October – 15 December 1952Potti Sriramulu's fast and death
1 October 1953Andhra state formed (capital Kurnool)
December 1953States Reorganisation Commission (Fazl Ali, K.M. Panikkar, H.N. Kunzru)
1955SRC report
1 November 1956States Reorganisation Act in force: 14 states, 6 UTs; Andhra Pradesh formed
1960Bombay divided into Maharashtra and Gujarat
1963Nagaland
1966Punjab, Haryana (and Chandigarh UT); hill areas to Himachal
1971Himachal Pradesh a state
1972Manipur, Tripura, Meghalaya states (Meghalaya autonomous 1970)
1975Sikkim (36th Amendment)
1987Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh, Goa
2000Chhattisgarh, Uttarakhand, Jharkhand
2014Telangana

Core notes

I44.1 Early hesitation

  • Dhar Commission (1948, S.K. Dhar): favoured administrative convenience over language; opposed immediate linguistic reorganisation.
  • JVP Committee (December 1948 – April 1949; Jawaharlal Nehru, Vallabhbhai Patel, Pattabhi Sitaramayya): emphasised unity, security and economic progress; advised postponement but conceded that strong public demand might be accepted (e.g. Andhra, excluding Madras city).
  • The 1950 Constitution classified states as Part A, B, C, D — not on linguistic lines.

I44.2 The Andhra movement and the SRC

  • Potti Sriramulu, a Gandhian, fasted for a separate Andhra state from 19 October 1952 and died on 15 December 1952 after 58 days; violent protests followed.
  • Nehru announced Andhra; formed on 1 October 1953 (capital Kurnool).
  • States Reorganisation Commission (December 1953): Fazl Ali (chairman), K.M. Panikkar, H.N. Kunzru; report (September 1955) — accepted language as the main basis but rejected "one language, one state" as the sole principle; also considered unity, security, financial and administrative viability.
  • States Reorganisation Act, 1956 (with the 7th Amendment): abolished the four-category classification; 14 states and 6 union territories; Kerala, Mysore (Karnataka), Andhra Pradesh (with Telangana), Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan (with Ajmer) reshaped.

I44.3 Later reorganisations

YearChangeBackground
1960Bombay split into Maharashtra and GujaratSamyukta Maharashtra Samiti and Mahagujarat movements; Bombay city to Maharashtra
1963NagalandNaga insurgency (Phizo)
1966Punjab and Haryana; Chandigarh UTPunjabi Suba movement (Master Tara Singh, Sant Fateh Singh); Shah Commission
1971–72Himachal Pradesh, Manipur, Tripura, MeghalayaNorth-Eastern Areas (Reorganisation) Act 1971
1975Sikkim36th Amendment after a referendum
1987Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh, GoaMizo Accord (1986, Laldenga)
2000Chhattisgarh (1 Nov), Uttarakhand (9 Nov), Jharkhand (15 Nov)Development and tribal identity, not language
2014Telangana (2 June)Long movement (1969 agitation; K. Chandrashekar Rao); Srikrishna Committee
2019J&K reorganised into two UTsArticle 370 provisions revoked

I44.4 Assessment

  • Fears: regional chauvinism, inter-state disputes (Belgaum, water), threat to unity.
  • Outcome: made administration accessible in people's languages; deepened democracy; removed a major source of discontent; regional identities proved compatible with national identity.
  • Later states show that development, ethnicity and administrative viability have replaced language as the main basis.
Debate

Bipan Chandra and Ramachandra Guha argue that linguistic reorganisation strengthened unity by accommodating diversity; critics point to persistent border and water disputes and the rise of regionalism. Most scholars today see it as a democratic success.

What historians say

Paul Brass (Language, Religion and Politics in North India) studied language movements. Ramachandra Guha (India after Gandhi, ch. "Ideas of India") and Bipan Chandra et al. (India since Independence) treat reorganisation as a key step in nation-building. Selig Harrison (India: The Most Dangerous Decades, 1960) predicted linguistic states would lead to fragmentation — a prediction that did not come true.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Dhar Commission was appointed in 1948.
  • The JVP Committee consisted of Nehru, Patel and Pattabhi Sitaramayya.
  • Potti Sriramulu died on 15 December 1952 after a 58-day fast.
  • Andhra state was formed on 1 October 1953 with Kurnool as its capital.
  • Fazl Ali chaired the States Reorganisation Commission.
  • K.M. Panikkar and H.N. Kunzru were members of the SRC.
  • The States Reorganisation Act came into force on 1 November 1956.
  • The 1956 reorganisation created 14 states and 6 union territories.
  • Bombay was divided into Maharashtra and Gujarat in 1960.
  • Nagaland became a state in 1963.
  • Punjab and Haryana were separated in 1966.
  • Sikkim became a state by the 36th Amendment in 1975.
  • Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh and Goa became states in 1987.
  • Chhattisgarh, Uttarakhand and Jharkhand were formed in 2000.
  • Telangana was formed on 2 June 2014.
Exam traps
  • Andhra (1953) was the first linguistic state after independence; Orissa (1936) before independence.
  • JVP = Jawaharlal, Vallabhbhai, Pattabhi.
  • SRC report 1955; Act in force 1 November 1956.
  • Andhra Pradesh (1956) ≠ Andhra state (1953).

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Did the linguistic reorganisation of states weaken national unity? Discuss.

How to frame it: Background → Process → Fears → Outcome → Conclusion

Model answer (92 words)

The Congress had promised linguistic provinces since 1920, but after partition the Dhar Commission and the JVP Committee advised caution for the sake of unity. Potti Sriramulu's death forced the creation of Andhra (1953); the Fazl Ali Commission led to the 1956 Act creating 14 states and 6 UTs, followed by Maharashtra, Gujarat, Punjab and others. Critics feared regional chauvinism, and border and water disputes did arise. Yet administration in people's languages deepened democracy, removed a major grievance and showed that regional and national identities could coexist. On balance it strengthened unity.

5 marks · 50 words

What was the States Reorganisation Commission?

How to frame it: Members → Recommendations → Result

Model answer (45 words)

Appointed in December 1953 under Fazl Ali, with K.M. Panikkar and H.N. Kunzru, it accepted language as the main basis of reorganisation while weighing unity, security and viability. Its 1955 report led to the States Reorganisation Act (1956), creating 14 states and 6 union territories.

2 marks · 15 words

Who was Potti Sriramulu?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Gandhian whose fatal fast (1952) for an Andhra state forced linguistic reorganisation.

  • Later: 1960 (Maharashtra, Gujarat), 1963 (Nagaland), 1966 (Punjab, Haryana), 1972, 1975 (Sikkim), 1987, 2000, 2014.

Part IV · Unit I45Nation-Building in the Nehru Era and Planning1947 – 1964

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Constituent Assembly; Planning Commission; Five-Year Plans; land reforms; Non-Alignment; Panchsheel; 1962 war; Bhoodan

Mains: GS-I: Post-independence consolidation — planning, land reforms, foreign policy; Nehru's legacy

Planning (Mahalanobis model), land reforms and Non-Alignment are frequently asked. Links to Economy and International Relations.

The big picture

India at independence faced partition refugees, communal violence, integration of states, poverty and illiteracy. Under Jawaharlal Nehru (Prime Minister 1947–64), the new state built its foundations: a democratic Constitution (1950), universal adult franchise (first elections 1951–52), a planned mixed economy with emphasis on heavy industry, land reforms, institutions of science and education, and a foreign policy of Non-Alignment. The 1962 war with China was the major setback.

Exam focus

Assess the Nehru era as a whole: achievements in democracy, institution-building and industrialisation; shortcomings in agriculture, land reform and defence.

WhenWhat happened
1946–49Constituent Assembly (constitution adopted 26 November 1949)
26 January 1950Constitution in force; India a republic
March 1950Planning Commission (Nehru chairman)
1951First Five-Year Plan begins; Bhoodan begins (Pochampalli, 18 April)
1951–52First general elections
1952National Development Council; Community Development Programme (2 October)
1954Panchsheel agreement with China
1955Bandung Conference; Avadi session — "socialistic pattern of society"
1956Industrial Policy Resolution; Second Plan (Mahalanobis)
1957Kerala: first elected Communist government
1959Balwant Rai Mehta panchayati raj launched at Nagaur (2 October)
1961Belgrade: first NAM summit; Goa liberated
October–November 1962Sino-Indian war
27 May 1964Death of Nehru

Core notes

I45.1 Constitution and democracy

  • Constituent Assembly first met on 9 December 1946; Rajendra Prasad president; B.R. Ambedkar chairman of the Drafting Committee; adopted 26 November 1949; in force 26 January 1950.
  • Making the Constitution (NCERT Class XII): M.N. Roy first proposed a constituent assembly (1934). Members were chosen by the provincial legislatures elected in 1945–46, not by universal franchise; about 82% were also Congress members, and the League boycotted it. Sachchidananda Sinha was provisional president before Rajendra Prasad. Nehru moved the Objectives Resolution (13 December 1946) and proposed the tricolour with the navy-blue wheel; Patel worked behind the scenes on key reports; B.N. Rau was constitutional adviser. Drafting Committee members included K.M. Munshi, Alladi Krishnaswamy Aiyar, N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar, Mohammad Saadulla, B.L. Mitter and D.P. Khaitan.
  • Debates: separate electorates were rejected (Patel blamed them for Partition; Begum Aizaz Rasul called them self-destructive for minorities); K. Santhanam defended states' rights while Ambedkar wanted a Centre stronger than under the 1935 Act; the Congress had accepted Hindustani in the 1930s, but the compromise made Hindi the official (not national) language, with English continuing for 15 years.
  • First general elections (October 1951 – February 1952): universal adult franchise; Election Commission under Sukumar Sen; Congress won a large majority.
  • Strengthening of parliamentary institutions, an independent judiciary, a free press, civilian control of the military.
  • Hindu Code Bills (1955–56) reformed Hindu personal law.
  • Panchayati Raj (Balwant Rai Mehta Committee, 1957): three-tier system first launched at Nagaur, Rajasthan (2 October 1959).

I45.2 Planning and the mixed economy

  • Background: Bombay Plan (1944, industrialists), Gandhian Plan (S.N. Agarwal), People's Plan (M.N. Roy), National Planning Committee (1938, Nehru).
  • Planning Commission (March 1950) — extra-constitutional, advisory; National Development Council (1952).
  • Industrial Policy Resolutions 1948 and 1956 (the "economic constitution"): Schedule A (state), Schedule B (mixed), Schedule C (private) — public sector to occupy the "commanding heights".
PlanFocusResult
First (1951–56)Agriculture, irrigation, power; Harrod–Domar model; Bhakra-Nangal, Damodar Valley, HirakudSuccessful — growth above target
Second (1956–61)Rapid heavy industrialisation; P.C. Mahalanobis model; steel plants at Bhilai (USSR), Rourkela (West Germany), Durgapur (UK)Foreign exchange crisis; industry grew
Third (1961–66)Self-reliance; agriculture and industryFailed — 1962 and 1965 wars, droughts; Plan holiday (1966–69)

Institutions: IITs (Kharagpur 1951), AIIMS (1956), Atomic Energy Commission (1948, Homi Bhabha), CSIR, ISRO's predecessor INCOSPAR (1962, Vikram Sarabhai), UGC (1953).

I45.3 Land reforms and rural development

  1. Abolition of zamindari (intermediaries) — largely completed by the mid-1950s; first and Ninth Schedule amendments protected the laws.
  2. Tenancy reforms: security of tenure, regulation of rent, ownership rights — weakly implemented.
  3. Ceiling on landholdings — evaded through benami and fictitious transfers; little surplus land.
  4. Consolidation of holdings — successful in Punjab, Haryana, western UP.
  5. Cooperative farming (Nagpur session, 1959) — opposed and abandoned.
  • Community Development Programme (2 October 1952) and National Extension Service (1953).
  • Bhoodan (land-gift) movement of Vinoba Bhave, begun at Pochampalli (Telangana, 18 April 1951); later Gramdan; about 4 million acres pledged, much of it poor land.

I45.4 Foreign policy

  • Non-Alignment: independence from Cold War blocs; Bandung (1955); Belgrade (1961) first NAM summit — Nehru, Tito, Nasser, Sukarno, Nkrumah.
  • Panchsheel (29 April 1954) with China: mutual respect for territorial integrity and sovereignty, non-aggression, non-interference, equality and mutual benefit, peaceful coexistence; "Hindi–Chini bhai bhai".
  • Roles in Korea (NNRC chairmanship), Indo-China (ICSC), Suez (1956), decolonisation, disarmament.
  • 1962 war: background — Tibet (Dalai Lama's asylum, 1959), McMahon Line and Aksai Chin disputes, "forward policy"; China attacked in October 1962 in NEFA and Ladakh; India defeated; Defence Minister V.K. Krishna Menon resigned; Henderson Brooks–Bhagat report.
  • Relations with Pakistan: Kashmir, Indus Waters Treaty (1960, World Bank mediation).

I45.5 Assessment of the Nehru era

Achievements: democratic institutions survived; secularism; integration; planned industrial base; scientific and educational institutions; independent foreign policy and global standing.

Shortcomings: agriculture neglected (food imports, PL-480); incomplete land reforms; licence-permit raj bureaucracy; slow poverty reduction; illiteracy; defeat in 1962; Kashmir unresolved.

What historians say

Bipan Chandra, Mridula and Aditya Mukherjee (India since Independence) offer a positive assessment of Nehruvian nation-building. Ramachandra Guha (India after Gandhi) emphasises the "unnatural nation" that survived. Sunil Khilnani (The Idea of India) analyses Nehru's vision. Critics such as B.R. Shenoy (1950s) questioned the Mahalanobis strategy; Jagdish Bhagwati criticised the licence raj.

Prelims fact bank

  • Sachchidananda Sinha was the provisional president of the Constituent Assembly.
  • Nehru moved the Objectives Resolution on 13 December 1946.
  • K. Santhanam of Madras was the chief defender of states' rights in the Constituent Assembly.
  • Gandhi Sagar, the first of four dams on the Chambal, was completed in 1960.
  • The Constitution was adopted on 26 November 1949 and came into force on 26 January 1950.
  • Sukumar Sen was the first Chief Election Commissioner.
  • The University Education Commission (1948–49) was chaired by S. Radhakrishnan; the Secondary Education Commission (1952–53) by A. Lakshmanaswami Mudaliar.
  • The first general elections were held in 1951–52.
  • The Planning Commission was set up in March 1950.
  • The National Development Council was set up in 1952.
  • The First Five-Year Plan was based on the Harrod–Domar model.
  • The Second Five-Year Plan was based on the Mahalanobis model.
  • Bhilai steel plant was built with Soviet help.
  • The Industrial Policy Resolution of 1956 is called the economic constitution of India.
  • The Community Development Programme began on 2 October 1952.
  • Vinoba Bhave began the Bhoodan movement at Pochampalli in 1951.
  • Panchayati raj was first launched at Nagaur on 2 October 1959.
  • The Panchsheel agreement was signed with China in 1954.
  • The first NAM summit was held at Belgrade in 1961.
  • The Avadi session (1955) adopted the socialistic pattern of society.
  • The Indus Waters Treaty was signed in 1960.
  • The Sino-Indian war was fought in 1962.
Exam traps
  • The Constitution was adopted on 26 November 1949 and members signed it on 24 January 1950; NCERT's timeline line "December 1949: Constitution is signed" is wrong.
  • First Plan: Harrod–Domar (agriculture); Second: Mahalanobis (heavy industry).
  • Bhilai (USSR), Rourkela (West Germany), Durgapur (UK).
  • Panchayati raj: Nagaur 1959 (not 1952 — that was CDP).
  • Bandung 1955 (Afro-Asian); Belgrade 1961 (first NAM summit).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Assess the achievements and limitations of planning in the Nehru era.

How to frame it: Framework → Plans → Achievements → Limitations

Model answer (96 words)

Nehru set up the Planning Commission (1950) and, through the 1956 Industrial Policy Resolution, gave the public sector the commanding heights of a mixed economy. The First Plan (Harrod–Domar) built irrigation and power and exceeded targets; the Second (Mahalanobis) created heavy industry — Bhilai, Rourkela, Durgapur — and institutions such as the IITs and the Atomic Energy Commission. But agriculture was neglected, leading to food imports; land reform remained incomplete; the licence raj bred inefficiency; and the Third Plan failed amid war and drought. Planning laid an industrial and scientific base but did not end poverty.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the main features of India's Non-Alignment policy under Nehru?

How to frame it: Meaning → Milestones → Limits

Model answer (42 words)

Non-Alignment meant independence from Cold War blocs while judging issues on merit. Nehru promoted it at Bandung (1955) and the Belgrade NAM summit (1961), with Panchsheel and peacekeeping roles in Korea and Indo-China. The 1962 war with China exposed its security limits.

2 marks · 15 words

What was the Bhoodan movement?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

Vinoba Bhave's land-gift movement, begun at Pochampalli (1951), seeking land for landless.

Revision card

Remember
  • Constitution 26 Nov 1949/26 Jan 1950; elections 1951–52 (Sukumar Sen).
  • Planning Commission 1950; NDC 1952; IPR 1956; Plans I (Harrod–Domar), II (Mahalanobis), III (failed).
  • Land reforms: zamindari abolition, tenancy, ceiling, consolidation; CDP 1952; Bhoodan 1951; PR Nagaur 1959.
  • Foreign: Panchsheel 1954, Bandung 1955, Belgrade 1961, Indus 1960, war 1962.

Part IV · Unit I46Science and Technology; Economic Reforms of 19911947 – 2000s

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Atomic energy and space programmes; Green and White Revolutions; IT; 1991 crisis and LPG reforms

Mains: GS-I: Post-independence India — science and technology, economic reforms

This closes the RAS history syllabus's "post-independence" section. Detailed science and economy content belongs in GS-III notebooks; here the historical narrative is enough.

The big picture

Independent India invested early in science and technology — atomic energy (Homi Bhabha), space (Vikram Sarabhai), agricultural research (M.S. Swaminathan) — as tools of self-reliance. The Green Revolution (late 1960s) ended dependence on food imports; Operation Flood made India the largest milk producer. The planned, regulated economy, however, ran into crisis: in 1991 India faced a balance-of-payments emergency, leading to liberalisation, privatisation and globalisation (LPG) under P.V. Narasimha Rao and Finance Minister Manmohan Singh.

Exam focus

Know the pioneers, the milestones and the causes, content and impact of the 1991 reforms.

WhenWhat happened
1948Atomic Energy Commission (Homi Bhabha)
1954Department of Atomic Energy
1956Apsara, Asia's first research reactor (Trombay)
1962INCOSPAR (Vikram Sarabhai)
1963First rocket launch from Thumba (21 November)
1965–66High-yielding wheat varieties introduced
1969ISRO founded; 14 banks nationalised
1970Operation Flood (Verghese Kurien, NDDB)
18 May 1974Pokhran-I (Smiling Buddha)

May 1998Pokhran-II (Operation Shakti)
2008Chandrayaan-1
2013–14Mangalyaan (Mars Orbiter Mission)

Core notes

I46.1 Science and technology

FieldPioneers and milestones
Atomic energyHomi J. Bhabha — TIFR (1945), Atomic Energy Commission (1948), Trombay (BARC, named 1967); three-stage nuclear programme (thorium); Apsara reactor (1956); Pokhran-I (1974, peaceful nuclear explosion); Pokhran-II (1998); NPT not signed
SpaceVikram Sarabhai — INCOSPAR (1962), Thumba launch (1963), ISRO (1969); Satish Dhawan; Aryabhata (1975, Soviet launch); SLV-3 (1980, A.P.J. Abdul Kalam project director); INSAT, PSLV, GSLV; Chandrayaan, Mangalyaan
DefenceDRDO (1958); IGMDP (1983, Abdul Kalam) — Prithvi, Agni, Trishul, Akash, Nag
AgricultureGreen Revolution — HYV seeds (Norman Borlaug's dwarf wheat), irrigation, fertilisers; M.S. Swaminathan; C. Subramaniam (agriculture minister); Punjab, Haryana, western UP
DairyOperation Flood (1970), Verghese Kurien, NDDB, Amul (Anand model) — White Revolution
Telecom and ITSam Pitroda — C-DOT (1984); software growth (Bangalore), Software Technology Parks (1991)

Green Revolution effects: food self-sufficiency; but regional and inter-personal inequality, soil and water degradation, wheat bias, rise of rich farmers.

I46.2 The 1991 crisis

  • Causes: fiscal deficits through the 1980s; rising external debt; Gulf War (1990–91) oil price rise and loss of remittances; political instability; collapse of the USSR (a major trade partner); foreign exchange reserves fell to about two weeks of imports; credit rating downgraded.
  • Emergency steps: gold pledged (Bank of England, Union Bank of Switzerland); IMF loan with conditions; rupee devalued (July 1991).

I46.3 The LPG reforms

  • Prime Minister P.V. Narasimha Rao, Finance Minister Manmohan Singh; New Industrial Policy (24 July 1991).
  • Liberalisation: industrial licensing abolished for most industries; MRTP restrictions eased; reduced reservation for the public sector.
  • Privatisation: disinvestment in public sector undertakings; private entry into reserved sectors.
  • Globalisation: import tariffs cut; quantitative restrictions removed; rupee made convertible on the current account (1993–94); foreign direct and portfolio investment encouraged; WTO (1995).
  • Financial sector: Narasimham Committee (1991) — banking reforms; SEBI given statutory powers (1992).
  • Tax reform: Raja Chelliah Committee.

Impact: higher growth rates (especially services and IT), larger foreign exchange reserves, expansion of the middle class; but critics point to jobless growth, agrarian distress, regional inequality and limited gains for the poor.

What historians say

Bipan Chandra et al. (India since Independence) discuss the shift from Nehruvian planning to reforms. Jagdish Bhagwati and Arvind Panagariya (India: The Emerging Giant) defend liberalisation; Jean Drèze and Amartya Sen (An Uncertain Glory) stress human development gaps. Itty Abraham (The Making of the Indian Atomic Bomb) and Gyan Prakash (Another Reason) study science and nationalism.

Prelims fact bank

  • Homi Bhabha founded TIFR in 1945.
  • The Atomic Energy Commission was set up in 1948.
  • Apsara (1956) was Asia's first research reactor.
  • The first rocket was launched from Thumba on 21 November 1963.
  • ISRO was founded in 1969.
  • Pokhran-I was conducted on 18 May 1974.
  • Aryabhata, India's first satellite, was launched in 1975.
  • SLV-3 placed Rohini in orbit in 1980.
  • The IGMDP began in 1983 under A.P.J. Abdul Kalam.
  • Verghese Kurien led Operation Flood.
  • M.S. Swaminathan is associated with the Green Revolution in India.
  • Sam Pitroda founded C-DOT in 1984.
  • Pokhran-II was conducted in May 1998.
  • The New Industrial Policy was announced on 24 July 1991.
  • P.V. Narasimha Rao was Prime Minister and Manmohan Singh Finance Minister during the 1991 reforms.
  • India joined the WTO in 1995.
Exam traps
  • Pokhran-I 1974 (Smiling Buddha); Pokhran-II 1998 (Shakti).
  • Aryabhata launched by a Soviet rocket; SLV-3 was India's first own launch to orbit (1980).
  • Green Revolution — wheat mainly; White Revolution — milk.
  • 1991 reforms: Rao (PM), Manmohan Singh (FM).

Previous-year questions (RAS)

ExamQuestionMarks
RAS Pre 2021When and where was the Central Electrochemical Research Institute set up? — Answer (RPSC final key): Karaikudi, 1953—

No verified RAS Mains question on this unit yet. RAS Pre questions come from the history sections of the 2021, 2023 and 2024 papers, paraphrased, with answers as per the RPSC final answer keys (2018 not used: its key was not available).

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

What were the causes and main features of the economic reforms of 1991?

How to frame it: Causes → Liberalisation → Privatisation → Globalisation → Impact

Model answer (93 words)

In 1991 large fiscal deficits, rising external debt, the Gulf War oil shock, lost remittances and the Soviet collapse left India with reserves for barely two weeks of imports. The Rao government, with Manmohan Singh as Finance Minister, pledged gold, took IMF help and devalued the rupee. The New Industrial Policy abolished most licensing and eased MRTP limits; public sector shares were disinvested; tariffs were cut, the rupee made current-account convertible and foreign investment invited; banking and capital markets were reformed. Growth and reserves rose, though critics note jobless growth and agrarian distress.

5 marks · 50 words

What were the achievements and limitations of the Green Revolution?

How to frame it: Achievements → Limitations

Model answer (42 words)

HYV seeds, irrigation and fertilisers, promoted by Swaminathan and C. Subramaniam from the mid-1960s, made India self-sufficient in food grains, mainly wheat. But gains were concentrated in Punjab, Haryana and western UP and among richer farmers, and caused soil and groundwater degradation.

2 marks · 15 words

Who was Homi Bhabha?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

Father of India's nuclear programme; founded TIFR and headed the Atomic Energy Commission.

Revision card

Remember
  • Atomic: Bhabha, AEC 1948, Apsara 1956, Pokhran 1974, 1998.
  • Space: Sarabhai, INCOSPAR 1962, Thumba 1963, ISRO 1969, Aryabhata 1975, SLV-3 1980.
  • Green Revolution (Swaminathan, Subramaniam); Operation Flood (Kurien); C-DOT (Pitroda).
  • 1991: BoP crisis; Rao–Manmohan; NIP 24 July 1991; LPG; WTO 1995.

Part IV · Unit I47Women's Reform Movement and Women's Empowerment1829 – 2001

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Social reform for women in the 19th century: sati, widow remarriage, female infanticide, child marriage, education; Women reformers: Savitribai Phule, Pandita Ramabai, Tarabai Shinde, Rukhmabai, D.K. Karve, Begum Rokeya; Women's organisations: WIA (1917), NCWI (1925), AIWC (1927); women in the freedom struggle; After 1947: constitutional guarantees, Hindu Code laws, Towards Equality (1974), NCW (1992), 73rd and 74th Amendments; Rajasthan link: Deorala (1987) and the Sati (Prevention) law; the Women's Development Programme and the Bhanwari Devi case leading to Vishaka (1997)

Mains: GS-I Part B: Post-independent India — Women empowerment and women reform movement; GS-I Part B: Socio-religious reform movements in the 19th and 20th century

New in the 2026 syllabus, so there is no RAS PYQ yet. Expect a 10-mark question on the course of the women's movement from social reform to empowerment, and short questions on laws, reports and reformers. Links: I35 (reform movements), I42 (women in Quit India), Law notebook (Domestic Violence Act, POSH Act).

The big picture

The women's question in India moved through three stages. First (c. 1820–1900), male reformers sought legal change for women: the ban on sati (1829), the Widow Remarriage Act (1856) and the Age of Consent Act (1891), with schools for girls. Women such as Savitribai Phule and Pandita Ramabai began speaking for themselves. Second (1900–1947), women organised: the Women's Indian Association (1917) and the All India Women's Conference (1927) demanded education, suffrage and legal reform, while thousands joined the Salt Satyagraha and Quit India. Third (after 1947), the Constitution promised equality, the Hindu Code laws (1955–56) reformed family law, the report Towards Equality (1974) showed how far reality lagged behind, and a new autonomous women's movement pressed for laws on dowry, rape and violence, leading to the National Commission for Women (1992) and one-third reservation in local bodies (73rd and 74th Amendments, 1992).

Exam focus

Answer frame: reform for women → movement by women → rights and empowerment through the state and law; end with a Rajasthan example (Deorala 1987, Bhanwari Devi and Vishaka 1997).

WhenWhat happened
4 December 1829Bengal Sati Regulation (Regulation XVII) under William Bentinck
1 January 1848Jyotiba and Savitribai Phule open a girls' school in Pune
1849J.E.D. Bethune founds a girls' school in Calcutta

1870Female Infanticide Prevention Act
1882Tarabai Shinde publishes Stri Purush Tulana; Pandita Ramabai founds the Arya Mahila Samaj
1891Age of Consent Act raises the age of consent to 12
1916D.K. Karve founds the Indian Women's University (now SNDT), Pune

1927All India Women's Conference, Pune

1931Karachi Congress resolution on fundamental rights includes equality of sexes

Core notes

I47.1 Stage 1: social reform for women (19th century)

IssueReformerOutcome
SatiRaja Rammohan RoyBengal Regulation XVII, 1829 (Bentinck)
Widow remarriageIshwar Chandra VidyasagarHindu Widows' Remarriage Act, 1856
Female infanticideBritish officials; reformersBengal regulations (1795, 1804); Female Infanticide Prevention Act, 1870
Child marriageBehramji Malabari; the Rukhmabai case (1884–88)Age of Consent Act, 1891 (12 years); Sarda Act, 1929
EducationPhules, Bethune, Vidyasagar, KarveGirls' schools (Pune 1848, Calcutta 1849); widows' home (1896) and women's university (1916) by Karve

Women's own voices. Savitribai Phule taught at the first girls' school and wrote poetry against caste and gender oppression. Tarabai Shinde's Stri Purush Tulana (1882) attacked double standards. Pandita Ramabai founded the Arya Mahila Samaj (1882) and Sharada Sadan (1889) for widows. Rukhmabai refused to accept a child marriage and became one of India's first women doctors. Begum Rokeya Sakhawat Hossain wrote Sultana's Dream (1905) and worked for Muslim girls' education in Bengal.

I47.2 Stage 2: women organise (1900–1947)

  • Organisations: Women's Indian Association (1917, Madras), National Council of Women in India (1925), and the All India Women's Conference (1927, Pune), which began with education and moved on to suffrage and legal reform.
  • Politics: Annie Besant presided over the Congress in 1917 and Sarojini Naidu in 1925, the first Indian woman to do so. Women won a limited franchise in the provinces in the 1920s and under the 1935 Act.
  • Freedom struggle: mass participation in the Salt Satyagraha (1930); in Quit India, Aruna Asaf Ali hoisted the flag at Gowalia Tank (9 August 1942) and Usha Mehta ran the underground Congress Radio; the INA's Rani of Jhansi Regiment was led by Lakshmi Swaminathan.
  • Karachi resolution (1931) promised equal rights regardless of sex: the bridge to the Constitution.

I47.3 Stage 3: rights and empowerment after 1947

  • Constitution: equality (Art. 14), no discrimination on grounds of sex with special provisions allowed for women (Art. 15(1), 15(3)), equal opportunity (Art. 16), equal pay for equal work (Art. 39(d)), maternity relief (Art. 42), and the duty to renounce practices derogatory to women (Art. 51A(e), added in 1976).
  • Hindu Code laws (1955–56): monogamy and divorce, daughters' inheritance, guardianship and adoption. The Special Marriage Act (1954) allowed civil marriage.
  • Other laws: Dowry Prohibition Act (1961), Maternity Benefit Act (1961), Equal Remuneration Act (1976).
  • Towards Equality (1974): the Committee on the Status of Women in India found falling sex ratios and widespread disadvantage; it gave rise to women's studies and a new women's movement.
  • Autonomous women's movement (1970s–80s): campaigns against dowry deaths and custodial rape (the Mathura case led to the 1983 criminal law amendment).
  • Institutions: National Commission for Women (1992, under the 1990 Act); 73rd and 74th Amendments (1992) reserving at least one-third of seats in panchayats and municipalities for women; National Policy for the Empowerment of Women (2001).

I47.4 Rajasthan link

  • Deorala, Sikar (1987): the sati of Roop Kanwar led to the Rajasthan Sati (Prevention) law and the central Commission of Sati (Prevention) Act, 1987.
  • Women's Development Programme (1984): village-level workers (sathins) campaigned against child marriage. The gang-rape of Bhanwari Devi, a sathin, in 1992 led to the Supreme Court's Vishaka guidelines (1997), later the basis of the POSH Act 2013 (Law notebook).
  • Rajasthan's women reformers and Praja Mandal activists are covered in the Rajasthan History notebook (R19, R20).

What historians say

Geraldine Forbes (Women in Modern India, 1996) traces the move from reform for women to women's organisations. Radha Kumar (The History of Doing, 1993) documents campaigns from the 19th century to the 1990s. Uma Chakravarti and Lata Mani argue that 19th-century reform debated women more than it listened to them; Mani shows the sati debate was really about scripture and colonial authority. Vina Mazumdar, member secretary of the 1974 committee, describes Towards Equality as the starting point of women's studies in India.

Prelims fact bank

  • Rashsundari Debi wrote the first full autobiography in modern Bengali (Amar Jiban).
  • Sati was banned in Bengal by Regulation XVII of 4 December 1829 under William Bentinck.
  • The Hindu Widows' Remarriage Act was passed in 1856 after Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar's campaign.
  • Jyotiba and Savitribai Phule opened a girls' school in Pune on 1 January 1848.
  • Tarabai Shinde wrote Stri Purush Tulana in 1882.
  • Pandita Ramabai founded the Arya Mahila Samaj in 1882 and Sharada Sadan in 1889.
  • The Age of Consent Act, 1891 raised the age of consent to 12 years.
  • D.K. Karve founded the Indian Women's University in Pune in 1916.
  • The Women's Indian Association was founded in Madras in 1917.
  • The All India Women's Conference held its first session in Pune in 1927.
  • The Child Marriage Restraint Act of 1929 is known as the Sarda Act.
  • Sarojini Naidu was the first Indian woman president of the Congress (Kanpur, 1925).
  • Towards Equality was the 1974 report of the Committee on the Status of Women in India.
  • The National Commission for Women was set up in 1992.
  • The 73rd and 74th Amendments reserve at least one-third of local body seats for women.
  • The Vishaka guidelines (1997) followed the Bhanwari Devi case in Rajasthan.
  • The Commission of Sati (Prevention) Act was passed in 1987 after the Deorala case.
Exam traps
  • Annie Besant (1917) was the first woman Congress president; Sarojini Naidu (1925) the first Indian woman.
  • Age of Consent Act 1891 (consent at 12) ≠ Sarda Act 1929 (marriage age).
  • Regulation XVII (1829) applied to Bengal first; it was extended to Madras and Bombay later.
  • NCW Act 1990; the commission was constituted in 1992.
  • Art. 51A(e) was added by the 42nd Amendment (1976), not in the original Constitution.

Women empowerment and the women reform movement enter the RAS Mains syllabus for the first time in 2026, so there is no previous question. The 2018 Mains question on the Indian Renaissance (I35) can be answered better with material from Stage 1 of this unit.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Trace the evolution of the women's movement in India from social reform to empowerment.

How to frame it: Stage 1: reform for women (sati, widow remarriage, education) → Stage 2: women's organisations and the freedom struggle → Stage 3: Constitution, Hindu Code, Towards Equality → Institutions: NCW, 73rd/74th Amendments → Rajasthan example → Conclusion

Model answer (101 words)

In the 19th century reformers sought laws for women: the sati ban (1829), widow remarriage (1856) and the Age of Consent Act (1891), with girls' schools started by the Phules. Women then organised through the WIA (1917) and AIWC (1927) and joined the Salt and Quit India movements. After 1947 the Constitution promised equality and the Hindu Code laws reformed family law, but Towards Equality (1974) exposed the gap, sparking campaigns against dowry and rape. The NCW (1992) and one-third reservation in local bodies followed. Rajasthan's Bhanwari Devi case produced the Vishaka guidelines (1997): reform became empowerment through law and participation.

5 marks · 50 words

What was the significance of the report "Towards Equality" (1974)?

How to frame it: Committee → Findings → Impact

Model answer (43 words)

Prepared by the Committee on the Status of Women in India, it showed a falling sex ratio and deep disadvantage in health, education and work despite constitutional equality. It launched women's studies, revived an autonomous women's movement and shaped later laws and institutions.

2 marks · 15 words

Who was Savitribai Phule?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (14 words)

Teacher and poet who, with Jyotiba Phule, opened a girls' school in Pune (1848).

Part VIndian Heritage (Indus to the British era)

4 units10 verified PYQs38 self-test questions

Part V · Unit I48Architecture: Indus to the British Erac. 2600 BCE – 1931 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Harappan town planning: Mohenjodaro, Dholavira, Lothal; Mauryan pillars, stupas and rock-cut caves; post-Mauryan stupas and chaityas; Temple architecture: Gupta beginnings; Nagara, Dravida and Vesara styles; Pallava, Chola, Chandela, Odisha, Hoysala; Indo-Islamic architecture: Sultanate, provincial styles, Vijayanagara, Mughal; Colonial architecture: Indo-Saracenic, New Delhi

Mains: GS-I Part B: Indian heritage — architecture from the Indus to the British era

Indian heritage (IH1) carried 55.5 marks across the five Mains papers 2016–2024, the sixth most-tested line. Architecture questions: Mauryan pillars (2023, 10 marks), indigenous impact on Mughal architecture (2024, 5 marks); the 2023 "continuity of culture" question (10 marks) is best answered with examples from this unit and I49–I51. Rajasthan forts, temples and havelis are in the Art, Culture and Heritage notebook (book 6).

The big picture

Indian architecture shows continuity with change. The Harappans built planned cities of baked brick with drains and a citadel. The Mauryas introduced monumental stone: polished pillars, the first stupas and rock-cut caves. Buddhist stupas and chaityas grew in the post-Mauryan centuries. The Gupta period gave the first structural temples, which matured into the Nagara (north), Dravida (south) and Vesara (Deccan) styles. From the 13th century the Indo-Islamic tradition added the true arch, dome and minaret; Indian craftsmen blended these with brackets, chhatris and lotus motifs, a synthesis that peaked under the Mughals. The British added the Indo-Saracenic style and the new capital at New Delhi.

Exam focus

For any architecture answer: structure (plan, materials, technique) → features → one or two named monuments with patron and date → what it shows about continuity or synthesis.

WhenWhat happened
c. 2600–1900 BCEMature Harappan cities: Great Bath at Mohenjodaro, dockyard at Lothal, reservoirs at Dholavira
3rd century BCEAshoka: polished sandstone pillars, core of the Sanchi stupa, Barabar caves for the Ajivikas
2nd century BCE – 1st century CEBharhut railings; gateways (toranas) of Sanchi; chaityas at Bhaja and Karle
5th century CEGupta temples: Sanchi Temple 17, Tigawa, Dashavatara temple at Deogarh, brick temple at Bhitargaon
7th–8th centuryPallava rathas at Mahabalipuram; Shore Temple under Narasimhavarman II (Rajasimha)
c. 1010Brihadeeswara (Rajarajeshwara) temple, Thanjavur, completed under Rajaraja I
c. 950–1050Chandela temples at Khajuraho (Kandariya Mahadeva)
13th centurySun temple at Konark under Narasimhadeva I (Eastern Ganga); Qutub Minar begun by Aibak, completed by Iltutmish
1311Alai Darwaza built by Alauddin Khalji
1565–72Humayun's tomb, Delhi, commissioned by Bega Begum; architect Mirak Mirza Ghiyas
1571–85Fatehpur Sikri, Akbar's capital
1632–c. 1653Taj Mahal, Agra, under Shah Jahan
1888Victoria Terminus, Bombay, designed by F.W. Stevens
1931New Delhi inaugurated (Lutyens and Baker)

Core notes

I48.1 Harappan and Mauryan foundations

  • Harappan: grid streets, covered drains, baked bricks in a standard 1:2:4 ratio, citadel and lower town. Mohenjodaro has the Great Bath and a large granary-like structure; Lothal a dockyard; Dholavira a three-part city with stone reservoirs.
  • Mauryan pillars: monolithic Chunar sandstone with a mirror polish, a plain tapering shaft without a base, and a capital carved from a separate block (inverted lotus, abacus, animal). The Sarnath lion capital is the national emblem.
  • Origin debate: some scholars saw Achaemenid (Persian) influence in the polish and bell capital. Others point to Indian features: shafts without a base (Persian columns stand on bases), monolithic shafts (Persian ones are built in segments), free-standing pillars rather than parts of buildings, and Indian animals and motifs. The 2023 Mains asked candidates to explain their indigenous origin.
  • Caves and stupas: the Barabar caves (Lomas Rishi has a carved chaitya façade) were given to the Ajivikas. Ashoka built the brick core of the Sanchi stupa; the stone casing and the four toranas came later.
  • Parts of a stupa: the hemispherical mound (anda); above it a balcony-like harmika (abode of the gods); a mast (yashti) carrying an umbrella (chhatri); a railing (vedika) around the mound, and gateways (toranas). Sanchi and Bharhut were plain except for railings and gateways; Amaravati and Shah-ji-ki-Dheri (Peshawar) carved the mound itself. Inscriptions record donors: Satavahana kings, monks, nuns, men and women, and guilds; the ivory workers of Vidisha paid for carving on one Sanchi gateway.
  • Reading the carvings: early sculptors showed the Buddha by symbols: an empty seat (meditation), a tree (enlightenment), a wheel (first sermon), a stupa (mahaparinibbana). Sanchi's northern gateway shows the Vessantara Jataka; the shalabhanjika (auspicious woman holding a tree) and Gajalakshmi panels show folk beliefs absorbed into Buddhism.

I48.2 Temple architecture

StyleRegionFeaturesExamples
NagaraNorth IndiaCurvilinear shikhara over the sanctum; amalaka on top; no boundary wall as a ruleKhajuraho (Kandariya Mahadeva), Konark, Lingaraja at Bhubaneswar
DravidaTamil countryStepped pyramidal tower (vimana); enclosure wall; gopuram gateways that later grow taller than the vimanaShore Temple, Brihadeeswara, Meenakshi temple (Madurai)
VesaraDeccanMix of the two; star-shaped plans under the HoysalasAihole and Pattadakal (Chalukya), Belur and Halebidu (Hoysala)

The common core stays the same throughout: garbhagriha (sanctum), mandapa (hall), a tower above the sanctum, and a circumambulatory path. Gupta temples such as Sanchi Temple 17 were flat-roofed; Deogarh shows an early shikhara.

I48.3 Indo-Islamic and Mughal synthesis

  • Sultanate: early buildings used corbelled arches built by Indian masons (Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque). The Alai Darwaza (1311) shows true arches and a true dome. Tughlaq buildings have sloping walls and little decoration.
  • Provincial styles: Gujarat (Jama Masjid, Ahmedabad) drew heavily on temple carving; the Deccan built the Gol Gumbaz at Bijapur; Jaunpur gave the Atala Masjid.
  • Vijayanagara: Dravida temples with tall gopurams, musical pillars and kalyana mandapas (Vitthala temple, Hampi); the Lotus Mahal blends Indo-Islamic arches.
  • Mughal: Humayun's tomb (1565–72) is the first full statement of the style: a central double dome, a tall arched gateway (pishtaq), the hasht bihisht ("eight paradises") plan of a central hall ringed by eight rooms, red sandstone edged with white marble, all set in a charbagh. Fatehpur Sikri mixes trabeate Indian construction with arches. Shah Jahan's buildings use white marble, pietra dura and cusped arches (Taj Mahal, Red Fort and Jama Masjid of Delhi).
  • Gardens: the charbagh is a walled garden split into four by water channels (Babur describes laying them out). A variant, the riverfront garden, puts the building at the river's edge instead of the centre; the Taj Mahal uses it, with the tomb on a terrace by the Yamuna and the garden to its south. Terraced charbaghs were built in Kashmir (Shalimar Bagh).
  • Court symbolism: Shah Jahan's audience halls in the Red Fort, Delhi, were pillared halls (chihil sutun, "forty pillars"); behind the throne in the Diwan-i-Am, pietra dura panels show Orpheus playing the lute, a symbol of just rule that brings harmony. Pietra dura (inlay of cut, polished coloured stones) was called parchin kari in Mughal India.
  • Indigenous impact on Mughal architecture (Mains 2024): chhatris, brackets and chhajjas, jharokhas, lotus and kalash finials, trabeate halls (Jodha Bai's palace, Panch Mahal) drawing on Gujarat and Malwa styles, the curved Bangla roof borrowed from Bengal's thatched huts, and the Hindu craftsmen of Rajasthan and Gujarat who built them.

I48.4 Exchange between regions

  • Trabeate and arcuate: trabeate (post-and-beam) construction places horizontal beams across vertical supports; arcuate construction carries the load on arches. Arcuate building spread from the 13th century, helped by lime mortar that set into concrete with stone chips.
  • Influence ran both ways: Vijayanagara's elephant stables show the arches and domes of the neighbouring Bijapur and Golconda sultanates; the Govind Deva temple at Vrindavan (built by Raja Man Singh of Amber, 1590) uses red sandstone and intersecting arches like Fatehpur Sikri.
  • Bengal temples (late 15th to 19th century): brick and terracotta temples copying the double roof (dochala) or four-part roof (chauchala) of village huts, on a square platform, with plain interiors and terracotta panels outside; the finest are at Vishnupur (Bankura district). Many were funded by artisan and trading groups whose fortunes rose with European trade.
  • Public works: Iltutmish's reservoir Hauz-i-Sultani at Delhi; rulers gained legitimacy by building tanks, wells, sarais and bazaars as well as places of worship.

I48.5 Colonial architecture

  • Neo-classical (geometric fronts with tall columns, from Roman models): the Town Hall, Bombay (1833) and Elphinstone Circle (now Horniman Circle), built in the cotton boom of the 1860s; Writers' Building, Calcutta.
  • Neo-Gothic (steep roofs, pointed arches, rich decoration): Bombay University and High Court, and above all Victoria Terminus (F.W. Stevens, 1888), Gothic with some Indian motifs. Indian donors paid for parts: Cowasjee Jehangir Readymoney (University Hall) and Premchand Roychand (the Rajabai clock tower, named after his mother).
  • Indo-Saracenic ("Indo" for Hindu, "Saracen" for Muslim; early 20th century): European plans with Indian domes, chhatris, jalis and arches: Madras High Court, Mysore Palace, the Taj Mahal Hotel and the Gateway of India (commemorating the 1911 royal visit; completed 1924). The bungalow took its name from the Bengali bangla hut.
  • New Delhi: capital moved from Calcutta in 1911; Edwin Lutyens and Herbert Baker designed it; inaugurated in 1931. The Viceroy's House (Rashtrapati Bhavan) uses a dome inspired by the Sanchi stupa and Mughal chhatris.
  • The Victoria Memorial, Calcutta (William Emerson, opened 1921) uses white Makrana marble from Rajasthan.

What historians say

Percy Brown (Indian Architecture, 2 volumes) set the Buddhist–Hindu and Islamic periods that most textbooks still use. Stella Kramrisch (The Hindu Temple) explains the temple as a cosmic diagram. Ebba Koch (Mughal Architecture) shows how the Mughals combined Timurid and Indian elements. On Mauryan pillars, John Irwin argued for an Indian pillar cult older than Ashoka, while older writers stressed Persian influence; the NCERT Class 11 text An Introduction to Indian Art presents both views.

Prelims fact bank

  • Harappan baked bricks follow a standard 1:2:4 ratio.
  • The Great Bath is at Mohenjodaro; the dockyard is at Lothal.
  • Mauryan pillars are monolithic Chunar sandstone with a mirror polish.
  • The Sarnath lion capital of Ashoka is India's national emblem.
  • The Barabar caves were dedicated to the Ajivikas.
  • The Dashavatara temple at Deogarh is a Gupta temple with an early shikhara.
  • The Shore Temple at Mahabalipuram was built under Narasimhavarman II (Rajasimha).
  • The Brihadeeswara temple at Thanjavur was completed around 1010 under Rajaraja I.
  • The Konark Sun temple was built under Narasimhadeva I of the Eastern Ganga dynasty.
  • The Alai Darwaza (1311) has the first true arches and dome in India.
  • Nagara temples have a curvilinear shikhara; Dravida temples have a pyramidal vimana and gopurams.
  • Humayun's tomb (1565–72) has a double dome, a hasht bihisht plan and a charbagh garden.
  • Pietra dura inlay was called parchin kari in Mughal India.
  • The Govind Deva temple at Vrindavan was built by Raja Man Singh of Amber in 1590.
  • The Durga temple at Aihole (Chalukya) has an apsidal (rounded-end) plan.
  • Dochala and chauchala temples of Bengal copy the roofs of thatched huts; Vishnupur has the best examples.
  • Victoria Terminus in Bombay was designed by F.W. Stevens and completed in 1888.
  • New Delhi was designed by Edwin Lutyens and Herbert Baker and inaugurated in 1931.
Exam traps
  • Victoria Terminus is NCERT's main example of neo-Gothic, not Indo-Saracenic; the Gateway of India is Indo-Saracenic.
  • The Gateway of India marks the 1911 visit of George V but was completed only in 1924.
  • Qutub Minar was begun by Aibak and completed by Iltutmish; Firoz Tughlaq repaired the top storeys.
  • The Shore Temple is structural; the five rathas are monolithic rock-cut shrines.
  • Ashoka built the brick core of Sanchi; the stone toranas are post-Mauryan.
  • Gol Gumbaz is at Bijapur (Adil Shahi), not Golconda.
  • NCERT Class 7 says Kandariya Mahadeva was built in 999 by Dhanga; most scholars date it c. 1025–50 under Vidyadhara. Safe Prelims answer: Chandela, Khajuraho.
  • Humayun's tomb was built by his widow Bega Begum (1565–72), not by Humayun or Akbar.
  • Vesara is the Deccan hybrid style, not a separate southern style.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

ExamQuestionMarks
RAS Mains 2023Mauryan pillars are of indigenous origin. Explain.10
RAS Mains 2024Examine the indigenous impact on Mughal architecture.5
RAS Mains 2023Continuity of culture is a unique feature of Indian civilization, which is unparalleled in world civilizations. Explain. (Use examples from I48–I51.)10

Questions as printed in the RPSC Mains papers (GS Paper I), lightly edited for grammar. Prelims questions on architecture are listed in the units on the relevant dynasties.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Mauryan pillars are of indigenous origin. Explain.

How to frame it: Describe the pillars → Persian influence view → Indian features: no base, monolithic, free-standing → Indian motifs and purpose → Conclusion

Model answer (106 words)

Ashoka's pillars are monolithic Chunar sandstone shafts with a mirror polish, crowned by an inverted lotus, an abacus and animals, as at Sarnath. Their polish and bell capital led some scholars to see Persian influence. But the differences are basic: Persian columns stand on bases, are built in segments and support roofs, whereas Mauryan shafts have no base, are single blocks and stand free. The bull, elephant and lion, the lotus and the dharma-chakra are Indian motifs, and the pillars carried Ashoka's dhamma message. Irwin traced them to an older Indian pillar cult. Foreign ideas may have been absorbed, but the form and purpose are Indian.

5 marks · 50 words

Examine the indigenous impact on Mughal architecture.

How to frame it: Indian elements → Examples → Craftsmen

Model answer (39 words)

Mughal buildings used Indian chhatris, brackets, chhajjas, jharokhas and lotus finials, and trabeate halls at Fatehpur Sikri (Panch Mahal, Jodha Bai's palace) in the Gujarat and Malwa manner, and the Bengal-style Bangla roof. Rajasthani and Gujarati craftsmen built them.

2 marks · 15 words

What is a gopuram?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (12 words)

The tall gateway tower in the enclosure wall of a Dravida-style temple.

Revision card

Remember
  • Harappan: grid, drains, 1:2:4 bricks; Great Bath (Mohenjodaro), dockyard (Lothal), reservoirs (Dholavira).
  • Mauryan: polished monolithic pillars, Sanchi core, Barabar caves (Ajivikas); indigenous-origin arguments.
  • Temples: Gupta (Deogarh) → Nagara / Dravida / Vesara; Shore Temple, Brihadeeswara c. 1010, Khajuraho, Konark.
  • Indo-Islamic: Alai Darwaza 1311 (true arch); Gol Gumbaz; Vitthala temple; Humayun's tomb 1565–72 (hasht bihisht, pishtaq), Fatehpur Sikri, Taj (riverfront garden).
  • Exchange: Bangla roof, Gujarat–Malwa at Fatehpur Sikri, Govind Deva temple 1590, Bengal dochala/chauchala (Vishnupur).
  • Colonial: neo-classical (Town Hall 1833), neo-Gothic (VT 1888), Indo-Saracenic (Gateway 1924); New Delhi 1931 (Lutyens, Baker).

Sources: NCERT Class 11, An Introduction to Indian Art, Part I (2002 edition).; Percy Brown, Indian Architecture (Buddhist and Hindu; Islamic Period).; Ebba Koch, Mughal Architecture (1991).; Archaeological Survey of India site notes (Sanchi, Thanjavur, Konark, Fatehpur Sikri).

Part V · Unit I49Sculpture and PaintingPrehistory – 1905 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Harappan sculpture: bronze, stone and terracotta; seals; Mauryan and post-Mauryan sculpture; Gandhara, Mathura and Amaravati schools; Gupta and early medieval sculpture: Sarnath, Elephanta, Ellora, Mahabalipuram; Chola bronzes; Painting: Bhimbetka, Ajanta, Bagh; Pala manuscripts; Mughal, Deccani and Pahari schools; Modern trends: Company painting, Raja Ravi Varma, the Bengal School

Mains: GS-I Part B: Indian heritage — sculpture and painting

The most-tested part of IH1. RAS Mains asked about the Dancing Girl (2016, 2 marks), Gandhara vs Mathura (2016, 5), Mughal painting (2016, 10), Arjuna's Penance (2018, 2) and Harappan bronzes (2018, 5). Rajasthani painting schools are in book 6.

The big picture

Indian sculpture begins with the small bronzes and steatite figures of the Harappans, rises to the polished Mauryan court style, and becomes a narrative and devotional art in the stupa railings of Bharhut and Sanchi. Around the 1st–3rd centuries CE three schools shaped the Buddha image: Gandhara (Greco-Roman style), Mathura (Indian, red sandstone) and Amaravati (narrative, white limestone). The Gupta Buddha of Sarnath set the classical ideal; Pallava, Rashtrakuta and Chola art carried it into rock and bronze. Painting runs from the rock shelters of Bhimbetka to the murals of Ajanta, then to manuscript illustration, and to the Mughal atelier, where Persian and Indian painters created a new naturalistic style that fed Rajasthani and Pahari painting. Under colonial rule came Company painting, oil painting (Raja Ravi Varma) and the national Bengal School.

WhenWhat happened
MesolithicRock paintings at Bhimbetka (Madhya Pradesh), a UNESCO World Heritage site
c. 2500 BCEBronze Dancing Girl and steatite Priest-King from Mohenjodaro
3rd century BCESarnath lion capital; Didarganj Yakshi (dated by many to the Mauryan period)
1st–3rd century CEGandhara, Mathura and Amaravati schools under the Kushanas and Satavahanas
5th centurySarnath Buddha (Gupta); Ajanta murals of Caves 1, 2, 16 and 17 (Vakataka period)
7th centuryArjuna's Penance (Descent of the Ganga), Mahabalipuram, under the Pallavas
8th centuryKailasa temple, Ellora (Rashtrakuta Krishna I); Elephanta Trimurti (c. 6th century)
10th–11th centuryChola bronzes: Nataraja
c. 1557–77Hamzanama painted in Akbar's atelier
1605–27Jahangir's reign: Mansur, Abul Hasan, Bishandas
Late 17th – 18th centuryPahari schools: Basohli (late 17th century), then Guler and Kangra (mid-18th century)
1890sRaja Ravi Varma's oleograph press
1905Abanindranath Tagore paints Bharat Mata

Core notes

I49.1 Harappan sculpture

  • Dancing Girl (Mohenjodaro, bronze): made by the lost-wax (cire perdue) method; nude figure with a hand on the hip, the other arm covered with bangles, a necklace, and a confident pose. Mortimer Wheeler and others praised its naturalism.
  • Other bronzes (Mains 2018): bronze buffalo and ram, and the bronze chariot and animals from Daimabad (Maharashtra). They show knowledge of alloying and lost-wax casting.
  • Stone: the steatite Priest-King with a trefoil-patterned shawl; red sandstone male torso from Harappa.
  • Terracotta mother-goddess figures, toys; seals of steatite with animals (unicorn, bull) and the "Pashupati" seal.

I49.2 From Mauryan to Gupta

FeatureGandharaMathuraAmaravati
Region and patronsNorth-west (Kushanas)Mathura (Kushanas)Andhra (Satavahanas, Ikshvakus)
MaterialGrey-blue schist, stuccoSpotted red sandstoneWhite marble-like limestone
InfluenceGreco-Roman (Hellenistic)Indian, from yaksha figuresIndian, narrative
Buddha imageSpiritual, wavy hair, heavy folded robes, moustache in someSmiling, energetic, shaven head or snail curls, thin robeMostly in narrative panels, dynamic figures
SubjectsMainly BuddhistBuddhist, Jain and BrahmanicalMainly Buddhist

Gupta: the Sarnath seated Buddha (preaching posture, transparent robe) and the Mathura standing Buddha set the classical ideal; the Varaha panel at Udayagiri shows Brahmanical sculpture.

I49.3 Rock-cut and bronze masterpieces

  • Arjuna's Penance / Descent of the Ganga (Mahabalipuram, Pallava, 7th century): a huge open-air relief on two boulders with a natural cleft for water; gods, sages, animals and an ascetic (identified as Arjuna or as Bhagiratha) and the famous cat imitating him.
  • Elephanta: the three-headed Shiva (Mahesamurti) in the main cave.
  • Ellora: 34 caves of Buddhist, Hindu and Jain faiths; the Kailasa temple (Cave 16) carved from the top down under Krishna I.
  • Chola bronzes: Nataraja dancing within a ring of fire, trampling the dwarf of ignorance, made by lost-wax.

I49.4 Painting

  • Ajanta: Buddhist murals on lime plaster showing Jataka stories; the Bodhisattva Padmapani of Cave 1 is the best known. Bagh (Madhya Pradesh) has related murals.
  • Manuscript painting: Pala palm-leaf Buddhist manuscripts (eastern India) and Jain paper manuscripts (western India).
  • Mughal painting (Mains 2016): Humayun brought Mir Sayyid Ali and Abd al-Samad from Persia. Under Akbar the atelier, with Indian painters such as Daswanth and Basawan, produced the Hamzanama, Razmnama and Akbarnama: narrative, crowded, lively. Under Jahangir it turned to portraits and nature studies: Ustad Mansur (birds and animals), Abul Hasan (Nadir-uz-Zaman). Under Shah Jahan it became formal and decorative; Aurangzeb withdrew patronage and painters moved to Rajput and Pahari courts.
  • Deccani (Bijapur, Golconda).
  • Pahari (hill states of present Himachal Pradesh and Jammu): Basohli, by the late 17th century, used bold, intense colours; Bhanudatta's Rasamanjari was its favourite text. Nadir Shah's sack of Delhi (1739) sent more Mughal-trained artists to the hills. Kangra, from the mid-18th century, drew on Vaishnava themes with soft, cool blues and greens and a lyrical line.
  • Colonial: Company painting for British patrons; Raja Ravi Varma's oil paintings and oleographs of mythological themes; Abanindranath Tagore's Bengal School, which rejected Western academic art (Bharat Mata, 1905).

What historians say

Ananda Coomaraswamy argued that the Buddha image was an Indian creation at Mathura, against Alfred Foucher, who credited Gandhara and Greek influence; most scholars now accept that both arose around the same time. Percy Brown and Milo Beach (Mughal and Rajput Painting) trace the Mughal atelier. Partha Mitter studies colonial art and the Bengal School.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Harappan Dancing Girl from Mohenjodaro is a bronze made by the lost-wax method.
  • The bronze chariot hoard from Daimabad is from Maharashtra.
  • The Priest-King figure from Mohenjodaro is made of steatite.
  • Gandhara sculpture used grey-blue schist and shows Greco-Roman influence.
  • Mathura sculpture used spotted red sandstone.
  • Amaravati sculpture used white limestone and is mainly narrative.
  • The Sarnath seated Buddha is a Gupta sculpture in the preaching posture.
  • Arjuna's Penance at Mahabalipuram is a Pallava relief of the 7th century.
  • The Kailasa temple at Ellora was carved under the Rashtrakuta king Krishna I.
  • Chola bronzes of Nataraja were made by the lost-wax method.
  • Mir Sayyid Ali and Abd al-Samad founded the Mughal atelier under Humayun and Akbar.
  • The Hamzanama was painted in Akbar's atelier.
  • Ustad Mansur painted birds and animals for Jahangir.
  • Abanindranath Tagore painted Bharat Mata in 1905.
  • Bhanudatta's Rasamanjari was the favourite text of Basohli painters.
  • Kangra painting is known for Vaishnava themes in soft blues and greens.
  • Ajanta has about 30 rock-cut Buddhist caves made in two phases, from the 2nd century BCE to about 480 CE.
Exam traps
  • Dancing Girl is bronze; the Priest-King is steatite (stone).
  • Mathura made Buddhist, Jain and Hindu images; Gandhara was mainly Buddhist.
  • Abul Hasan was titled Nadir-uz-Zaman; Mansur was Nadir-ul-Asr.
  • The Kailasa temple is Ellora Cave 16 (Rashtrakuta), not Elephanta.

Questions as printed in the RPSC Mains papers (GS Paper I), lightly edited for grammar.

Mains answer writing

10 marks · 100 words

Write a detailed note on Mughal painting.

How to frame it: Origins under Humayun → Akbar's atelier → Jahangir: portraits and nature → Shah Jahan and decline → Legacy

Model answer (94 words)

Humayun brought Mir Sayyid Ali and Abd al-Samad from Persia. Akbar organised a large atelier where Persian masters trained Indian painters such as Daswanth and Basawan; works like the Hamzanama and Razmnama are lively, crowded narratives that blend Persian line with Indian colour. Jahangir preferred single portraits and nature studies: Mansur painted birds and animals, Abul Hasan court scenes, and European perspective and shading appeared. Under Shah Jahan painting became formal and ornate. Aurangzeb withdrew patronage, so painters moved to Rajput and Pahari courts, where the Mughal style shaped Kishangarh, Kangra and other schools.

5 marks · 50 words

Write a short note on bronze images found from Harappan sites.

How to frame it: Technique → Examples → Significance

Model answer (39 words)

Harappans cast bronzes by the lost-wax method. The Dancing Girl of Mohenjodaro is the best known; a buffalo and a ram are also known, and Daimabad yielded a chariot with animals. They show skill in alloying and lifelike modelling.

2 marks · 15 words

Briefly describe the "Arjun's Penance" sculpture.

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (13 words)

A 7th-century Pallava open-air relief at Mahabalipuram showing the descent of the Ganga.

Revision card

Remember
  • Harappan: Dancing Girl (bronze, lost-wax), Priest-King (steatite), Daimabad bronzes, seals, terracotta.
  • Gandhara (schist, Greek) · Mathura (red sandstone, Indian, all faiths) · Amaravati (limestone, narrative).
  • Gupta Sarnath Buddha; Arjuna's Penance (Pallava); Elephanta Trimurti; Ellora Kailasa (Krishna I); Chola Nataraja.
  • Painting: Bhimbetka → Ajanta/Bagh → Pala, Jain → Mughal (Akbar narrative, Jahangir portrait) → Pahari.
  • Colonial: Company painting, Ravi Varma, Bengal School (Bharat Mata 1905).
