Part V · Unit I50Music, Dance and TheatreVedic age – 1943 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Origins: Samaveda, Bharata's Natyashastra; ragas and talas; Medieval music: Amir Khusrau, Sangita Ratnakara, Tansen, the Bhakti composers; Hindustani and Carnatic traditions; Classical dances recognised by the Sangeet Natak Akademi; Theatre: Sanskrit drama, Koodiyattam, folk theatre, modern and political theatre

Mains: GS-I Part B: Indian heritage — performing arts

No RAS Mains question on music or dance yet, but Prelims PI7 names medieval music directly, and IH1 is wide enough for a 5-mark question on Natyashastra, Hindustani vs Carnatic, or a classical dance. Rajasthan's folk dances, instruments and gharanas are in book 6.

The big picture

India's performing arts start from ritual: the hymns of the Samaveda were sung, and Bharata's Natyashastra (dated broadly between the 2nd century BCE and the 2nd century CE) treated drama, dance and music as one art built on rasa (aesthetic emotion). In the medieval period temple and court patronage, the Bhakti movement and Persian influence produced two classical systems: Hindustani in the north, shaped by Amir Khusrau and Tansen, and Carnatic in the south, systematised by Purandara Dasa and later the Trinity. The Sangeet Natak Akademi recognises eight classical dances. Theatre ran from Sanskrit drama through regional folk forms to the political stage of the colonial era.

WhenWhat happened
c. 1000 BCE onwardsSamaveda: Rigvedic verses set to melody
c. 2nd century BCE – 2nd century CENatyashastra attributed to Bharata
13th centurySangita Ratnakara by Sharngadeva (court of the Yadavas of Devagiri)
1253–1325Amir Khusrau, credited with qawwali and with developing the khayal tradition
15th–16th centuryRaja Man Singh Tomar of Gwalior patronises dhrupad
16th centuryPurandara Dasa systematises Carnatic teaching; Tansen at Akbar's court
18th–19th centuryCarnatic Trinity: Tyagaraja, Muthuswami Dikshitar, Syama Sastri
1860Nil Darpan by Dinabandhu Mitra (Bengali play on indigo planters)
1876Dramatic Performances Act to censor political plays
1943Indian People's Theatre Association (IPTA) founded
1953Sangeet Natak Akademi set up

Core notes

I50.1 Music

FeatureHindustaniCarnatic
RegionNorth IndiaSouth India
InfluencesIndian base with Persian and court influencesLargely indigenous, temple and devotional
FormsDhrupad, khayal, thumri, tappa, taranaKriti, varnam, kirtanam
ImprovisationLarge scope; raga elaboration (alap)Compositions central; improvisation within them
Key figuresAmir Khusrau, Swami Haridas, TansenPurandara Dasa, Tyagaraja, Dikshitar, Syama Sastri

Instruments are classed in the Natyashastra as tata (stringed), sushira (wind), avanaddha (membrane) and ghana (solid). Amir Khusrau is linked with qawwali, the sitar and tabla by tradition, though historians treat these attributions with caution.

I50.2 Classical dance

The Sangeet Natak Akademi recognises eight classical dances:

DanceStateNote
BharatanatyamTamil NaduRevived by Rukmini Devi Arundale
KathakNorth IndiaLucknow, Jaipur and Banaras gharanas
KathakaliKeralaDance-drama with elaborate make-up; revived by Vallathol
KuchipudiAndhra PradeshNamed after a village; tarangam on a brass plate
OdissiOdishaTribhanga posture; links with the Konark sculptures
ManipuriManipurVaishnava ras lila themes
MohiniyattamKeralaGraceful solo dance by women
SattriyaAssamFrom Sankardeva's sattras; recognised in 2000

How Kathak grew: the name comes from katha (story). Kathaks were a caste of temple storytellers in north India. With the Bhakti movement (15th–16th centuries) their gestures merged with the Radha–Krishna rasa lila folk plays. At the Mughal and later courts it gained fast footwork and rich costume. The Lucknow gharana grew under Wajid Ali Shah, the last Nawab of Awadh; the Jaipur gharana at the Rajput courts. British officials of the 19th century looked down on it; it was recognised as classical after independence.

I50.3 Theatre

  • Sanskrit drama: Bhasa, Kalidasa (Abhijnanashakuntalam), Shudraka (Mrichchhakatika), Vishakhadatta (Mudrarakshasa); see I51 for the historical plays.
  • Koodiyattam (Kerala): the surviving form of Sanskrit theatre; proclaimed by UNESCO in 2001 as a masterpiece of intangible heritage.
  • Folk theatre: Yakshagana (Karnataka), Jatra (Bengal), Nautanki (Uttar Pradesh), Tamasha (Maharashtra), Bhavai (Gujarat); Rajasthan's Khyal and Gavari in book 6.
  • Colonial era: Parsi theatre companies; Nil Darpan (1860) on indigo oppression; the Dramatic Performances Act (1876); the IPTA (1943) took theatre to political themes such as the Bengal famine.

What historians say

Kapila Vatsyayan (Classical Indian Dance in Literature and the Arts) links dance to sculpture and texts. Allyn Miner and Katherine Butler Brown study Mughal music and question many traditional attributions to Amir Khusrau. Sunil Kothari documents the classical dances.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Samaveda set Rigvedic verses to melody.
  • The Natyashastra is attributed to Bharata.
  • The Natyashastra classifies instruments into tata, sushira, avanaddha and ghana.
  • Sangita Ratnakara was written by Sharngadeva in the 13th century.
  • Purandara Dasa systematised the teaching of Carnatic music.
  • The Carnatic Trinity are Tyagaraja, Muthuswami Dikshitar and Syama Sastri.
  • Tansen was a musician at Akbar's court.
  • Raja Man Singh Tomar of Gwalior patronised dhrupad.
  • The Sangeet Natak Akademi recognises eight classical dances.
  • Sattriya of Assam was recognised as a classical dance in 2000.
  • Koodiyattam was proclaimed by UNESCO in 2001.
  • Nil Darpan (1860) was written by Dinabandhu Mitra.
  • The Dramatic Performances Act was passed in 1876.
  • The Indian People's Theatre Association was founded in 1943.
  • The word Kathak comes from katha, meaning story.
  • The Lucknow gharana of Kathak grew under Wajid Ali Shah, the last Nawab of Awadh.
Exam traps
  • Sangita Ratnakara (Sharngadeva) ≠ Natyashastra (Bharata).
  • Kathakali and Mohiniyattam are both from Kerala; Kuchipudi is from Andhra Pradesh.
  • Older NCERT texts say "six" classical dances; the current Sangeet Natak Akademi list has eight.
  • Sattriya comes from Sankardeva's monasteries in Assam, not Manipur.
  • Nil Darpan is a play (Dinabandhu Mitra), not a newspaper.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

No RAS Mains question on music, dance or theatre in 2016–2024. The 2016 question on historical plays of Sanskrit literature is covered in I51.

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

Distinguish between Hindustani and Carnatic music.

How to frame it: Region and influence → Forms → Improvisation → Figures

Model answer (41 words)

Hindustani music developed in the north with Persian and court influence; its forms include dhrupad, khayal and thumri, with wide improvisation (Tansen, Amir Khusrau). Carnatic music in the south is temple-based and centred on compositions such as kritis (Purandara Dasa, Tyagaraja).

5 marks · 50 words

What is the significance of the Natyashastra?

How to frame it: Author and date → Content → Rasa → Legacy

Model answer (34 words)

Attributed to Bharata, it is the oldest Indian treatise on drama, dance and music. It explains stagecraft, gestures, instruments and the theory of rasa, and remains the base of classical dance and aesthetic theory.

2 marks · 15 words

What is Koodiyattam?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (11 words)

Kerala's surviving form of Sanskrit theatre, proclaimed by UNESCO in 2001.

Revision card

Remember
  • Samaveda → Natyashastra (Bharata; rasa; four instrument classes) → Sangita Ratnakara (Sharngadeva, 13th century).
  • Hindustani: Khusrau, Man Singh Tomar (dhrupad), Haridas, Tansen. Carnatic: Purandara Dasa, Trinity.
  • Eight SNA classical dances; Sattriya 2000.
  • Theatre: Sanskrit drama, Koodiyattam (UNESCO 2001), folk forms, Nil Darpan 1860, Act 1876, IPTA 1943.

  6. How many classical dances does the Sangeet Natak Akademi recognise?
    Show answer

    Eight.

  7. Which classical dance comes from Assam?
    Show answer

    Sattriya.

  8. Who wrote Nil Darpan?
    Show answer

    Dinabandhu Mitra (1860).

  9. When was the IPTA founded?
    Show answer

    1943.

Part V · Unit I51Literature: Vedic to the British Erac. 1500 BCE – 1913 CE

Where this fits in the syllabus

Prelims: Vedic literature; epics and Puranas; Buddhist and Jain texts in Pali and Prakrit; Sangam literature; Classical Sanskrit: Kalidasa, Bana, historical plays and chronicles; Medieval literature: Persian, Bhakti literature in regional languages; Modern literature: Bharatendu Harishchandra, Bankim Chandra, Tagore, Premchand

Mains: GS-I Part B: Indian heritage — literature; GS-I Part B: Socio-religious reform and the Renaissance (modern literature)

RAS Mains asked about the historical plays of Sanskrit literature (2016, 5 marks) and Bharatendu Harishchandra as precursor of modern Hindi literature (2018, 5 marks). Rajasthani literature (Prithviraj Raso, dingal and pingal) is in book 6.

The big picture

India's literature is one of the longest continuous traditions in the world. The Vedas were transmitted orally for centuries; the epics and Puranas carried religious and social ideas to wider audiences. Buddhist and Jain texts used Pali and Prakrit, the language of the people. The Sangam poems record early Tamil society. Classical Sanskrit produced Kalidasa's plays, Bana's prose and Kalhana's chronicle. In the medieval period Persian became the court language while the Bhakti saints made regional languages literary. In the 19th century print, reform and nationalism produced modern prose, the novel and political poetry.

WhenWhat happened
c. 1500–1000 BCERigveda
c. 1000–500 BCELater Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas, Upanishads
c. 400 BCE – 400 CEMahabharata and Ramayana take their present form
c. 3rd century BCE – 3rd century CESangam literature (Tamil); Tolkappiyam (grammar)
4th–5th centuryKalidasa
7th centuryBanabhatta's Harshacharita at Harsha's court
1148–49Kalhana's Rajatarangini (history of Kashmir)
13th–14th centuryAmir Khusrau's Persian and Hindavi works
16th centuryBaburnama (in Turki); Akbarnama by Abul Fazl; Razmnama (Persian Mahabharata)
1574–77Tulsidas's Ramcharitmanas in Awadhi
1868–1885Bharatendu Harishchandra's journals and plays (Kavivachan Sudha, 1868)
1882Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay's Anandamath, containing Vande Mataram
1913Rabindranath Tagore wins the Nobel Prize for Literature for Gitanjali

Core notes

I51.1 Ancient literature

  • Vedic: four Vedas (Rig, Sama, Yajur, Atharva), each with Brahmanas, Aranyakas and Upanishads; Vedangas (phonetics, ritual, grammar, etymology, metre, astronomy).
  • Epics and Puranas: Mahabharata (attributed to Vyasa, includes the Bhagavad Gita) and Ramayana (Valmiki); eighteen major Puranas.
  • Buddhist and Jain: the Tripitaka in Pali; Jataka tales; Jain Agamas in Prakrit.
  • Sangam: Tamil anthologies (Ettuthogai, Pattuppattu); later the epics Silappadikaram and Manimekalai; Tiruvalluvar's Tirukkural.

I51.2 Classical Sanskrit and historical plays

WorkAuthorHistorical subject
SvapnavasavadattaBhasaKing Udayana of Vatsa and Vasavadatta
MalavikagnimitramKalidasaAgnimitra, Shunga king, and Malavika
MudrarakshasaVishakhadattaChanakya's plan to win Rakshasa, minister of the Nandas, for Chandragupta Maurya
DevichandraguptamVishakhadatta (fragments only)Chandragupta II rescuing his brother Ramagupta's queen from the Shakas
Harshacharita (prose)BanabhattaEarly life of Harshavardhana
Rajatarangini (verse chronicle)KalhanaKings of Kashmir

These works mix history with drama and praise, so historians use them alongside inscriptions and coins. Other classics: Kalidasa's Abhijnanashakuntalam, Meghaduta and Raghuvamsha; Shudraka's Mrichchhakatika; Panchatantra.

I51.3 Medieval literature

  • Persian: Amir Khusrau; Ziauddin Barani's Tarikh-i-Firoz Shahi; Abul Fazl's Akbarnama and Ain-i-Akbari; translations of the Mahabharata (Razmnama) and Upanishads (Dara Shikoh's Sirr-i-Akbar).
  • Turki: Babur's memoirs, Baburnama.
  • Bhakti in regional languages: Kabir, Surdas and Tulsidas (Hindi dialects), Mirabai (Rajasthani and Braj), Tukaram and Jnaneshwar (Marathi), Chaitanya's followers (Bengali), Shankardev (Assamese), Alvars and Nayanars (Tamil), Basavanna's vachanas (Kannada).
  • Southern classics: Kamban's Tamil Ramayana; Pampa in Kannada; Krishnadevaraya's Amuktamalyada in Telugu.
  • Malayalam: the Chera kingdom of Mahodayapuram (9th century) used Malayalam language and script in its inscriptions, one of the earliest official uses of a regional language. The 14th-century Lilatilakam, on grammar and poetics, was written in Manipravalam ("diamonds and corals"), a blend of Sanskrit and the regional language.
  • Bengali: one stream drew on Sanskrit (translations of the epics, Mangalakavyas on local deities, lives of Chaitanya); another was oral and independent of it (Nath songs of Maynamati and Gopichandra, tales of Dharma Thakur, ballads).

I51.4 Modern literature and the Renaissance

  • Bharatendu Harishchandra (1850–1885), father of modern Hindi literature: Hindi prose in Khari Boli, the journals Kavivachan Sudha and Harishchandra Magazine, plays such as Andher Nagari and Bharat Durdasha that satirised colonial rule and social evils. The years 1868–1900 are called the Bharatendu Yug.
  • Bengali: Bankim Chandra's Anandamath (1882); Tagore's Gitanjali (Nobel Prize, 1913), the first Nobel for an Asian.
  • Urdu: Ghalib; Altaf Hussain Hali's Musaddas; Muhammad Iqbal.
  • Hindi–Urdu fiction: Premchand's realist stories and novels (Godan, 1936) on peasants and the poor.
  • Print, journals and reform debates made literature a vehicle of the Indian Renaissance (I35).

What historians say

A.L. Basham (The Wonder That Was India) surveys classical literature. Romila Thapar uses the historical plays and chronicles as sources while warning about their poetic purpose. Ramchandra Shukla (Hindi Sahitya ka Itihas, 1929) named the Bharatendu period and set the periods of Hindi literature.

Prelims fact bank

  • The Mahabharata is attributed to Vyasa and the Ramayana to Valmiki.
  • The Buddhist Tripitaka is in Pali.
  • Silappadikaram and Manimekalai are post-Sangam Tamil epics.
  • Svapnavasavadatta was written by Bhasa.
  • Malavikagnimitram by Kalidasa features the Shunga king Agnimitra.
  • Mudrarakshasa by Vishakhadatta tells how Chanakya won over Rakshasa for Chandragupta Maurya.
  • Harshacharita was written by Banabhatta.
  • Kalhana wrote the Rajatarangini in 1148–49.
  • The Baburnama was written in Turki.
  • Tulsidas wrote the Ramcharitmanas in Awadhi.
  • The Lilatilakam (14th century) is written in Manipravalam, a mix of Sanskrit and Malayalam.
  • The Cheras of Mahodayapuram used Malayalam in their 9th-century inscriptions.
  • Bharatendu Harishchandra started the journal Kavivachan Sudha in 1868.
  • Anandamath by Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay was published in 1882.
  • Tagore received the Nobel Prize for Literature in 1913.
  • Premchand's novel Godan was published in 1936.
Exam traps
  • Mudrarakshasa and Devichandraguptam are both by Vishakhadatta; Devichandraguptam survives only in fragments.
  • Harshacharita is prose by Bana; Ratnavali and Nagananda are plays by Harsha himself.
  • Baburnama is in Turki, not Persian; its Persian translation was made under Akbar.
  • Ramcharitmanas is in Awadhi, not Braj; Surdas wrote in Braj.

Previous-year questions (RAS)

ExamQuestionMarks
RAS Mains 2016Write a short note on the historical plays of Sanskrit literature.5
RAS Mains 2018Bharatendu Harishchandra was the precursor of modern trends in Hindi literature. Discuss.5

Questions as printed in the RPSC Mains papers (GS Paper I), lightly edited for grammar.

Mains answer writing

5 marks · 50 words

Write a short note on the historical plays of Sanskrit literature.

How to frame it: Examples with authors → Value as sources

Model answer (34 words)

Bhasa's Svapnavasavadatta (Udayana), Kalidasa's Malavikagnimitram (Agnimitra Shunga), and Vishakhadatta's Mudrarakshasa (Chanakya and Chandragupta) and Devichandraguptam (Chandragupta II) dramatise history. They add political detail but mix fact with poetry, so must be checked against inscriptions.

5 marks · 50 words

Bharatendu Harishchandra was the precursor of modern trends in Hindi literature. Discuss.

How to frame it: Khari Boli prose → Journals → Plays with social and national themes → Legacy

Model answer (38 words)

He made Khari Boli the language of Hindi prose, founded journals like Kavivachan Sudha (1868), and wrote plays such as Bharat Durdasha and Andher Nagari attacking colonial exploitation and social evils. The period 1868–1900 is named after him.

2 marks · 15 words

What is the Rajatarangini?

How to frame it: Answer

Model answer (10 words)

Kalhana's 12th-century Sanskrit verse chronicle of the kings of Kashmir.

Revision card

Remember
  • Vedas → Brahmanas → Aranyakas → Upanishads; epics; Puranas; Pali Tripitaka; Sangam, Tirukkural.
  • Historical plays: Svapnavasavadatta (Bhasa), Malavikagnimitram (Kalidasa), Mudrarakshasa and Devichandraguptam (Vishakhadatta); Harshacharita; Rajatarangini 1148.
  • Medieval: Persian (Khusrau, Barani, Abul Fazl), Turki Baburnama, Bhakti in regional languages, Ramcharitmanas (Awadhi).
  • Modern: Bharatendu (1868 journal), Anandamath 1882, Gitanjali Nobel 1913, Godan 1936.

    Show answer

    Banabhatta.

    Turki.

    Show answer

    Kavivachan Sudha.

    Show answer

    Gitanjali, 1913.
